Blow-Up Dynamics for the L 2 L^{2} -critical case of the Two-Dimensional Zakharov-Kuznetsov equation
Francisc Bozgan, Tej-Eddine Ghoul, Nader Masmoudi, Kai Yang

Abstract
We study blow-up dynamics for the L 2 L^{2} -critical cubic Zakharov–Kuznetsov equation in two dimensions, ∂ t u + ∂ x 1 ( Δ ​ u + u 3 ) = 0 on ​ ℝ 2 . \partial_{t}u+\partial_{x_{1}}(\Delta u+u^{3})=0\qquad\text{on }\mathbb{R}^{2}. For a class of localized H 1 H^{1} perturbations of the ground state Q Q , we establish a trichotomy near the soliton manifold: exit from a small L 2 L^{2} -tube, global asymptotic stability, or finite-time blow-up. In the stable blow-up regime, the solution concentrates a single bubble and λ ⁡ ( t ) ∼ ℓ 0 ​ ( T − t ) 1 / ( 3 − c ) , \lambda(t)\sim\ell_{0}(T-t)^{1/(3-c)}, where ℓ 0 > 0 \ell_{0}>0 depends on the initial datum and c ∈ ( 1 , 2 ) c\in(1,2) is an explicit constant determined by the transverse tail of the first-order approximate profile. Consequently, ‖ ∇ u ​ ( t ) ‖ L 2 ∼ ‖ ∇ Q ‖ L 2 ℓ 0 ​ ( T − t ) 1 / ( 3 − c ) . \|\nabla u(t)\|_{L^{2}}\sim\frac{\|\nabla Q\|_{L^{2}}}{\ell_{0}(T-t)^{1/(3-c)}}. After subtraction of the concentrating soliton, the radiation converges strongly in L p ​ ( ℝ 2 ) L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2}) for every 2 ≤ p < ∞ 2\leq p<\infty to a common nonzero profile u ∗ u^{*} , while u ∗ ∉ H s ​ ( ℝ 2 ) for every ​ s ≥ c 2 . u^{*}\notin H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\qquad\text{for every }s\geq\frac{c}{2}. The stable blow-up branch is open in the relative H 1 H^{1} topology of the localized class. Finally, every non-soliton datum in this class with non-positive energy blows up in finite time. Interval-arithmetic computer-assisted proofs certify the numerical inputs to the virial coercivity argument. They also yield a rigorous enclosure of c c , justifying the polynomial moment of order 21 21 imposed on the initial data.



Chapter 1
Introduction

The generalized Zakharov-Kuznetsov (gZK) equation with initial data
u_{0}
reads as

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}u+\partial_{x_{1}}(\Delta u+u^{p})=0,\mbox{ }x=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N})\in\mathbb{R}^{N},\mbox{ }p\in\mathbb{N},p\geq 2,\mbox{ }t\geq 0,\\
u(0,x_{1},\ldots,x_{N})=u_{0}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N}),\end{cases}

(where
\Delta
is the Laplacian on
\mathbb{R}^{N}
) which is a higher-dimensional extension of the generalized Korteweg-de Vries (KdV) model for shallow water waves

\partial_{t}u+\partial_{x}(\partial_{x}^{2}u+u^{p})=0,\mbox{ }x\in\mathbb{R},\mbox{ }t\geq 0.

When
p=2
, the above equation is called the KdV equation and if
p=3
it is called the modified KdV equation.
In this book, we are interested in the gZK equation, which was originally derived by Zakharov and Kuznetsov to describe weakly magnetized ion-acoustic waves in a plasma comprising cold ions and hot isothermal electrons in the presence of a uniform magnetic field in
3
D
[
30
]
.
During the lifespan of the solution
u(t),
we have the conserved mass and energy: for
x\in\mathbb{R}^{N},

M[u(t)]=M[u(0)]=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}u(t,x)^{2}dx

and

E[u(t)]=E[u(0)]=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}|\nabla u(t,x)|^{2}dx-\frac{1}{p+1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}[u(t,x)]^{p+1}dx.

The scaling symmetry is
u_{\lambda}=\lambda^{\frac{2}{p-1}}u(\lambda^{3}t,\lambda x)
and

\|u_{\lambda}(0,\cdot)\|_{\dot{H}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}=\lambda^{\frac{2}{p-1}+s-\frac{N}{2}}\|u_{0}\|_{\dot{H}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{N})},

so the two-dimensional cubic ZK equation is
L^{2}
-critical when
s=0
.
From now on, we will discuss only the two-dimensional case, namely
N=2
and we call the spatial variables
(x_{1},x_{2}).

The generalized ZK equation has a family of traveling waves (or solitary waves, solitons) and they travel in the
x_{1}
-direction

u(t,x_{1},x_{2})=Q_{\kappa}(x_{1}-\kappa t,x_{2})

with
Q_{\kappa}(x_{1},x_{2})\rightarrow 0
as
|(x_{1},x_{2})|\rightarrow+\infty.
Here, for
\kappa>0
,
Q_{\kappa}
is the dilation of the ground state:

Q_{\kappa}(x)={\kappa}^{\frac{1}{p-1}}Q({\kappa}^{\frac{1}{2}}x),\mbox{ }x=(x_{1},x_{2}),

with
Q
being the unique radial positive solution in
H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
of the nonlinear elliptic equation
-\Delta Q+Q-Q^{p}=0
(for existence, see
[
54
]
,
[
3
]
, for uniqueness
[
32
]
). From
[
23
]
, we note that
Q\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),

\partial_{r}Q(r)<0
for any
r=|x|>0,
and for any multi-index
\alpha,

|\partial^{\alpha}Q(x)|\leq c(\alpha)e^{-|x|}\mbox{ for any }x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}.

In terms of the stability of the traveling wave, we start with the following definition.

Definition 1.1
.

Denote

U_{\alpha}=\Big\{u\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}):\inf_{\vec{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}\|u(\cdot)-Q(\cdot+\vec{y})\|_{H^{1}}\leq\alpha\Big\}.

We say that
Q
, the radially symmetric solution of
-\Delta Q+Q-Q^{p}=0
, is stable if for all
\alpha>0,
there exists
\delta>0
such that if
u_{0}\in U_{\delta},
then the corresponding solution
u(t)
is defined for all
t\geq 0
and
u(t)\in U_{\alpha}
for all
t\geq 0.
Otherwise, we say
Q
is unstable.

In her pivotal work on dispersive solitary waves across higher dimensions, de Bouard
[
14
]
demonstrated that in dimension two, the stability of traveling waves of the form
Q(x_{1}-t,x_{2})
depends critically on the nonlinearity exponent
p
. Specifically, these waves are stable for
p<1+4/N=3
and become unstable for
p>1+4/N=3
. This analysis draws upon foundational concepts developed by Bona, Souganidis, and Strauss
[
5
]
for instability, as well as stability frameworks by Grillakis, Shatah, and Strauss
[
24
]
. For more on the stability of the solutions, see
[
31
]
.

For the
L^{2}
-critical case in two dimensions, where
p=3
, Farah, Holmer, and Roudenko
[
21
]
applied methods initially established by Merle and Martel
[
37
]
to demonstrate the instability of solitons within this regime. Further explorations by the same group
[
20
]
offered an alternative proof of instability in the supercritical case for
p>3
, utilizing techniques adapted from Combet’s work
[
11
]
on the generalized KdV equation. Moreover, Côte, Muñoz, Pilod, and Simpson
[
12
]
proved the asymptotic stability for
p=2
.

From now on, we will consider only
p=3.

It follows from the conservation of the mass and energy, together with the sharp Gagliardo–Nirenberg inequality
[
56
]
, that if
\|u\|_{L^{2}}<\|Q\|_{L^{2}}
we have

\|\nabla u\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\leq\frac{2E(u)}{1-\frac{\|u\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}{\|Q\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}}.

In this case, we will have global well-posedness for the equation, thus blow-up can only occur in the case
\|u\|_{L^{2}}\geq\|Q\|_{L^{2}}.

The local well-posedness theory is well-known and we state the following known results:

Theorem 1.2
.

The cubic 2D ZK equation is locally well-posed in
H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, with
s\geq\frac{1}{4}.
(
[
53
]
,
[
27
]
). Moreover, if
\|u_{0}\|_{L^{2}}<\|Q\|_{L^{2}},
we have global well-posedness in
H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
in
[
34
]
, which was further improved to
H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
with
s>\frac{53}{63}
in
[
35
]
and
H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
with
s>\frac{3}{4}
in
[
4
]
.

We define
T>0
to be the maximal time of existence for the cubic 2D ZK equation. For the blow-up question of the gZK, we saw previously that we need
\|Q\|_{L^{2}}\leq\|u\|_{L^{2}}.
From the local well-posedness theory (
[
18
]
,
[
35
]
,
[
53
]
), if
T<+\infty,
then

\lim_{t\nearrow T}\|\nabla u(t)\|_{L^{2}_{x_{1}x_{2}}}=+\infty.

If
T=+\infty,
then either

\lim_{t\to+\infty}\|\nabla u(t)\|_{L^{2}_{x_{1}x_{2}}}=+\infty\mbox{ or }\liminf_{t\to+\infty}\|\nabla u(t)\|_{L^{2}_{x_{1}x_{2}}}<+\infty

are a priori possible. In either case
T<+\infty
or
T=+\infty,
we say that
u(t)
blows up at forward time
T
if

\liminf_{t\nearrow T}\|\nabla u(t)\|_{L^{2}_{x_{1}x_{2}}}=+\infty.

In terms of blow-up results for the
L^{2}
critical ZK equation, we have the following result:

Theorem 1.3
.

[
19
]
.
There exists
\alpha_{0}>0
such that the following holds. Suppose
u(t)
is a solution in
H^{1}
of the 2D cubic ZK equation with
E_{0}<0
and

0<\|u\|_{L^{2}}^{2}-\|Q\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq\alpha_{0}.

Then
u(t)
blows up in finite or infinite forward time.

Their proof is based on the blow-up analysis for the
L^{2}
critical gKdV equation in
[
50
]
and
[
41
]
.

Chapter 2
Main Result

2.1
Preliminaries and Motivation

In this section, we introduce the notation and preliminary concepts necessary for our main result. Let
Q
denote the unique, positive, radial solution in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
to the nonlinear elliptic equation

-\Delta Q+Q-Q^{3}=0.

This solution is fundamental in our analysis of the generalized Zakharov-Kuznetsov equation. Define
g_{Q}(x_{2})
as

g_{Q}(x_{2})=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}x_{2}Q_{x_{2}}(x_{1},x_{2})\,dx_{1}.

(2.1)

The Fourier transform of
g_{Q}
, denoted
\widehat{g_{Q}}(\xi)
, is taken over the real line
\mathbb{R}
.

The crucial constant
c
is then defined by the formula:

c=\frac{\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{2}{\xi^{2}+1}|\widehat{g_{Q}}(\xi)|^{2}\,d\xi}{\int_{\mathbb{R}}|\widehat{g_{Q}}(\xi)|^{2}\,d\xi}.

(2.2)

It is easy to see that
0<c<2
. We can say something even stronger.

Proposition 2.1
.

The constant
c
is strictly greater than
1
.

The proof is in Appendix
D
. For a sharper estimate, Theorem
E.2
provides the rigorous interval certificate

c\in[1.66126332085379,1.66170874135113]\subset\left(\frac{21}{13},\frac{5}{3}\right).

See Appendix
E
. Importantly, this constant
c
is related to the exponent of the blow-up rate in our main theorem concerning the blow-up behavior of solutions to the
L^{2}
-critical Zakharov-Kuznetsov equation in two dimensions,

\begin{cases}u_{t}+\partial_{x_{1}}(\Delta u+u^{3})=0,\quad(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2},t\geq 0,\\
u(0,x_{1},x_{2})=u_{0}(x_{1},x_{2})\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}).\end{cases}

(2.3)

The constant
c
determines the exponent
1/(3-c)
appearing in
the blow-up law stated below. Its analytic origin is tied to the
transverse structure of the first-order approximate profile and will
be derived in the subsequent modulation analysis.

We study the dynamics near the soliton and compare the resulting
blow-up rate with the numerical conjecture of Klein, Roudenko, and
Stoilov
[
28
]
. Their conjecture proposes the
exponent
1/2
for the
L^{2}
-critical generalized Zakharov–Kuznetsov
equation and is stated below.

Conjecture 2.2
.

Consider the critical 2D ZK equation (
2.3
). If
u_{0}\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
is sufficiently localized and
\|u_{0}\|_{L^{2}}>\|Q\|_{L^{2}},
then the solution blows up in finite time
T
such that as
t\rightarrow T

u(x_{1},x_{2},t)-\frac{1}{\lambda(t)}Q\Big(\frac{x_{1}-x_{1}(t)}{\lambda(t)},\frac{x_{2}-x_{2}(t)}{\lambda(t)}\Big)\rightarrow\widetilde{u}\in L^{2},

with

\|\partial_{x_{1}}u(t)\|_{L^{2}}\sim\frac{1}{(T-t)^{\frac{1}{2}}},\quad\lambda(t)\sim(T-t)^{\frac{1}{2}},\quad x_{1}(t)\sim\frac{1}{T-t},\quad x_{2}(t)\rightarrow y^{*}\in\mathbb{R}.

Our investigation targets specific initial data close to the ground state
Q
, the unique positive radial solution of the equation
-\Delta Q+Q-Q^{3}=0
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. For
i\in\mathbb{N}
, we define the set of initial conditions by:

\mathcal{A}_{\alpha_{0}}^{(i)}=\left\{u_{0}=Q+\varepsilon_{0}:\|\varepsilon_{0}\|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}<\alpha_{0}\text{ and }\int_{x_{1}>0}x_{1}^{i}\varepsilon_{0}^{2}(x_{1},x_{2})\,dx_{1}\,dx_{2}<1\right\}.

To analyze the behavior of solutions, we consider the
L^{2}
-modulated tube surrounding the soliton manifold:

\mathcal{T}_{\alpha^{*}}=\left\{u\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}):\inf_{\lambda_{0}>0,(\tilde{x}_{1},\tilde{x}_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left\|u-\frac{1}{\lambda_{0}}Q\left(\frac{\cdot-\tilde{x}_{1}}{\lambda_{0}},\frac{\cdot-\tilde{x}_{2}}{\lambda_{0}}\right)\right\|_{L^{2}_{x_{1}x_{2}}}<\alpha^{*}\right\}.

The constants
\alpha_{0}
and
\alpha^{*}
are chosen such that
0<\alpha_{0}\ll\alpha^{*}\ll 1
.

2.2
Main Theorem

Theorem 2.3
.

For universal constants
0<\alpha_{0}\ll\alpha^{*}\ll 1
, the following
conclusions hold for solutions
u(t)
of equation (
2.3
) with initial data
u_{0}\in\mathcal{A}_{\alpha_{0}}^{(21)}
:

(a)

If the energy
E(u_{0})\leq 0
and
u_{0}
is not the soliton up to symmetries, then
u(t)
blows up in finite time
T
, and for all
t\in[0,T)
,
u(t)
remains within
\mathcal{T}_{\alpha^{*}}
.

(b)

Assume that
u(t)
blows up in finite time
T
and remains
in
\mathcal{T}_{\alpha^{*}}
for every
t\in[0,T)
. Then there exists

\ell_{0}=\ell_{0}(u_{0})>0
such that, with
c
defined in
(
2.2
),

\|\nabla u(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\sim\frac{\|\nabla Q\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}}{\ell_{0}(T-t)^{1/(3-c)}}\qquad\text{as }t\uparrow T.

Moreover, there exist modulation parameters

\lambda(t)
,
x_{1}(t)
,
x_{2}(t)
and a nonzero function

u^{*}\in\bigcap_{2\leq p<\infty}L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2})

such that, for every
2\leq p<\infty
,

\left\|u(t,x_{1},x_{2})-\frac{1}{\lambda(t)}Q\left(\frac{x_{1}-x_{1}(t)}{\lambda(t)},\frac{x_{2}-x_{2}(t)}{\lambda(t)}\right)-u^{*}\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\longrightarrow 0\qquad\text{as }t\uparrow T.

The limiting radiation satisfies

\|u^{*}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}=\|u_{0}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}-\|Q\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2},

and

u^{*}\notin H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\qquad\text{for every }s\geq\frac{c}{2}.

Finally,

\lambda(t)\sim\ell_{0}(T-t)^{1/(3-c)},\qquad x_{1}(t)\sim\frac{3-c}{c-1}\,\frac{1}{\ell_{0}^{2}(T-t)^{(c-1)/(3-c)}},\qquad x_{2}(t)\longrightarrow x_{2,\infty}\in\mathbb{R}.

(c)

Openness of the stable blow-up branch. Let
T_{u_{0}}
denote the maximal
time of existence of the solution with initial datum
u_{0}
, and define

\mathcal{O}=\left\{u_{0}\in H^{1}:u(t)\in\mathcal{T}_{\alpha^{*}}\text{ for all }t\in[0,T_{u_{0}})\right\},

and denote the subset of solutions that blow up in finite time by

\mathcal{O}_{b}=\left\{u_{0}\in\mathcal{O}:T_{u_{0}}<\infty\text{ and }\|\nabla u(t)\|_{L^{2}}\to\infty\right\}.

Then

\mathcal{O}_{b}\cap\mathcal{A}_{\alpha_{0}}^{(21)}

is open in
\mathcal{A}_{\alpha_{0}}^{(21)}
for the relative
H^{1}

topology.

2.3
Detailed Comments

Comments:

(1).

There has been substantial progress concerning finite-time blow-up dynamics for the

L^{2}
-critical generalized Korteweg–de Vries equation

\begin{cases}u_{t}+\partial_{x}(u_{xx}+u^{5})=0,\quad x\in\mathbb{R},\ t\geq 0,\\
u(0,x)=u_{0}(x),\quad x\in\mathbb{R},\end{cases}

see
[
41
,
50
,
44
,
42
,
38
,
40
,
39
,
10
,
45
]
. The analysis in
[
38
]
yields stable finite-time blow-up near the ground state, asymptotic laws for the modulation
parameters, and stability of this regime under perturbations of the
initial data. These conclusions have counterparts in
Theorem
2.3
. The blow-up exponent and the regularity of
the limiting radiation differ, as described below.

(2).

The blow-up rate
\|\nabla u(t)\|_{L^{2}}
is significantly faster, behaving as
\frac{1}{(T-t)^{\frac{1}{3-c}}}
, compared to the self-similar blow-up rate of
\frac{1}{(T-t)^{\frac{1}{3}}}
. In particular, for
c>1
, the blow-up locus in the
x_{1}
direction recedes to infinity, while in the
x_{2}
direction it converges to a fixed point. This distinction underscores the critical role of the decay behavior in
x_{1}
at infinity in the initial data, a feature highlighted by the specific weighting in the variable
x_{1}
for
u_{0}\in\mathcal{A}_{\alpha_{0}}^{(21)}
. In contrast, the
x_{2}
variable does not require similar decay conditions. See Theorem
7.1
for more details. The weight
x_{1}^{21}
in the definition of
\mathcal{A}_{\alpha_{0}}^{(21)}
is not optimal. For example, observe that a smaller weight, i.e.
x_{1}^{18},
would be sufficient to prove the blow-up dynamics for the negative energy case. The moment of order
21
is required by the proof of blow-up for the zero energy case (see Remark
7.4
).

(3).

Employing the sharp Gagliardo–Nirenberg inequality
[
56
]
, we find:

\frac{1}{2}\|\nabla u\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\left(1-\frac{\|u_{0}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}}{\|Q\|^{2}_{L^{2}}}\right)\leq E_{0},

indicating that
E(u_{0})\leq 0
necessarily implies
\|u_{0}\|_{L^{2}}>\|Q\|_{L^{2}}
, unless
u_{0}
is equivalent to
\pm Q
up to scaling and translations. For data in
\mathcal{A}_{\alpha_{0}}^{(21)}
, Theorem
2.3
proves blow-up in the non-soliton case.

(4).

The behavior of the limiting radiation differs sharply from the

L^{2}
-critical gKdV case. In
[
38
]
, the asymptotic
radiation belongs to
H^{1}
, and strong convergence in
H^{1}
is
conjectured. Here, the radiation converges strongly in

L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
for every
2\leq p<\infty
, but the limiting profile
satisfies

u^{*}\notin H^{\varsigma}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\qquad\text{for every }\varsigma\geq\frac{c}{2}.

Thus the failure of strong
H^{1}
convergence is intrinsic: the terminal
profile itself does not belong to the energy space. The use of a
Duhamel–dispersive argument is closer in spirit to the NLS blow-up
analysis in
[
47
]
, but the regularity obstruction here
is genuinely two-dimensional. It is generated by the transverse tail of
the first-order profile and is detected in the anisotropic high-frequency
region

|\xi_{1}|\asymp|\xi_{2}|^{1-c},\qquad|\xi_{2}|\to\infty.

See Theorem
9.5
.

(5).

Conjecture
2
in
[
28
]
predicts, for the
L^{2}
-critical two-dimensional ZK equation, the blow-up rate
\|\partial_{x_{1}}u(t)\|_{L^{2}}\sim(T-t)^{-1/2}
.

In the stable near-soliton regime, the modulated decomposition and
(
7.13
) – (
7.14
) give

\lambda\|\partial_{x_{1}}u\|_{2}\to\|Q_{x_{1}}\|_{2}>0
and

\lambda\|\nabla u\|_{2}\to\|\nabla Q\|_{2}>0
. Thus the two derivative
norms have the same blow-up exponent. Consequently, the rate conjectured in
[
28
]
would correspond, in our notation, to the formal value
c=1.

For initial data in the perturbative class
\mathcal{A}_{\alpha_{0}}^{(21)}
, our analysis gives the constant
c
defined in (
2.2
), and the numerical computation yields
c\in[1.66126332085379,1.66170874135113],
hence
\frac{1}{3-c}\in[0.7469728853905,0.7472214987115]
.
In Appendix
E
we include the rigorous proof of the enclosure of
c.

The experiments in
[
28
]
include data such as

1.1Q
and
3e^{-x_{1}^{2}-x_{2}^{2}}
; their membership in the sufficiently
small class
\mathcal{A}_{\alpha_{0}}^{(21)}
is not established here.
The conjecture is based on numerical observations, whereas our result
concerns the stable perturbative regime near the ground state. In this
regime, the exponent is
1/(3-c)
and differs from
1/2
. This comparison
does not classify possible blow-up regimes outside that class.

(6).

This extends the result of
[
19
]
to the zero-energy case and provides the corresponding dynamical classification.

(7).

After the first version of this manuscript was posted, Chen et al. posted an article
[
9
]
deriving a similar result to ours. However, they use a different approach by considering the adjoint problem which leads to a different energy functional.

The following trichotomy describes the dynamics near the soliton, in the same three regimes as in the critical gKdV analysis of
[
38
]
.

Theorem 2.4
.

There exist universal constants
0<\alpha_{0}\ll\alpha^{*}\ll 1
such that the following holds. Let
u_{0}\in\mathcal{A}_{\alpha_{0}}^{(21)}.
Then, we have a trichotomy for the dynamics up to the first exit from the soliton tube, namely exactly one of the following three cases occurs:

(1)

(Exit of Tube) There exists
t^{*}\in(0,T)
such that
u(t^{*})\notin\mathcal{T}_{\alpha^{*}}.

(2)

(Stable Blow-up) For all
t\in[0,T),u(t)\in\mathcal{T}_{\alpha^{*}}
and the solution blows up in finite time
T<+\infty
in the way described by Theorem
2.3
.

(3)

(Asymptotic Stability) The solution is global, with
u(t)\in\mathcal{T}_{\alpha^{*}},
for all
t\geq 0
and there exists
\lambda_{\infty}>0,x_{1}(t)\in C^{1},x_{2,\infty}\in\mathbb{R}
such that

\lambda_{\infty}u(t,\lambda_{\infty}\cdot+x_{1}(t),\lambda_{\infty}\cdot+x_{2,\infty})\rightarrow Q\mbox{ in }H^{1}_{loc}\mbox{ as }t\rightarrow+\infty,

with
|\lambda_{\infty}-1|\leq o_{\alpha_{0}\rightarrow 0}(1)
and
x_{1}(t)\sim\frac{t}{\lambda_{\infty}^{2}}
as
t\rightarrow+\infty.

Notation.
Throughout this book, we denote the spatial variable by
x=(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and the time variable by
t
. We reserve
y=(y_{1},y_{2})
and
s
for the rescaled spatial and time variables, respectively.

We define the linearized operator
L
around the ground state
Q
by

L=-\Delta+1-3Q^{2}.

We also introduce the scaling operator
\Lambda
, defined as

\Lambda f=f+x\cdot\nabla f=f+x_{1}\partial_{x_{1}}f+x_{2}\partial_{x_{2}}f.

Regarding asymptotic parameters, for any
0<\alpha\ll 1
, the notation
\delta(\alpha)
represents a generic small positive constant that satisfies

\lim_{\alpha\to 0}\delta(\alpha)=0.

We write
H^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})=\bigcap_{m\geq 0}H^{m}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
.
Finally, unless otherwise specified,
(\cdot,\cdot)
denotes the standard scalar product of real-valued functions in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
:

(f,g)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}f(x)g(x)\,dx.

2.4
Heuristics and Construction of the Approximate Profile

The proof relies on the construction of an approximate blow-up profile that captures the self-similar nature of the collapse. We seek an approximate solution to the Zakharov-Kuznetsov (ZK) equation of the form:

u(t,x_{1},x_{2})\approx\frac{1}{\lambda(t)}Q_{b(t)}\left(\frac{x_{1}-x_{1}(t)}{\lambda(t)},\frac{x_{2}-x_{2}(t)}{\lambda(t)}\right).

Here, the dynamics of the scaling parameter
\lambda(t)
, the translation parameters
(x_{1}(t),x_{2}(t))
, and the auxiliary scaling parameter
b(t)
are governed by the system:

\frac{ds}{dt}=\frac{1}{\lambda^{3}(t)},\quad b=-\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda},\quad\frac{(x_{1})_{s}}{\lambda}=1,\quad\frac{(x_{2})_{s}}{\lambda}=0.

Substituting this ansatz into (
2.3
) yields an approximate self-similar equation for the profile
Q_{b}
:

b_{s}\frac{\partial Q_{b}}{\partial b}+b\Lambda Q_{b}+(\Delta Q_{b}-Q_{b}+Q_{b}^{3})_{x_{1}}=0,

where
\Lambda
is the scaling operator. To ensure solvability, we expand
Q_{b}
and the evolution law for
b
into powers of
b
:

Q_{b}=Q+bP_{1}+b^{2}P_{2}+\ldots,\quad b_{s}=-a_{0}-a_{1}b-a_{2}b^{2}-\ldots

By matching the powers of
b
, we determine the coefficients:

•

At
O(1)
:
The equation reduces to
(\Delta Q-Q+Q^{3})_{x_{1}}=0
. Since
Q
is the ground state solitary wave, this is satisfied, implying
a_{0}=0
.

•

At
O(b)
:
The linearized equation is
a_{1}P_{1}+(LP_{1})_{x_{1}}-\Lambda Q=0
. Solvability requires orthogonality conditions.
After integrating in
y_{1}
, solvability of the equation for
P
requires the resulting right-hand side to be orthogonal to
\operatorname{ker}L=\operatorname{span}\{Q_{y_{1}},Q_{y_{2}}\}
. The
Q_{y_{1}}
-condition follows from the critical identity
(\Lambda Q,Q)=0
, while the
Q_{y_{2}}
-condition follows by parity. This simplifies to
a_{1}=0
and defines
P_{1}
via
(LP_{1})_{x_{1}}=\Lambda Q
. Note that
P_{1}
is not in
L^{2}
, necessitating the use of cut-off functions later.

•

At
O(b^{2})
:
The equation is

-a_{2}P_{1}+\Lambda P_{1}+(3QP_{1}^{2})_{x_{1}}-(LP_{2})_{x_{1}}=0.

Pairing with
Q
gives the solvability condition

a_{2}(P_{1},Q)=\bigl(\Lambda P_{1}+(3QP_{1}^{2})_{x_{1}},Q\bigr),

which fixes
a_{2}=c>0
according to (
2.2
).

Consequently, the formal dynamics are encapsulated by the reduced system:

b_{s}+cb^{2}=0,\quad b=-\lambda_{s}\lambda^{-1}.

Integrating this system elucidates the phase portrait. If we set
\lambda(0)=1
, we observe a trichotomy based on the initial scaling velocity
b_{0}
:

•

b_{0}<0
:

\lambda(t)
grows indefinitely (Exit Case).

•

b_{0}=0
:

\lambda(t)\equiv 1
(Threshold/Soliton Case).

•

b_{0}>0
:
Finite time blow-up occurs at
T=[(3-c)b_{0}]^{-1}
, with the blow-up rate
\lambda(t)\sim(T-t)^{\frac{1}{3-c}}
.

2.5
Outline of the Proof

The proof follows the modulation–monotonicity strategy developed for the
mass-critical generalized Korteweg–de Vries equation, with several genuinely
two-dimensional modifications. In the gKdV problem, the nonlocalized part of
the first-order profile has a constant tail and leads to

b_{s}+2b^{2}=0.

Here the left-hand tail depends on the transverse variable
y_{2}
; the
associated transverse elliptic equation produces the constant
c\in(1,2)

and the formal law

b_{s}+cb^{2}=0.

This changes the physical blow-up rate from
\lambda(t)\sim T-t
to

\lambda(t)\sim(T-t)^{\frac{1}{3-c}}.

Throughout this outline,
\|\varepsilon\|_{H^{1}_{w}}
and

\|\varepsilon\|_{L^{2}_{w}}
denote the weighted norms introduced below; they
measure the radiation near the soliton and propagate its polynomial
localization in the positive
y_{1}
-direction.

Decomposition and Orthogonality Conditions

As long as the solution remains in a sufficiently small
L^{2}
-neighborhood
of the soliton manifold, we write

u(t,x_{1},x_{2})=\frac{1}{\lambda(t)}\left(Q_{b(t)}+\varepsilon\right)\left(s,\frac{x_{1}-x_{1}(t)}{\lambda(t)},\frac{x_{2}-x_{2}(t)}{\lambda(t)}\right),\qquad\frac{ds}{dt}=\frac{1}{\lambda^{3}(t)}.

The approximate profile is

Q_{b}(y)=Q(y)+b\chi\left(|b|^{\gamma}y_{1}\right)P(y),\qquad 0<\gamma<1,

where

(LP)_{y_{1}}=\Lambda Q,\qquad L=-\Delta+1-3Q^{2}.

The cut-off is needed because
P
has a nonzero left-hand tail and is not in

L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
.

The four parameters
\lambda,b,x_{1},x_{2}
are fixed by

(\varepsilon,Q)=(\varepsilon,\varphi Q_{y_{2}})=(\varepsilon,\varphi\Lambda Q)=(\varepsilon,\varphi Q_{y_{1}})=0,\qquad\varphi(y_{1})=1+e^{y_{1}/\alpha_{1}}.

(2.4)

This choice serves simultaneously in the modulation decomposition and in the
coercivity estimates for both
L
and the virial operator introduced below.
The direction
Q
determines
b
, since
(P,Q)>0
, and removes the
negative direction of
L
; the odd direction
\varphi Q_{y_{2}}
determines

x_{2}
; and
\varphi\Lambda Q
,
\varphi Q_{y_{1}}
determine
\lambda

and
x_{1}
. Their nondegeneracy is encoded by

M^{*}=\begin{pmatrix}(\Lambda Q,\varphi\Lambda Q)&(Q_{y_{1}},\varphi\Lambda Q)\\
(\Lambda Q,\varphi Q_{y_{1}})&(Q_{y_{1}},\varphi Q_{y_{1}})\end{pmatrix}.

Indeed, the Jacobian determinant factors, up to sign, as

(P,Q)(Q_{y_{2}},\varphi Q_{y_{2}})\det M^{*},

and all three factors are nonzero.

Differentiating (
2.4
) gives the rough modulation bounds

\left|\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda}+b\right|+\left|\frac{(x_{1})_{s}}{\lambda}-1\right|+\left|\frac{(x_{2})_{s}}{\lambda}\right|\lesssim b^{2}+\|\varepsilon\|_{H^{1}_{w}}.

These equations contain terms linear in the localized radiation. Projecting
instead against suitable primitive and localized functions gives the sharper
law

|b_{s}+cb^{2}|\lesssim|b|^{3}+|b|\|\varepsilon\|_{L^{2}_{w}}+\|\varepsilon\|_{H^{1}_{w}}^{2}.

Thus the geometric modulation equations retain linear radiative errors, while
the equation governing the leading scaling dynamics has only quadratic or
higher-order forcing after correction.

Mixed Energy Functional and Dispersive Analysis

The radiation is controlled by mixed energy–virial functionals

\mathcal{F}_{i}(s)=\iint\left\{|\nabla\varepsilon|^{2}\psi(y_{1})+\varepsilon^{2}\phi_{i}(y_{1})-\frac{1}{2}\left[(Q_{b}+\varepsilon)^{4}-Q_{b}^{4}-4Q_{b}^{3}\varepsilon\right]\psi(y_{1})\right\}.

The gradient weight
\psi
and mass weights
\phi_{i}
decay exponentially
on the left, while near the soliton
\phi_{i}^{\prime}
is proportional to

\varphi^{\prime}
, reproducing the virial form associated with the same weight

\varphi
as in (
2.4
). On the right,
\phi_{i}
grows
polynomially to propagate the localization of the initial data. They are also chosen
to control the scaling drift
(\lambda_{s}/\lambda)\Lambda\varepsilon
in the
derivative of
\mathcal{F}_{i}/\lambda^{j}
. The improved modulation equations
are obtained separately by projection; the role of the mixed energy estimate
is to make their radiative errors integrable.

For the relevant
j\geq 0
, we prove

\frac{d}{ds}\left(\frac{\mathcal{F}_{i}}{\lambda^{j}}\right)+\frac{\|\varepsilon\|_{H^{1}_{w}}^{2}}{\lambda^{j}}\lesssim\frac{b^{4}}{\lambda^{j}}.

The source
b^{4}
comes from the localized profile error. The case
j=0

gives scale-invariant spacetime control, while positive
j
yield the
stronger estimates required as
\lambda
becomes small.

This functional differs from that of
[
38
]
. In the gKdV
argument, the weighted mass term carries an auxiliary time-dependent factor
that cancels an interaction between the scaling dynamics and slowly localized
modulation quantities. Here the weights and the direct treatment of the
scaling term in
d(\mathcal{F}_{i}/\lambda^{j})/ds
allow us to work without such
an internal correction. Two separate spectral inputs are used: coercivity of

L
makes
\mathcal{F}_{i}
equivalent to the weighted
H^{1}
-norm, whereas
coercivity of the anisotropic virial operator produces the negative spacetime
term.

Virial Estimate and Coercivity

On the soliton core, the principal quadratic form is

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{\varphi}(\varepsilon)=\iint\Big[

\displaystyle(3\varepsilon_{y_{1}}^{2}+\varepsilon_{y_{2}}^{2})\varphi^{\prime}+\left(\varphi^{\prime}-\varphi^{\prime\prime\prime}-3Q^{2}\varphi^{\prime}+6QQ_{y_{1}}\varphi\right)\varepsilon^{2}\Big].

The coefficients
3
and
1
reflect the anisotropy of the ZK dispersion.
For a general increasing
\varphi
, the conjugation

v=\sqrt{\varphi^{\prime}}\,\varepsilon

identifies this form with the self-adjoint operator

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\varphi}={}

\displaystyle-3\partial_{y_{1}}^{2}-\partial_{y_{2}}^{2}+1+\frac{1}{2}\frac{\varphi^{\prime\prime\prime}}{\varphi^{\prime}}-\frac{3}{4}\left(\frac{\varphi^{\prime\prime}}{\varphi^{\prime}}\right)^{2}-3Q^{2}+6QQ_{y_{1}}\frac{\varphi}{\varphi^{\prime}}.

For
\varphi(y_{1})=1+e^{y_{1}/\alpha_{1}}
, this becomes

\mathcal{L}=-3\partial_{y_{1}}^{2}-\partial_{y_{2}}^{2}+\left(1-\frac{1}{4\alpha_{1}^{2}}\right)-3Q^{2}+6\alpha_{1}\left(1+e^{-y_{1}/\alpha_{1}}\right)QQ_{y_{1}}.

Since
\alpha_{1}>1
,

\varphi^{\prime}-\varphi^{\prime\prime\prime}=\left(1-\frac{1}{\alpha_{1}^{2}}\right)\varphi^{\prime}>0,\qquad 1-\frac{1}{4\alpha_{1}^{2}}>0.

Choosing
\alpha_{1}
sufficiently close to one also places all weighted
modulation directions in
H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, and

\frac{\varphi}{\varphi^{\prime}}=\alpha_{1}\left(1+e^{-y_{1}/\alpha_{1}}\right).

This ratio is bounded on the right, where
QQ_{y_{1}}<0
, and large on the
left, where
QQ_{y_{1}}>0
; hence the negative part of the potential is
controlled while the favorable part is reinforced. Its precise value is also
chosen so that the finite-dimensional inverse-Gram condition below has the
required sign.

The comparison with the gKdV virial argument is useful. For the linear weight

\varphi(y)=y
, the one-dimensional computation gives

\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{gKdV}}=-3\partial_{y}^{2}+1-5Q^{4}+20yQ^{3}Q_{y},

the operator used in
[
38
]
. That weight cannot be used in
the ZK problem: by parity,

(Q_{y_{2}},y_{1}Q_{y_{2}})=0,

so the transverse translation would not be determined, and

6y_{1}QQ_{y_{1}}<0
on both sides of the soliton. The positive exponential
weight avoids both defects.

The classical one-constraint Weinstein criterion in
[
57
]
treats an operator with one
negative direction. Since
\mathcal{L}
has two negative directions while the
modulation decomposition provides four constraints, we use the following
generalized form, stated precisely in
Proposition
B.1
. Let
T
be self-adjoint
with an
n
-dimensional negative spectral subspace. For constraint
vectors
f_{1},\ldots,f_{m}\in\operatorname{Ran}T
, choose

g_{k}\in D(T)
with
Tg_{k}=f_{k}
, and form

G_{k\ell}=(g_{k},f_{\ell}).

This form is independent of the preimages: their differences belong to

\ker T
, which is orthogonal to
\operatorname{Ran}T
. If
G
has an

n
-dimensional strictly negative subspace, the constraints

(u,f_{k})=0
remove the negative spectral directions. Kernel directions
are treated separately as in the cited proposition. In the virial
application,
0\in\rho(T)
and the preimages are
T^{-1}f_{k}
; the
spectral gap then yields strict coercivity. This formulation also
applies to the linearized operator
L
, whose translation kernel is
removed by the additional orthogonality conditions.

After conjugation, the four conditions become

(v,f_{1})=(v,f_{2})=(v,f_{3})=(v,f_{o})=0,

where, up to harmless normalizations,

\displaystyle f_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{Q}{\sqrt{\varphi^{\prime}}},

\displaystyle f_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{\varphi Q_{y_{1}}}{\sqrt{\varphi^{\prime}}},

\displaystyle f_{3}

\displaystyle=\frac{\varphi\Lambda Q}{\sqrt{\varphi^{\prime}}},

\displaystyle f_{o}

\displaystyle=\frac{\varphi Q_{y_{2}}}{\sqrt{\varphi^{\prime}}}.

(2.5)

The first three functions are even in
y_{2}
, whereas
f_{o}
is odd, and

\mathcal{L}
preserves this decomposition. The spectral analysis shows that

\mathcal{L}
has exactly two negative eigenvalues, one in each parity
sector, and no zero eigenvalue. In the odd sector,

(\mathcal{L}^{-1}f_{o},f_{o})<0.

In the even sector, the matrix

\left((\mathcal{L}^{-1}f_{k},f_{\ell})\right)_{1\leq k,\ell\leq 3}

has a negative direction, so there exists

0\neq f_{e}\in\operatorname{span}\{f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}\}
with

(\mathcal{L}^{-1}f_{e},f_{e})<0.

Because
v
is orthogonal to
f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}
, it is also orthogonal to

f_{e}
. Parity gives
(\mathcal{L}^{-1}f_{e},f_{o})=0
; hence the inverse Gram matrix on

\operatorname{span}\{f_{e},f_{o}\}
is negative definite. The generalized
criterion yields

(\mathcal{L}v,v)\gtrsim\|v\|_{H^{1}}^{2},

and undoing the conjugation gives coercivity of
\mathcal{B}_{\varphi}
.
The spectral and inverse-pairing certificates are given in
Appendix
E
; the abstract criterion and its application to

L
and
\mathcal{L}
are proved in Appendix
B
.

Rigidity and Asymptotic Dynamics

The refined modulation equations produce a corrected quantity
\mathcal{G}(s)
,
obtained by multiplying
b/\lambda^{c}
by a positive factor of the form

1+O\bigl(\|\varepsilon(s)\|_{L^{2}_{w}}\bigr),

such that

\int_{0}^{s^{*}}|\mathcal{G}^{\prime}(s)|\,ds<\infty,

where
s^{*}
is the endpoint of the modulation interval. Hence, for some
c_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
,

\mathcal{G}(s)\longrightarrow c_{0}\qquad\text{as }s\to s^{*}.

Since the correction factor is positive and close to one, the sign of
c_{0}

selects the three regimes.

If
c_{0}>0
, the refined modulation estimates make the error in the scaling equation
o(b/\lambda^{2})
. Then eventually
b>0
and

b(t)\sim c_{0}\lambda^{c}(t),\qquad\lambda_{t}\sim-\frac{b}{\lambda^{2}}\sim-c_{0}\lambda^{c-2}.

Because
c<3
,
\lambda
reaches zero at a finite time
T
, with

\lambda(t)\sim\ell_{0}(T-t)^{\frac{1}{3-c}}.

Integration of the remaining modulation equations gives the asymptotics of

b(t)
,
x_{1}(t)
, and
x_{2}(t)
. This is the stable blow-up regime.

If
c_{0}=0
, the weighted estimates imply

b(t)\to 0,\qquad\|\varepsilon(t)\|_{H^{1}_{w}}\to 0,\qquad\lambda(t)\to\lambda_{\infty}>0.

Moreover,

x_{1}(t)\sim\frac{t}{\lambda_{\infty}^{2}},

and
x_{2}(t)
converges. This is the asymptotic-stability regime. If

c_{0}<0
, then eventually
b<0
, the scale increases, and the solution is
driven to the boundary of the prescribed tube: this is the Exit regime.

The organization differs from
[
38
]
. There, a separation
or domination time is introduced: after it,

|b|\gg\|\varepsilon\|_{H^{1}_{w}}^{2}

is trapped, and the sign of
b
distinguishes blow-up from exit; if no such
time exists, the solution belongs to the soliton regime. Here the primary
organizer is instead the limit of the corrected quantity comparable to

b/\lambda^{c}
. Domination estimates are still used locally, notably in the
case
c_{0}=0
and in the proof of openness, but they do not define the
classification.

For openness, choose a late time along a reference blow-up solution at which

b/\lambda^{c}
has a fixed positive margin and the weighted radiation is much
smaller. Continuous dependence of the flow and modulation parameters, together
with propagation of the high spatial moment, transfers these inequalities to
nearby data; a trapping argument then preserves the positive sign and forces
the same finite-time blow-up regime.

Asymptotic Radiation: Strong
L^{p}
Convergence and Sobolev Obstruction

In the blow-up regime, write

u=\mathcal{Q}+\widetilde{u},

where

\mathcal{Q}(t,x)=\frac{1}{\lambda(t)}Q_{b(t)}\left(\frac{x_{1}-x_{1}(t)}{\lambda(t)},\frac{x_{2}-x_{2}(t)}{\lambda(t)}\right)

is the concentrating approximate profile and

\widetilde{u}(t,x)=\frac{1}{\lambda(t)}\varepsilon\left(t,\frac{x_{1}-x_{1}(t)}{\lambda(t)},\frac{x_{2}-x_{2}(t)}{\lambda(t)}\right)

is the physical radiation. It satisfies a forced ZK equation whose
source contains the approximate-profile error and the modulation errors.

Rather than propagating localized
H^{1}
monotonicity estimates
progressively farther from the soliton, as in

[
38
]
, we compare two time translates of the
radiation through Duhamel’s formula, following ideas from the

L^{2}
-supercritical NLS analysis of
[
46
]
and the

L^{2}
-supercritical gKdV analysis of
[
33
]
. The refined
inhomogeneous Strichartz estimate of
[
22
]
, together with the
sharp modulation asymptotics and the weighted spacetime bounds, shows
that
\widetilde{u}(t)
is Cauchy in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
as
t\uparrow T
.
The interpolated dispersive estimate for the linear ZK group, applied on
a shrinking terminal time interval, extends the same argument to every

2<p<\infty
. Hence there exists a common profile

u^{*}\in\bigcap_{2\leq p<\infty}L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2})

such that
\widetilde{u}(t)\to u^{*}
strongly in every finite
L^{p}
.
The profile bounds and
b\sim c_{0}\lambda^{c}
also give, in the physical
variables, for
2\leq p<\infty
,

\left\|\mathcal{Q}(t,\cdot)-\frac{1}{\lambda(t)}Q\!\left(\frac{\cdot-x(t)}{\lambda(t)}\right)\right\|_{L^{p}}\longrightarrow 0.

Thus subtracting the exact rescaled ground state gives the same limit.

Mass decoupling gives

\|u^{*}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}=\|u_{0}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}-\|Q\|_{L^{2}}^{2}.

In the gKdV problem, the possible minimal-mass case is excluded using
the classification in
[
43
]
; here minimal mass is excluded within
the stable blow-up branch, see Remark
7.5
. Consequently,

u^{*}\not\equiv 0
.

We then determine the Sobolev regularity of the radiation. A
quantitative expansion of the left-hand tail of
P
isolates the leading
cutoff-transition contribution to the profile error. After passing the
Duhamel formula to the terminal time in
L^{2}
on bounded frequency
regions, a uniform phase analysis identifies a nonvanishing Fourier
component in the anisotropic region

|\xi_{2}|\sim R,\qquad|\xi_{1}|\sim R^{1-c}.

The leading contribution has
L^{2}_{\xi}
size comparable to
R^{-c/2}
,
whereas the initial radiation, the remaining profile and modulation
forcing, and the nonlinear Duhamel term are all of smaller order. A
dyadic summation therefore gives

u^{*}\notin H^{\varsigma}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\qquad\text{for every }\varsigma\geq\frac{c}{2};

see Theorem
9.5
. In particular,
u^{*}\notin H^{1}

because
c<2
. This is a genuinely two-dimensional difference from the
critical gKdV result, where the limiting radiation belongs to
H^{1}
.

Non-positive Energy

A variational compactness argument first excludes the Exit regime when

E_{0}\leq 0
, leaving only blow-up and asymptotic stability. If
E_{0}<0
, the
latter would imply, by conservation of energy and decay of the radiation,

\lambda^{2}(t)|E_{0}|+\|\nabla\varepsilon(t)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\longrightarrow 0.

This forces
\lambda(t)\to 0
, contradicting

\lambda(t)\to\lambda_{\infty}>0
; hence the solution blows up in finite time.

For
E_{0}=0
, energy conservation under the asymptotic-stability hypothesis
gives only

\|\nabla\varepsilon(t)\|_{L^{2}}\longrightarrow 0.

To recover the missing
L^{2}
-compactness, we study the localized mass

\iint\varepsilon^{2}(t,y)\varrho_{D}(y_{1})\,dy,

where
\varrho_{D}
localizes on a longitudinal scale
D
. Its physical-time
derivative contains transport, scaling, profile, and nonlinear terms, all
controlled by the refined modulation equations and weighted spacetime
estimates. Repeated time integration, the hierarchy of polynomially weighted
bounds, the high right-hand moment, and a localized interpolation estimate
allow first
t\to\infty
and then
D\to\infty
, yielding

\|\varepsilon(t)\|_{L^{2}}\longrightarrow 0.

Mass conservation gives
\|u_{0}\|_{L^{2}}=\|Q\|_{L^{2}}
, and equality in the
sharp Gagliardo–Nirenberg inequality, together with
E(u_{0})=0
, places

u_{0}
on the solitary-wave orbit generated by scaling, translations, and
sign. Therefore every non-soliton datum with non-positive energy belongs to the
finite-time blow-up branch.

The endpoint is analogous to the zero-energy classification in

[
38
]
: asymptotic stability forces critical mass, after
which the variational characterization identifies a soliton. The compactness
mechanism is different. The gKdV proof obtains uniform spatial tails for the
physical solution from localized mass and energy monotonicity; here we work
directly with the localized mass of the renormalized error and combine its
evolution with the hierarchy of weighted energy estimates and the high
right-hand moment.

2.6
Interval-Arithmetic Verification of the Numerical Inputs

The virial argument above and the choice of the polynomial weight in

\mathcal{A}_{\alpha_{0}}^{(21)}
rest on three numerical facts: the
operator
\mathcal{L}
has exactly two negative eigenvalues and a
quantitative spectral gap; the even inverse Gram matrix
A
and the
odd inverse pairing
m_{oo}
introduced below satisfy
\det A<0
and

m_{oo}<0
; and the constant
c
lies in
(21/13,5/3)
. These facts
are verified in Appendix
E
by interval arithmetic, and
they are collected in Theorem
E.2
. The verification
has two branches. The first
determines the negative index and a spectral gap of
\mathcal{L}
and
encloses the inverse pairings required by the generalized Weinstein
criterion of Proposition
B.1
; the second
encloses the constant
c
governing the blow-up rate. We now describe
what the interval certificates recorded in that appendix establish.
Every error bound includes rounding and truncation errors, and every
integral over an unbounded region is accompanied by an explicit
estimate of its tail.

We first describe the enclosure of the ground state; see
Section
E.1
. Writing
r=|y|
, the radial profile solves

Q^{\prime\prime}+\frac{1}{r}Q^{\prime}-Q+Q^{3}=0,\qquad Q^{\prime}(0)=0.

The construction treats the three intervals
[0,r_{0}]
,
[r_{0},R]
, and

[R,\infty)
separately, the value
a=Q(0)
being initially unknown.
Near the origin, the regular solution is represented by an even power
series whose first coefficient is
a
; substitution into the equation
determines every subsequent coefficient, and a convergent geometric
majorant bounds the entire omitted series together with its derivatives.
This gives initial interval enclosures at a positive radius
r_{0}

without evaluating the singular coefficient
1/r
at zero.

On
[r_{0},R]
, the equation is a regular first-order system for
Q
and

Q^{\prime}
. The CAPD solver encloses the solution on each accepted step and bounds its Taylor coefficients and its entire order-
21
remainder. The resulting Taylor models therefore enclose every point of the step, and their endpoint enclosures initialize the next step; see Lemma
E.3
. The calculation starts
at
r_{0}=1/512
and reaches
R=15
through
184
cells with Taylor order
20
. A simultaneous integration of the parameter sensitivity encloses the Jacobian needed for matching, and the exported profile is recomputed on the validated matching interval.

On
[R,\infty)
, the decaying solution is constructed from the modified
Bessel functions: an integral equation expresses it as a multiple of the
decaying linear solution
K_{0}
plus a nonlinear correction, and the
corresponding map is a contraction in the weighted norm

\sup_{r\geq R}e^{r}\sqrt{r}\,|Q(r)|
on an explicitly specified ball. Its
fixed point therefore exists and is unique there, and it satisfies
bounds of the form
|Q^{(j)}(r)|\leq C_{j}e^{-r}/\sqrt{r}
. These estimates
control the whole exterior interval, so that the computation does not
stop at a large finite radius.

The interior parameter
a
and the tail parameter are then determined by
matching the values of
Q
and
Q^{\prime}
at
R
. Differentiating the interior
and the tail equations with respect to their parameters encloses the
Jacobian of this two-dimensional matching problem, and a regular
interval Newton inclusion proves that the chosen box contains a unique
matching pair. Positivity checks on the finite interval and on the tail
finally identify the resulting decaying solution with the positive
radial ground state, by the uniqueness theorem of
[
32
]
; see
Proposition
E.4
. Hence the finite profile enclosures
and the exterior estimates concern the same function
Q
as the
theoretical argument.

We next turn to the potential and to the spectral bounds. In the
notation of Appendix
E
, the virial operator
\mathcal{L}

introduced above is written as

\mathcal{L}=-3\partial_{y_{1}}^{2}-\partial_{y_{2}}^{2}+\kappa_{\alpha_{1}}+W,\qquad\kappa_{\alpha_{1}}=\frac{7701}{10201},

where
\kappa_{\alpha_{1}}=1-\frac{1}{4\alpha_{1}^{2}}
and

W=-3Q^{2}+6\alpha_{1}\left(1+e^{-y_{1}/\alpha_{1}}\right)QQ_{y_{1}}

is the potential of
\mathcal{L}
; see
(
E.1
)–(
E.2
). The regularity and decay
of
Q
from Lemma
3.3
imply that
W
is bounded and tends to
zero, so that the essential spectrum of
\mathcal{L}
starts at the
positive number
\kappa_{\alpha_{1}}
. To obtain quantitative spectral
bounds, the calculation encloses the exact potential directly from the
validated profile: on
\Omega=(-16,16)^{2}
, it multiplies
W
by a cutoff
and interpolates the resulting function bilinearly. The explicit
potential
W_{h}
obtained in this way has a certified error, given by the
sum of the cutoff, interpolation, and nodal errors, and the uncertainty
in
Q
is carried by the interval values and derivative bounds entering
these estimates. The exterior estimate follows directly from the
exponential bounds for
Q
and
Q^{\prime}
. The corresponding quantitative bounds are given in Proposition
E.5
and its recorded certificate.

The Dirichlet and the Neumann problems on this square play different
roles; see Section
E.3
. Two finite sine trial
functions give an upper bound for the second Dirichlet eigenvalue:
their mass matrix is positive definite, so
that coefficient vectors and trial functions span spaces of the same
dimension, and an interval test shows that the shifted Ritz form is
strictly negative on this two-dimensional space. The min–max principle
therefore gives
\mu_{1}^{D}\leq\mu_{2}^{D}<-5/2
, and no assertion about the
convergence of a sequence of numerical eigenvalues is needed.

For the Neumann problem, the cosine basis splits the space into finitely
many retained modes and their orthogonal complement. The kinetic energy
of the complementary modes has an explicit lower bound, which, after
allowing for the potential, gives a lower threshold
\tau
for the
complementary block. At a level
t<\tau
, completion of the square
reduces the eigenvalue count to a finite Schur complement, and the
coupling to all complementary modes is bounded by a residual Gram matrix
whose entries are finite integrals of
W_{h}
and
W_{h}^{2}
. An interval
congruence test shows that the resulting lower matrix has at most two
negative directions at
t=5/8
. Hence
\mu_{3}^{N}\geq 5/8
, all modes outside
the retained space being accounted for; see
Proposition
E.6
.

The potential error changes each interior eigenvalue by at most its
uniform bound. Zero extension of the Dirichlet trial functions produces
at least two negative directions for the whole-space operator;
conversely, Neumann decoupling enlarges the form domain, while the
exterior operator is positive by the tail estimate. Dirichlet–Neumann
bracketing therefore excludes any further negative direction and gives

\operatorname{ind}_{-}(\mathcal{L})=2,\qquad\operatorname{dist}(0,\sigma(\mathcal{L}))\geq 0.62076895409267;

see Proposition
E.7
. In particular,
\mathcal{L}^{-1}

exists on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, and the spectral theorem bounds its norm
by the reciprocal of this gap.

We now describe the enclosure of the inverse pairings. Recall the
constraint functions
f_{1},f_{2},f_{3},f_{o}
and their normalization from
(
2.5
); the normalization adopted in
Appendices
B
and
E
is (
E.4
).
The first three functions are even in
y_{2}
, whereas
f_{o}
is odd, and
reflection in
y_{2}
commutes with
\mathcal{L}
and with its inverse.
Thus the inverse Gram matrix separates into the even matrix
A
, with
entries
A_{ij}=(\mathcal{L}^{-1}f_{i},f_{j})
for
1\leq i,j\leq 3
, and the
odd pairing
m_{oo}=(\mathcal{L}^{-1}f_{o},f_{o})
, the cross pairings being
zero.

For each constraint, the inverse solution is approximated by a compact,
globally
C^{2}
cubic spline
u_{h,a}
, whose residual is the exact
function
r_{a}=f_{a}-\mathcal{L}u_{h,a}
. Cellwise interval quadrature and
the explicit tails of
f_{a}
bound
\|r_{a}\|_{L^{2}}
on the whole plane, and
the global regularity of the spline ensures that its second derivatives
carry no boundary distributions. A symmetric correction then gives

\displaystyle(\mathcal{L}^{-1}f_{a},f_{b})={}

\displaystyle(u_{h,a},f_{b})+(f_{a},u_{h,b})-\mathcal{B}(u_{h,a},u_{h,b})

\displaystyle+(\mathcal{L}^{-1}r_{a},r_{b}),

where
\mathcal{B}
denotes the symmetric bilinear form of
\mathcal{L}
,

\mathcal{B}(u,v)=3(u_{y_{1}},v_{y_{1}})+(u_{y_{2}},v_{y_{2}})+\bigl((\kappa_{\alpha_{1}}+W)u,v\bigr),\qquad u,v\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),

which coincides with
(\mathcal{L}u,v)
for
u\in H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
; see
(
E.3
). The first three terms are finite integrals, whereas
the remaining error
is at most
\|r_{a}\|_{L^{2}}\|r_{b}\|_{L^{2}}
divided by the spectral gap, hence
quadratic in the residual sizes; see Proposition
E.8
.
The recorded enclosures give
\det A<0
and
m_{oo}<0
.

Since
A
is real and symmetric, its negative determinant supplies a
negative direction among the even constraints, and the odd pairing
supplies a second one, with zero cross pairing by parity. The inverse
constraint form is therefore negative definite on a two-dimensional
space, which matches the negative index of
\mathcal{L}
. Together with
the gap, these are precisely the numerical inputs of
Lemma
B.8
, where Proposition
B.1
turns them into the coercivity of the virial form.

Finally, we describe the enclosure of the constant
c
. For the function

g_{Q}
defined in (
2.1
), the constant
c
of
(
2.2
) can be written as

c=\frac{2\bigl((-d^{2}/dz^{2}+1)^{-1}g_{Q},g_{Q}\bigr)}{\|g_{Q}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}},

where the inner product and the norm are now those of
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
.
Radiality expresses
g_{Q}
as a one-dimensional integral involving

Q^{\prime}(r)/r
, whose value at zero is given by the regular power series.
Validated radial quadrature gives interval values at a finite set of
transverse nodes, and piecewise linear interpolation, bounds for the
second transverse derivative, and explicit exterior tails then produce
an approximation
g_{h}
with a certified whole-line
L^{2}
error.

The inverse in this quotient has kernel
\tfrac{1}{2}e^{-|z-t|}
, and an
exact one-sided convolution recurrence integrates the piecewise linear
approximation against this kernel; thus the computation uses the
whole-line inverse, including its exponential tails. Since this inverse
has operator norm one, the
L^{2}
error controls the errors in both
quadratic integrals of the quotient, and a positive lower bound for the
denominator permits interval division and yields

c\in[1.66126332085379,1.66170874135113]\subset\left(\frac{21}{13},\frac{5}{3}\right);

see Proposition
E.9
. In particular,

\lfloor 8c/(c-1)\rfloor+1=21
, which identifies the polynomial weight
required in the theoretical estimates; see (
E.76
).
This last calculation uses only the validated ground state; the
spectral and the inverse-pairing certificates are needed for the virial
branch.

The complete source code and implementation files are available in the public GitHub repository at
https://github.com/kk10-genius/ZK2d3p-blowup
.

2.7
Organization of the Book

The book is organized as follows. In
Chapter
3
we construct
the approximate profile, and in Chapter
4
we
derive the modulation equations. The mixed-energy method is developed
in Chapters
5
and
6
,
leading to the rigidity theorem in Chapter
7
.
The openness of the stable blow-up branch is proved in
Chapter
8
. Strong
L^{p}
convergence of the
radiation and the Sobolev obstruction for its limiting profile are
established in Chapter
9
. Finally, the
non-positive-energy case is treated in Chapter
10
.

Chapter 3
Construction of the Approximate Solution

3.1
Properties of the Linearized Operator

In this section, we denote the spatial variables by
x=(x_{1},x_{2})
. We begin by recalling well-known spectral properties of the linearized operator defined by

L=-\Delta+1-3Q^{2}.

Lemma 3.1
.

(
[
57
]
,
[
32
]
,
[
8
]
)
The operator
L
satisfies the following properties:

•

L
is self-adjoint, and its essential spectrum is given by
\sigma_{\mathrm{ess}}(L)=[1,+\infty).

•

The kernel of
L
is nondegenerate and is generated by the translations of the soliton:

\ker(L)=\operatorname{span}\{Q_{x_{1}},Q_{x_{2}}\}.

•

L
possesses a unique negative eigenvalue
-\lambda_{-}
(with
\lambda_{-}>0
), associated with a strictly positive, radially symmetric eigenfunction
\chi_{0}
. Furthermore,
\chi_{0}
decays exponentially (see
[
2
]
); there exists
\delta>0
such that

|\chi_{0}(x)|\lesssim e^{-\delta|x|}\quad\text{for all }x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}.

In addition to the spectral structure, we recall specific algebraic identities and positivity properties associated with
L
.

Lemma 3.2
.

The following identities and inequalities hold:

•

Action on the scaling mode:
L(\Lambda Q)=-2Q
. Moreover, the orthogonality condition
\iint Q\Lambda Q=0
holds.

•

The operator is negative in the direction of the soliton:

(LQ,Q)=-2\int Q^{4}<0.

We also require precise pointwise decay estimates for the soliton and its derivatives.

Lemma 3.3
.

For any
0<\eta\ll 1
and any multi-index
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
, there exists a constant
C_{\alpha,Q,\eta}>0
such that

|\partial^{\alpha}Q(x_{1},x_{2})|\leq C_{\alpha,Q,\eta}e^{-(1-\eta)|x_{1}|-\sqrt{2\eta-\eta^{2}}|x_{2}|}.

In particular, for any
0<\eta\ll 1
, there exists
C_{\Lambda,Q,\eta}>0
such that

|\Lambda Q(x_{1},x_{2})|\leq C_{\Lambda,Q,\eta}e^{-(1-\eta)|x_{1}|-(2\eta-\eta^{2})|x_{2}|}.

Proof.

It was established in
[
7
]
,
[
6
]
that there exists a constant
C_{\alpha,Q}
such that
|\partial^{\alpha}Q(x_{1},x_{2})|\leq C_{\alpha,Q}e^{-\sqrt{x_{1}^{2}+x_{2}^{2}}}
for all multi-indices
\alpha\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{2}
. Noticing the elementary inequality
\sqrt{x_{1}^{2}+x_{2}^{2}}\geq(1-\eta)|x_{1}|+\sqrt{2\eta-\eta^{2}}|x_{2}|
, the result follows immediately.

For the estimate on
\Lambda Q
, we use

|\Lambda Q(x)|\leq|Q(x)|+|x||\nabla Q(x)|\lesssim(1+|x|)e^{-|x|}.

Fix
0<\eta\ll 1
. Since

(1-\eta)^{2}+(2\eta-\eta^{2})^{2}<(1-\eta/2)^{2}

for
\eta>0
sufficiently small, we have

(1-\eta/2)|x|\geq(1-\eta)|x_{1}|+(2\eta-\eta^{2})|x_{2}|.

Moreover,

(1+|x|)e^{-|x|}\leq C_{\eta}e^{-(1-\eta/2)|x|}.

Therefore,

|\Lambda Q(x_{1},x_{2})|\leq C_{\Lambda,Q,\eta}e^{-(1-\eta)|x_{1}|-(2\eta-\eta^{2})|x_{2}|}.

∎

We now proceed to establish the coercivity property of the operator
L
. Throughout the remainder of this section, we fix the following constants:

\alpha_{1}=1.01\quad\text{and}\quad\alpha_{2}=1.005=\alpha_{1}-\frac{1}{200}.

(3.1)

Let us define the weight function
\varphi:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
by

\varphi(x_{1})=1+e^{\frac{x_{1}}{\alpha_{1}}}.

(3.2)

Note that
\varphi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
. Combining the definition of
\varphi
with Lemma
3.3
, it follows that the functions
\varphi(x_{1})Q_{x_{1}}
,
\varphi(x_{1})Q_{x_{2}}
, and
\varphi(x_{1})\Lambda Q
belong to
H^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
. Furthermore, each of these functions is bounded in absolute value by a constant multiple of

e^{-\frac{1}{200\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}|x_{1}|-(1-\frac{1}{\alpha_{2}^{2}})|x_{2}|}.

(3.3)

We introduce the matrix
M^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}
defined by

M^{*}=\begin{bmatrix}(\Lambda Q,\varphi(x_{1})\Lambda Q)&(\Lambda Q,\varphi(x_{1})Q_{x_{1}})\\
(\Lambda Q,\varphi(x_{1})Q_{x_{1}})&(Q_{x_{1}},\varphi(x_{1})Q_{x_{1}})\end{bmatrix}.

(3.4)

Observe that
M^{*}
is precisely the Gram matrix associated with the linearly independent pair
\{\Lambda Q,Q_{x_{1}}\}
under the weighted inner product defined by
(f,g)_{\varphi}:=(f,g\varphi)
. Consequently,
M^{*}
is positive definite and, in particular,
\det M^{*}\neq 0
.

Lemma 3.4
.

Define the subspace
\mathcal{X}\subset H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
by

\mathcal{X}=\left\{u\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}):(u,Q)=(u,\varphi(x_{1})Q_{x_{2}})=(u,\varphi(x_{1})\Lambda Q)=(u,\varphi(x_{1})Q_{x_{1}})=0\right\},

and define the associated quadratic form of
L
by

(Lu,u):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left(|\nabla u|^{2}+(1-3Q^{2})u^{2}\right)\,dx,\qquad u\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}).

a)

Coercivity on
\mathcal{X}
:
There exists
\delta>0
such that for all
u\in\mathcal{X}
,

(Lu,u)\geq\delta\|u\|_{H^{1}}^{2}.

b)

Global Coercivity:
There exists
\tilde{\delta}>0
such that for any
u\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
,

(Lu,u)\geq\tilde{\delta}\|u\|_{H^{1}}^{2}-\frac{1}{\tilde{\delta}}\left((u,Q)^{2}+(u,\varphi(x_{1})Q_{x_{2}})^{2}+(u,\varphi(x_{1})\Lambda Q)^{2}+(u,\varphi(x_{1})Q_{x_{1}})^{2}\right).

(3.5)

The proof of this result can be found in Appendix

B.2
.

3.2
Construction and Properties of the

First-Order Correction
P

In this section, we seek an approximation of the soliton
Q(x_{1},x_{2})
such that we obtain a sufficient approximate self-similar equation:

b_{s}\frac{\partial Q_{b}}{\partial b}+b\Lambda Q_{b}+(\Delta Q_{b}-Q_{b}+Q_{b}^{3})_{x_{1}}=0.

By writing
Q_{b}=Q+bP+\ldots,
this implies
(LP)_{x_{1}}=\Lambda Q.
Therefore, we need to prove that we can invert the operator
\partial_{x_{1}}L.
This solvability is ensured by the spectral properties of
L
and the orthogonality condition
(\Lambda Q,Q)=0.
Throughout this section, we use
x=(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
as spatial variables.

Lemma 3.5
.

The fundamental solution of
-\Delta+1
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
is
\frac{1}{2\pi}K_{0}(|\cdot|),
where
K_{0}
is the modified Bessel function of the second kind. It satisfies
K_{0}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{\ast}_{+})
and we have the following properties:

a).

There exists
\delta>0
, such that for all
r>\delta,

\frac{\sqrt{\pi}e^{-r}}{\sqrt{2(r+\frac{1}{4})}}<K_{0}(r)<\frac{\sqrt{\pi}e^{-r}}{\sqrt{2r}},

K_{0}(r)\sim_{r\rightarrow 0}-\ln(r).

b).

For all
r>0
,
K_{0}(r)>0.

c).

\int_{\mathbb{R}}K_{0}(|x|)dx
is finite.

All these results can be found in
[
1
]
and
[
58
]
. As a corollary of this lemma, we find that

K_{0}(|\cdot|)\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}).

Lemma 3.6
.

Suppose that
f\in H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
satisfies, for some
A>0
and

k>0
,

|(-\Delta f+f)(x)|\leq A(1+|x|)^{k}e^{-|x|}\qquad\text{for all }x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}.

Then there exists
C(k)>0
, independent of
A
,
f
, and
x
, such that

|f(x)|\leq C(k)A(1+|x|)^{k+\frac{3}{2}}e^{-|x|}\qquad\text{for all }x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}.

Proof.

Let

g=(-\Delta+1)f.

Since
f\in H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, we have
g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
.
Moreover, the assumed pointwise bound implies that

g\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
. The fundamental-solution representation
therefore gives

f=\frac{1}{2\pi}K_{0}\ast g.

Using
K_{0}\geq 0
, for every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
we obtain

\displaystyle|f(x)|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2\pi}\left\lVert\frac{e^{|\cdot|}g(\cdot)}{(1+|\cdot|)^{k}}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}K_{0}(|x-y|)e^{-|y|}(1+|y|)^{k}\,dy\leq\frac{A}{2\pi}h(x),

where

h(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}K_{0}(|x-y|)e^{-|y|}(1+|y|)^{k}\,dy.

Since
K_{0}(|\cdot|)
and

e^{-|\cdot|}(1+|\cdot|)^{k}
are radial, their convolution
h
is
radial.

The estimates on
K_{0}
recalled above imply the global pointwise bound

K_{0}(r)\lesssim r^{-\frac{1}{2}}e^{-r},\qquad r>0.

Indeed, this is the stated estimate for large
r
, while for
0<r\leq 1

it follows from

K_{0}(r)\lesssim 1+|\log r|\lesssim r^{-\frac{1}{2}}.

Let

F(r)=e^{-r}(1+r)^{k},

for
r\geq 0.

Since

F^{\prime}(r)=e^{-r}(1+r)^{k-1}(k-1-r),

we distinguish the following cases.

i).

Suppose that
0<k<1
. Then
F
is strictly decreasing on

[0,\infty)
. Since
F(0)=1
and
F(r)\to 0
as
r\to\infty
, there
exists
\tilde{r}_{0}>0
such that

F(\tilde{r}_{0})=\frac{1}{2}.

For
0\leq|x|\leq\tilde{r}_{0}
, we have

F(|x|)\geq 1/2
. Since
F(|y|)\leq 1
and

K_{0}(|\cdot|)\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
,

\displaystyle h(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}K_{0}(|x-y|)F(|y|)\,dy\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}K_{0}(|x-y|)\,dy\lesssim F(|x|).

ii).

Suppose that
k\geq 1
. Then
F
is increasing on
[0,k-1]
and
decreasing on
[k-1,\infty)
. Since
F(r)\to 0
as
r\to\infty
, there
exists
\tilde{r}_{1}\in(k-1,\infty)
such that

F(\tilde{r}_{1})=\frac{F(k-1)}{2}.

For
0\leq|x|\leq\tilde{r}_{1}
, set

\alpha=\min\{F(0),F(\tilde{r}_{1})\}>0.

Then
F(|x|)\geq\alpha
, while
F(|y|)\leq F(k-1)
for every

y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Consequently,

\displaystyle h(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}K_{0}(|x-y|)F(|y|)\,dy\leq F(k-1)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}K_{0}(|x-y|)\,dy\leq C(k)F(|x|).

iii).

It remains to consider either

0<k<1,\qquad|x|\geq\tilde{r}_{0},

or

k\geq 1,\qquad|x|\geq\tilde{r}_{1}.

In the first case, define

\tilde{x}_{0}=\inf\left\{t\in[0,|x|]:F(t)\leq 2F(|x|)\right\}.

This quantity is well defined because

2F(|x|)\leq 2F(\tilde{r}_{0})=F(0),

and
\tilde{x}_{0}<|x|
.

In the second case, define

\tilde{x}_{1}=\inf\left\{t\in[k-1,|x|]:F(t)\leq 2F(|x|)\right\}.

This quantity is well defined because

2F(|x|)\leq 2F(\tilde{r}_{1})=F(k-1),

and again
\tilde{x}_{1}<|x|
.

In both cases, the monotonicity of
F
on the relevant interval gives

F(|y|)\leq 2F(|x|)\qquad\text{whenever }\tilde{x}_{i}\leq|y|\leq|x|,\quad i=0,1.

We now split the integral defining
h
into three regions.

a).

On the region
\{|y|\geq|x|\}
, the monotonicity of
F
gives

F(|y|)\leq F(|x|)
. Hence

\displaystyle\int_{\{|y|\geq|x|\}}K_{0}(|x-y|)e^{-|y|}(1+|y|)^{k}\,dy

\displaystyle\leq F(|x|)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}K_{0}(|x-y|)\,dy\lesssim F(|x|).

b).

On the region

\{\tilde{x}_{i}\leq|y|\leq|x|\}
, we have

F(|y|)\leq 2F(|x|)
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\int_{\{\tilde{x}_{i}\leq|y|\leq|x|\}}K_{0}(|x-y|)e^{-|y|}(1+|y|)^{k}\,dy

\displaystyle\leq 2F(|x|)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}K_{0}(|x-y|)\,dy\lesssim F(|x|).

c).

On the region
\{|y|\leq\tilde{x}_{i}\}
, we have

(1+|y|)^{k}\leq(1+|x|)^{k}.

Using the global upper bound on
K_{0}
and the triangle inequality, we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{\{|y|\leq\tilde{x}_{i}\}}K_{0}(|x-y|)e^{-|y|}(1+|y|)^{k}\,dy

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{B(0,\tilde{x}_{i})}\frac{e^{-|x-y|}e^{-|y|}}{|x-y|^{1/2}}(1+|y|)^{k}\,dy

\displaystyle\leq e^{-|x|}(1+|x|)^{k}\int_{B(0,\tilde{x}_{i})}\frac{dy}{|x-y|^{1/2}}.

Since
\tilde{x}_{i}<|x|
, we have

B(0,\tilde{x}_{i})\subset B(x,2|x|).

Consequently,

\displaystyle\int_{B(0,\tilde{x}_{i})}\frac{dy}{|x-y|^{1/2}}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{B(x,2|x|)}\frac{dy}{|x-y|^{1/2}}=2\pi\int_{0}^{2|x|}r^{1/2}\,dr\lesssim|x|^{3/2}.

Thus,

\displaystyle\int_{\{|y|\leq\tilde{x}_{i}\}}K_{0}(|x-y|)e^{-|y|}(1+|y|)^{k}\,dy

\displaystyle\lesssim|x|^{3/2}e^{-|x|}(1+|x|)^{k}\lesssim e^{-|x|}(1+|x|)^{k+\frac{3}{2}}.

Combining the estimates in all regions, we conclude that

h(x)\lesssim e^{-|x|}(1+|x|)^{k+\frac{3}{2}}.

Therefore,

|f(x)|\leq C(k)A(1+|x|)^{k+\frac{3}{2}}e^{-|x|},

which completes the proof.
∎

Denote by
\mathcal{Y}
the set of functions

\mathcal{Y}=\left\{f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2}):\begin{aligned} &\forall i,j\geq 0,\exists r_{i,j},C_{i,j}>0\text{ such that}\\
&\bigl|\partial^{i}_{x_{1}}\partial^{j}_{x_{2}}f(x)\bigr|\leq C_{i,j}(1+|x|)^{r_{i,j}}e^{-|x|},\quad\forall x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\end{aligned}\right\}.

Lemma 3.7
.

Suppose
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
and that
Lf
, interpreted in the distributional sense, belongs to
\mathcal{Y}
.
Then
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\cap H^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
and
f\in\mathcal{Y}.

Proof.

We start by proving by induction that
f\in H^{m}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),
for all
m\geq 0.

Suppose that
f\in H^{m},
for some
m\geq 0.
Since multiplication by
Q^{2}
preserves every Sobolev space and
Lf\in H^{m},
then

(-\Delta+1)f=Lf+3Q^{2}f\in H^{m}\implies f\in H^{m+2}.

As
f\in L^{2},
iteration gives
f\in H^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
and consequently
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
and every derivative of
f
is bounded. Now, for every multi-index
\alpha,
we have

(-\Delta+1)\partial^{\alpha}f=\partial^{\alpha}Lf+3\sum_{\beta\leq\alpha}\binom{\alpha}{\beta}\partial^{\beta}(Q^{2})\partial^{\alpha-\beta}f.

Every derivative of
f
in this expression is bounded, while every derivative of
Q^{2}
decays exponentially. Since
Lf\in\mathcal{Y},

|(-\Delta+1)\partial^{\alpha}f(x)|\lesssim C_{\alpha}(1+|x|)^{m_{\alpha}}e^{-|x|}\text{ for all }x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}.

Applying Lemma
3.6
to
\partial^{\alpha}f
yields
\mathcal{Y}-
type decay of
\partial^{\alpha}f
for every multi-index
\alpha,
thus
f\in\mathcal{Y}.

∎

Lemma 3.8
.

For any function
h\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
orthogonal to
\nabla Q
for the
L^{2}
scalar product, there exists a unique function
f\in H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
orthogonal to
\nabla Q
such that
Lf=h.
Moreover, if
h
is even in both variables, then
f
is even in both variables.

This is a consequence of the Fredholm alternative. We now move to the construction of
P.

Lemma 3.9
.

There exists a unique smooth function
P
, even in
x_{2}
, such that

P_{x_{1}}\in\mathcal{Y}
,

(LP)_{x_{1}}=\Lambda Q,\qquad(P,Q_{x_{1}})=(P,Q_{x_{2}})=0,

and, for every
x_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
,

\lim_{x_{1}\to+\infty}P(x_{1},x_{2})=\lim_{x_{1}\to+\infty}P_{x_{2}x_{2}}(x_{1},x_{2})=0.

For every
0<\eta<1
, this function satisfies

|P(x_{1},x_{2})|\lesssim e^{-(1-\eta)|x_{2}|}\qquad\text{for all }(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2},

and

|P(x_{1},x_{2})|\lesssim e^{-(1-\eta)x_{1}-(2\eta-\eta^{2})|x_{2}|},\qquad x_{1}>0.

Its pairing with
Q
is

(P,Q)=\frac{1}{4}\left\lVert\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\Lambda Q\,dx_{1}\right\rVert_{L^{2}_{x_{2}}}^{2}>0.

For
b
small, the profile
\overline{Q}_{b}=Q+bP
satisfies

\left\lVert(\Delta\overline{Q}_{b}-\overline{Q}_{b}+\overline{Q}_{b}^{3})_{x_{1}}+b\Lambda\overline{Q}_{b}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim b^{2}.

Proof.

By the Lax–Milgram theorem, there is a unique
U\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
such
that

(-\Delta+1)U=\Lambda Q.

Elliptic regularity gives
U\in H^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
. Since

(-\Delta+1)\partial^{\beta}U=\partial^{\beta}\Lambda Q\in\mathcal{Y},

Lemma
3.6
applied to every derivative yields

U\in\mathcal{Y}
. Uniqueness also implies that
U
is even in both
variables.

Set

G(x_{1},x_{2})=\int_{x_{1}}^{\infty}U(z,x_{2})\,dz,\qquad H(x_{1},x_{2})=\int_{x_{1}}^{\infty}\Lambda Q(z,x_{2})\,dz.

A direct computation gives

(-\Delta+1)G=H,\qquad R:=LG-H=-3Q^{2}G.

The decay of
Q
and
U
, together with Leibniz’s rule, gives

R\in\mathcal{Y}
. Moreover,

(R,Q_{x_{1}})=-(H,Q_{x_{1}})=-(\Lambda Q,Q)=0,\qquad(R,Q_{x_{2}})=0,

the second identity following by parity. Lemmas
3.7
and

3.8
therefore yield an even-in-
x_{2}
function

\widetilde{P}\in\mathcal{Y}
such that

L\widetilde{P}=R,\qquad(\widetilde{P},Q_{x_{1}})=(\widetilde{P},Q_{x_{2}})=0.

Define

P=\widetilde{P}-G+\frac{(U,Q)}{\|Q_{x_{1}}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}Q_{x_{1}}.

Then

LP=-H,\qquad(LP)_{x_{1}}=\Lambda Q.

Since
(G,Q_{x_{1}})=(U,Q)
by integration by parts, we have

(P,Q_{x_{1}})=0
, while
(P,Q_{x_{2}})=0
by parity. Furthermore,

P_{x_{1}}=\widetilde{P}_{x_{1}}+U+\frac{(U,Q)}{\|Q_{x_{1}}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}Q_{x_{1}x_{1}}\in\mathcal{Y}.

The construction gives the stated limits at
+\infty
and shows that

P
is even in
x_{2}
.

For every
0<\eta<1
, the definition of
\mathcal{Y}
gives constants

C_{\eta},b_{\eta}>0
such that

|U(z,x_{2})|\leq C_{\eta}e^{-(1-\eta)|x_{2}|}e^{-b_{\eta}|z|}.

Thus
G
satisfies the uniform transverse estimate
|G(x_{1},x_{2})|\lesssim e^{-(1-\eta)|x_{2}|}
for all
(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
; since

\widetilde{P},Q_{x_{1}}\in\mathcal{Y}
, the same is true of
P
. Also,

\lim_{x_{1}\to-\infty}P(x_{1},x_{2})=-\int_{\mathbb{R}}U(z,x_{2})\,dz,

which is even and exponentially decaying. For the right-hand estimate,
set
a=1-\eta
and
d=2\eta-\eta^{2}
. Since
a^{2}+d^{2}<1
, choose

\sqrt{a^{2}+d^{2}}<\rho<1
. From
P_{x_{1}}\in\mathcal{Y}
,

|P_{x_{1}}(x)|\lesssim e^{-\rho|x|}
, and therefore, for
x_{1}>0
,

|P(x_{1},x_{2})|\leq\int_{x_{1}}^{\infty}|P_{x_{1}}(z,x_{2})|\,dz\lesssim e^{-ax_{1}-d|x_{2}|}.

To prove uniqueness, let
W=P-P_{0},
where
P_{0}
is another function with the same
properties, including the right-end normalization. Since

W_{x_{1}}\in\mathcal{Y}
and
W(+\infty,x_{2})=0
, the representation

W(x_{1},x_{2})=-\int_{x_{1}}^{\infty}W_{x_{1}}(z,x_{2})\,dz

may be differentiated in
x_{2}
under the integral sign. The defining
bounds for
\mathcal{Y}
also give convergence of the resulting
integrals as
x_{1}\to-\infty
. Since
(LW)_{x_{1}}=0
,

LW=\Gamma(x_{2})
. Passing to the limit at

+\infty
gives
\Gamma=0
. The limit

W_{-}(x_{2})=\lim_{x_{1}\to-\infty}W(x_{1},x_{2})
exists and decays exponentially at
\pm\infty
;
letting
x_{1}\to-\infty
in
LW=0
gives
-W_{-}^{\prime\prime}+W_{-}=0
, hence
W_{-}=0
.
Using the two zero end limits and
W_{x_{1}}\in\mathcal{Y}
gives

W\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
. Since

(-\Delta+1)W=3Q^{2}W,

we have
W\in H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, and therefore

W\in\ker L=\operatorname{span}\{Q_{x_{1}},Q_{x_{2}}\}.

The orthogonality conditions imply
W=0
.

It remains to compute
(P,Q)
. Since
LP=-H
,

LP(+\infty,x_{2})=0,\qquad LP(-\infty,x_{2})=-\int_{\mathbb{R}}\Lambda Q\,dx_{1}.

Using
L(\Lambda Q)=-2Q
and integrating first on finite strips,

\displaystyle-(P,Q)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(P,L\Lambda Q)=\frac{1}{2}(LP,(LP)_{x_{1}})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\int_{\mathbb{R}}[(LP)^{2}]_{x_{1}=-\infty}^{x_{1}=+\infty}\,dx_{2}=-\frac{1}{4}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\Lambda Q\,dx_{1}\right)^{2}dx_{2}.

Finally, if
h(x_{2})=\int_{\mathbb{R}}Q(x_{1},x_{2})\,dx_{1}
, then

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\Lambda Q\,dx_{1}=x_{2}h^{\prime}(x_{2}).

If this vanished identically,
h
would be constant; since
Q\in L^{1}

and
Q>0
, this is impossible. Hence
(P,Q)>0
.

For
\overline{Q}_{b}=Q+bP
, expansion gives

\displaystyle(\Delta\overline{Q}_{b}-\overline{Q}_{b}+\overline{Q}_{b}^{3})_{x_{1}}+b\Lambda\overline{Q}_{b}

\displaystyle=b\bigl(-(LP)_{x_{1}}+\Lambda Q\bigr)+b^{2}\bigl((3QP^{2})_{x_{1}}+\Lambda P\bigr)+b^{3}(P^{3})_{x_{1}}=O_{L^{\infty}}(b^{2}),

where the last estimate uses
b
small, the construction of
P
, and

P_{x_{1}}\in\mathcal{Y}
. This completes the proof.
∎

Chapter 4
Modulation Equation

4.1
Derivation of the Modulation Equation

Let
u
be a solution to the equation
u_{t}+\Delta\partial_{x_{1}}u+3u^{2}\partial_{x_{1}}u=0
. We introduce the rescaled function
\mathfrak{u}
defined by:

\mathfrak{u}(t,y_{1},y_{2})=\lambda(t)u\Big(t,\lambda(t)y_{1}+x_{1}(t),\lambda(t)y_{2}+x_{2}(t)\Big).

Substituting this ansatz into the original equation and multiplying by
\lambda^{3}
, we express the evolution of
\mathfrak{u}
as follows (using the relations
\mathfrak{u}_{y_{i}}=\lambda^{2}u_{x_{i}}
and
\mathfrak{u}_{y_{i}y_{j}y_{k}}=\lambda^{4}u_{x_{i}x_{j}x_{k}}
):

\begin{split}\lambda^{3}\mathfrak{u}_{t}&=\lambda^{2}\lambda_{t}\mathfrak{u}+\lambda^{4}u_{t}+\lambda^{2}\lambda_{t}y_{1}\mathfrak{u}_{y_{1}}+\lambda^{2}\lambda_{t}y_{2}\mathfrak{u}_{y_{2}}+\lambda^{2}(x_{1})_{t}\mathfrak{u}_{y_{1}}+\lambda^{2}(x_{2})_{t}\mathfrak{u}_{y_{2}}\\
&=\lambda^{2}\lambda_{t}\mathfrak{u}+\lambda^{4}(-u_{x_{1}x_{1}x_{1}}-u_{x_{2}x_{2}x_{1}}-3u^{2}u_{x_{1}})\\
&\quad+\lambda^{2}\lambda_{t}(y\cdot\nabla\mathfrak{u})+\lambda^{2}(x_{1})_{t}\mathfrak{u}_{y_{1}}+\lambda^{2}(x_{2})_{t}\mathfrak{u}_{y_{2}}\\
&=\lambda^{2}\lambda_{t}\mathfrak{u}-(\mathfrak{u}_{y_{1}y_{1}y_{1}}+\mathfrak{u}_{y_{2}y_{2}y_{1}}+3\mathfrak{u}^{2}\mathfrak{u}_{y_{1}})+\lambda^{2}\lambda_{t}(y\cdot\nabla\mathfrak{u})+\lambda^{2}(x_{1})_{t}\mathfrak{u}_{y_{1}}+\lambda^{2}(x_{2})_{t}\mathfrak{u}_{y_{2}}.\end{split}

We introduce the renormalized time variable
s
defined by:

s=\int_{0}^{t}\frac{dt^{\prime}}{\lambda(t^{\prime})^{3}}\quad\implies\quad\frac{ds}{dt}=\frac{1}{\lambda^{3}}.

Consequently, the parameter derivatives satisfy
\lambda_{s}=\lambda^{3}\lambda_{t}
and
(x_{i})_{s}=\lambda^{3}(x_{i})_{t}
. The equation for
\mathfrak{u}
in the
s
variable becomes:

\mathfrak{u}_{s}-\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda}\Lambda\mathfrak{u}-\frac{(x_{1})_{s}}{\lambda}\mathfrak{u}_{y_{1}}-\frac{(x_{2})_{s}}{\lambda}\mathfrak{u}_{y_{2}}+\partial_{y_{1}}(\Delta\mathfrak{u}+\mathfrak{u}^{3})=0,

(4.1)

where
\Lambda\mathfrak{u}=\mathfrak{u}+y_{1}\mathfrak{u}_{y_{1}}+y_{2}\mathfrak{u}_{y_{2}}
is the
L^{2}
-scaling generator.

Decomposition and Error Equation

We construct the approximate profile using a localized radiation term. Let
\chi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
be a cut-off function such that
0\leq\chi\leq 1
,
\chi^{\prime}\geq 0
, with
\chi(x)=0
for
x\leq-2
and
\chi(x)=1
for
x\geq-1
. We define the modulated profile
Q_{b}
as:

Q_{b}(y_{1},y_{2})=Q(y_{1},y_{2})+b\chi\left(|b|^{\gamma}y_{1}\right)P(y_{1},y_{2}),

for some positive
\gamma<1
and decompose the solution as
\mathfrak{u}(y_{1},y_{2},s)=Q_{b(s)}(y_{1},y_{2})+\varepsilon(y_{1},y_{2},s)
. Noticing that
\varepsilon_{s}=\mathfrak{u}_{s}-(Q_{b})_{s}
and using the identity
\partial_{y_{1}}(-Q+\Delta Q+Q^{3})=0
in (
4.1
), the evolution equation for the error
\varepsilon
is given by:

\begin{split}\varepsilon_{s}-(L\varepsilon)_{y_{1}}&=\left(\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda}+b\right)\Lambda Q_{b}+\left(\frac{(x_{1})_{s}}{\lambda}-1\right)(Q_{b})_{y_{1}}+\frac{(x_{2})_{s}}{\lambda}(Q_{b})_{y_{2}}\\
&\quad+\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda}\Lambda\varepsilon+\left(\frac{(x_{1})_{s}}{\lambda}-1\right)\varepsilon_{y_{1}}+\frac{(x_{2})_{s}}{\lambda}\varepsilon_{y_{2}}\\
&\quad+\Phi_{b}+\Psi_{b}-R_{b}(\varepsilon)_{y_{1}}-R_{NL}(\varepsilon)_{y_{1}},\end{split}

(4.2)

where we have defined the following terms:

\displaystyle\chi_{b}(y_{1})

\displaystyle=\chi(|b|^{\gamma}y_{1}),\Phi_{b}=-b_{s}\left(\chi_{b}+\gamma y_{1}(\chi_{b})_{y_{1}}\right)P,\Psi_{b}=(-\Delta Q_{b}+Q_{b}-Q_{b}^{3})_{y_{1}}-b\Lambda Q_{b},

\displaystyle R_{b}(\varepsilon)

\displaystyle=3(Q_{b}^{2}-Q^{2})\varepsilon,R_{NL}(\varepsilon)=(\varepsilon+Q_{b})^{3}-Q_{b}^{3}-3Q_{b}^{2}\varepsilon.

The term
\Psi_{b}
represents the error generated by the approximate profile
Q_{b}
. Recalling that
(-\Delta Q+Q-Q^{3})_{y_{1}}=0
and
(LP)_{y_{1}}=\Lambda Q
, we compute
\Psi_{b}
as follows:

\displaystyle\Psi_{b}

\displaystyle=b\Big[(\chi_{b})_{y_{1}}P-3(\chi_{b})_{y_{1}}P_{y_{1}y_{1}}-3(\chi_{b})_{y_{1}y_{1}}P_{y_{1}}-(\chi_{b})_{y_{1}y_{1}y_{1}}P-3(\chi_{b})_{y_{1}}Q^{2}P-(\chi_{b})_{y_{1}}P_{y_{2}y_{2}}\Big]

(4.3)

\displaystyle\quad+b(\chi_{b}-1)\Lambda Q+b^{2}\left(-3(\chi_{b}^{2}P^{2}Q)_{y_{1}}-\Lambda(\chi_{b}P)\right)-b^{3}(\chi_{b}^{3}P^{3})_{y_{1}}.

Estimates on the Profile Error

We now provide pointwise bounds on the error term
\Psi_{b}
.

Lemma 4.1
.

For
0<|b|\ll 1
and every fixed integer
k\geq 0
, the following
estimates hold, with implicit constants depending on
k
. At
b=0
,

\Psi_{0}=0
, and the inequalities are understood by continuity:

\displaystyle|\Psi_{b}|

\displaystyle\lesssim|b|^{1+\gamma}\mathbf{1}_{[-\frac{2}{|b|^{\gamma}},-\frac{1}{|b|^{\gamma}}]}(y_{1})e^{-\frac{|y_{2}|}{2}}+b^{2}\left(\mathbf{1}_{[-\frac{2}{|b|^{\gamma}},0]}(y_{1})+(1+|y_{1}|)e^{-\frac{|y_{1}|}{2}}\right)e^{-\frac{|y_{2}|}{4}},

\displaystyle|(\Psi_{b})_{y_{2}y_{2}}|

\displaystyle\lesssim|b|^{1+\gamma}\mathbf{1}_{[-\frac{2}{|b|^{\gamma}},-\frac{1}{|b|^{\gamma}}]}(y_{1})e^{-\frac{|y_{2}|}{2}}+b^{2}\left(\mathbf{1}_{[-\frac{2}{|b|^{\gamma}},0]}(y_{1})+(1+|y_{1}|)e^{-\frac{|y_{1}|}{2}}\right)e^{-\frac{|y_{2}|}{4}},

\displaystyle|\partial_{y_{1}}^{k}\Psi_{b}|

\displaystyle\lesssim|b|^{1+(k+1)\gamma}\mathbf{1}_{[-\frac{2}{|b|^{\gamma}},-\frac{1}{|b|^{\gamma}}]}(y_{1})e^{-\frac{|y_{2}|}{2}}+b^{2}\left(\mathbf{1}_{[-\frac{2}{|b|^{\gamma}},0]}(y_{1})+(1+|y_{1}|)e^{-\frac{|y_{1}|}{2}}\right)e^{-\frac{|y_{2}|}{4}}.

Proof.

We estimate the terms in expansion (
4.3
) by analyzing the support of the cut-off function derivatives.

1. The Cut-off Transition Region:

Terms involving derivatives of
\chi_{b}
are supported in the region where
|b|^{\gamma}y_{1}\in[-2,-1]
. In this region, we have:

\displaystyle|b(\chi_{b})_{y_{1}}P|

\displaystyle\lesssim|b|^{1+\gamma}\mathbf{1}_{[-\frac{2}{|b|^{\gamma}},-\frac{1}{|b|^{\gamma}}]}(y_{1})e^{-\frac{|y_{2}|}{2}},

\displaystyle|b(\chi_{b})_{y_{1}}P_{y_{2}y_{2}}|

\displaystyle\lesssim|b|^{1+\gamma}\mathbf{1}_{[-\frac{2}{|b|^{\gamma}},-\frac{1}{|b|^{\gamma}}]}(y_{1})e^{-\frac{|y_{2}|}{2}},

\displaystyle|b(\chi_{b})_{y_{1}}P_{y_{1}y_{1}}|

\displaystyle\lesssim|b|^{1+\gamma}\mathbf{1}_{[-\frac{2}{|b|^{\gamma}},-\frac{1}{|b|^{\gamma}}]}(y_{1})e^{-\frac{|y_{1}|+|y_{2}|}{2}}\lesssim|b|^{3}e^{-\frac{|y_{1}|}{4}}e^{-\frac{|y_{2}|}{2}},

\displaystyle|b(\chi_{b})_{y_{1}y_{1}}P_{y_{1}}|

\displaystyle\lesssim|b|^{1+2\gamma}\mathbf{1}_{[-\frac{2}{|b|^{\gamma}},-\frac{1}{|b|^{\gamma}}]}(y_{1})e^{-\frac{|y_{1}|+|y_{2}|}{2}}\lesssim|b|^{3}e^{-\frac{|y_{1}|}{4}-\frac{|y_{2}|}{2}}.

Similar bounds hold for terms involving
(\chi_{b})_{y_{1}y_{1}y_{1}}P
and
(\chi_{b})_{y_{1}}Q^{2}P
.

2. The Localization Error:

For the term involving
(1-\chi_{b})
, the support is
y_{1}\in(-\infty,-|b|^{-\gamma}]
. Using the exponential decay of
Q
, we obtain:

|b(1-\chi_{b})\Lambda Q|\lesssim|b|\mathbf{1}_{(-\infty,-\frac{1}{|b|^{\gamma}}]}(y_{1})e^{-\frac{|y_{1}|}{2}}e^{-\frac{|y_{2}|}{2}}\lesssim|b|e^{-\frac{1}{4|b|^{\gamma}}}e^{-\frac{|y_{1}|}{4}}e^{-\frac{|y_{2}|}{2}}\lesssim|b|^{3}e^{-\frac{|y_{1}|}{4}}e^{-\frac{|y_{2}|}{2}}.

3. Higher Order Terms (
O(b^{2})
):

Using the formula
\Lambda(\chi_{b}P)=\chi_{b}P+y_{1}(\chi_{b})_{y_{1}}P+y_{1}\chi_{b}P_{y_{1}}+y_{2}\chi_{b}P_{y_{2}}
, we estimate the quadratic terms:

\displaystyle|b^{2}\chi_{b}P|

\displaystyle\lesssim b^{2}\left(\mathbf{1}_{[-\frac{2}{|b|^{\gamma}},0]}(y_{1})+(1+|y_{1}|)e^{-\frac{|y_{1}|}{2}}\right)e^{-\frac{|y_{2}|}{2}},

\displaystyle|b^{2}y_{1}(\chi_{b})_{y_{1}}P|

\displaystyle\lesssim b^{2}\frac{2}{|b|^{\gamma}}\mathbf{1}_{[-\frac{2}{|b|^{\gamma}},-\frac{1}{|b|^{\gamma}}]}(y_{1})|b|^{\gamma}|P|\lesssim b^{2}\mathbf{1}_{[-\frac{2}{|b|^{\gamma}},-\frac{1}{|b|^{\gamma}}]}(y_{1})e^{-\frac{|y_{2}|}{2}}.

The gradient terms satisfy:

|b^{2}y_{1}\chi_{b}P_{y_{1}}|\lesssim b^{2}|y_{1}|e^{-\frac{|y_{1}|+|y_{2}|}{2}},

and

|b^{2}y_{2}\chi_{b}P_{y_{2}}|\lesssim b^{2}|y_{2}|e^{-\frac{|y_{2}|}{2}}\left(\mathbf{1}_{[-\frac{2}{|b|^{\gamma}},0]}(y_{1})+\mathbf{1}_{[0,\infty)}(y_{1})e^{-\frac{|y_{1}|}{2}}\right)\lesssim b^{2}\left(\mathbf{1}_{[-\frac{2}{|b|^{\gamma}},0]}(y_{1})+e^{-\frac{|y_{1}|}{2}}\right)e^{-\frac{|y_{2}|}{4}}.

Finally, the nonlinear interaction satisfies
|-3b^{2}(\chi_{b}^{2}P^{2}Q)_{y_{1}}|\lesssim b^{2}e^{-\frac{|y_{1}|+|y_{2}|}{2}}
.

The remaining cubic term
-b^{3}(\chi_{b}^{3}P^{3})_{y_{1}}
satisfies the
same envelope: on the transition interval, derivatives of
\chi_{b}

contribute nonnegative powers of
|b|^{\gamma}
, and away from it one uses
the decay of
P
and its derivatives, together with
|b|^{3}\leq b^{2}
.
Summing these contributions yields the bound for
|\Psi_{b}|
. The estimates for
|(\Psi_{b})_{y_{2}y_{2}}|
and
|\partial_{y_{1}}^{k}\Psi_{b}|
follow from identical computations.
∎

Modulated Flow

Since
u_{0}\in\mathcal{T}_{\alpha^{*}}
, we assume there exists
t_{0}>0
such that for all
t\in[0,t_{0}]
,
u(t)\in\mathcal{T}_{\alpha^{*}}
. This implies the existence of parameters
\lambda^{\sharp}(t)>0
and
(x^{\sharp}_{1}(t),x^{\sharp}_{2}(t))\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
such that:

\left\|Q-\lambda^{\sharp}(t)u\Big(t,\lambda^{\sharp}(t)y_{1}+x^{\sharp}_{1}(t),\lambda^{\sharp}(t)y_{2}+x^{\sharp}_{2}(t)\Big)\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}<\nu<\nu_{0},

for some sufficiently small
\nu_{0}>0
.

Lemma 4.2
.

(modulated flow) Let
I=[0,t_{0}]
, and let
u\in C(I;H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
. Assume that for every

t\in I
there exist
\lambda^{\sharp}(t)>0
and

x^{\sharp}(t)=(x^{\sharp}_{1}(t),x^{\sharp}_{2}(t))\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
such that

\left\|Q-\lambda^{\sharp}(t)u\bigl(t,\lambda^{\sharp}(t)y_{1}+x^{\sharp}_{1}(t),\lambda^{\sharp}(t)y_{2}+x^{\sharp}_{2}(t)\bigr)\right\|_{L^{2}}<\nu,

where
0<\nu<\nu_{0}
and
\nu_{0}
