is universal and sufficiently small. Then
there exist differentiable functions

\lambda:I\to(0,\infty),\qquad x_{1},x_{2},b:I\to\mathbb{R}

such that

\varepsilon(t,y)=\lambda(t)u(t,\lambda(t)y_{1}+x_{1}(t),\lambda(t)y_{2}+x_{2}(t))-Q_{b(t)}(y_{1},y_{2})

satisfies

(\varepsilon(t),Q)=(\varepsilon(t),\varphi\Lambda Q)=(\varepsilon(t),\varphi Q_{y_{1}})=(\varepsilon(t),\varphi Q_{y_{2}})=0.

(4.4)

Moreover,

\left|\frac{\lambda(t)}{\lambda^{\sharp}(t)}-1\right|+|b(t)|+\Bigg|\frac{(x_{1}(t),x_{2}(t))-(x^{\sharp}_{1}(t),x^{\sharp}_{2}(t))}{\lambda^{\sharp}(t)}\Bigg|+\|\varepsilon(t)\|_{L^{2}}\leq\delta(\nu),

where
\delta(\nu)\to 0
as
\nu\to 0
.

If, in addition, for some
\alpha^{\sharp}>0
and for the same admissible parameters,

\left\|Q-\lambda^{\sharp}(t)u\bigl(t,\lambda^{\sharp}(t)y_{1}+x^{\sharp}_{1}(t),\lambda^{\sharp}(t)y_{2}+x^{\sharp}_{2}(t)\bigr)\right\|_{H^{1}}<\alpha^{\sharp},

(4.5)

then

\|\varepsilon(t)\|_{H^{1}}\leq C\alpha^{\sharp}+C\delta(\nu).

(4.6)

Proof.

Step 1: Local Decomposition around the Soliton.

Let

\zeta_{1}=Q,\qquad\zeta_{2}=\varphi(y_{1})Q_{y_{2}},\qquad\zeta_{3}=\varphi(y_{1})\Lambda Q,\qquad\zeta_{4}=\varphi(y_{1})Q_{y_{1}}.

For
\alpha>0
, set

U_{\alpha}=\{u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}):\|u-Q\|_{L^{2}}\leq\alpha\}.

For
u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
and parameters

p=(\lambda_{1},\hat{x}_{1},\hat{x}_{2},b)

near

p_{0}=(1,0,0,0),

define

\varepsilon_{p}(y_{1},y_{2})=\lambda_{1}u(\lambda_{1}y_{1}+\hat{x}_{1},\lambda_{1}y_{2}+\hat{x}_{2})-Q-b\chi(|b|^{\gamma}y_{1})P.

We do not use the implicit function theorem for the map

b\mapsto Q_{b}

as an
L^{2}
-valued map. Instead, we apply it to the finite-dimensional
scalar map

\rho=(\rho^{1},\rho^{2},\rho^{3},\rho^{4}),\qquad\rho^{k}(u,p):=(\varepsilon_{p},\zeta_{k}),\quad k=1,\dots,4.

We claim that
\rho
is
C^{1}
from a neighborhood of

(Q,p_{0})
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\times\mathbb{R}^{4}
into
\mathbb{R}^{4}
.

Indeed, for the dependence on
(\lambda_{1},\hat{x}_{1},\hat{x}_{2})
, we write

\left(\lambda_{1}u(\lambda_{1}y+\hat{x}),\zeta_{k}(y)\right)_{y}=\lambda_{1}^{-1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}u(z)\,\zeta_{k}\!\left(\frac{z-\hat{x}}{\lambda_{1}}\right)\,dz,

where
y=(y_{1},y_{2})
and
\hat{x}=(\hat{x}_{1},\hat{x}_{2}).
Since each
\zeta_{k}
is smooth and exponentially decaying, this expression
is
C^{1}
in
(\lambda_{1},\hat{x}_{1},\hat{x}_{2})
for
u\in L^{2}
.

The only point requiring care is the
b
-dependence. For each
k
, define

\Theta_{k}(b)=b\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\chi(|b|^{\gamma}y_{1})P(y)\zeta_{k}(y)\,dy.

Although
P\notin L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, the product
P\zeta_{k}
is integrable,
because
P
has only a bounded left tail in
y_{1}
, with decay in
y_{2}
,
whereas
\zeta_{k}
is exponentially localized. Hence

\frac{\Theta_{k}(b)-\Theta_{k}(0)}{b}=\iint\chi(|b|^{\gamma}y_{1})P\zeta_{k}\longrightarrow\iint P\zeta_{k}\qquad\text{as }b\to 0.

Moreover, for
b\neq 0
,

\Theta_{k}^{\prime}(b)=\iint\left[\chi(|b|^{\gamma}y_{1})+\gamma|b|^{\gamma}y_{1}\chi^{\prime}(|b|^{\gamma}y_{1})\right]P\zeta_{k}.

The first term converges to
(P,\zeta_{k})
by dominated convergence. The
second term is supported where

y_{1}\sim-|b|^{-\gamma},

and therefore tends to zero because
\zeta_{k}
decays exponentially in

|y_{1}|
. Thus
\Theta_{k}\in C^{1}
near
b=0
, with

\Theta_{k}^{\prime}(0)=(P,\zeta_{k}).

Consequently,

\partial_{b}\rho^{k}(Q,p_{0})=-(P,\zeta_{k}),

where this derivative is understood as a derivative of the scalar functional

\rho^{k}
, not as an
L^{2}
-derivative of
Q_{b}
.

Step 2: Jacobian Analysis.

We follow the approach of Lemma 2 in
[
48
]
. We define four functionals corresponding to the orthogonality conditions:

\displaystyle\rho^{1}(u,\lambda_{1},\hat{x}_{1},\hat{x}_{2},b)

\displaystyle=\iint\varepsilon_{\lambda_{1},\hat{x}_{1},\hat{x}_{2},b}\,Q,

\displaystyle\rho^{2}(u,\lambda_{1},\hat{x}_{1},\hat{x}_{2},b)

\displaystyle=\iint\varepsilon_{\lambda_{1},\hat{x}_{1},\hat{x}_{2},b}\,\varphi(y_{1})Q_{y_{2}},

\displaystyle\rho^{3}(u,\lambda_{1},\hat{x}_{1},\hat{x}_{2},b)

\displaystyle=\iint\varepsilon_{\lambda_{1},\hat{x}_{1},\hat{x}_{2},b}\,\varphi(y_{1})\Lambda Q,

\displaystyle\rho^{4}(u,\lambda_{1},\hat{x}_{1},\hat{x}_{2},b)

\displaystyle=\iint\varepsilon_{\lambda_{1},\hat{x}_{1},\hat{x}_{2},b}\,\varphi(y_{1})Q_{y_{1}}.

We verify the solvability of the system
\rho^{i}=0
near the reference point
p_{0}=(\lambda_{1}=1,\hat{x}_{1}=0,\hat{x}_{2}=0,b=0)
with
u=Q
. At this point, the partial derivatives of
\varepsilon
are:

\frac{\partial\varepsilon}{\partial\lambda_{1}}\Big|_{p_{0}}=\Lambda Q,\quad\frac{\partial\varepsilon}{\partial\hat{x}_{1}}\Big|_{p_{0}}=Q_{y_{1}},\quad\frac{\partial\varepsilon}{\partial\hat{x}_{2}}\Big|_{p_{0}}=Q_{y_{2}},\quad\frac{\partial\varepsilon}{\partial b}\Big|_{p_{0}}=-P.

We compute the Jacobian matrix of the vector
\rho=(\rho^{1},\rho^{2},\rho^{3},\rho^{4})
with respect to
(\lambda_{1},\hat{x}_{1},\hat{x}_{2},b)
at
p_{0}
:

1. Derivatives of
\rho^{1}
(Orthogonality against
Q
):

\displaystyle\partial_{\lambda_{1}}\rho^{1}

\displaystyle=(\Lambda Q,Q)=0,

\displaystyle\partial_{\hat{x}_{1}}\rho^{1}

\displaystyle=(Q_{y_{1}},Q)=0,

\displaystyle\partial_{\hat{x}_{2}}\rho^{1}

\displaystyle=(Q_{y_{2}},Q)=0,

\displaystyle\partial_{b}\rho^{1}

\displaystyle=-(P,Q)=-(P,Q).

2. Derivatives of
\rho^{2}
(Orthogonality against
\varphi Q_{y_{2}}
):

Using parity arguments (
\Lambda Q,\varphi(y_{1})
are even in
y_{2}
, while
Q_{y_{2}}
is odd):

\displaystyle\partial_{\lambda_{1}}\rho^{2}

\displaystyle=(\Lambda Q,\varphi Q_{y_{2}})=0,

\displaystyle\partial_{\hat{x}_{1}}\rho^{2}

\displaystyle=(Q_{y_{1}},\varphi Q_{y_{2}})=0,

\displaystyle\partial_{\hat{x}_{2}}\rho^{2}

\displaystyle=(Q_{y_{2}},\varphi Q_{y_{2}})\neq 0,

\displaystyle\partial_{b}\rho^{2}

\displaystyle=-(P,\varphi Q_{y_{2}})=0\quad\text{(since $P$ is even in $y_{2}$)}.

3. Derivatives of
\rho^{3}
(Orthogonality against
\varphi\Lambda Q
):

\displaystyle\partial_{\lambda_{1}}\rho^{3}

\displaystyle=(\Lambda Q,\varphi\Lambda Q),

\displaystyle\partial_{\hat{x}_{1}}\rho^{3}

\displaystyle=(Q_{y_{1}},\varphi\Lambda Q),

\displaystyle\partial_{\hat{x}_{2}}\rho^{3}

\displaystyle=(Q_{y_{2}},\varphi\Lambda Q)=0,

\displaystyle\partial_{b}\rho^{3}

\displaystyle=-(P,\varphi\Lambda Q).

4. Derivatives of
\rho^{4}
(Orthogonality against
\varphi Q_{y_{1}}
):

\displaystyle\partial_{\lambda_{1}}\rho^{4}

\displaystyle=(\Lambda Q,\varphi Q_{y_{1}}),

\displaystyle\partial_{\hat{x}_{1}}\rho^{4}

\displaystyle=(Q_{y_{1}},\varphi Q_{y_{1}}),

\displaystyle\partial_{\hat{x}_{2}}\rho^{4}

\displaystyle=(Q_{y_{2}},\varphi Q_{y_{1}})=0,

\displaystyle\partial_{b}\rho^{4}

\displaystyle=-(P,\varphi Q_{y_{1}}).

At
(u,p)=(Q,p_{0})
, the Jacobian matrix
J=D_{p}\rho(Q,p_{0})
is:

J=\begin{bmatrix}0&0&0&-(P,Q)\\
0&0&(Q_{y_{2}},\varphi Q_{y_{2}})&0\\
(\Lambda Q,\varphi\Lambda Q)&(Q_{y_{1}},\varphi\Lambda Q)&0&-(P,\varphi\Lambda Q)\\
(\Lambda Q,\varphi Q_{y_{1}})&(Q_{y_{1}},\varphi Q_{y_{1}})&0&-(P,\varphi Q_{y_{1}})\end{bmatrix}.

Expanding the determinant, we find:

\det J=(P,Q)\cdot(Q_{y_{2}},\varphi Q_{y_{2}})\cdot\det M^{*}\neq 0,

where
M^{*}
is defined in (
3.4
). The non-vanishing of this determinant guarantees the local invertibility.

Step 3: Conclusion.

By the finite-dimensional implicit function theorem applied in
L^{2}
to the scalar
map
\rho
, there exist
\overline{\alpha}>0
, a neighborhood

V\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}
of
(1,0,0,0)
, and a unique
C^{1}
map

u\mapsto(\lambda_{1}(u),\hat{x}_{1}(u),\hat{x}_{2}(u),b(u))\in V

for
u\in U_{\overline{\alpha}}
, such that

\rho^{k}(u,\lambda_{1}(u),\hat{x}_{1}(u),\hat{x}_{2}(u),b(u))=0,\qquad k=1,\dots,4.

Equivalently, the corresponding error

\varepsilon_{\lambda_{1},\hat{x}_{1},\hat{x}_{2},b}=\lambda_{1}u(\lambda_{1}y+\hat{x})-Q_{b}

satisfies the four orthogonality conditions

(\varepsilon,Q)=(\varepsilon,\varphi Q_{y_{2}})=(\varepsilon,\varphi\Lambda Q)=(\varepsilon,\varphi Q_{y_{1}})=0.

Finally, shrinking
\overline{\alpha}
if necessary, the parameters satisfy

|\lambda_{1}-1|+|\hat{x}_{1}|+|\hat{x}_{2}|+|b|\lesssim\|u-Q\|_{L^{2}}.

Moreover,

\|\varepsilon_{\lambda_{1},\hat{x}_{1},\hat{x}_{2},b}\|_{L^{2}}\leq\delta(\|u-Q\|_{L^{2}}).

Now, take
\nu<\min\{\overline{\alpha},\nu_{0}\}.
For all time
t\in[0,t_{0}]
, there are parameters
\lambda^{\sharp}(t)>0,

(x^{\sharp}_{1}(t),x^{\sharp}_{2}(t))\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
such that

\|Q-\lambda^{\sharp}(t)u(t,\lambda^{\sharp}(t)x_{1}+x^{\sharp}_{1}(t),\lambda^{\sharp}(t)x_{2}+x^{\sharp}_{2}(t))\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}<\nu.

Applying the preceding claim to the rescaled function
\lambda^{\sharp}(t)u(t,\lambda^{\sharp}(t)x_{1}+x^{\sharp}_{1}(t),\lambda^{\sharp}(t)x_{2}+x^{\sharp}_{2}(t))
, define

\lambda(t)=\lambda^{\sharp}(t)\lambda_{1}(t),\quad x_{1}(t)=\lambda^{\sharp}(t)\hat{x}_{1}(t)+x^{\sharp}_{1}(t),\quad x_{2}(t)=\lambda^{\sharp}(t)\hat{x}_{2}(t)+x^{\sharp}_{2}(t).

Therefore

\varepsilon(t,x_{1},x_{2})=\lambda(t)u(t,\lambda(t)x_{1}+x_{1}(t),\lambda(t)x_{2}+x_{2}(t))-Q_{b}(x_{1},x_{2})
satisfies

\varepsilon(t)\perp Q,\varepsilon(t)\perp\varphi(y_{1})\Lambda Q,\varepsilon(t)\perp\varphi(y_{1})Q_{y_{1}},\varepsilon(t)\perp\varphi(y_{1})Q_{y_{2}},

and

\|\varepsilon(t)\|_{L^{2}}+\Bigg|\frac{\lambda(t)}{\lambda^{\sharp}(t)}-1\Bigg|+|b(t)|+\Bigg|\frac{(x_{1}(t),x_{2}(t))-(x^{\sharp}_{1}(t),x^{\sharp}_{2}(t))}{\lambda^{\sharp}(t)}\Bigg|<\delta(\nu),

for all
t\in[0,t_{0}].

Finally, assume the additional
H^{1}
closeness
(
4.5
). The translation and scaling maps are
continuous on
H^{1}
near the identity, and

\|Q_{b}-Q\|_{H^{1}}\lesssim|b|^{1-\gamma/2}\lesssim\delta(\nu).

Consequently,

\|\varepsilon(t)\|_{H^{1}}\leq C\alpha^{\sharp}+\delta(\nu),

which gives (
4.6
).

Since the IFT gives local uniqueness, parameter maps constructed at nearby times agree on overlaps. This gives continuous global parameters on the interval on which the solution remains in the tube. Differentiability in time can then be obtained from the differentiated orthogonality equations and the evolution equation.
∎

4.2
Modulation estimates

We define a locally Lipschitz weight
\vartheta
with

|\nabla\vartheta|\leq C
almost everywhere by

\vartheta(y_{1},y_{2})=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{\alpha_{1}}|y_{1}|+\left(1-\frac{1}{\alpha_{2}^{2}}\right)|y_{2}|&\text{if }y_{1}<-1,\\
\frac{1}{200\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}|y_{1}|+\left(1-\frac{1}{\alpha_{2}^{2}}\right)|y_{2}|&\text{if }y_{1}\geq 0,\end{cases}

(4.7)

where
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}
are defined in (
3.1
), and we use a
smooth interpolation of the
y_{1}
-dependent part on
[-1,0]
. We further
define the localized mass and energy functionals:

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}(s)

\displaystyle=\iint\varepsilon^{2}(s)e^{-\vartheta(y_{1},y_{2})}\quad\left(\lesssim\|\varepsilon\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\right),

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}(s)

\displaystyle=\iint(|\nabla\varepsilon|^{2}+\varepsilon^{2})(s)e^{-\vartheta(y_{1},y_{2})}.

Consequently, all functions depending on
t\in[0,t_{0}]
can be viewed as depending on
s\in[0,s_{0}]
, where
s_{0}=s(t_{0})
.

We mention a Sobolev-type inequality that we are going to use to bound the nonlinearity terms in the modulation equation.

Lemma 4.3

(Weighted Sobolev Lemma)
.

Let
\theta\in W^{1,1}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
be a positive function such that there exists
C_{\theta}>1
with
|\nabla\theta|\leq C_{\theta}\theta
. Suppose that
u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\cap H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\theta dx_{1}dx_{2}),
then

\iint u^{4}\theta dx_{1}dx_{2}\leq 3C_{\theta}^{2}\|u\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\iint\Big(u^{2}+u_{x_{1}}^{2}+u_{x_{2}}^{2})\theta dx_{1}dx_{2},

\iint|u|^{3}\theta dx_{1}dx_{2}\leq\sqrt{3}C_{\theta}\|u\|_{L^{2}}\iint\Big(u^{2}+u_{x_{1}}^{2}+u_{x_{2}}^{2})\theta dx_{1}dx_{2}.

We include a proof of the lemma in Appendix
A
(see Lemma
A.1
).

We now derive rough estimates for the modulation parameters.

Lemma 4.4

(Estimates for modulated coefficients)
.

Suppose that, for
t\in[0,t_{0}]
,

\|\varepsilon(t)\|_{L^{2}}\leq\hat{\nu},

where
0<\hat{\nu}<\nu_{0}
is a sufficiently small universal constant and

\nu_{0}
is defined in Lemma
4.2
. Then the map

s\in[0,s_{0}]\longmapsto\bigl(\lambda(s),x_{1}(s),x_{2}(s),b(s)\bigr)

is
C^{1}
, and

(\varepsilon,Q)=(\varepsilon,\varphi(y_{1})Q_{y_{1}})=(\varepsilon,\varphi(y_{1})\Lambda Q)=(\varepsilon,\varphi(y_{1})Q_{y_{2}})=0.

Moreover, the modulation parameters satisfy

\begin{split}|b_{s}+cb^{2}|&\lesssim|b|\mathcal{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}+\mathcal{M}+\|\varepsilon\|_{L^{2}}\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}+|b|^{3},\\
|b_{s}|&\lesssim b^{2}+\mathcal{M}+\|\varepsilon\|_{L^{2}}\widetilde{\mathcal{M}},\\
\left|\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda}+b\right|+\left|\frac{(x_{1})_{s}}{\lambda}-1\right|+\left|\frac{(x_{2})_{s}}{\lambda}\right|&\lesssim b^{2}+\mathcal{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}+\|\varepsilon\|_{L^{2}}\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}.\end{split}

(4.8)

Proof.

The
C^{1}
regularity of the parameters and the orthogonality conditions
follow from Lemma
4.2
. It remains to estimate their
derivatives. Set

\text{Mod}(s):=\left|\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda}+b\right|+\left|\frac{(x_{1})_{s}}{\lambda}-1\right|+\left|\frac{(x_{2})_{s}}{\lambda}\right|.

Step 1.

Taking the
L^{2}
inner product of (
4.2
) with

Q
, and using

(\varepsilon,Q)=0,\qquad((L\varepsilon)_{y_{1}},Q)=-(\varepsilon,LQ_{y_{1}})=0,

gives

\begin{split}&\left(\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda}+b\right)(\Lambda Q_{b},Q)+\left(\frac{(x_{1})_{s}}{\lambda}-1\right)((Q_{b})_{y_{1}},Q)+\frac{(x_{2})_{s}}{\lambda}((Q_{b})_{y_{2}},Q)\\
&\qquad-b_{s}\bigl((\chi_{b}+\gamma y_{1}(\chi_{b})_{y_{1}})P,Q\bigr)\\
&=-\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda}(\Lambda\varepsilon,Q)-\left(\frac{(x_{1})_{s}}{\lambda}-1\right)(\varepsilon_{y_{1}},Q)-\frac{(x_{2})_{s}}{\lambda}(\varepsilon_{y_{2}},Q)-(\Psi_{b},Q)\\
&\qquad+(R_{b}(\varepsilon)_{y_{1}},Q)+(R_{NL}(\varepsilon)_{y_{1}},Q).\end{split}

The identities
(\Lambda Q,Q)=0
,
(Q,\nabla Q)=0
, and

(P,\nabla Q)=0
imply

(\Lambda Q_{b},Q)=-b(\chi_{b}P,\Lambda Q)=b(\Lambda P,Q)+O(|b|^{4}),

|((Q_{b})_{y_{1}},Q)|=|-b(\chi_{b}P,Q_{y_{1}})|\lesssim|b|^{4},\qquad|((Q_{b})_{y_{2}},Q)|=|-b(\chi_{b}P,Q_{y_{2}})|\lesssim|b|^{4}.

Moreover,

\displaystyle\bigl((\chi_{b}+\gamma y_{1}(\chi_{b})_{y_{1}})P,Q\bigr)

\displaystyle=(P,Q)-\bigl([(1-\chi_{b})+\gamma y_{1}(1-\chi_{b})_{y_{1}}]P,Q\bigr).

The function in square brackets vanishes for

y_{1}\geq-|b|^{-\gamma}
, equals
1
for

y_{1}\leq-2|b|^{-\gamma}
, and is uniformly bounded on the transition
region. Hence, using the decay of
PQ
,

\begin{split}&\left|\iint[(1-\chi_{b})+\gamma y_{1}(1-\chi_{b})_{y_{1}}]PQ\right|\\
&\qquad\lesssim\iint_{\{y_{1}\leq-|b|^{-\gamma}\}}e^{-\frac{|y_{1}|+|y_{2}|}{2}}\,dy_{1}dy_{2}\lesssim e^{-\frac{1}{2|b|^{\gamma}}}\lesssim|b|^{4}.\end{split}

Consequently,

\displaystyle\bigl((\chi_{b}+\gamma y_{1}(\chi_{b})_{y_{1}})P,Q\bigr)

\displaystyle=(P,Q)+O(|b|^{4})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\left(\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\Lambda Q\,dy_{1}\right)^{2}dy_{2}+O(|b|^{4}).

Furthermore, the expansion (
4.3
) gives

\displaystyle(\Psi_{b},Q)

\displaystyle=\bigl((-\Delta Q_{b}+Q_{b}-Q_{b}^{3})_{y_{1}}-b\Lambda Q_{b},Q\bigr)

\displaystyle=-b^{2}\bigl(\Lambda P+(3QP^{2})_{y_{1}},Q\bigr)-c_{\Psi}b^{3}+O(|b|^{4}).

Claim.

We have

\bigl(\Lambda P+(3QP^{2})_{y_{1}},Q\bigr)=\frac{c}{4}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\left(\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\Lambda Q\,dy_{1}\right)^{2}dy_{2}=c(P,Q),

where
c
is defined in (
2.2
).

Proof of the Claim.

Since
(LP)_{y_{1}}=\Lambda Q
, differentiation of
LP
yields

6QQ_{y_{1}}P=L(P_{y_{1}})-(LP)_{y_{1}}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle-((3QP^{2})_{y_{1}},Q)

\displaystyle=(3QP^{2},Q_{y_{1}})=\frac{1}{2}\bigl(L(P_{y_{1}})-(LP)_{y_{1}},P\bigr),

while, since
\Lambda^{*}=-\Lambda
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
,

-(\Lambda P,Q)=(P,\Lambda Q)=(P,(LP)_{y_{1}}).

Adding the two identities, integrating first on

[-R,R]\times\mathbb{R}
, and then letting
R\to\infty
, gives

\displaystyle-\bigl(\Lambda P+(3QP^{2})_{y_{1}},Q\bigr)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\bigl(L(P_{y_{1}}),P\bigr)+\frac{1}{2}\bigl((LP)_{y_{1}},P\bigr)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\left[P(y_{1},y_{2})LP(y_{1},y_{2})\right]_{y_{1}=-\infty}^{y_{1}=+\infty}dy_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}F(y_{2})\left(\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\Lambda Q\,dy_{1}\right)dy_{2}.

Here we used the decay at
+\infty
and the limits at
-\infty

established in Lemma
3.9
. For every
y_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
,
denote

F(y_{2})=\lim_{y_{1}\to-\infty}P(y_{1},y_{2}).

(4.9)

The function
F
is even, exponentially decaying, and satisfies

\begin{cases}-F_{y_{2}y_{2}}+F=-\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\Lambda Q\,dy_{1}=-g_{Q}(y_{2}),\\
\displaystyle\lim_{y_{2}\to-\infty}F(y_{2})=\displaystyle\lim_{y_{2}\to+\infty}F(y_{2})=0,\end{cases}

(4.10)

where
g_{Q}
is defined in (
2.1
). Taking the Fourier
transform in (
4.10
) gives

(1+\xi^{2})\widehat{F}(\xi)=-\widehat{g_{Q}}(\xi).

Thus, by Plancherel’s theorem and (
2.2
),

\frac{\frac{1}{2}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}F\left(-\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\Lambda Q\,dy_{1}\right)dy_{2}}{\frac{1}{4}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\left(\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\Lambda Q\,dy_{1}\right)^{2}dy_{2}}=\frac{\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{2}{1+\xi^{2}}|\widehat{g_{Q}}(\xi)|^{2}\,d\xi}{\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}|\widehat{g_{Q}}(\xi)|^{2}\,d\xi}=c.

(4.11)

This proves the claim.

It remains to estimate the terms containing
\varepsilon
.

Linear and drift terms.

The decay from Lemma
3.3
, integration by parts, and
Cauchy–Schwarz give

\displaystyle|(\varepsilon_{y_{1}},Q)|+|(\varepsilon_{y_{2}},Q)|

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\iint\varepsilon^{2}e^{-\vartheta}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\iint|\nabla Q|^{2}e^{\vartheta}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathcal{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}.

The same argument gives

|(\varepsilon,\Lambda Q)|+|(\varepsilon,PQQ_{y_{1}})|\lesssim\mathcal{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}.

All weighted integrals involving
Q
converge because its exponential
decay dominates the growth of
e^{\vartheta}
.

Linearized modulation error
(R_{b})
.

Since
R_{b}(\varepsilon)=3(Q_{b}^{2}-Q^{2})\varepsilon
,

\displaystyle(R_{b}(\varepsilon)_{y_{1}},Q)

\displaystyle=-(R_{b}(\varepsilon),Q_{y_{1}})

\displaystyle=-6b(\chi_{b}PQQ_{y_{1}},\varepsilon)-3b^{2}(\chi_{b}^{2}P^{2}Q_{y_{1}},\varepsilon)

\displaystyle=-6b(PQQ_{y_{1}},\varepsilon)+O(|b|^{4}+\mathcal{M}).

Nonlinear terms
(R_{NL})
.

Using

|R_{NL}(\varepsilon)|\lesssim|Q_{b}|\varepsilon^{2}+|\varepsilon|^{3}

and Lemma
4.3
with

\theta=e^{-\vartheta}
, for which

|\nabla\theta|\lesssim\theta
, we obtain

\displaystyle|(R_{NL}(\varepsilon)_{y_{1}},Q)|

\displaystyle=|(R_{NL}(\varepsilon),Q_{y_{1}})|\lesssim\iint\varepsilon^{2}e^{-\vartheta}+\iint|\varepsilon|^{3}e^{-\vartheta}\lesssim\mathcal{M}+\|\varepsilon\|_{L^{2}}\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}.

Combining the preceding expansions and remainder estimates yields

\begin{split}&\left|(b_{s}+cb^{2})\frac{1}{4}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\left(\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\Lambda Q\,dy_{1}\right)^{2}dy_{2}+\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda}(\varepsilon,\Lambda Q)\right.\\
&\left.\qquad-b\left[6(\varepsilon,PQQ_{y_{1}})+\left(\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda}+b\right)(\Lambda P,Q)\right]+c_{\Psi}b^{3}\right|\\
&\qquad\lesssim\left(\left|\frac{(x_{1})_{s}}{\lambda}-1\right|+\left|\frac{(x_{2})_{s}}{\lambda}\right|\right)\bigl(|b|^{4}+\mathcal{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}\bigr)+|b|^{4}+\mathcal{M}+\|\varepsilon\|_{L^{2}}\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}.\end{split}

(4.12)

Since

\left|\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda}\right|\leq\left|\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda}+b\right|+|b|,

we infer

|b_{s}+cb^{2}|\lesssim\text{Mod}(s)\bigl(|b|+\mathcal{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}\bigr)+|b|\mathcal{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}+\mathcal{M}+\|\varepsilon\|_{L^{2}}\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}+|b|^{3}.

(4.13)

In particular,

|b_{s}|\lesssim\text{Mod}(s)\bigl(|b|+\mathcal{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}\bigr)+b^{2}+\mathcal{M}+\|\varepsilon\|_{L^{2}}\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}+|b|^{3}.

(4.14)

Step 2.

Let
g\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
satisfy

\sum_{\ell=0}^{3}|\nabla^{\ell}g|\lesssim(1+|y|)\varphi(y_{1})e^{-|y|},\qquad(\varepsilon(s),g)=0\quad\text{on }[0,s_{0}].

This class contains the three functions used below. Taking the inner
product of (
4.2
) with
g
gives

\displaystyle(\varepsilon,L(g_{y_{1}}))={}

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda}+b\right)(\Lambda Q_{b},g)+\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda}(\Lambda\varepsilon,g)

\displaystyle+\left(\frac{(x_{1})_{s}}{\lambda}-1\right)((Q_{b})_{y_{1}}+\varepsilon_{y_{1}},g)+\frac{(x_{2})_{s}}{\lambda}((Q_{b})_{y_{2}}+\varepsilon_{y_{2}},g)

\displaystyle+(\Phi_{b}+\Psi_{b},g)+(R_{b}(\varepsilon)+R_{NL}(\varepsilon),g_{y_{1}}).

For constants
c_{\Lambda},c_{1},c_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
depending only on
g
,
the localization of
g
and the decay of
P
give

(\Lambda Q_{b}-\Lambda Q,g)=c_{\Lambda}b+O(|b|^{4}),

((Q_{b})_{y_{1}}-Q_{y_{1}},g)=c_{1}b+O(|b|^{4}),\qquad((Q_{b})_{y_{2}}-Q_{y_{2}},g)=c_{2}b+O(|b|^{4}).

Choose
\beta
such that

\alpha_{1}=1+\frac{1}{100}>\alpha_{2}=1+\frac{1}{200}>\beta>\frac{\sqrt{2}\,\alpha_{1}}{\sqrt{\alpha_{1}^{2}+1}}\approx 1.004963>1.

Then

\frac{1}{\alpha_{1}}+\frac{1}{\alpha_{2}}-\frac{2}{\beta}<0,\qquad\frac{2}{\beta^{2}}-1-\frac{1}{\alpha_{1}^{2}}<0.

Moreover,

|\Lambda g|\lesssim(1+|y|)^{3}\varphi(y_{1})e^{-|y|}\lesssim\varphi(y_{1})e^{-\frac{|y_{1}|}{\beta}-\left(1-\frac{1}{\beta^{2}}\right)|y_{2}|}.

Using the definition of
\vartheta
, we obtain

\displaystyle\iint|\Lambda g|^{2}e^{\vartheta}

\displaystyle\lesssim\iint e^{\left(\frac{1}{\alpha_{1}}+\frac{1}{\alpha_{2}}-\frac{2}{\beta}\right)|y_{1}|+\left(\frac{2}{\beta^{2}}-1-\frac{1}{\alpha_{1}^{2}}\right)|y_{2}|}<\infty.

Consequently, Cauchy–Schwarz and integration by parts yield

|(\Lambda\varepsilon,g)|=|(\varepsilon,\Lambda g)|\lesssim\mathcal{M}^{\frac{1}{2}},

and

|(\varepsilon_{y_{1}},g)|+|(\varepsilon_{y_{2}},g)|\lesssim\mathcal{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Thus,

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda}\right||(\Lambda\varepsilon,g)|+\left|\frac{(x_{1})_{s}}{\lambda}-1\right||(\varepsilon_{y_{1}},g)|+\left|\frac{(x_{2})_{s}}{\lambda}\right||(\varepsilon_{y_{2}},g)|\lesssim\bigl(\text{Mod}(s)+|b|\bigr)\mathcal{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Because

\operatorname{supp}\bigl(1-\chi_{b}-\gamma y_{1}(\chi_{b})_{y_{1}}\bigr)\subset\{y_{1}\leq-|b|^{-\gamma}\}

and
g
is exponentially localized,

\displaystyle((\chi_{b}+\gamma y_{1}(\chi_{b})_{y_{1}})P,g)

\displaystyle=(P,g)+O(|b|^{4})=c_{g}+O(|b|^{4}),\qquad c_{g}\in\mathbb{R}.

Hence

\displaystyle(\Phi_{b},g)

\displaystyle=-b_{s}c_{g}+O(|b_{s}||b|^{4})=cb^{2}c_{g}-(b_{s}+cb^{2})c_{g}+O(|b_{s}||b|^{4}).

Using (
4.13
) and (
4.14
), we obtain

\displaystyle(\Phi_{b},g)=cb^{2}c_{g}+O\Big(

\displaystyle\text{Mod}(s)(|b|+\mathcal{M}^{\frac{1}{2}})+|b|^{3}+|b|\mathcal{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle+\mathcal{M}+\|\varepsilon\|_{L^{2}}\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}\Big).

Recall that

S_{P}=3(QP^{2})_{y_{1}}+\Lambda P.

The profile error satisfies

(\Psi_{b},g)=-b^{2}(S_{P},g)+O(|b|^{3}).

Furthermore, the decay of
g_{y_{1}}
,
Q
, and
P
gives

|(R_{b}(\varepsilon),g_{y_{1}})|\lesssim|b|\mathcal{M}^{\frac{1}{2}},

while Lemma
4.3
yields

|(R_{NL}(\varepsilon),g_{y_{1}})|\lesssim\mathcal{M}+\|\varepsilon\|_{L^{2}}\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}.

Substitution into the projected equation gives

\displaystyle(\varepsilon,L(g_{y_{1}}))={}

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda}+b\right)(\Lambda Q,g)+\left(\frac{(x_{1})_{s}}{\lambda}-1\right)(Q_{y_{1}},g)+\frac{(x_{2})_{s}}{\lambda}(Q_{y_{2}},g)+\tilde{c}_{g}b^{2}+\mathcal{R}_{g},

(4.15)

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{g}=O\Big(

\displaystyle\text{Mod}(s)(|b|+\mathcal{M}^{\frac{1}{2}})+|b|^{3}+|b|\mathcal{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}+\mathcal{M}+\|\varepsilon\|_{L^{2}}\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}\Big).

Setting
g=\varphi Q_{y_{2}}
in (
4.15
), using parity and

(Q_{y_{2}},\varphi Q_{y_{2}})>0,\qquad|(\varepsilon,L((\varphi Q_{y_{2}})_{y_{1}}))|\lesssim\mathcal{M}^{\frac{1}{2}},

gives

\left|\frac{(x_{2})_{s}}{\lambda}\right|\lesssim\text{Mod}(s)(|b|+\mathcal{M}^{\frac{1}{2}})+b^{2}+\mathcal{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}+\|\varepsilon\|_{L^{2}}\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}.

(4.16)

Applying (
4.15
) with

g=\varphi Q_{y_{1}}
and
g=\varphi\Lambda Q
, and using the same
weighted Cauchy–Schwarz estimate for

(\varepsilon,L(g_{y_{1}}))
, gives

\displaystyle\left|\left(\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda}+b\right)(\Lambda Q,\varphi(y_{1})Q_{y_{1}})+\left(\frac{(x_{1})_{s}}{\lambda}-1\right)(\varphi(y_{1})Q_{y_{1}},Q_{y_{1}})\right|

(4.17)

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathcal{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}+b^{2}+\|\varepsilon\|_{L^{2}}\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}+\text{Mod}(s)(|b|+\mathcal{M}^{\frac{1}{2}})

and

\displaystyle\left|\left(\frac{(x_{1})_{s}}{\lambda}-1\right)(\varphi(y_{1})\Lambda Q,Q_{y_{1}})+\left(\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda}+b\right)(\varphi(y_{1})\Lambda Q,\Lambda Q)\right|

(4.18)

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathcal{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}+b^{2}+\|\varepsilon\|_{L^{2}}\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}+\text{Mod}(s)(|b|+\mathcal{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}).

Step 3.

Denote

K=\text{Mod}(s)(|b|+\mathcal{M}^{\frac{1}{2}})+\mathcal{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}+b^{2}+\|\varepsilon\|_{L^{2}}\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}.

Then (
4.18
) and (
4.17
) become

\left|\left(\frac{(x_{1})_{s}}{\lambda}-1\right)(\varphi(y_{1})\Lambda Q,Q_{y_{1}})+\left(\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda}+b\right)(\varphi(y_{1})\Lambda Q,\Lambda Q)\right|\lesssim K

(4.19)

and

\left|\left(\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda}+b\right)(\Lambda Q,\varphi(y_{1})Q_{y_{1}})+\left(\frac{(x_{1})_{s}}{\lambda}-1\right)(\varphi(y_{1})Q_{y_{1}},Q_{y_{1}})\right|\lesssim K.

(4.20)

These inequalities form the linear system with coefficient matrix

M^{*}
from (
3.4
). Applying Cramer’s rule gives

|\det M^{*}|\left|\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda}+b\right|\lesssim K

(4.21)

and

|\det M^{*}|\left|\frac{(x_{1})_{s}}{\lambda}-1\right|\lesssim K.

(4.22)

Since
\det M^{*}\neq 0
, estimates (
4.16
),
(
4.21
), and (
4.22
) imply

\text{Mod}(s)\leq C\left[\text{Mod}(s)(|b|+\mathcal{M}^{\frac{1}{2}})+\mathcal{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}+b^{2}+\|\varepsilon\|_{L^{2}}\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}\right].

By Lemma
4.2
,

|b|
is uniformly small, while

\mathcal{M}^{1/2}\lesssim\|\varepsilon\|_{L^{2}}\leq\hat{\nu}
.
Choosing
\nu_{0}
and
\hat{\nu}
sufficiently small allows the first term
on the right-hand side to be absorbed. Thus,

\left|\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda}+b\right|+\left|\frac{(x_{1})_{s}}{\lambda}-1\right|+\left|\frac{(x_{2})_{s}}{\lambda}\right|\lesssim\mathcal{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}+b^{2}+\|\varepsilon\|_{L^{2}}\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}.

(4.23)

Finally, substituting (
4.23
) into
(
4.13
) and (
4.14
), and using the
smallness of
|b|+\mathcal{M}^{1/2}
, gives

|b_{s}|\lesssim\mathcal{M}+b^{2}+\|\varepsilon\|_{L^{2}}\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}

(4.24)

and

|b_{s}+cb^{2}|\lesssim|b|\mathcal{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}+\mathcal{M}+|b|^{3}+\|\varepsilon\|_{L^{2}}\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}.

(4.25)

This proves (
4.8
).
∎

4.3
Sharp Modulation Estimates

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
\langle y_{2}\rangle=(1+y_{2}^{2})^{1/2}
and define the weight

\Omega\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
by

\Omega(y_{1},y_{2})=\begin{cases}e^{-\frac{|y_{1}|}{\alpha_{1}}-(1-\frac{1}{\alpha_{2}^{2}})\langle y_{2}\rangle},&\text{if }y_{1}<-1,\\
e^{-(1-\frac{1}{\alpha_{2}^{2}})\langle y_{2}\rangle}y_{1}^{2},&\text{if }y_{1}>1,\end{cases}

with any smooth positive interpolation on
[-1,1]
such that

e^{-\vartheta}\lesssim\Omega
. Denote

\widehat{\mathcal{M}}(s)=\iint\varepsilon^{2}(s)\Omega(y_{1},y_{2})\,dy_{1}dy_{2}.

Write

C_{Q}=\frac{1}{4}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\left(\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\Lambda Q\,dy_{1}\right)^{2}dy_{2}>0,\qquad\widetilde{C}_{Q}=\frac{1}{4}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\left(\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}Q_{y_{2}}\,dy_{1}\right)^{2}dy_{2}>0,

(4.26)

and denote

J_{1}(s)=\left(\varepsilon(s),\frac{1}{2C_{Q}}\int_{-\infty}^{y_{1}}\Lambda Q\right),\qquad J_{2}(s)=\left(\varepsilon(s),\frac{1}{2\widetilde{C}_{Q}}\int_{-\infty}^{y_{1}}Q_{y_{2}}\right).

Under the assumptions of Lemma
4.4
, with

\|\varepsilon\|_{L^{2}}\leq\delta(\nu^{*})
, and the additional bound

\sup_{s\in[0,s_{0}]}\widehat{\mathcal{M}}(s)\leq 1,

we have:

a)

|J_{1}(s)|,|J_{2}(s)|\lesssim\widehat{\mathcal{M}}(s)^{1/2}
;

b)

\left|\frac{d}{ds}J_{1}(s)+\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda}J_{1}(s)-\left(\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda}+b\right)\right|\lesssim b^{2}(s)+\widehat{\mathcal{M}}(s)+\delta(\nu^{*})\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}(s);

c)

\left|\frac{d}{ds}J_{2}(s)+\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda}J_{2}(s)-\frac{(x_{2})_{s}}{\lambda}\right|\lesssim b^{2}(s)+\widehat{\mathcal{M}}(s)+\delta(\nu^{*})\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}(s).

Proof.

Set

q(y_{2})=\int_{\mathbb{R}}Q(y_{1},y_{2})\,dy_{1}.

Then

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\Lambda Q\,dy_{1}=y_{2}q^{\prime}(y_{2}),\qquad\int_{\mathbb{R}}Q_{y_{2}}\,dy_{1}=q^{\prime}(y_{2}).

Since
q
is positive, even, and tends to zero at infinity, it is not
constant; hence
q^{\prime}\not\equiv 0
, and both constants in
(
4.26
) are strictly positive.

Let
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
be fixed and time-independent, with

\sum_{|\beta|\leq 2}|\partial^{\beta}f(y)|\lesssim e^{-\vartheta(y)}.

(4.27)

The implicit constants below may depend on
f
, but are independent of

s
and
b
. Set

h(y_{1},y_{2})=\int_{-\infty}^{y_{1}}f(z,y_{2})\,dz,\qquad J_{f}=(\varepsilon,h).

Writing
\beta_{0}=1-\alpha_{2}^{-2}>0
, the weighted derivative bounds
justify differentiation under the integral and give, for
j=0,1,2
,

|\partial_{y_{2}}^{j}h(y_{1},y_{2})|\lesssim\begin{cases}e^{y_{1}/\alpha_{1}-\beta_{0}|y_{2}|},&y_{1}<-1,\\
e^{-\beta_{0}|y_{2}|},&y_{1}\geq-1.\end{cases}

These derivatives converge as
y_{1}\to+\infty
to exponentially
decaying functions of
y_{2}
. The definition of
\Omega
therefore gives

\iint\frac{h^{2}+y_{1}^{2}f^{2}+y_{2}^{2}h_{y_{2}}^{2}+f^{2}+h_{y_{2}}^{2}}{\Omega}<\infty.

Cauchy–Schwarz therefore gives

|J_{f}|+|(\varepsilon,y_{1}f)|+|(\varepsilon,y_{2}h_{y_{2}})|+|(\varepsilon,f)|+|(\varepsilon,h_{y_{2}})|\lesssim\widehat{\mathcal{M}}^{1/2}.

The functions
\Lambda Q
and
Q_{y_{2}}
satisfy
(
4.27
), so these estimates prove part a).
Also,
|Lf|\lesssim e^{-\vartheta}
, and hence

|(\varepsilon,Lf)|\lesssim\mathcal{M}^{1/2}
.
Notice that

e^{-\vartheta}\lesssim\Omega
, and hence

\mathcal{M}\lesssim\widehat{\mathcal{M}}.

We now project (
4.2
) onto
h
. Since

(\Lambda\varepsilon,h)=-(\varepsilon,\Lambda h)=-(\varepsilon,h)-(\varepsilon,y_{1}f+y_{2}h_{y_{2}}),

and
h_{y_{1}}=f
, integration by parts gives

\displaystyle(J_{f})_{s}+\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda}J_{f}

\displaystyle=-(\varepsilon,Lf)+\left(\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda}+b\right)(\Lambda Q,h)+\left(\frac{(x_{1})_{s}}{\lambda}-1\right)(Q_{y_{1}},h)+\frac{(x_{2})_{s}}{\lambda}(Q_{y_{2}},h)

\displaystyle-\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda}(\varepsilon,y_{1}f+y_{2}h_{y_{2}})-\left(\frac{(x_{1})_{s}}{\lambda}-1\right)(\varepsilon,f)-\frac{(x_{2})_{s}}{\lambda}(\varepsilon,h_{y_{2}})

\displaystyle+b\left(\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda}+b\right)(\Lambda(\chi_{b}P),h)-b\left(\frac{(x_{1})_{s}}{\lambda}-1\right)(\chi_{b}P,f)-b\frac{(x_{2})_{s}}{\lambda}(\chi_{b}P,h_{y_{2}})

\displaystyle-b_{s}\bigl([\chi_{b}+\gamma y_{1}(\chi_{b})_{y_{1}}]P,h\bigr)+(\Psi_{b},h)+(R_{NL}(\varepsilon)+R_{b}(\varepsilon),f).

All terms are well defined by the preceding weighted estimates and the
decay of
P
from Lemma
3.9
.

Using (
4.8
),

\mathcal{M}\lesssim\widehat{\mathcal{M}}
, and

\widehat{\mathcal{M}}^{1/2}\leq 1
, the terms containing

\varepsilon
and the geometric parameters satisfy

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda}\right|\bigl(|(\varepsilon,y_{1}f)|+|(\varepsilon,y_{2}h_{y_{2}})|\bigr)+\left|\frac{(x_{1})_{s}}{\lambda}-1\right|\,|(\varepsilon,f)|+\left|\frac{(x_{2})_{s}}{\lambda}\right|\,|(\varepsilon,h_{y_{2}})|

\displaystyle\lesssim|b|\widehat{\mathcal{M}}^{1/2}+\widehat{\mathcal{M}}+\delta(\nu^{*})\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}.

The left-hand decay of
h
and the right-hand and transverse decay of

P
make the pairings involving
P
uniformly bounded; hence

\displaystyle\left|b\left(\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda}+b\right)(\Lambda(\chi_{b}P),h)\right|+\left|b\left(\frac{(x_{1})_{s}}{\lambda}-1\right)(\chi_{b}P,f)\right|

\displaystyle+\left|b\frac{(x_{2})_{s}}{\lambda}(\chi_{b}P,h_{y_{2}})\right|\lesssim|b|^{3}+|b|\widehat{\mathcal{M}}^{1/2}+\delta(\nu^{*})\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}.

Moreover, the cutoff differences are supported in

y_{1}\leq-|b|^{-\gamma}
, where the decay of
h
makes their pairings
with
P
exponentially small in
|b|^{-\gamma}
. In particular,

\bigl([\chi_{b}+\gamma y_{1}(\chi_{b})_{y_{1}}]P,h\bigr)=(P,h)+O(|b|^{4}).

Thus, by (
4.24
) and (
4.25
),

\displaystyle(\Phi_{b},h)

\displaystyle=-b_{s}\bigl[(P,h)+O(|b|^{4})\bigr]=c_{f}b^{2}+O\bigl(|b|\widehat{\mathcal{M}}^{1/2}+|b|^{3}+\widehat{\mathcal{M}}+\delta(\nu^{*})\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}\bigr).

The same cutoff estimate and (
4.3
) give

(\Psi_{b},h)=-b^{2}(S_{P},h)+O(|b|^{3}),\qquad S_{P}=3(QP^{2})_{y_{1}}+\Lambda P.

Here the cubic term is
O(|b|^{3})
after integration by parts, since

P
is bounded and
f
is integrable. The weighted Sobolev estimate
and (
4.27
) also yield

\displaystyle|(R_{NL}(\varepsilon),f)|

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathcal{M}+\|\varepsilon\|_{L^{2}}\widetilde{\mathcal{M}},

\displaystyle|(R_{b}(\varepsilon),f)|

\displaystyle\lesssim|b|\mathcal{M}^{1/2}.

Combining these estimates, we obtain

\displaystyle(J_{f})_{s}+\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda}J_{f}

\displaystyle=-(\varepsilon,Lf)+\left(\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda}+b\right)(\Lambda Q,h)+\left(\frac{(x_{1})_{s}}{\lambda}-1\right)(Q_{y_{1}},h)

(4.28)

\displaystyle+\frac{(x_{2})_{s}}{\lambda}(Q_{y_{2}},h)+\widehat{c}_{f}b^{2}+\mathcal{R}_{f},

where

\mathcal{R}_{f}=O\bigl(|b|^{3}+|b|\widehat{\mathcal{M}}^{1/2}+\widehat{\mathcal{M}}+\delta(\nu^{*})\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}\bigr).

Apply (
4.28
) first with
f=\Lambda Q
. If

h=\int_{-\infty}^{y_{1}}\Lambda Q
, then

(\varepsilon,L\Lambda Q)=-2(\varepsilon,Q)=0,\qquad(Q_{y_{1}},h)=-(Q,\Lambda Q)=0,\qquad(Q_{y_{2}},h)=0,

and

(\Lambda Q,h)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\Lambda Q\,dy_{1}\right)^{2}dy_{2}=2C_{Q}.

Dividing by
2C_{Q}
, for some
c_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
we obtain

\left|\frac{d}{ds}J_{1}+\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda}J_{1}-\left(\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda}+b\right)+c_{1}b^{2}\right|\lesssim|b|^{3}+|b|\widehat{\mathcal{M}}^{1/2}+\widehat{\mathcal{M}}+\delta(\nu^{*})\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}.

Finally, apply (
4.28
) with
f=Q_{y_{2}}
. If

h=\int_{-\infty}^{y_{1}}Q_{y_{2}}
, then

(\varepsilon,LQ_{y_{2}})=0,\qquad(\Lambda Q,h)=0,\qquad(Q_{y_{1}},h)=-(Q,Q_{y_{2}})=0,

and

(Q_{y_{2}},h)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}Q_{y_{2}}\,dy_{1}\right)^{2}dy_{2}=2\widetilde{C}_{Q}.

Therefore, for some
c_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
,

\left|\frac{d}{ds}J_{2}+\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda}J_{2}-\frac{(x_{2})_{s}}{\lambda}+c_{2}b^{2}\right|\lesssim|b|^{3}+|b|\widehat{\mathcal{M}}^{1/2}+\widehat{\mathcal{M}}+\delta(\nu^{*})\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}.

Since
|b|
is small,

|b|^{3}+|b|\widehat{\mathcal{M}}^{1/2}\lesssim b^{2}+\widehat{\mathcal{M}},

which proves b) and c).
∎

We now prove a sharp equation that will control the dynamics of the quantity
b/\lambda^{c}.

Lemma 4.6
.

Define

\rho(y_{1},y_{2})=\frac{1}{C_{Q}}\Big(P(y_{1},y_{2})-F(y_{2})-\frac{c}{2}\int_{-\infty}^{y_{1}}\Lambda Q(z,y_{2})dz+\frac{(\Lambda P,Q)}{\|\sqrt{\varphi}\Lambda Q\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}\varphi(y_{1})\Lambda Q(y_{1},y_{2})\Big),

where
C_{Q},F,
respectively
c
are defined in (
4.26
), (
4.9
), respectively (
4.11
). Denote
J(s)=(\varepsilon(s),\rho).
Then, under the assumptions of Lemma
4.5
,

|J(s)|\lesssim\widehat{\mathcal{M}}^{\frac{1}{2}}(s),

(4.29)

and

\Bigg|\frac{d}{ds}\left\{\frac{b}{\lambda^{c}}\right\}+\frac{b}{\lambda^{c}}\left(J_{s}+\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda}J\right)+\widehat{c}\frac{b^{3}}{\lambda^{c}}\Bigg|\lesssim\frac{\widehat{\mathcal{M}}+\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}+b^{4}}{\lambda^{c}}.

(4.30)

Proof.

From the estimates (
4.12
) and (
4.8
), we have

b_{s}+cb^{2}+c_{b}b^{3}-C_{Q}^{-1}b\left[\left(\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda}+b\right)D_{P}+6I_{P}\right]+C_{Q}^{-1}\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda}(\varepsilon,\Lambda Q)=\mathcal{R}_{b},

(4.31)

where we denote
D_{P}=(\Lambda P,Q),I_{P}=(\varepsilon,PQQ_{y_{1}}),
and the estimate
\mathcal{R}_{b}=O(\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}+b^{4}).

Now from (
4.15
) and (
4.8
),

(\varepsilon,L[(\varphi\Lambda Q)_{y_{1}}])=\left(\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda}+b\right)(\Lambda Q,\varphi\Lambda Q)+\left(\frac{(x_{1})_{s}}{\lambda}-1\right)(Q_{y_{1}},\varphi\Lambda Q)+c_{1}b^{2}+\mathcal{R}_{\Lambda},

(4.32)

where
|\mathcal{R}_{\Lambda}|\lesssim|b|^{3}+\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}+|b|\mathcal{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}.

We observe from the definition of
F,

\lim_{y_{1}\rightarrow-\infty}\rho(y_{1},y_{2})=0
and

\lim_{y_{1}\rightarrow+\infty}\rho(y_{1},y_{2})=-C_{Q}^{-1}\left(F(y_{2})+\frac{c}{2}\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\Lambda Q(z,y_{2})dz\right)=G(y_{2}),

with
|G(y_{2})|\lesssim e^{-|y_{2}|/2},
for all
y_{2}\in\mathbb{R}.
Therefore,

|J(s)|\lesssim\widehat{\mathcal{M}}^{\frac{1}{2}}(s).

Using the properties of
P
and the parity of
Q_{y_{1}},Q_{y_{2}}
we have

C_{Q}(Q_{y_{1}},\rho)=(Q_{y_{1}},P-F)+\frac{c}{2}(\Lambda Q,Q)+\frac{D_{P}}{\|\sqrt{\varphi}\Lambda Q\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}(\varphi\Lambda Q,Q_{y_{1}})=\frac{D_{P}}{\|\sqrt{\varphi}\Lambda Q\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}(\varphi\Lambda Q,Q_{y_{1}}),

C_{Q}(Q_{y_{2}},\rho)=(Q_{y_{2}},P-F)+\frac{c}{2}(\int_{-\infty}^{y_{1}}\Lambda Q,Q_{y_{2}})+\frac{D_{P}}{\|\sqrt{\varphi}\Lambda Q\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}(\varphi\Lambda Q,Q_{y_{2}})=0.

Moreover, using
\Lambda^{*}=-\Lambda
and (
4.11
),

(\Lambda Q,\rho)=C_{Q}^{-1}[(P,\Lambda Q)+2cC_{Q}-cC_{Q}+(\Lambda P,Q)]=c.

Since
L(P_{y_{1}})=\Lambda Q+6PQQ_{y_{1}},
we note that

L(\rho_{y_{1}})=C_{Q}^{-1}\Big\{\Lambda Q+6PQQ_{y_{1}}+cQ+\frac{D_{P}}{\|\sqrt{\varphi}\Lambda Q\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}L[(\varphi\Lambda Q)_{y_{1}}]\Big\}.

The function
\rho_{y_{1}}
satisfies
(
4.27
). Since

\rho(-\infty,y_{2})=0
, its primitive is
\rho
, and applying
(
4.28
) with
f=\rho_{y_{1}}
gives

D_{\rho}=J_{s}+\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda}J=-(\varepsilon,L(\rho_{y_{1}}))+\left(\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda}+b\right)(\Lambda Q,\rho)+\left(\frac{(x_{1})_{s}}{\lambda}-1\right)(Q_{y_{1}},\rho)+c_{\rho}b^{2}+\mathcal{R}_{\rho}

(4.33)

where
\mathcal{R}_{\rho}=O(|b|\widehat{\mathcal{M}}^{\frac{1}{2}}+\widehat{\mathcal{M}}+\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}+|b|^{3})
and we used that
(Q_{y_{2}},\rho)=0.

Plugging (
4.32
) into (
4.33
), we get

D_{\rho}=-C_{Q}^{-1}(\varepsilon,\Lambda Q)-C_{Q}^{-1}6I_{P}+\left(\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda}+b\right)(c-C_{Q}^{-1}D_{P})+\tilde{c}b^{2}+\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{\rho}.

with
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{\rho}=O(|b|^{3}+|b|\widehat{\mathcal{M}}^{\frac{1}{2}}+\widehat{\mathcal{M}}+\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}).

Therefore, using this equation and (
4.31
), we have

\begin{split}\lambda^{c}\left(\frac{b}{\lambda^{c}}\right)_{s}+bD_{\rho}&=b_{s}+cb^{2}-cb\left(\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda}+b\right)+bD_{\rho}\\
&=-\hat{c}b^{3}-C_{Q}^{-1}\left(\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda}+b\right)(\varepsilon,\Lambda Q)+\mathcal{R}_{b}^{\rho}\end{split}

where
\mathcal{R}_{b}^{\rho}=\mathcal{R}_{b}+b\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{\rho}=O(\widehat{\mathcal{M}}+\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}+b^{4}).

From (
4.8
) we have

\left|\left(\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda}+b\right)(\varepsilon,\Lambda Q)\right|\lesssim|b|^{4}+\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}.

The conclusion (
4.30
) follows.
∎

Remark 4.7
.

The occurrence of
\widehat{\mathcal{M}}
in (
4.30
) is a genuinely two-dimensional feature. Although the correction
\rho
is localized as
y_{1}\to-\infty
, as
y_{1}\to+\infty
it approaches a transverse profile depending only on
y_{2}
. Consequently,
J=(\varepsilon,\rho)
and the remainders in its evolution cannot be controlled solely by a norm localized near the soliton: on the right, one also needs a quadratic moment in
y_{1}
, together with transverse decay. By contrast, in the critical gKdV analysis of
[
38
]
, the analogous correction belongs to the fully localized class
\mathcal{Y}
, and the finite-difference estimate for
b/\lambda^{2}
is controlled by the first weighted radiation norm. Here the quadratic moment costs one higher weighted level, and its time integration costs one further level (see (
6.6
)). This loss propagates through the bootstrap hierarchy: the basic rigidity argument closes with an
x_{1}^{18}
moment, while the additional level required in the zero-energy argument leads to the
x_{1}^{21}
localization imposed on the initial data.

4.4
Conservation Laws

Lemma 4.8

(Estimates Induced by Conservation Laws)
.

The following estimates hold regarding the approximate profile
Q_{b}
and the error
\varepsilon
:

(i)

Mass of the Profile:
The mass of the approximate profile satisfies the expansion:

\left|\iint Q_{b}^{2}-\iint Q^{2}-2b(P,Q)\right|\lesssim|b|^{2-\gamma}.

(ii)

Energy of the Profile:
The energy of the approximate profile expands as:

E(Q_{b})=-b(P,Q)+O(|b|^{2-\gamma}).

(iii)

Mass Control of Error:
The
L^{2}
norm of the error is controlled by the scaling parameter and the initial mass difference:

\|\varepsilon\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\lesssim|b|+\left|\iint u_{0}^{2}-\iint Q^{2}\right|.

(4.34)

(iv)

Energy Conservation relation:
The kinetic energy of the error relates to the system’s total energy
E_{0}
and the scaling parameter via:

\left|2\lambda^{2}E_{0}+2b(P,Q)-\|\nabla\varepsilon\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\right|\lesssim|b|^{\frac{3-\gamma}{2}}+\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}+\left(\|\varepsilon\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+|b|^{\frac{1-\gamma}{2}}\right)\|\nabla\varepsilon\|_{L^{2}}^{2}.

(4.35)

Proof.

Proof of
i):
We compute directly

\displaystyle\left|\iint Q_{b}^{2}-\iint Q^{2}-2b(P,Q)\right|

\displaystyle\leq 2|b|\,\bigl|((1-\chi_{b})P,Q)\bigr|+b^{2}\iint\chi_{b}^{2}P^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim|b|^{2-\gamma}.

Proof of
ii):
Recall the definition of energy,
E(u)=\frac{1}{2}\iint|\nabla u|^{2}-\frac{1}{4}\iint u^{4}.

E[(Q_{b}+\varepsilon)(s)]=E(\mathfrak{u}(s))=\frac{\lambda^{2}(s)}{2}\iint|\nabla u|^{2}-\frac{\lambda^{2}(s)}{4}\iint u^{4}=\lambda^{2}(s)E(u)=\lambda^{2}(s)E_{0}

Also, using that
E(Q)=0
and
\Delta Q+Q^{3}=Q,
we have

E(Q_{b})=E(Q)-b\iint\chi_{b}P\Delta Q-b\iint\chi_{b}PQ^{3}+O(|b|^{2-\gamma})=-b(P,Q)+O(|b|^{2-\gamma}).

Proof of
iii):
By the conservation of mass,

\iint(Q_{b}+\varepsilon)^{2}=\iint u_{0}^{2}

so, using the orthogonality
(\varepsilon,Q)=0,

\begin{split}\iint u_{0}^{2}-\iint Q^{2}&=\iint Q_{b}^{2}-\iint Q^{2}+\iint\varepsilon^{2}+2(\varepsilon,Q_{b})\\
&=\iint\varepsilon^{2}+b^{2}\iint\chi_{b}^{2}P^{2}+2b\iint\chi_{b}PQ+2b\iint\varepsilon\chi_{b}P\end{split}

Since,

\iint\chi_{b}^{2}P^{2}\lesssim|b|^{-\gamma},\text{ }\Big|\iint\chi_{b}PQ\Big|\lesssim 1,\text{ }\Big|\iint\varepsilon\chi_{b}P\Big|\lesssim|b|^{-\frac{\gamma}{2}}\|\varepsilon\|_{L^{2}}

we get

\iint\varepsilon^{2}\lesssim|b|+|b|^{2-\gamma}+|b|^{1-\frac{\gamma}{2}}\|\varepsilon\|_{L^{2}}+\Big|\iint u_{0}^{2}-\iint Q^{2}\Big|

and, using
|b(t)|\leq\hat{\nu}\ll 1
we get

\|\varepsilon\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq C|b|+C|b|^{2-\gamma}+\frac{1}{2}\|\varepsilon\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\Big|\iint u_{0}^{2}-\iint Q^{2}\Big|

so

\|\varepsilon\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\lesssim|b|+\Big|\iint u_{0}^{2}-\iint Q^{2}\Big|.

Proof of
iv):
Using
ii),
i.e.
E(Q_{b})=-b(P,Q)+O(|b|^{2-\gamma})
and the orthogonality condition
(\varepsilon,Q)=0,
we get

\displaystyle\lambda^{2}E_{0}={}

\displaystyle E(Q_{b})+\frac{1}{2}\iint|\nabla\varepsilon|^{2}-\iint\varepsilon\bigl[\Delta(Q_{b}-Q)+(Q_{b}^{3}-Q^{3})\bigr]

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4}\iint\bigl[(Q_{b}+\varepsilon)^{4}-Q_{b}^{4}-4Q_{b}^{3}\varepsilon\bigr]

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle-b(P,Q)+O(|b|^{2-\gamma})+\frac{1}{2}\iint|\nabla\varepsilon|^{2}-b\iint\varepsilon\Delta(\chi_{b}P)-\iint\varepsilon(Q_{b}^{3}-Q^{3})

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4}\iint\bigl[(Q_{b}+\varepsilon)^{4}-Q_{b}^{4}-4Q_{b}^{3}\varepsilon\bigr].

We have

\Big|\iint\varepsilon\Delta(\chi_{b}P)\Big|=\Big|\iint\nabla\varepsilon\cdot\nabla(\chi_{b}P)\Big|\lesssim\iint|\varepsilon_{y_{1}}(\chi_{b})_{y_{1}}P|+\iint|\varepsilon_{y_{1}}\chi_{b}P_{y_{1}}|+\iint|\varepsilon_{y_{2}}\chi_{b}P_{y_{2}}|

We estimate each of the terms:

\iint|\varepsilon_{y_{1}}(\chi_{b})_{y_{1}}P|\lesssim|b|^{\frac{\gamma}{2}}\|\varepsilon_{y_{1}}\|_{L^{2}}\mbox{, }\iint|\varepsilon_{y_{1}}\chi_{b}P_{y_{1}}|\lesssim\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\mbox{, }\iint|\varepsilon_{y_{2}}\chi_{b}P_{y_{2}}|\lesssim|b|^{-\frac{\gamma}{2}}\|\varepsilon_{y_{2}}\|_{L^{2}},

which yields

\Big|\iint\varepsilon\Delta(b\chi_{b}P)\Big|\lesssim|b|\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}^{\frac{1}{2}}+|b|^{1+\frac{\gamma}{2}}\|\varepsilon_{y_{1}}\|_{L^{2}}+|b|^{1-\frac{\gamma}{2}}\|\varepsilon_{y_{2}}\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim|b|^{\frac{3-\gamma}{2}}+\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}+|b|^{\frac{1-\gamma}{2}}\|\nabla\varepsilon\|_{L^{2}}^{2}.

The other term is

\iint|\varepsilon(Q_{b}^{3}-Q^{3})|\lesssim|b|\iint|\varepsilon Q^{2}\chi_{b}P|+b^{2}\iint|\varepsilon Q(\chi_{b}P)^{2}|+|b|^{3}\iint|\varepsilon(\chi_{b}P)^{3}|

We estimate each of the terms:

|b|\iint|\varepsilon Q^{2}\chi_{b}P|\lesssim|b|\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}^{\frac{1}{2}},b^{2}\iint|\varepsilon Q(\chi_{b}P)^{2}|\lesssim b^{2}\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}^{\frac{1}{2}},|b|^{3}\iint|\varepsilon(\chi_{b}P)^{3}|\lesssim|b|^{3-\gamma}\|\varepsilon\|_{L^{2}},

which yields

\iint|\varepsilon(Q_{b}^{3}-Q^{3})|\lesssim|b|\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}^{\frac{1}{2}}+|b|^{3-\gamma}\|\varepsilon\|_{L^{2}}.

For the last one, from Gagliardo-Nirenberg inequality and Lemma
3.3
,

\iint|(Q_{b}+\varepsilon)^{4}-Q_{b}^{4}-4Q_{b}^{3}\varepsilon|\lesssim\iint Q^{2}\varepsilon^{2}+b^{2}\iint\varepsilon^{2}(\chi_{b}P)^{2}+\iint\varepsilon^{4}

\lesssim\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}+\|\varepsilon\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\|\nabla\varepsilon\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+b^{2}\|\varepsilon\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

Combining the profile, interaction, and nonlinear estimates gives

\Big|\lambda^{2}E_{0}+b(P,Q)-\frac{1}{2}\iint|\nabla\varepsilon|^{2}\Big|\lesssim|b|^{\frac{3-\gamma}{2}}+\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}+(\|\varepsilon\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+|b|^{\frac{1-\gamma}{2}})\|\nabla\varepsilon\|_{L^{2}}^{2}.

∎

Chapter 5
Energy Estimates

5.1
Choice of Weights

Recall the definition of
\alpha_{1}
from (
3.1
).
Let
\widetilde{\delta}>0
be the constant in the global coercivity estimate
(
3.5
). All implicit constants below are independent of

B
, although they may depend on
i,\alpha_{1},\sigma,M
, and on the fixed
cut-off functions.

Choose

0<\sigma\leq\min\Big(\frac{\sqrt{\widetilde{\delta}}}{2},\frac{1}{2\alpha_{1}}\Big),

(5.1)

and then choose

\rho\in\bigl(\alpha_{1}^{-2},1\bigr),\qquad\kappa=1-\rho>0.

Finally choose
M\gg 1
, independently of
B
, such that

\sigma\left(M-\frac{1}{2}\right)>\frac{1}{\alpha_{1}}.

(5.2)

For
B\geq B_{0}
, set

C_{B}=1+e^{-B/(2\alpha_{1})},\qquad E_{B}(y_{1})=e^{-B/(2\alpha_{1})}e^{y_{1}/\alpha_{1}},

and define

S_{B}(y_{1})=\frac{e^{\sigma(y_{1}+MB)}}{1+e^{\sigma(y_{1}+MB)}}.

Let
\varrho\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
be non-decreasing, with

0\leq\varrho\leq 1,\qquad\varrho(r)=0\quad\text{for }r\leq 0,\qquad\varrho(r)=1\quad\text{for }r\geq 1,

and with all derivatives bounded. Put

\varsigma_{B}(y_{1})=\varrho\left(y_{1}+\frac{B}{2}+1\right),\qquad\widetilde{S}_{B}=(1-\varsigma_{B})S_{B}+\varsigma_{B}.

Then

\widetilde{S}_{B}=S_{B}\quad\text{for }y_{1}\leq-\frac{B}{2}-1,\qquad\widetilde{S}_{B}=1\quad\text{for }y_{1}\geq-\frac{B}{2}.

On the region where
\widetilde{S}_{B}=S_{B}
,

S^{\prime}_{B}=\sigma S_{B}(1-S_{B}),

and

S^{\prime\prime}_{B}=\sigma^{2}S_{B}(1-S_{B})(1-2S_{B}),\qquad S^{\prime\prime\prime}_{B}=\sigma^{3}S_{B}(1-S_{B})(1-6S_{B}+6S_{B}^{2}).

Since
1-6S+6S^{2}\leq 1
for
0\leq S\leq 1
, we have

S^{\prime\prime\prime}_{B}\leq\sigma^{2}S^{\prime}_{B}.

Moreover,

|S^{\prime\prime}_{B}|+|S^{\prime\prime\prime}_{B}|\lesssim_{\sigma}S^{\prime}_{B}.

(5.3)

Let

\mathcal{R}_{B}=\left[-\frac{B}{2}-1,-\frac{B}{2}\right].

By (
5.2
), on
\mathcal{R}_{B}
,

1-S_{B}+|S_{B}^{\prime}|+|S_{B}^{\prime\prime}|+|S_{B}^{\prime\prime\prime}|\lesssim e^{-\sigma(M-1/2)B}\ll e^{-B/\alpha_{1}}.

Consequently, using the boundedness of the derivatives of
\varsigma_{B}
,

1-\widetilde{S}_{B}+|\widetilde{S}_{B}^{\prime}|+|\widetilde{S}_{B}^{\prime\prime}|+|\widetilde{S}_{B}^{\prime\prime\prime}|\lesssim e^{-\sigma(M-1/2)B}\ll e^{-B/\alpha_{1}}\quad\text{on }\mathcal{R}_{B}.

(5.4)

Weight for the Mass Part

For
y_{1}\leq B/2
, define

\phi_{i,B}(y_{1})=E_{B}(y_{1})+C_{B}\widetilde{S}_{B}(y_{1}).

(5.5)

Since
\widetilde{S}_{B}=1
on
[-B/2,B/2]
, recalling the definition of
\varphi
from (
3.2
), this gives

\phi_{i,B}(y_{1})=1+\varphi(y_{1})e^{-B/(2\alpha_{1})}\quad\text{for }|y_{1}|\leq\frac{B}{2}.

For
i\geq 1
, the full mass weight is

\phi_{i,B}(y_{1})=\begin{cases}E_{B}(y_{1})+C_{B}\widetilde{S}_{B}(y_{1}),&y_{1}\leq\frac{B}{2},\\[2.84526pt]
\text{the interpolation of Lemma \ref{lem:right_transition_clean_corrected}},&\frac{B}{2}\leq y_{1}\leq B,\\[2.84526pt]
B\left(\dfrac{y_{1}}{B}\right)^{i},&y_{1}\geq B.\end{cases}

(5.6)

Lemma 5.1
.

Let

F_{B}(y_{1})=1+\varphi(y_{1})e^{-B/(2\alpha_{1})}=1+e^{-B/(2\alpha_{1})}+e^{(y_{1}-B/2)/\alpha_{1}},

and let

P_{i,B}(y_{1})=B\left(\frac{y_{1}}{B}\right)^{i}.

For
B
sufficiently large, there exists a smooth interpolation

\phi_{i,B}
on
[B/2,B]
such that

\phi_{i,B}=F_{B}\quad\text{near }B/2,\qquad\phi_{i,B}=P_{i,B}\quad\text{near }B.

Moreover, on
[B/2,B]
,

0<c_{i}\leq\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}\leq C_{i}=\max\{5/2,i\},

(5.7)

|\phi_{i,B}^{\prime\prime}|\leq\widetilde{C}_{i}\phi_{i,B}^{\prime},

(5.8)

and

\phi_{i,B}^{\prime\prime\prime}\leq(1-\kappa)\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}.

(5.9)

The constants
c_{i},C_{i},\widetilde{C}_{i}>0
are independent of
B
.

Proof.

Choose
r_{*}\in(2/(3\alpha_{1}),1)
. Let

\eta\in C^{\infty}([0,\infty))
satisfy

0\leq\eta\leq 1,\qquad\eta^{\prime}\geq 0,\qquad\eta^{\prime\prime}\leq 0,

\eta(r)=r\quad(0\leq r\leq r_{*}),\qquad\eta(r)=1\quad(r\geq R),

for some fixed
R>1
. Since
\eta
is concave and
\eta(0)=0
,

r\eta^{\prime}(r)\leq\eta(r),\qquad r\geq 0.

For
H\in[3/2,5/2]
, define

q_{L,H}(y_{1})=H\,\eta\left(\frac{F_{B}^{\prime}(y_{1})}{H}\right).

Since
F_{B}^{\prime}(B/2)=1/\alpha_{1}
, we have

\frac{F_{B}^{\prime}(B/2)}{H}\leq\frac{2}{3\alpha_{1}}<r_{*}.

Thus
q_{L,H}=F_{B}^{\prime}
near
B/2
. Also
q_{L,H}=H
once

F_{B}^{\prime}\geq RH
, so there exists
K_{L}>0
, independent of
B
and
H
,
such that
q_{L,H}=H
for
y_{1}\geq B/2+K_{L}
. If

r=F_{B}^{\prime}/H
, then

q_{L,H}^{\prime}=\frac{H}{\alpha_{1}}r\eta^{\prime}(r),\qquad q_{L,H}^{\prime\prime}=\frac{H}{\alpha_{1}^{2}}\bigl(r^{2}\eta^{\prime\prime}(r)+r\eta^{\prime}(r)\bigr).

Therefore

|q_{L,H}^{\prime}|\lesssim q_{L,H},\qquad q_{L,H}^{\prime\prime}\leq\alpha_{1}^{-2}q_{L,H}\leq\rho q_{L,H}.

Moreover
H\geq q_{L,H}\geq q_{L,H}(B/2)=1/\alpha_{1}
.

Next choose a fixed
\zeta\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
such that

0\leq\zeta\leq 1,\qquad\zeta=0\ \text{for }s\leq 0,\qquad\zeta=1\ \text{for }s\geq 1.

Let
\ell\gg 1
be fixed, independently of
B
, and for

y_{1}\in[B-\ell,B]
put

s=\frac{y_{1}-(B-\ell)}{\ell},\qquad q_{R,H}(y_{1})=(1-\zeta(s))H+\zeta(s)P_{i,B}^{\prime}(y_{1}).

Then
q_{R,H}=H
near
B-\ell
, while
q_{R,H}=P_{i,B}^{\prime}
near
B
.
For
B\geq 2\ell
,

P_{i,B}^{\prime}(y_{1})=i\left(\frac{y_{1}}{B}\right)^{i-1}\geq i2^{1-i},\qquad B-\ell\leq y_{1}\leq B.

Hence
\max\{H,i\}\geq q_{R,H}\geq\min\{H,i2^{1-i}\}>0
. Since

|P_{i,B}^{\prime}|\lesssim_{i}1,\qquad|P_{i,B}^{\prime\prime}|\lesssim_{i}B^{-1},\qquad|P_{i,B}^{\prime\prime\prime}|\lesssim_{i}B^{-2},

we get

|q_{R,H}^{\prime}|\leq\widetilde{C}_{i}q_{R,H}.

Furthermore,

q_{R,H}^{\prime\prime}=\frac{\zeta^{\prime\prime}(s)}{\ell^{2}}(P_{i,B}^{\prime}-H)+2\frac{\zeta^{\prime}(s)}{\ell}P_{i,B}^{\prime\prime}+\zeta(s)P_{i,B}^{\prime\prime\prime}.

Choosing
\ell
sufficiently large and then
B
sufficiently large gives

q_{R,H}^{\prime\prime}\leq\rho q_{R,H}.

For
B
large enough that
B/2+K_{L}<B-\ell
, define

q_{H}(y_{1})=\begin{cases}q_{L,H}(y_{1}),&B/2\leq y_{1}\leq B/2+K_{L},\\[2.84526pt]
H,&B/2+K_{L}\leq y_{1}\leq B-\ell,\\[2.84526pt]
q_{R,H}(y_{1}),&B-\ell\leq y_{1}\leq B.\end{cases}

Then
q_{H}
is smooth and satisfies

c_{i}\leq q_{H}\leq C_{i}=\max\{H,i\},\qquad|q_{H}^{\prime}|\leq\widetilde{C}_{i}q_{H},\qquad q_{H}^{\prime\prime}\leq\rho q_{H}.

Let

I_{B}(H)=\int_{B/2}^{B}q_{H}(y_{1})\,dy_{1}.

Uniformly for
H\in[3/2,5/2]
,

I_{B}(H)=H\left(\frac{B}{2}-K_{L}-\ell\right)+O_{i}(1).

Hence

I_{B}(3/2)=\frac{3B}{4}+O_{i}(1),\qquad I_{B}(5/2)=\frac{5B}{4}+O_{i}(1).

On the other hand,

P_{i,B}(B)-F_{B}(B/2)=B-\left(2+e^{-B/(2\alpha_{1})}\right)=B+O(1).

Thus, for
B
large, the intermediate value theorem gives

H_{B}\in[3/2,5/2]
such that

\int_{B/2}^{B}q_{H_{B}}(y_{1})\,dy_{1}=P_{i,B}(B)-F_{B}(B/2).

Define

\phi_{i,B}(y_{1})=F_{B}(B/2)+\int_{B/2}^{y_{1}}q_{H_{B}}(z)\,dz,\qquad B/2\leq y_{1}\leq B.

Then
\phi_{i,B}=F_{B}
at
B/2
,
\phi_{i,B}=P_{i,B}
at
B
, and

\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}=q_{H_{B}},\qquad\phi_{i,B}^{\prime\prime}=q_{H_{B}}^{\prime},\qquad\phi_{i,B}^{\prime\prime\prime}=q_{H_{B}}^{\prime\prime}.

This proves (
5.7
),
(
5.8
), and
(
5.9
).
∎

Lemma 5.2
.

For
B\geq B_{0}
, the weight defined by (
5.5
)
satisfies

\phi_{i,B}>0,\qquad\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}>0\quad\text{for }y_{1}\leq B/2.

(5.10)

Moreover,

\phi_{i,B}^{\prime\prime\prime}\leq(1-\kappa)\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}\quad\text{for }y_{1}\leq B/2,

(5.11)

and

|\phi_{i,B}^{\prime\prime}|+|\phi_{i,B}^{\prime\prime\prime}|\lesssim\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}\quad\text{for }y_{1}\leq B/2.

(5.12)

Define

A_{B}(y_{1})=1+\varphi(y_{1})e^{-B/(2\alpha_{1})}-\phi_{i,B}(y_{1}).

(5.13)

Then

\frac{A_{B}^{2}(y_{1})}{\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}(y_{1})}\lesssim\begin{cases}e^{-\sigma(y_{1}+MB)},&y_{1}<-\frac{B}{2}-1,\\[2.84526pt]
e^{-2\sigma(M-\frac{1}{2})B}e^{B/\alpha_{1}},&-\frac{B}{2}-1\leq y_{1}\leq-\frac{B}{2},\\[2.84526pt]
0,&-\frac{B}{2}<y_{1}<\frac{B}{2}.\end{cases}

(5.14)

For all sufficiently large
B
and
y_{1}<B/2
,

\phi_{i,B}(y_{1})\lesssim e^{\varpi B}\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}(y_{1}),\qquad\varpi=\frac{\sigma(M+\frac{1}{2})}{1+\sigma\alpha_{1}}.

(5.15)

Consequently,

\phi_{i,B}^{2}(y_{1})\lesssim e^{\varpi B}\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}(y_{1}),\qquad y_{1}<-B/2.

(5.16)

Finally, the following spatially localized estimate also holds:

\phi_{i,B}^{2}(y_{1})\lesssim e^{\sigma MB}e^{\sigma y_{1}}\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}(y_{1}),\qquad y_{1}<-B/2.

(5.17)

Proof.

We have

\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}=E_{B}^{\prime}+C_{B}\widetilde{S}_{B}^{\prime},

where
E_{B}^{\prime}>0
and
\widetilde{S}_{B}^{\prime}\geq 0
. Thus
(
5.10
) holds.

On
y_{1}\leq-B/2-1
,
\widetilde{S}_{B}=S_{B}
, and hence

\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}=E_{B}^{\prime}+C_{B}S_{B}^{\prime},\qquad\phi_{i,B}^{\prime\prime\prime}=E_{B}^{\prime\prime\prime}+C_{B}S_{B}^{\prime\prime\prime}.

Since
E_{B}^{\prime\prime\prime}=\alpha_{1}^{-2}E_{B}^{\prime}
and

S_{B}^{\prime\prime\prime}\leq\sigma^{2}S_{B}^{\prime}
, while

\sigma^{2}<\rho
and
\alpha_{1}^{-2}<\rho
, we obtain

\phi_{i,B}^{\prime\prime\prime}\leq\rho(E_{B}^{\prime}+C_{B}S_{B}^{\prime})=\rho\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}.

The estimate (
5.12
) follows there from
(
5.3
) and the corresponding identities for

E_{B}
.

On
[-B/2,B/2]
,

\phi_{i,B}=1+\varphi e^{-B/(2\alpha_{1})},

and therefore

\phi_{i,B}^{\prime\prime\prime}=\alpha_{1}^{-2}\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}\leq\rho\phi_{i,B}^{\prime},

with the same Sobolev-type estimate. On the collar
\mathcal{R}_{B}
, we have

E_{B}^{\prime}\gtrsim e^{-B/\alpha_{1}}
, whereas
(
5.4
) gives

C_{B}\bigl(|\widetilde{S}_{B}^{\prime\prime}|+|\widetilde{S}_{B}^{\prime\prime\prime}|\bigr)\ll E_{B}^{\prime}.

Thus, after increasing
B_{0}
,

C_{B}|\widetilde{S}_{B}^{\prime\prime\prime}|\leq(\rho-\alpha_{1}^{-2})E_{B}^{\prime},

which proves (
5.11
) on
\mathcal{R}_{B}
, and
(
5.12
) follows similarly.

We next prove (
5.14
). On
y_{1}\leq-B/2-1
,

A_{B}=C_{B}(1-S_{B}),

so, using
S_{B}^{\prime}=\sigma S_{B}(1-S_{B})
,

\frac{A_{B}(y_{1})^{2}}{\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}(y_{1})}\lesssim\frac{(1-S_{B})^{2}}{S_{B}(1-S_{B})}=e^{-\sigma(y_{1}+MB)}.

On
\mathcal{R}_{B}
,
A_{B}=C_{B}(1-\widetilde{S}_{B})
, and therefore

\frac{A_{B}(y_{1})^{2}}{\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}(y_{1})}\lesssim e^{-2\sigma(M-1/2)B}e^{B/\alpha_{1}}.

Finally,
A_{B}=0
on
(-B/2,B/2)
.

It remains to prove (
5.15
). On

[-B/2-1,B/2]
,
\phi_{i,B}\lesssim 1
, while

\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}\geq E_{B}^{\prime}\gtrsim e^{-B/\alpha_{1}}.

Since (
5.2
) implies

\varpi=\frac{\sigma(M+\frac{1}{2})}{1+\sigma\alpha_{1}}>\frac{1}{\alpha_{1}},

we get
\phi_{i,B}\lesssim e^{\varpi B}\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}
on this region.

On
y_{1}\leq-B/2-1
, write
z=y_{1}+MB
. Then

\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}=E_{B}^{\prime}+C_{B}S_{B}^{\prime}\gtrsim e^{z/\alpha_{1}-(M+1/2)B/\alpha_{1}}+S_{B}(1-S_{B}).

If
S_{B}\leq 1/2
, then
S_{B}\lesssim S_{B}^{\prime}\lesssim\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}
, and
also
E_{B}\lesssim E_{B}^{\prime}\lesssim\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}
. Hence
(
5.15
) holds without any exponential loss.

If
S_{B}\geq 1/2
, then
z\geq 0
and
S_{B}(1-S_{B})\gtrsim e^{-\sigma z}
.
Thus

\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}\gtrsim e^{z/\alpha_{1}-(M+1/2)B/\alpha_{1}}+e^{-\sigma z}.

For all
z\geq 0
,

\max\left\{e^{z/\alpha_{1}-(M+1/2)B/\alpha_{1}},e^{-\sigma z}\right\}\geq\exp\left(-\frac{\sigma(M+\frac{1}{2})}{1+\alpha_{1}\sigma}B\right).

Since
\phi_{i,B}\lesssim 1
on
y_{1}<B/2
, this proves
(
5.15
). The estimate (
5.16
) follows
because
\phi_{i,B}\lesssim 1
for
y_{1}<-B/2
.

Finally, on
y_{1}<-B/2
,

\phi_{i,B}^{2}\lesssim E_{B}^{2}+\widetilde{S}_{B}^{2}.

Since

\frac{E_{B}^{2}}{e^{\sigma y_{1}}E_{B}^{\prime}}=\alpha_{1}E_{B}e^{-\sigma y_{1}}=\alpha_{1}e^{-B/(2\alpha_{1})}e^{(1/\alpha_{1}-\sigma)y_{1}}\lesssim 1,

we have

E_{B}^{2}\lesssim e^{\sigma y_{1}}\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}.

On
y_{1}\leq-B/2-1
,

\frac{S_{B}^{2}}{e^{\sigma y_{1}}S_{B}^{\prime}}=\frac{1}{\sigma}e^{\sigma MB},

so

S_{B}^{2}\lesssim e^{\sigma MB}e^{\sigma y_{1}}\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}.

On the collar
\mathcal{R}_{B}
,
\widetilde{S}_{B}\leq 1
and

e^{\sigma MB}e^{\sigma y_{1}}\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}\gtrsim e^{\sigma(M-\frac{1}{2})B}e^{-B/\alpha_{1}}\gtrsim 1

by (
5.2
). This proves (
5.17
).
∎

Lemma 5.3
.

For all
y_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
,

\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}\lesssim\phi_{i,B},\qquad\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}\lesssim\phi_{i+1,B}^{\prime}.

Consequently,

\phi_{i,B}\lesssim\phi_{i+1,B}.

Moreover,

|\phi_{i,B}^{\prime\prime}|\lesssim\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}\qquad\text{for all }y_{1}\in\mathbb{R}.

(5.18)

Proof.

On
y_{1}<B/2
, the first estimate follows from

|E_{B}^{\prime}|\lesssim E_{B}
and
|\widetilde{S}_{B}^{\prime}|\lesssim\widetilde{S}_{B}
.
On
B/2\leq y_{1}\leq B
, Lemma

5.1
gives

\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}\leq C_{i}
, while
\phi_{i,B}\geq 2
. On
y_{1}\geq B
,

\frac{\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}}{\phi_{i,B}}=\frac{i}{y_{1}}\leq\frac{i}{B}.

This proves
\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}\lesssim\phi_{i,B}
.

The weights
\phi_{i,B}
and
\phi_{i+1,B}
agree on
y_{1}<B/2
, hence
their derivatives agree there. On
B/2\leq y_{1}\leq B
, the derivative upper
and lower bounds in Lemma
5.1
give

\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}\leq\frac{C_{i}}{c_{i+1}}\phi_{i+1,B}^{\prime}.

On
y_{1}\geq B
,

\frac{\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}}{\phi_{i+1,B}^{\prime}}=\frac{i}{i+1}\frac{B}{y_{1}}\leq 1.

Integrating the derivative comparison from
B/2
, where the two weights have
the same value up to harmless constants, gives

\phi_{i,B}\lesssim\phi_{i+1,B}
. Finally,
(
5.18
) follows from (
5.12
),
(
5.8
), and the explicit polynomial expression on

y_{1}\geq B
.
∎

Weight for the Gradient Part

Define

\psi_{B}(y_{1})=\widetilde{S}_{B}(y_{1})^{2}.

(5.19)

Then
0<\psi_{B}\leq 1
,
\psi_{B}^{\prime}\geq 0
, and

\psi_{B}=1\quad\text{for }y_{1}\geq-\frac{B}{2}.

Lemma 5.4
.

The gradient weight satisfies

0<\psi_{B}\leq\phi_{i,B},\qquad 0\leq\psi_{B}^{\prime}\lesssim\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}\qquad\text{for all }y_{1}\in\mathbb{R}.

(5.20)

Define

R_{\psi_{B}}=\frac{2\psi_{B}^{\prime\prime}\psi_{B}-[\psi_{B}^{\prime}]^{2}}{4\psi_{B}}.

Then

|R_{\psi_{B}}|\leq\sigma^{2}\psi_{B},\qquad y_{1}\leq-\frac{B}{2}.

(5.21)

Moreover,

\frac{(1-\psi_{B})^{2}}{\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}}\lesssim\begin{cases}e^{-\sigma(y_{1}+MB)},&y_{1}<-\frac{B}{2}-1,\\[2.84526pt]
e^{-2\sigma(M-\frac{1}{2})B}e^{B/\alpha_{1}},&-\frac{B}{2}-1\leq y_{1}\leq-\frac{B}{2},\end{cases}

(5.22)

and

\frac{[\psi_{B}^{\prime}]^{2}}{\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}}\lesssim\begin{cases}1,&y_{1}<-\frac{B}{2}-1,\\[2.84526pt]
e^{-2\sigma(M-\frac{1}{2})B}e^{B/\alpha_{1}},&-\frac{B}{2}-1\leq y_{1}\leq-\frac{B}{2}.\end{cases}

(5.23)

There exists
\mathcal{C}_{B}>0
such that

|\psi_{B}^{\prime\prime}|^{2}\lesssim\mathcal{C}_{B}\psi_{B}^{\prime}\phi_{i,B}^{\prime},\qquad y_{1}\leq-B/2.

(5.24)

Also,

\psi_{B}(y_{1})\lesssim e^{\varpi B}\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}(y_{1})\qquad\text{for all }y_{1}\in\mathbb{R},

(5.25)

where

\varpi=\frac{\sigma(M+\frac{1}{2})}{1+\sigma\alpha_{1}}.

On
y_{1}<-B/2
,

|\psi_{B}^{\prime\prime\prime}|\leq\frac{1}{2}\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}+\psi_{B}^{\prime}.

(5.26)

Finally,

|y_{1}|\psi_{B}^{\prime}\lesssim B\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}\qquad\text{for all }y_{1}\in\mathbb{R}.

(5.27)

Proof.

Since
0<\widetilde{S}_{B}\leq 1
, we have
0<\psi_{B}\leq 1
. The estimate

\psi_{B}\leq\phi_{i,B}
follows from the definition on
y_{1}<B/2
, from

\phi_{i,B}\geq 1
on the central and right regions, and from the monotonicity
of the right interpolation. The estimate

\psi_{B}^{\prime}\lesssim\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}
is immediate on

y_{1}\leq-B/2-1
from

\psi_{B}^{\prime}=2S_{B}S_{B}^{\prime},\qquad\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}\geq C_{B}S_{B}^{\prime},

while on the collar it follows from (
5.4
) and

\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}\gtrsim e^{-B/\alpha_{1}}
. For
y_{1}\geq-B/2
,

\psi_{B}^{\prime}=0
. This proves (
5.20
).

Since

\psi_{B}^{\prime}=2\widetilde{S}_{B}\widetilde{S}_{B}^{\prime},\qquad\psi_{B}^{\prime\prime}=2(\widetilde{S}_{B}^{\prime})^{2}+2\widetilde{S}_{B}\widetilde{S}_{B}^{\prime\prime},

we have the identity

R_{\psi_{B}}=\widetilde{S}_{B}\widetilde{S}_{B}^{\prime\prime}.

On
y_{1}\leq-B/2-1
, this becomes

R_{\psi_{B}}=\sigma^{2}S_{B}^{2}(1-S_{B})(1-2S_{B}).

Since

-\frac{1}{8}\leq(1-S)(1-2S)\leq 1\qquad(0\leq S\leq 1),

we get

-\frac{\sigma^{2}}{8}\psi_{B}\leq R_{\psi_{B}}\leq\sigma^{2}\psi_{B}\quad\text{on }y_{1}\leq-B/2-1.

On the collar,

|R_{\psi_{B}}|\lesssim e^{-\sigma(M-1/2)B}\psi_{B}.

Thus, after increasing
B_{0}
,

|R_{\psi_{B}}|\leq\sigma^{2}\psi_{B}\quad\text{for }y_{1}\leq-B/2,

which proves (
5.21
).

On
y_{1}\leq-B/2-1
,

1-\psi_{B}=(1-S_{B})(1+S_{B}),\qquad\psi_{B}^{\prime}=2S_{B}S_{B}^{\prime}.

Using
\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}\geq C_{B}S_{B}^{\prime}
, we obtain

\frac{(1-\psi_{B})^{2}}{\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}}\lesssim\frac{1-S_{B}}{S_{B}}=e^{-\sigma(y_{1}+MB)},

and

\frac{[\psi_{B}^{\prime}]^{2}}{\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}}\lesssim S_{B}^{2}S_{B}^{\prime}\lesssim 1.

On
\mathcal{R}_{B}
, (
5.4
) and

\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}\gtrsim e^{-B/\alpha_{1}}
give the collar bounds in
(
5.22
) and (
5.23
).

We next prove (
5.24
). On
y_{1}\leq-B/2-1
,

\psi_{B}^{\prime}=2\sigma S_{B}^{2}(1-S_{B}),

and

\psi_{B}^{\prime\prime}=2\sigma^{2}S_{B}^{2}(1-S_{B})(2-3S_{B}).

Since
\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}\gtrsim S_{B}(1-S_{B})
, we get

\frac{|\psi_{B}^{\prime\prime}|^{2}}{\psi_{B}^{\prime}\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}}\lesssim S_{B}(2-3S_{B})^{2}\lesssim 1.

On the collar, Glaeser’s inequality applied to

F=\psi_{B}^{\prime}\geq 0
gives

|\psi_{B}^{\prime\prime}|^{2}\leq C(B)\psi_{B}^{\prime}.

Since
\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}\gtrsim e^{-B/\alpha_{1}}
there, this proves
(
5.24
) for a suitable
\mathcal{C}_{B}>0
.

The estimate (
5.25
) follows on
y_{1}<B/2
from

\psi_{B}\leq\phi_{i,B}
and (
5.15
). For

B/2\leq y_{1}\leq B
,
\psi_{B}=1
and

\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}\geq c_{i}
. For
y_{1}\geq B
,

\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}=i\left(\frac{y_{1}}{B}\right)^{i-1}\geq i.

Hence (
5.25
) holds globally.

For (
5.26
), on
y_{1}<-B/2-1
a direct computation gives

\frac{|\psi_{B}^{\prime\prime\prime}|}{\psi_{B}^{\prime}}=\sigma^{2}|4-15S_{B}+12S_{B}^{2}|\leq 4\sigma^{2}.

Since
\sigma\leq 1/2
, this gives

|\psi_{B}^{\prime\prime\prime}|\leq\psi_{B}^{\prime}.

On
\mathcal{R}_{B}
,

|\psi_{B}^{\prime\prime\prime}|\lesssim|\widetilde{S}_{B}^{\prime}||\widetilde{S}_{B}^{\prime\prime}|+|\widetilde{S}_{B}^{\prime\prime\prime}|\ll e^{-B/\alpha_{1}}\lesssim\phi_{i,B}^{\prime},

and the factor
1/2
follows by increasing
B_{0}
.

Finally, on
y_{1}\leq-B/2-1
,

\frac{|y_{1}|\psi_{B}^{\prime}}{\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}}\lesssim|y_{1}|S_{B}.

If
-MB\leq y_{1}\leq-B/2-1
, then
|y_{1}|S_{B}\lesssim MB
. If

y_{1}<-MB
, write
x=-y_{1}-MB
. Since
S_{B}\lesssim e^{-\sigma x}
,

|y_{1}|S_{B}\lesssim(MB+x)e^{-\sigma x}\lesssim B.

On
\mathcal{R}_{B}
,
|y_{1}|\lesssim B
,

\psi_{B}^{\prime}\lesssim e^{-\sigma(M-1/2)B}
, and

\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}\gtrsim e^{-B/\alpha_{1}}
, so

|y_{1}|\psi_{B}^{\prime}\lesssim B\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}

by (
5.2
). For
y_{1}\geq-B/2
,

\psi_{B}^{\prime}=0
. This proves (
5.27
).
∎

5.2
Defining the Norms

We introduce a set of weighted norms to track the evolution of the error
\varepsilon
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{i}(s)

\displaystyle=\iint\left[(\varepsilon_{y_{1}}^{2}+\varepsilon_{y_{2}}^{2})\psi_{B}+\varepsilon^{2}\phi_{i,B}\right],

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{i,loc}(s)

\displaystyle=\iint\varepsilon^{2}\phi_{i,B}^{\prime},

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}_{i}(s)

\displaystyle=\iint(|\nabla\varepsilon|^{2}+\varepsilon^{2})\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}.

We have the bounds as a consequence of Lemma
5.3
:

\mathcal{N}_{i,loc}(s)\lesssim\mathcal{N}_{i+1,loc}(s),\quad\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}_{i}(s)\lesssim\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}_{i+1}(s),\quad\mathcal{N}_{i}(s)\lesssim\mathcal{N}_{i+1}(s).

We also have the important bound

\mathcal{N}_{i}(s)\lesssim e^{\varpi B}\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}_{i+1}(s).

(5.28)

Indeed, for the interval
y_{1}<\frac{B}{2},
this is a consequence of (
5.15
) and (
5.25
). On the right transition interval, we use (
5.7
), while on
y_{1}>B
we have
\phi_{i,B}=\frac{B}{i+1}\phi_{i+1,B}^{\prime}.
The conclusion follows.

Lemma 5.5
.

For all sufficiently large fixed
B
and
i\geq 1,

\begin{split}\mathcal{M}(s)&\leq Ce^{B/(2\alpha_{1})}\mathcal{N}_{i,\mathrm{loc}}(s),\\
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}(s)&\leq Ce^{B/(2\alpha_{1})}\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}_{i}(s),\\
\mathcal{M}(s)&\leq\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}(s)\leq C\mathcal{N}_{i}(s),\end{split}

(5.29)

and

\widehat{\mathcal{M}}(s)\lesssim B^{2}\mathcal{N}_{i}(s),\text{ for }i\geq 2.

(5.30)

Proof.

We first prove the first two pointwise estimates from (
5.29
). Recall the definition of
\vartheta
from (
4.7
). On
y_{1}\leq B/2
,

\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}\geq E_{B}^{\prime}=\frac{1}{\alpha}_{1}e^{-B/(2\alpha_{1})}e^{y_{1}/\alpha_{1}}.

Thus, on the left side
y_{1}<0
,

e^{-\vartheta}\lesssim e^{y_{1}/\alpha_{1}}\lesssim e^{B/(2\alpha_{1})}\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}.

On the bounded central interval
0\leq y_{1}\leq B/2
, one has

e^{-\vartheta}\lesssim 1
, while

\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}\geq ce^{-B/(2\alpha_{1})}.

Hence again

e^{-\vartheta}\lesssim e^{B/(2\alpha_{1})}\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}.

On
y_{1}>B/2
, the right interpolation and the polynomial branch give

e^{-\vartheta}\lesssim\frac{1}{B}\lesssim\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}
, and this is certainly bounded by

Ce^{B/(2\alpha_{1})}\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}
. Therefore

e^{-\vartheta}\lesssim e^{B/(2\alpha_{1})}\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}\qquad\text{for all }y_{1}\in\mathbb{R}.

Next we prove the third estimate of (
5.29
). The inequality

\mathcal{M}\leq\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}
is immediate. It remains to show
that the weight
e^{-\vartheta}
is dominated by the weights in

\mathcal{N}_{i}
. For the gradient part, since
2\sigma\leq 1/\alpha_{1}
, the
far-left asymptotic
\psi_{B}\sim e^{2\sigma(y_{1}+MB)}
implies

e^{y_{1}/\alpha_{1}}\lesssim\psi_{B}\qquad(y_{1}\ll-1).

On the collar and central/right regions,
\psi_{B}\sim 1
, while

e^{-\vartheta}\lesssim 1
. Hence

e^{-\vartheta}\lesssim\psi_{B}.

For the mass part, since
\sigma\leq 1/\alpha_{1}
, the far-left asymptotic

\phi_{i,B}\gtrsim S_{B}\sim e^{\sigma(y_{1}+MB)}
gives

e^{y_{1}/\alpha_{1}}\lesssim\phi_{i,B}.

Again the collar, central, and right regions are immediate. Hence

e^{-\vartheta}\lesssim\phi_{i,B}.

Together these two pointwise estimates imply

\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}\lesssim\mathcal{N}_{i}.

It remains to prove (
5.30
). Since the transverse factor in

\Omega
is bounded by
1
, it suffices to compare the
y_{1}
-weights.
For
y_{1}\leq-B/2-1
, the choice
2\sigma\leq\alpha_{1}^{-1}
gives

\Omega(y_{1},y_{2})\lesssim e^{y_{1}/\alpha_{1}}\lesssim S_{B}^{2}(y_{1})=\psi_{B}(y_{1})\leq\phi_{i,B}(y_{1}).

On
[-B/2-1,B]
, one has
\Omega\lesssim B^{2}\lesssim B^{2}\phi_{i,B}
, while for
y_{1}\geq B
and
i\geq 2
,

\Omega(y_{1},y_{2})\leq y_{1}^{2}\leq B\phi_{i,B}(y_{1}).

Thus
\Omega\lesssim B^{2}\phi_{i,B}
, and therefore

\widehat{\mathcal{M}}(s)\lesssim B^{2}\iint\varepsilon^{2}\phi_{i,B}\lesssim B^{2}\mathcal{N}_{i}(s).

∎

5.3
The Mixed Energy Functional

We fix the certified value of
\alpha_{1}
and the coercivity constants
first. For the finitely many weight indices and scaling exponents used
below, we also fix the constants
\mathcal{C}_{i}>1
in (
H2
).
We then fix
\sigma,\rho,M
as above and choose
B
sufficiently
large. The perturbation size
\nu^{*}
is chosen after these weight
parameters, followed by
\alpha^{*}\ll\nu^{*}
and

\alpha_{0}\ll\alpha^{*}
. Constants denoted by
C(B)
may depend on these
fixed weights, indices, cut-offs, and constants
\mathcal{C}_{i}
, but not
on the solution or time.
Terms depending only on the soliton and required to be small as

B\to\infty
will retain their explicit decay in
B
; terms with a fixed

C(B)
are made small by the subsequent choice of
\nu^{*}
.

For
i\in\mathbb{N}
with
i\geq 1
, we define the Lyapunov functional (mixed energy) by

\begin{split}\mathcal{F}_{i}=\iint(\varepsilon_{y_{1}}^{2}+\varepsilon_{y_{2}}^{2})\psi_{B}+\varepsilon^{2}\phi_{i,B}-\frac{1}{2}\Big((\varepsilon+Q_{b})^{4}-Q_{b}^{4}-4Q_{b}^{3}\varepsilon\Big)\psi_{B}\end{split}

(5.31)

Proposition 5.6
.

Fix
i\in\mathbb{N},i\geq 1
,
j\in\mathbb{R},j\geq 0
, and a constant
\mathcal{C}_{i}>1
. There exists
\mu>0,

B>0
large enough (to be fixed later) and
0<\nu^{*}<\nu_{0}
such that the following holds: Suppose
\varepsilon(t,y_{1},y_{2})
satisfies the modulation equation and the orthogonality conditions from Lemma
4.2
on
[0,t_{0}]
and the following a priori estimates hold on
[0,s_{0}]
where
s(t_{0})=s_{0}:

\displaystyle\|\varepsilon(s)\|_{L^{2}}+|b(s)|+\mathcal{N}_{i}(s)\leq\nu^{*},

(
H1
)

\displaystyle\frac{|b(s)|+\mathcal{N}_{i}(s)}{\lambda^{c}(s)}\leq\mathcal{C}_{i},

(
H2
)

\displaystyle\int_{y_{1}>0}y_{1}^{K_{i}}\varepsilon^{2}(s)\leq 2\bigg(1+\frac{1}{\lambda^{K_{i}}(s)}\bigg)\quad\text{for }K_{i}=\left\lfloor\frac{c(i+3)}{c-1}\right\rfloor+1.

(
H3
)

Then we have the following:

(i)

Lyapunov control:

\frac{d}{ds}\Bigg\{\frac{\mathcal{F}_{i}}{\lambda^{j}}\Bigg\}+\frac{\mu}{\lambda^{j}}\iint(\varepsilon_{y_{1}}^{2}+\varepsilon_{y_{2}}^{2}+\varepsilon^{2})\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}\lesssim\frac{|b|^{4}}{\lambda^{j}}.

(5.32)

(ii)

Coercivity of
\mathcal{F}_{i}
:

\mathcal{N}_{i}\lesssim\mathcal{F}_{i}\lesssim\mathcal{N}_{i}.

(5.33)

Proof.

(i)
. For
i\geq 1
,
j\geq 0
we have

\begin{split}\lambda^{j}\frac{d}{ds}\Bigg\{\frac{\mathcal{F}_{i}}{\lambda^{j}}\Bigg\}=&2\iint\psi_{B}(\varepsilon_{y_{1}})_{s}\varepsilon_{y_{1}}+\psi_{B}(\varepsilon_{y_{2}})_{s}\varepsilon_{y_{2}}+2\varepsilon_{s}\{\varepsilon\phi_{i,B}-\psi_{B}[(\varepsilon+Q_{b})^{3}-Q_{b}^{3}]\}\\
&-2\iint\psi_{B}(Q_{b})_{s}[(Q_{b}+\varepsilon)^{3}-Q_{b}^{3}-3Q_{b}^{2}\varepsilon]-j\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda}\mathcal{F}_{i}.\end{split}

We use the modulated flow equation (
4.2
),

\begin{split}\varepsilon_{s}-\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda}\Lambda\varepsilon=&\Big(-\Delta\varepsilon+\varepsilon-[(Q_{b}+\varepsilon)^{3}-Q_{b}^{3}]\Big)_{y_{1}}+\Big(\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda}+b\Big)\Lambda Q_{b}+\Big(\frac{(x_{1})_{s}}{\lambda}-1\Big)(Q_{b}+\varepsilon)_{y_{1}}\\
&+\Big(\frac{(x_{2})_{s}}{\lambda}\Big)(Q_{b}+\varepsilon)_{y_{2}}+\Phi_{b}+\Psi_{b},\end{split}

where
\Psi_{b}=[(-\Delta Q_{b}+Q_{b}-Q_{b}^{3})_{y_{1}}-b\Lambda Q_{b}]
and
\Phi_{b}=-b_{s}(\chi_{b}+\gamma y_{1}(\chi_{b})_{y_{1}})P.
We decompose the derivative into three contributions:

\lambda^{j}\frac{d}{ds}\left(\frac{\mathcal{F}_{i}}{\lambda^{j}}\right)=f_{1}+f_{2}+f_{3},

where

f_{1}=2\iint(\varepsilon_{s}-\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda}\Lambda\varepsilon)\Big(-(\psi_{B}\varepsilon_{y_{1}})_{y_{1}}-(\psi_{B}\varepsilon_{y_{2}})_{y_{2}}+\varepsilon\phi_{i,B}-\psi_{B}[(Q_{b}+\varepsilon)^{3}-Q_{b}^{3}]\Big),

f_{2}=2\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda}\iint\Lambda\varepsilon\Big(-(\psi_{B}\varepsilon_{y_{1}})_{y_{1}}-(\psi_{B}\varepsilon_{y_{2}})_{y_{2}}+\varepsilon\phi_{i,B}-\psi_{B}[(Q_{b}+\varepsilon)^{3}-Q_{b}^{3}]\Big)

-j\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda}\mathcal{F}_{i},

f_{3}=-2\iint\psi_{B}(Q_{b})_{s}[(Q_{b}+\varepsilon)^{3}-Q_{b}^{3}-3Q_{b}^{2}\varepsilon].

Here
f_{1}
contains the terms generated by the equation for
\varepsilon
which will give the damping term,

f_{2}
contains the scaling drift, and
f_{3}
contains the derivative of the
profile
Q_{b}
. The following three lemmas estimate these contributions.

Lemma 5.7
.

There exists
\mu>0
and
C>0
such that the term
f_{1}
is bounded by

f_{1}\leq-\mu\iint(|\nabla\varepsilon|^{2}+\varepsilon^{2})\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}+Cb^{4}.

Lemma 5.8
.

For the value
\mu
appearing in Lemma
5.7
, there exists
\tilde{C}>0
such that the term
f_{2}
is bounded by

f_{2}\leq\frac{\mu}{4}\iint(|\nabla\varepsilon|^{2}+\varepsilon^{2})\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}+\tilde{C}b^{4}.

The proofs of Lemmas
5.7
and
5.8
are given in the following sections.

Lemma 5.9
.

For the value
\mu
appearing in Lemma
5.7
, the term
f_{3}
is bounded by

f_{3}\leq\frac{\mu}{4}\iint(|\nabla\varepsilon|^{2}+\varepsilon^{2})\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}.

Proof of Lemma
5.9
.

Recall that

f_{3}=-2\iint\psi_{B}(Q_{b})_{s}[(Q_{b}+\varepsilon)^{3}-Q_{b}^{3}-3Q_{b}^{2}\varepsilon].

Estimates (
4.24
) and (
5.29
) give

|(Q_{b})_{s}|\lesssim|b_{s}|\lesssim e^{B/(2\alpha_{1})}\mathcal{N}_{i}+b^{2}.

Furthermore, (
5.25
) and the weighted Sobolev Lemma
4.3
for the weight
\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}
satisfying the gradient bound (
5.18
),
imply

\begin{split}\Big|\iint\psi_{B}[(Q_{b}+\varepsilon)^{3}-Q_{b}^{3}-3Q_{b}^{2}\varepsilon]\Big|&\lesssim e^{\varpi B}\Big(\iint|\varepsilon|^{3}\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}+\|Q_{b}\|_{L_{y_{1}y_{2}}^{\infty}}\iint\varepsilon^{2}\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}\Big)\\
&\lesssim e^{\varpi B}\Big(\|\varepsilon\|_{L^{2}}\iint(\varepsilon_{y_{1}}^{2}+\varepsilon_{y_{2}}^{2}+\varepsilon^{2})\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}+\iint\varepsilon^{2}\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}\Big).\end{split}

Therefore, after fixing
B
and choosing
\nu^{*}
sufficiently
small,

\begin{split}f_{3}&\lesssim(e^{\frac{B}{2\alpha_{1}}}\mathcal{N}_{i}+b^{2})e^{\varpi B}\Big(\|\varepsilon\|_{L^{2}}\iint(\varepsilon_{y_{1}}^{2}+\varepsilon_{y_{2}}^{2}+\varepsilon^{2})\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}+\iint\varepsilon^{2}\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}\Big)\\
&\leq\frac{\mu}{4}\iint(\varepsilon_{y_{1}}^{2}+\varepsilon_{y_{2}}^{2}+\varepsilon^{2})\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}.\end{split}

∎

Lemmas
5.7
–
5.9
imply that, for some

\widetilde{\mu}>0
,

\lambda^{j}\frac{d}{ds}\Bigg\{\frac{\mathcal{F}_{i}}{\lambda^{j}}\Bigg\}+\widetilde{\mu}\iint(|\nabla\varepsilon|^{2}+\varepsilon^{2})\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}\lesssim b^{4},

so

\frac{d}{ds}\Bigg\{\frac{\mathcal{F}_{i}}{\lambda^{j}}\Bigg\}+\frac{\widetilde{\mu}}{\lambda^{j}}\iint(|\nabla\varepsilon|^{2}+\varepsilon^{2})\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}\lesssim\frac{b^{4}}{\lambda^{j}}.

(ii)
. For the upper coercivity bound, the nonlinear part satisfies

\begin{split}\Big|\iint\Big((\varepsilon+Q_{b})^{4}-Q_{b}^{4}-4Q_{b}^{3}\varepsilon\Big)\psi_{B}\Big|&\lesssim\iint\Big(\varepsilon^{4}+Q_{b}^{2}\varepsilon^{2}\Big)\psi_{B}\\
&\lesssim\|\varepsilon\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\iint(|\nabla\varepsilon|^{2}+\varepsilon^{2})\psi_{B}+\|Q_{b}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}\iint\varepsilon^{2}\psi_{B}\\
&\lesssim\|\varepsilon\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\iint(\varepsilon_{y_{1}}^{2}+\varepsilon_{y_{2}}^{2})\psi_{B}+\|Q_{b}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}\iint\varepsilon^{2}\phi_{i,B}\\
&\lesssim\mathcal{N}_{i}(s).\end{split}

Hence
\mathcal{F}_{i}\lesssim\mathcal{N}_{i}.

For the lower bound, we write it as

\begin{split}\mathcal{F}_{i}&=\iint(\varepsilon_{y_{1}}^{2}+\varepsilon_{y_{2}}^{2})\psi_{B}+\varepsilon^{2}\phi_{i,B}-\frac{1}{2}\Big((\varepsilon+Q_{b})^{4}-Q_{b}^{4}-4Q_{b}^{3}\varepsilon\Big)\psi_{B}\\
&=\iint[(\varepsilon_{y_{1}}^{2}+\varepsilon_{y_{2}}^{2})\psi_{B}+\varepsilon^{2}\phi_{i,B}-3Q^{2}\varepsilon^{2}\psi_{B}]\\
&-\frac{1}{2}\iint\Big((\varepsilon+Q_{b})^{4}-Q_{b}^{4}-4Q_{b}^{3}\varepsilon-6Q_{b}^{2}\varepsilon^{2}\Big)\psi_{B}-3\iint(Q_{b}^{2}-Q^{2})\varepsilon^{2}\psi_{B}.\end{split}

Since

\begin{split}\Big|\iint\Big((\varepsilon+Q_{b})^{4}&-Q_{b}^{4}-4Q_{b}^{3}\varepsilon-6Q_{b}^{2}\varepsilon^{2}\Big)\psi_{B}\Big|\lesssim\iint\Big(\varepsilon^{4}+|Q_{b}||\varepsilon|^{3}\Big)\psi_{B}\\
&\lesssim\|\varepsilon\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\iint(\varepsilon_{y_{1}}^{2}+\varepsilon_{y_{2}}^{2}+\varepsilon^{2})\psi_{B}+\|Q_{b}\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\varepsilon\|_{L^{2}}\iint(\varepsilon_{y_{1}}^{2}+\varepsilon_{y_{2}}^{2}+\varepsilon^{2})\psi_{B}\end{split}

and

\Big|\iint(Q_{b}^{2}-Q^{2})\varepsilon^{2}\psi_{B}\Big|\lesssim|b|\iint\varepsilon^{2}\phi_{i,B},

it is sufficient to prove that there exists
\overline{\mu}>0

\iint[(\varepsilon_{y_{1}}^{2}+\varepsilon_{y_{2}}^{2})\psi_{B}+\varepsilon^{2}\phi_{i,B}-3Q^{2}\varepsilon^{2}\psi_{B}]\geq\overline{\mu}\iint[(\varepsilon_{y_{1}}^{2}+\varepsilon_{y_{2}}^{2})\psi_{B}+\varepsilon^{2}\phi_{i,B}].

Since
\psi_{B}\leq\phi_{i,B}
, it is sufficient to prove that

\iint[(\varepsilon_{y_{1}}^{2}+\varepsilon_{y_{2}}^{2})+\varepsilon^{2}-3Q^{2}\varepsilon^{2}]\psi_{B}\geq\overline{\mu}\iint(\varepsilon_{y_{1}}^{2}+\varepsilon_{y_{2}}^{2}+\varepsilon^{2})\psi_{B}

By integration by parts, we compute

\begin{split}(L(\varepsilon\sqrt{\psi_{B}}),\varepsilon\sqrt{\psi_{B}})&=\iint(\varepsilon_{y_{1}}^{2}+\varepsilon_{y_{2}}^{2}+\varepsilon^{2}-3Q^{2}\varepsilon^{2})\psi_{B}+\varepsilon^{2}\frac{[\psi_{B}^{\prime}]^{2}}{4\psi_{B}}+\varepsilon\varepsilon_{y_{1}}\psi_{B}^{\prime}\\
&=\iint(\varepsilon_{y_{1}}^{2}+\varepsilon_{y_{2}}^{2}+\varepsilon^{2}-3Q^{2}\varepsilon^{2})\psi_{B}-\iint\varepsilon^{2}\frac{2\psi_{B}^{\prime\prime}\psi_{B}-[\psi_{B}^{\prime}]^{2}}{4\psi_{B}},\end{split}

(5.34)

and also

\|\varepsilon\sqrt{\psi_{B}}\|_{H^{1}}^{2}=\iint(\varepsilon_{y_{1}}^{2}+\varepsilon_{y_{2}}^{2}+\varepsilon^{2})\psi_{B}-\iint\varepsilon^{2}\frac{2\psi_{B}^{\prime\prime}\psi_{B}-[\psi_{B}^{\prime}]^{2}}{4\psi_{B}}.

(5.35)

Since
1-\psi_{B}
is supported in
\{y_{1}<-B/2\}
, the orthogonality
conditions (
4.4
) and Cauchy–Schwarz give

|(\varepsilon\sqrt{\psi_{B}},Q)|=|(Q,(1-\sqrt{\psi_{B}})\varepsilon)|=\iint_{\{y_{1}<-\frac{B}{2}\}}|Q(1-\sqrt{\psi_{B}})\varepsilon|

\leq\Big(\iint\varepsilon^{2}\psi_{B}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big(\iint_{\{y_{1}<-\frac{B}{2}\}}\frac{Q^{2}(1-\sqrt{\psi_{B}})^{2}}{\psi_{B}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq e^{-\frac{B}{2}}\Big(\iint\varepsilon^{2}\psi_{B}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

By the definition of
\psi_{B},

\frac{(1-\sqrt{\psi_{B}})^{2}}{\psi_{B}}\begin{cases}=e^{-2\sigma(y_{1}+MB)}\qquad\text{ for }y_{1}\leq-B/2-1,\\
\lesssim e^{-2\sigma(M-\frac{1}{2})B}\qquad\text{ for }-B/2-1\leq y_{1}\leq-B/2.\end{cases}

(5.36)

Together with the decay
Q(y_{1},y_{2})\lesssim e^{-\frac{|y_{1}|}{\alpha_{1}}-C_{\alpha_{1}}|y_{2}|},
we obtain

\Big(\iint_{\{y_{1}<-\frac{B}{2}\}}\frac{Q^{2}(1-\sqrt{\psi_{B}})^{2}}{\psi_{B}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\lesssim e^{-\big(\frac{1}{2\alpha_{1}}+\sigma(M-\frac{1}{2})\big)B}\lesssim e^{-\frac{B}{\alpha_{1}}}.

Therefore,

|(\varepsilon\sqrt{\psi_{B}},Q)|\lesssim e^{-\frac{B}{\alpha_{1}}}\Big(\iint\varepsilon^{2}\psi_{B}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Similarly, we have

|(\varepsilon\sqrt{\psi_{B}},\varphi(y_{1})Q_{y_{1}})|+|(\varepsilon\sqrt{\psi_{B}},\varphi(y_{1})\Lambda Q)|+\Big|(\varepsilon\sqrt{\psi_{B}},\varphi(y_{1})Q_{y_{2}})\Big|\leq e^{-\frac{B}{\alpha_{1}}}\Big(\iint\varepsilon^{2}\psi_{B}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Applying the global coercivity estimate (
3.5
) to

\varepsilon\sqrt{\psi_{B}}
and using (
5.34
)–(
5.35
),
we find a constant
\widetilde{\delta}>0
such that

\iint(\varepsilon_{y_{1}}^{2}+\varepsilon_{y_{2}}^{2}+\varepsilon^{2}-3Q^{2}\varepsilon^{2})\psi_{B}\geq

\geq\tilde{\delta}\iint(\varepsilon_{y_{1}}^{2}+\varepsilon_{y_{2}}^{2}+\varepsilon^{2})\psi_{B}+(1-\tilde{\delta})\iint\varepsilon^{2}\frac{2\psi_{B}^{\prime\prime}\psi_{B}-[\psi_{B}^{\prime}]^{2}}{4\psi_{B}}-4\frac{e^{-\frac{B}{\alpha_{1}}}}{\tilde{\delta}}\Big(\iint\varepsilon^{2}\psi_{B}\Big)
