\displaystyle+\frac{(x_{1})_{t}}{\lambda}\varepsilon_{y_{1}}+\frac{(x_{2})_{t}}{\lambda}\varepsilon_{y_{2}},

where

f(\varepsilon)=(Q_{b}+\varepsilon)^{3}-Q_{b}^{3},\qquad\zeta_{b}=\chi_{b}+\gamma y_{1}(\chi_{b})_{y_{1}},

and

\mathfrak{H}(t,y_{1},y_{2})=\frac{1}{\lambda^{3}}\Big[-\Psi_{b}-(\lambda^{2}\lambda_{t}+b)\Lambda Q_{b}-(\lambda^{2}(x_{1})_{t}-1)(Q_{b})_{y_{1}}-\lambda^{2}(x_{2})_{t}(Q_{b})_{y_{2}}\Big].

Set

M_{D}(t):=\iint\varepsilon^{2}(t,y_{1},y_{2})\varrho_{D}(y_{1})\,dy_{1}dy_{2}.

A direct integration by parts gives

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}M_{D}^{\prime}(t)={}

\displaystyle-\frac{3}{2\lambda^{3}}\iint\varepsilon_{y_{1}}^{2}(\varrho_{D})_{y_{1}}-\frac{1}{2\lambda^{3}}\iint\varepsilon_{y_{2}}^{2}(\varrho_{D})_{y_{1}}+\frac{1}{2\lambda^{3}}\iint\varepsilon^{2}(\varrho_{D})_{y_{1}y_{1}y_{1}}

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{\lambda^{3}}\iint f(\varepsilon)_{y_{1}}\varepsilon\varrho_{D}-\iint\mathfrak{H}\varepsilon\varrho_{D}-b_{t}\iint\zeta_{b}P\varepsilon\varrho_{D}

\displaystyle+\frac{\lambda_{t}}{\lambda}\iint\varepsilon\Lambda\varepsilon\varrho_{D}+\frac{(x_{1})_{t}}{\lambda}\iint\varepsilon\varepsilon_{y_{1}}\varrho_{D}+\frac{(x_{2})_{t}}{\lambda}\iint\varepsilon\varepsilon_{y_{2}}\varrho_{D}.

Since
\varrho_{D}
depends only on
y_{1}
,

\iint\varepsilon\varepsilon_{y_{2}}\varrho_{D}=0,\qquad\iint\varepsilon\Lambda\varepsilon\varrho_{D}=-\frac{1}{2}\iint\varepsilon^{2}y_{1}(\varrho_{D})_{y_{1}},

and

\iint\varepsilon\varepsilon_{y_{1}}\varrho_{D}=-\frac{1}{2}\iint\varepsilon^{2}(\varrho_{D})_{y_{1}}.

Using
\lambda\sim 1
and the boundedness of the derivatives of
\varrho
, we
therefore obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}M_{D}^{\prime}(t)\geq{}

\displaystyle-C\|\nabla\varepsilon(t)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}-C\iint\varepsilon^{2}|(\varrho_{D})_{y_{1}y_{1}y_{1}}|-C\left|\iint f(\varepsilon)_{y_{1}}\varepsilon\varrho_{D}\right|-C\left|\iint\mathfrak{H}\varepsilon\varrho_{D}\right|

\displaystyle-b_{t}\iint\zeta_{b}P\varepsilon\varrho_{D}-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\lambda_{t}}{\lambda}\iint\varepsilon^{2}y_{1}(\varrho_{D})_{y_{1}}-C\left|\iint\varepsilon^{2}(\varrho_{D})_{y_{1}}\right|.

(10.5)

Step 1: The third-derivative term.

By (
10.4
),

\displaystyle\iint\varepsilon^{2}|(\varrho_{D})_{y_{1}y_{1}y_{1}}|

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{D^{3}}\iint\varepsilon^{2}\left|\varrho_{y_{1}y_{1}y_{1}}\left(\frac{y_{1}}{D}\right)\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1}{D^{3}}\iint_{|y_{1}|\leq D}\varepsilon^{2}\lesssim\frac{1}{D}\mathcal{N}_{3}(t).

Step 2: The nonlinear term.

Since

f(\varepsilon)=3Q_{b}^{2}\varepsilon+3Q_{b}\varepsilon^{2}+\varepsilon^{3},

an integration by parts yields the exact identity

\displaystyle\iint f(\varepsilon)_{y_{1}}\varepsilon\varrho_{D}={}

\displaystyle 3\iint Q_{b}(Q_{b})_{y_{1}}\varepsilon^{2}\varrho_{D}-\frac{3}{2}\iint Q_{b}^{2}\varepsilon^{2}(\varrho_{D})_{y_{1}}

\displaystyle+\iint(Q_{b})_{y_{1}}\varepsilon^{3}\varrho_{D}-2\iint Q_{b}\varepsilon^{3}(\varrho_{D})_{y_{1}}-\frac{3}{4}\iint\varepsilon^{4}(\varrho_{D})_{y_{1}}.

The pointwise estimates for
Q_{b}
, the two-dimensional
Gagliardo–Nirenberg inequality, and the smallness of

\|\varepsilon\|_{L^{2}}
give

\displaystyle\left|\iint Q_{b}(Q_{b})_{y_{1}}\varepsilon^{2}\right|+\iint Q_{b}^{2}\varepsilon^{2}\lesssim\mathcal{N}_{1}+b^{2},

\displaystyle\left|\iint(Q_{b})_{y_{1}}\varepsilon^{3}\right|+\left|\iint Q_{b}\varepsilon^{3}\right|\lesssim\mathcal{N}_{1}+\bigl(|b|+|b|^{1+\gamma}\bigr)\|\nabla\varepsilon\|_{L^{2}}+|b|\mathcal{N}_{1},

\displaystyle\iint\varepsilon^{4}\lesssim\|\varepsilon\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\|\nabla\varepsilon\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\lesssim\|\nabla\varepsilon\|_{L^{2}}^{2}.

Consequently, by (
10.1
), (
10.2
), and

\mathcal{N}_{1}\lesssim\mathcal{N}_{3}
,

\left|\iint f(\varepsilon)_{y_{1}}\varepsilon\varrho_{D}\right|\lesssim\mathcal{N}_{1}+b^{2}+\|\nabla\varepsilon\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\lesssim\mathcal{N}_{3}(t).

Step 3: The term involving
\mathfrak{H}
.

Lemma
4.1
and the decay of
Q
and
P
give

\displaystyle\left|\iint\Lambda Q_{b}\,\varepsilon\varrho_{D}\right|+\left|\iint(Q_{b})_{y_{2}}\varepsilon\varrho_{D}\right|\lesssim\mathcal{N}_{1}^{1/2}+|b|^{1-\gamma/2},

\displaystyle\left|\iint(Q_{b})_{y_{1}}\varepsilon\varrho_{D}\right|\lesssim\mathcal{N}_{1}^{1/2}+|b|^{1+\gamma/2},\qquad\left|\iint\Psi_{b}\varepsilon\varrho_{D}\right|\lesssim|b|^{1+\gamma/2}.

Using (
4.8
),
ds/dt=\lambda^{-3}
,

\lambda\sim 1
, and (
10.2
), we obtain

\displaystyle\left|\iint\mathfrak{H}\varepsilon\varrho_{D}\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim|b|^{1+\gamma/2}+(b^{2}+\mathcal{N}_{2}^{1/2})(\mathcal{N}_{2}^{1/2}+|b|^{1-\gamma/2})

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathcal{N}_{2}+|b|^{2-\gamma}+|b|^{1+\gamma/2}\lesssim\mathcal{N}_{3}(t).

Step 4: The longitudinal transport term.

Since
\lambda(t)\to\lambda_{\infty}>0
and

(x_{1})_{t}(t)\to\lambda_{\infty}^{-2}
, the coefficient

|(x_{1})_{t}/\lambda|
is bounded for
t\geq T_{0}
. Hence

\left|\frac{(x_{1})_{t}}{\lambda}\iint\varepsilon\varepsilon_{y_{1}}\varrho_{D}\right|\lesssim\left|\iint\varepsilon^{2}(\varrho_{D})_{y_{1}}\right|.

Step 5: Integration in time.

Integrating (
10.5
) over

[t_{0},+\infty)
, and using (
10.3
),
(
6.10
), Steps 1–4, and
\lambda\sim 1
, gives

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}M_{D}(t)-M_{D}(t_{0})\geq{}

\displaystyle-C\mathcal{N}_{4}(t_{0})-C\left|\int_{t_{0}}^{\infty}b_{t}\iint\zeta_{b}P\varepsilon\varrho_{D}\,dt\right|

\displaystyle-C\left|\int_{t_{0}}^{\infty}\frac{\lambda_{t}}{\lambda}\iint\varepsilon^{2}y_{1}(\varrho_{D})_{y_{1}}\,dt\right|-C\int_{t_{0}}^{\infty}\left|\iint\varepsilon^{2}(\varrho_{D})_{y_{1}}\right|dt.

Indeed, the terms bounded pointwise by
\mathcal{N}_{3}
are bounded by

\mathcal{N}_{4}(t_{0})
after time integration, while the Step 1 term is at
most
D^{-1}\mathcal{N}_{4}(t_{0})\lesssim\mathcal{N}_{4}(t_{0})
.
Moreover, (
10.4
), the hierarchy

\mathcal{N}_{3}\lesssim\mathcal{N}_{5}
, and (
7.19
) imply

|M_{D}(t)|\lesssim D^{2}\mathcal{N}_{3}(t)\longrightarrow 0\qquad\text{as }t\to+\infty.

Here
D
is fixed when taking the time limit. Therefore,

\displaystyle M_{D}(t_{0})\lesssim{}

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{4}(t_{0})+\left|\int_{t_{0}}^{\infty}b_{t}\iint\zeta_{b}P\varepsilon\varrho_{D}\,dt\right|+\left|\int_{t_{0}}^{\infty}\frac{\lambda_{t}}{\lambda}\iint\varepsilon^{2}y_{1}(\varrho_{D})_{y_{1}}\,dt\right|

\displaystyle+\int_{t_{0}}^{\infty}\left|\iint\varepsilon^{2}(\varrho_{D})_{y_{1}}\right|dt.

(10.6)

Step 6: The
b_{t}
-term.

Define

f_{D}(t):=\iint\chi_{b}(y_{1})\varrho_{D}(y_{1})P(y_{1},y_{2})\varepsilon(t,y_{1},y_{2})\,dy_{1}dy_{2}.

By Cauchy–Schwarz,

\displaystyle|f_{D}(t)|

\displaystyle\leq\left(\iint\varepsilon^{2}(t,y_{1},y_{2})\varrho_{D}(y_{1})\,dy_{1}dy_{2}\right)^{1/2}\left(\iint\varrho_{D}\chi_{b}^{2}P^{2}\,dy_{1}dy_{2}\right)^{1/2}

(10.7)

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\varepsilon(t)\|_{L^{2}}\min\left(D,|b(t)|^{-\gamma}\right)^{1/2}\leq\delta(\alpha_{0})D^{1/2}.

Furthermore,

|b(t)f_{D}(t)|\lesssim\left(\iint\varepsilon^{2}(t,y_{1},y_{2})\varrho_{D}(y_{1})\,dy_{1}dy_{2}\right)^{1/2}|b(t)|^{1-\gamma/2}\longrightarrow 0

(10.8)

as
t\to+\infty
.

Set

\widetilde{\varrho}(t,y_{1},y_{2}):=\varrho_{D}(y_{1})\chi_{b}(y_{1})P(y_{1},y_{2}),\qquad h_{D}(t):=\iint\widetilde{\varrho}\,\varepsilon_{t}\,dy_{1}dy_{2}.

The regularity of the modulation parameters and the equation for
\varepsilon
imply that
bf_{D}
is locally absolutely continuous. Since
\partial_{t}(b\chi_{b}P)=b_{t}\zeta_{b}P
,

(bf_{D})_{t}=b_{t}\iint\zeta_{b}P\varepsilon\varrho_{D}\,dy_{1}dy_{2}+b\iint\chi_{b}P\varepsilon_{t}\varrho_{D}\,dy_{1}dy_{2}.

(10.9)

Lemma 10.4
.

Let
\theta\in C_{c}^{3}(\mathbb{R})
satisfy

\operatorname{supp}\theta\subset[-1,1]
, and define

\theta_{D}(y_{1}):=\theta\left(\frac{y_{1}}{D}\right),\qquad\Theta_{D}(t,y_{1},y_{2}):=\theta_{D}(y_{1})\chi_{b}(y_{1})P(y_{1},y_{2}).

Then, for all

t\geq T_{0}
,

\left|b(t)\iint\Theta_{D}(t,y_{1},y_{2})\varepsilon_{t}(t,y_{1},y_{2})\,dy_{1}dy_{2}\right|\lesssim\mathcal{N}_{3}(t),

where the implicit constant is independent of
D
and
t
.

Proof.

Set

m_{D}(t):=\min\bigl(D,|b(t)|^{-\gamma}\bigr),

with the convention
m_{D}(t)=D
when
b(t)=0
.

The decay of
P
and the support properties of
\chi_{b}
and
\theta_{D}
give

\displaystyle\|(\Theta_{D})_{y_{1}}\|_{L^{2}}+\|(\Theta_{D})_{y_{1}y_{1}y_{1}}\|_{L^{2}}+\|(\Theta_{D})_{y_{1}y_{2}y_{2}}\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim 1,

(10.10)

\displaystyle\|\Theta_{D}\|_{L^{2}}+\|(\Theta_{D})_{y_{2}}\|_{L^{2}}+\|\Theta_{D}+y_{1}(\Theta_{D})_{y_{1}}+y_{2}(\Theta_{D})_{y_{2}}\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim m_{D}^{1/2},

\displaystyle\iint|\Theta_{D}\zeta_{b}P|\lesssim m_{D}.

Indeed, a
y_{1}
-derivative falling on
\theta_{D}
produces a factor

D^{-1}
, while one falling on
\chi_{b}
produces a factor
|b|^{\gamma}
.
All
y_{1}
-derivatives of
P
are exponentially localized; the only
nonlocalized contribution is the undifferentiated left-hand tail of
P
,
whose effective length is bounded by
m_{D}
.

Using the equation for
\varepsilon_{t}
and integrating by parts in

(y_{1},y_{2})
, we obtain

\displaystyle\iint\Theta_{D}\varepsilon_{t}={}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\lambda^{3}}\iint\bigl[(\Theta_{D})_{y_{1}y_{1}y_{1}}+(\Theta_{D})_{y_{1}y_{2}y_{2}}\bigr]\varepsilon+\frac{1}{\lambda^{3}}\iint(\Theta_{D})_{y_{1}}f(\varepsilon)

\displaystyle-\iint\Theta_{D}\mathfrak{H}-b_{t}\iint\Theta_{D}\zeta_{b}P-\frac{(x_{1})_{t}}{\lambda}\iint(\Theta_{D})_{y_{1}}\varepsilon-\frac{(x_{2})_{t}}{\lambda}\iint(\Theta_{D})_{y_{2}}\varepsilon

\displaystyle+\frac{\lambda_{t}}{\lambda}\iint\Theta_{D}\Lambda\varepsilon,

(10.11)

where

\iint\Theta_{D}\Lambda\varepsilon=-\iint\bigl[\Theta_{D}+y_{1}(\Theta_{D})_{y_{1}}+y_{2}(\Theta_{D})_{y_{2}}\bigr]\varepsilon.

Moreover,

\displaystyle\left|\iint(\Theta_{D})_{y_{1}}f(\varepsilon)\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\|(\Theta_{D})_{y_{1}}\|_{L^{2}}\bigl(\|Q_{b}^{2}\varepsilon\|_{L^{2}}+\|\varepsilon^{3}\|_{L^{2}}\bigr)+\|(\Theta_{D})_{y_{1}}Q_{b}\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\varepsilon\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim 1+\|\varepsilon\|_{L^{2}}\|\nabla\varepsilon\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\lesssim 1.

By Lemma
4.1
, (
4.8
),
and
\lambda\sim 1
,

\|\mathfrak{H}\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim|b|^{1+\gamma/2}+b^{2}+\mathcal{N}_{2}^{1/2},\quad|b_{t}|\lesssim b^{2}+\mathcal{N}_{2},\quad\left|\frac{\lambda_{t}}{\lambda}\right|+\left|\frac{(x_{2})_{t}}{\lambda}\right|\lesssim|b|+\mathcal{N}_{2}^{1/2},\quad\left|\frac{(x_{1})_{t}}{\lambda}\right|\lesssim 1.

Thus (
10.11
) and
(
10.10
) imply

\displaystyle\left|\iint\Theta_{D}\varepsilon_{t}\right|\lesssim{}

\displaystyle 1+m_{D}^{1/2}\bigl(|b|^{1+\gamma/2}+b^{2}+\mathcal{N}_{2}^{1/2}\bigr)+(b^{2}+\mathcal{N}_{2})m_{D}

\displaystyle+(|b|+\mathcal{N}_{2}^{1/2})m_{D}^{1/2}.

For
b\neq 0
,
m_{D}\leq|b|^{-\gamma}
, and hence

\left|\iint\Theta_{D}\varepsilon_{t}\right|\lesssim 1+(\mathcal{N}_{2}m_{D})^{1/2}+\mathcal{N}_{2}m_{D}\lesssim 1+\mathcal{N}_{2}m_{D}.

Multiplying by
|b|
and using (
10.2
) and

\mathcal{N}_{2}\lesssim\mathcal{N}_{3}
, we obtain

\left|b\iint\Theta_{D}\varepsilon_{t}\right|\lesssim|b|+|b|^{1-\gamma}\mathcal{N}_{2}\lesssim\mathcal{N}_{3}.

If
b(t)=0
, the conclusion is immediate.
∎

Applying Lemma
10.4
with
\theta=\varrho
gives

|b(t)h_{D}(t)|\lesssim\mathcal{N}_{3}(t).

(10.12)

Integrating (
10.9
) over
[t_{0},R]
, letting
R\to+\infty
, and using
(
10.8
), (
10.12
), and (
6.10
), yields

\displaystyle\left|\int_{t_{0}}^{\infty}b_{t}\iint\zeta_{b}P\varepsilon\varrho_{D}\,dy_{1}dy_{2}dt\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim|b(t_{0})f_{D}(t_{0})|+\int_{t_{0}}^{\infty}\mathcal{N}_{3}(t)\,dt

\displaystyle\lesssim|b(t_{0})|^{1-\gamma/2}M_{D}(t_{0})^{1/2}+\mathcal{N}_{4}(t_{0}).

Step 7: The scaling term.

We claim that

\displaystyle\left|\int_{t_{0}}^{\infty}\frac{\lambda_{t}}{\lambda}\iint\varepsilon^{2}(t,y_{1},y_{2})y_{1}(\varrho_{D})_{y_{1}}(y_{1})\,dy_{1}dy_{2}dt\right|

(10.13)

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathcal{N}_{4}(t_{0})+\min\left(\mathcal{N}_{2}^{1/2}(t_{0}),D\mathcal{N}_{4}(t_{0})\right).

We prove two complementary bounds.

First estimate.

Let
C_{\varrho}:=\|r\varrho^{\prime}(r)\|_{L^{\infty}}
. By
(
10.4
), (
10.2
), and
(
10.1
),

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\lambda_{t}}{\lambda}\iint\varepsilon^{2}y_{1}(\varrho_{D})_{y_{1}}\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim(|b|+\mathcal{N}_{1}^{1/2})C_{\varrho}\left(\iint_{|y_{1}|\leq D}\varepsilon^{2}\right)^{1/2}\|\varepsilon\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim D(|b|+\mathcal{N}_{1}^{1/2})\mathcal{N}_{3}^{1/2}\lesssim D\mathcal{N}_{3}(t).

The last inequality uses
\mathcal{N}_{1}\lesssim\mathcal{N}_{3}
,

|b|\lesssim\mathcal{N}_{3}
, and the uniform smallness of

\mathcal{N}_{3}
.
Therefore, by (
6.10
),

\left|\int_{t_{0}}^{\infty}\frac{\lambda_{t}}{\lambda}\iint\varepsilon^{2}y_{1}(\varrho_{D})_{y_{1}}\,dt\right|\lesssim D\mathcal{N}_{4}(t_{0}).

(10.14)

Second estimate.

Set

\iota(r):=r\varrho^{\prime}(r),\qquad\iota_{D}(y_{1}):=\iota\left(\frac{y_{1}}{D}\right).

Then
\iota\in C_{c}^{3}(\mathbb{R})
and

y_{1}(\varrho_{D})_{y_{1}}=\iota_{D}
. The sharp scaling law in
Lemma
4.5
reads

\frac{\lambda_{t}}{\lambda}=-\frac{b}{\lambda^{3}}+(J_{1})_{t}+\frac{\lambda_{t}}{\lambda}J_{1}+O\left(\frac{b^{2}+\mathcal{N}_{2}}{\lambda^{3}}\right),

(10.15)

where

J_{1}(t)=\frac{1}{2C_{Q}}\left(\varepsilon(t),\int_{-\infty}^{y_{1}}\Lambda Q\right),\qquad|J_{1}(t)|\lesssim\mathcal{N}_{2}^{1/2}(t).

Substitution into the scaling term gives

\displaystyle\int_{t_{0}}^{\infty}\frac{\lambda_{t}}{\lambda}\iint\varepsilon^{2}\iota_{D}={}

\displaystyle-\int_{t_{0}}^{\infty}\frac{b}{\lambda^{3}}\iint\varepsilon^{2}\iota_{D}+\int_{t_{0}}^{\infty}\frac{\lambda_{t}}{\lambda}J_{1}\iint\varepsilon^{2}\iota_{D}

\displaystyle+\int_{t_{0}}^{\infty}(J_{1})_{t}\iint\varepsilon^{2}\iota_{D}+\int_{t_{0}}^{\infty}O\left(\frac{b^{2}+\mathcal{N}_{2}}{\lambda^{3}}\right)\iint\varepsilon^{2}\iota_{D}.

Using (
10.2
), (
6.10
),
(
6.5
), and
\lambda\sim 1
, the three terms not
containing
(J_{1})_{t}
satisfy

\displaystyle\left|\int_{t_{0}}^{\infty}\frac{b}{\lambda^{3}}\iint\varepsilon^{2}\iota_{D}\right|\lesssim\mathcal{N}_{4}(t_{0}),

\displaystyle\left|\int_{t_{0}}^{\infty}\frac{\lambda_{t}}{\lambda}J_{1}\iint\varepsilon^{2}\iota_{D}\right|+\left|\int_{t_{0}}^{\infty}O\left(\frac{b^{2}+\mathcal{N}_{2}}{\lambda^{3}}\right)\iint\varepsilon^{2}\iota_{D}\right|\lesssim\mathcal{N}_{3}(t_{0}).

For the remaining term, we integrate by parts on
[t_{0},R]
and then let
R\to+\infty
, obtaining

\displaystyle\int_{t_{0}}^{\infty}(J_{1})_{t}\iint\varepsilon^{2}\iota_{D}={}

\displaystyle-J_{1}(t_{0})\iint\varepsilon^{2}(t_{0},y_{1},y_{2})\iota_{D}(y_{1})\,dy_{1}dy_{2}

\displaystyle-\int_{t_{0}}^{\infty}J_{1}(t)\frac{d}{dt}\left(\iint\varepsilon^{2}(t,y_{1},y_{2})\iota_{D}(y_{1})\,dy_{1}dy_{2}\right)dt.

The boundary term at
+\infty
vanishes because

\mathcal{N}_{2}(t)\to 0
, and the boundary term at
t_{0}
is bounded by

C\mathcal{N}_{2}^{1/2}(t_{0})
.

The localized-mass identity with
\iota_{D}
in place of
\varrho_{D}
is

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\iint\varepsilon^{2}\iota_{D}={}

\displaystyle-\frac{3}{2\lambda^{3}}\iint\varepsilon_{y_{1}}^{2}(\iota_{D})_{y_{1}}-\frac{1}{2\lambda^{3}}\iint\varepsilon_{y_{2}}^{2}(\iota_{D})_{y_{1}}+\frac{1}{2\lambda^{3}}\iint\varepsilon^{2}(\iota_{D})_{y_{1}y_{1}y_{1}}

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{\lambda^{3}}\iint f(\varepsilon)_{y_{1}}\varepsilon\iota_{D}-\iint\mathfrak{H}\varepsilon\iota_{D}-b_{t}\iint\zeta_{b}P\varepsilon\iota_{D}

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\lambda_{t}}{\lambda}\iint\varepsilon^{2}y_{1}(\iota_{D})_{y_{1}}+\frac{(x_{1})_{t}}{\lambda}\iint\varepsilon\varepsilon_{y_{1}}\iota_{D}.

Using part (a) of Lemma
4.5
,
(
10.1
), (
10.4
),
(
4.8
), Steps 1–4, and Cauchy–Schwarz, all
terms except the
b_{t}
-term obey

\displaystyle|J_{1}|\left(\left|\iint\varepsilon_{y_{1}}^{2}(\iota_{D})_{y_{1}}\right|+\left|\iint\varepsilon_{y_{2}}^{2}(\iota_{D})_{y_{1}}\right|+\left|\iint\varepsilon^{2}(\iota_{D})_{y_{1}y_{1}y_{1}}\right|\right)\lesssim\mathcal{N}_{3},

\displaystyle|J_{1}|\left(\left|\iint f(\varepsilon)_{y_{1}}\varepsilon\iota_{D}\right|+\left|\iint\mathfrak{H}\varepsilon\iota_{D}\right|+\left|\frac{\lambda_{t}}{\lambda}\iint\varepsilon^{2}y_{1}(\iota_{D})_{y_{1}}\right|+\left|\frac{(x_{1})_{t}}{\lambda}\iint\varepsilon\varepsilon_{y_{1}}\iota_{D}\right|\right)\lesssim\mathcal{N}_{3}.

In particular, the third-derivative contribution is bounded by

|J_{1}|D^{-3}\iint_{|y_{1}|\leq D}\varepsilon^{2}\lesssim|J_{1}|D^{-1}\mathcal{N}_{3}\lesssim\mathcal{N}_{3}.

For the scaling contribution in the second line, use the global

L^{2}
bound and

|J_{1}|\,|\lambda_{t}/\lambda|\lesssim\mathcal{N}_{2}^{1/2}(|b|+\mathcal{N}_{1}^{1/2})\lesssim\mathcal{N}_{3}
.

It remains to estimate

\int_{t_{0}}^{\infty}J_{1}(t)b_{t}(t)\iint\zeta_{b}P\varepsilon\iota_{D}\,dy_{1}dy_{2}dt.

Define

g_{D}(t):=b(t)\iint\chi_{b}(y_{1})\iota_{D}(y_{1})P(y_{1},y_{2})\varepsilon(t,y_{1},y_{2})\,dy_{1}dy_{2}.

Then
g_{D}
is locally absolutely continuous and

(g_{D})_{t}=b_{t}\iint\zeta_{b}P\varepsilon\iota_{D}+b\iint\chi_{b}P\varepsilon_{t}\iota_{D},

and, by the Cauchy–Schwarz estimate used in (
10.7
),

|g_{D}(t)|\lesssim|b(t)|m_{D}(t)^{1/2}\lesssim|b(t)|^{1-\gamma/2}.

A second integration by parts on
[t_{0},R]
, followed by
R\to+\infty
, gives

\displaystyle\int_{t_{0}}^{\infty}J_{1}b_{t}\iint\zeta_{b}P\varepsilon\iota_{D}={}

\displaystyle-J_{1}(t_{0})g_{D}(t_{0})-\int_{t_{0}}^{\infty}(J_{1})_{t}g_{D}\,dt

\displaystyle-\int_{t_{0}}^{\infty}J_{1}b\iint\chi_{b}P\varepsilon_{t}\iota_{D}\,dy_{1}dy_{2}dt.

Here
J_{1}(t)g_{D}(t)\to 0
as
t\to+\infty
. By
(
10.15
),

(J_{1})_{t}=\frac{\lambda_{t}}{\lambda}+\frac{b}{\lambda^{3}}-\frac{\lambda_{t}}{\lambda}J_{1}+O\left(\frac{b^{2}+\mathcal{N}_{2}}{\lambda^{3}}\right).

Thus, using part (a) of Lemma
4.5
,
(
4.8
), (
10.2
), and
(
6.10
),

|J_{1}(t_{0})g_{D}(t_{0})|\lesssim\mathcal{N}_{3}(t_{0}),\qquad\int_{t_{0}}^{\infty}|(J_{1})_{t}g_{D}|\,dt\lesssim\mathcal{N}_{4}(t_{0}).

Finally, Lemma
10.4
, applied with

\theta=\iota
, gives

\left|b(t)\iint\chi_{b}P\varepsilon_{t}\iota_{D}\,dy_{1}dy_{2}\right|\lesssim\mathcal{N}_{3}(t),

so the last integral is also bounded by
C\mathcal{N}_{4}(t_{0})
. We have
proved

\left|\int_{t_{0}}^{\infty}(J_{1})_{t}\iint\varepsilon^{2}\iota_{D}\,dt\right|\lesssim\mathcal{N}_{2}^{1/2}(t_{0})+\mathcal{N}_{4}(t_{0}),

and hence

\left|\int_{t_{0}}^{\infty}\frac{\lambda_{t}}{\lambda}\iint\varepsilon^{2}\iota_{D}\,dt\right|\lesssim\mathcal{N}_{2}^{1/2}(t_{0})+\mathcal{N}_{4}(t_{0}).

Combining the first and second estimates gives

\displaystyle\left|\int_{t_{0}}^{\infty}\frac{\lambda_{t}}{\lambda}\iint\varepsilon^{2}y_{1}(\varrho_{D})_{y_{1}}\,dt\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\min\left(D\mathcal{N}_{4}(t_{0}),\mathcal{N}_{2}^{1/2}(t_{0})+\mathcal{N}_{4}(t_{0})\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathcal{N}_{4}(t_{0})+\min\left(\mathcal{N}_{2}^{1/2}(t_{0}),D\mathcal{N}_{4}(t_{0})\right).

Step 8: Closing the estimate.

Substituting Steps 6 and 7 into
(
10.6
), we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}M_{D}(t_{0})\leq{}

\displaystyle C\mathcal{N}_{4}(t_{0})+C|b(t_{0})|^{1-\gamma/2}M_{D}(t_{0})^{1/2}+C\min\left(\mathcal{N}_{2}^{1/2}(t_{0}),D\mathcal{N}_{4}(t_{0})\right)

\displaystyle+C\int_{t_{0}}^{\infty}\left|\iint\varepsilon^{2}(t,y_{1},y_{2})(\varrho_{D})_{y_{1}}(y_{1})\,dy_{1}dy_{2}\right|dt.

Young’s inequality gives

C|b(t_{0})|^{1-\gamma/2}M_{D}(t_{0})^{1/2}\leq\frac{1}{4}M_{D}(t_{0})+C|b(t_{0})|^{2-\gamma}.

By (
10.2
), the hierarchy of the
\mathcal{N}_{i}
, and the
smallness of the solution,

|b(t_{0})|^{2-\gamma}\lesssim\mathcal{N}_{4}(t_{0})
. Absorbing the first term
on the right and renaming
t_{0}
as
t
proves the lemma.
∎

We now apply Lemma
10.3
to prove global
L^{2}
decay of the
error.

Lemma 10.5
.

We have

\|\varepsilon(t)\|_{L^{2}_{y_{1}y_{2}}}\longrightarrow 0\qquad\text{as }t\to+\infty.

Proof.

Choose
\rho\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
such that

\operatorname{supp}\rho\subset[-1,1]
,
0\leq\rho\leq 1
,

\rho=1
on
[-1/2,1/2]
, and
\rho
is monotone on each transition
interval. We may also arrange that
|\rho^{\prime}|\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
.
For example, let

\varsigma(r)=\begin{cases}e^{-1/r},&r>0,\\
0,&r\leq 0,\end{cases}\qquad\Xi(r)=\frac{\varsigma(r)}{\varsigma(r)+\varsigma(1-r)},

and set

\rho(y_{1})=\Xi(2(y_{1}+1))\Xi(2(1-y_{1})).

Then
\rho=1
on
[-1/2,1/2]
, all derivatives vanish at the endpoints
of the transition intervals, and
|\rho^{\prime}|
is smooth and compactly
supported.

Apply Lemma
10.3
with
\varrho=|\rho^{\prime}|
and multiply the
result by
D^{-1}
. Since

|(\rho_{D})_{y_{1}}|=D^{-1}|\rho^{\prime}(y_{1}/D)|
, we obtain

\displaystyle\iint\varepsilon^{2}(t,y_{1},y_{2})|(\rho_{D})_{y_{1}}(y_{1})|\,dy_{1}dy_{2}\lesssim{}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{D}\mathcal{N}_{4}(t)+\frac{1}{D}\min\left(\mathcal{N}_{2}^{1/2}(t),D\mathcal{N}_{4}(t)\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{D^{2}}\int_{t}^{\infty}\left|\iint\varepsilon^{2}(t^{\prime},y_{1},y_{2})\bigl(|\rho^{\prime}|\bigr)^{\prime}\left(\frac{y_{1}}{D}\right)\,dy_{1}dy_{2}\right|dt^{\prime}.

Since
\bigl|(|\rho^{\prime}|)^{\prime}\bigr|\leq|\rho^{\prime\prime}|
and

\operatorname{supp}\rho^{\prime\prime}\subset[-1,1]
,
(
10.4
) and (
6.10
) bound the last term by

\frac{C}{D^{2}}\int_{t}^{\infty}\iint_{|y_{1}|\leq D}\varepsilon^{2}(t^{\prime})\,dt^{\prime}\lesssim\int_{t}^{\infty}\mathcal{N}_{3}(t^{\prime})\,dt^{\prime}\lesssim\mathcal{N}_{4}(t).

Also,

D^{-1}\min\{\mathcal{N}_{2}^{1/2}(t),D\mathcal{N}_{4}(t)\}\leq\mathcal{N}_{4}(t)
. Hence

\iint\varepsilon^{2}(t,y_{1},y_{2})|(\rho_{D})_{y_{1}}(y_{1})|\,dy_{1}dy_{2}\lesssim\mathcal{N}_{4}(t),\qquad D\geq 1.

(10.16)

Applying Lemma
10.3
once more, now with

\varrho=\rho
, and using the preceding estimate, we find

\displaystyle\iint_{-D/2\leq y_{1}\leq D/2}\varepsilon^{2}(t,y_{1},y_{2})\,dy_{1}dy_{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\iint\varepsilon^{2}(t,y_{1},y_{2})\rho_{D}(y_{1})\,dy_{1}dy_{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathcal{N}_{4}(t)+\mathcal{N}_{2}^{1/2}(t)+\int_{t}^{\infty}\mathcal{N}_{4}(t^{\prime})\,dt^{\prime}

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathcal{N}_{5}(t)+\mathcal{N}_{2}^{1/2}(t),

where the last inequality follows from (
6.10
) and the
hierarchy
\mathcal{N}_{4}\lesssim\mathcal{N}_{5}
. Letting
D\to+\infty
and
using monotone convergence and (
7.19
), we conclude that

\|\varepsilon(t)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\lesssim\mathcal{N}_{5}(t)+\mathcal{N}_{2}^{1/2}(t)\longrightarrow 0\qquad\text{as }t\to+\infty.

∎

Finally, using
(\varepsilon(t),Q)=0
and mass conservation,

\displaystyle\iint u_{0}^{2}\,dx_{1}dx_{2}

\displaystyle=\iint\bigl[Q_{b(t)}(y_{1},y_{2})+\varepsilon(t,y_{1},y_{2})\bigr]^{2}\,dy_{1}dy_{2}

\displaystyle=\iint Q^{2}+2b(t)\iint\chi_{b(t)}PQ+2b(t)\iint\chi_{b(t)}P\varepsilon(t)+\|\varepsilon(t)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+b^{2}(t)\iint\chi_{b(t)}^{2}P^{2}

\displaystyle=\iint Q^{2}+O\bigl(|b(t)|^{1-\gamma/2}\bigr)+\|\varepsilon(t)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\longrightarrow\iint Q^{2}

as
t\to+\infty
. Thus
\|u_{0}\|_{L^{2}}=\|Q\|_{L^{2}}
. Since

E_{0}=E(Q)=0
, the sharp Weinstein inequality, see (
C.1
),
implies that
u_{0}
belongs to the solitary-wave orbit, a contradiction.

Remark 10.6
.

More generally, if
E_{0}=0
for
u_{0}\in\mathcal{A}_{\alpha_{0}}
, then either the solution blows up or
u_{0}
belongs to
the solitary-wave orbit.

Appendix A
Weighted Inequalities

Lemma A.1

(Weighted Sobolev Lemma)
.

Let
\theta\in W^{1,1}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
be a positive function such that there exists
C=C_{\theta}>1
with
|\nabla\theta|\leq C\theta
. Suppose that
u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\cap H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\theta dx_{1}dx_{2}),
then

\iint u^{4}\theta dx_{1}dx_{2}\leq 3C^{2}\|u\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\iint\Big(u^{2}+u_{x_{1}}^{2}+u_{x_{2}}^{2})\theta dx_{1}dx_{2},

\iint|u|^{3}\theta dx_{1}dx_{2}\leq\sqrt{3}C\|u\|_{L^{2}}\iint\Big(u^{2}+u_{x_{1}}^{2}+u_{x_{2}}^{2})\theta dx_{1}dx_{2}.

Proof.

We first prove the estimate for smooth compactly supported functions and then pass to the general case by the usual truncation and approximation argument.
Set, by assumptions, the finite quantities

\mathcal{S}=\|u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})},\qquad A_{0}^{2}=\iint u^{2}\theta,\qquad A_{1}^{2}=\iint u_{x_{1}}^{2}\theta,\qquad A_{2}^{2}=\iint u_{x_{2}}^{2}\theta.

Since
\theta>0
and
|\partial_{x_{i}}\theta|\leq C\theta
, we have

\left|\partial_{x_{i}}(\sqrt{\theta})\right|=\frac{|\partial_{x_{i}}\theta|}{2\sqrt{\theta}}\leq\frac{C}{2}\sqrt{\theta}.

Define

F(x_{1},x_{2})=u^{2}(x_{1},x_{2})\sqrt{\theta(x_{1},x_{2})}.

Then

\|F\|_{L^{1}}=\iint|u|\,|u|\sqrt{\theta}\leq\|u\|_{L^{2}}\left(\iint u^{2}\theta\right)^{1/2}=\mathcal{S}A_{0}.

Moreover, for
i=1,2

\partial_{x_{i}}F=2uu_{x_{i}}\sqrt{\theta}+u^{2}\partial_{x_{i}}(\sqrt{\theta}),

so that

\|\partial_{x_{i}}F\|_{L^{1}}\leq 2\iint|u|\,|u_{x_{i}}|\sqrt{\theta}+\frac{C}{2}\iint u^{2}\sqrt{\theta}\leq 2\mathcal{S}A_{i}+\frac{C}{2}\mathcal{S}A_{0}.

We use the elementary anisotropic Sobolev inequality

\|F\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}\leq\|\partial_{x_{1}}F\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\|\partial_{x_{2}}F\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}.

Therefore,

\iint u^{4}\theta=\|F\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq\mathcal{S}^{2}\left(2A_{1}+\frac{C}{2}A_{0}\right)\left(2A_{2}+\frac{C}{2}A_{0}\right)\leq 3C^{2}\mathcal{S}^{2}(A_{0}^{2}+A_{1}^{2}+A_{2}^{2}).

For the cubic estimate, Hölder’s inequality gives

\iint|u|^{3}\theta\leq\left(\iint u^{2}\theta\right)^{1/2}\left(\iint u^{4}\theta\right)^{1/2}.

Using the quartic estimate, we obtain

\iint|u|^{3}\theta\leq A_{0}\sqrt{3}C\,\mathcal{S}(A_{0}^{2}+A_{1}^{2}+A_{2}^{2})^{1/2}\leq\sqrt{3}C\,\mathcal{S}(A_{0}^{2}+A_{1}^{2}+A_{2}^{2}),

which is the desired cubic estimate.

For general
u
, one applies the previous argument to standard smooth compactly supported approximations of
u
, or equivalently first to
\chi_{R}u
, where
\chi_{R}
is a smooth cut-off, and then lets
R\to\infty
.
∎

Lemma A.2
.

There exists
C_{B}>0
, depending on
B
, such that for all

u
for which the right-hand side is finite,

\displaystyle\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}u^{2}|\nabla u|^{2}\psi_{B}^{\prime}+\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}u^{4}\psi_{B}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq C_{B}\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{2}\left[\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(|D^{2}u|^{2}+u^{2})\psi_{B}^{\prime}+\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(|\nabla u|^{2}+u^{2})\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}\right].

(A.1)

Moreover,

\displaystyle\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|u|^{6}\psi_{B}^{\prime}\leq C_{B}\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{4}\left[\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(|D^{2}u|^{2}+u^{2})\psi_{B}^{\prime}+\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(|\nabla u|^{2}+u^{2})\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}\right].

(A.2)

Proof.

We prove the estimate first for
u\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
. The general case
follows by density in the weighted norm appearing on the right-hand side.
From (
5.24
) there exists
D_{B}>0
such that

|\psi_{B}^{\prime\prime}|^{2}\leq D_{B}\psi_{B}^{\prime}\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}.

(A.3)

Moreover,
\lim_{y_{1}\uparrow-\frac{B}{2}}\frac{|\psi_{B}^{\prime\prime}|^{2}}{\psi_{B}^{\prime}}=0.

Set

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}

\displaystyle=\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})},

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}

\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}u^{2}|\nabla u|^{2}\psi_{B}^{\prime},

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}

\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(|D^{2}u|^{2}+u^{2})\psi_{B}^{\prime},

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}

\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(|\nabla u|^{2}+u^{2})\phi_{i,B}^{\prime},

and

\mathcal{I}_{6}=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|u|^{6}\psi_{B}^{\prime}.

We first show that

\mathcal{I}_{6}\leq C\mathcal{S}^{2}\mathcal{I}+C\mathcal{S}^{4}\mathcal{E}.

(A.4)

Let

\mathcal{I}_{3}=u^{3}\sqrt{\psi_{B}^{\prime}}.

All identities involving
[\psi_{B}^{\prime}]^{-1/2}
are used only on the open set

\{\psi_{B}^{\prime}>0\}=\Omega_{B}
. The endpoint
y_{1}=-B/2
has measure zero, and

\mathcal{I}_{3}
has zero trace there because
\psi_{B}^{\prime}(-B/2)=0
. Thus no boundary term
is created by extending
\mathcal{I}_{3}
by zero to the region
y_{1}\geq-B/2
.

On
\Omega_{B}
,

\partial_{y_{2}}\mathcal{I}_{3}=3u^{2}u_{y_{2}}\sqrt{\psi_{B}^{\prime}},

while

\partial_{y_{1}}\mathcal{I}_{3}=3u^{2}u_{y_{1}}\sqrt{\psi_{B}^{\prime}}+\frac{1}{2}u^{3}\frac{\psi_{B}^{\prime\prime}}{\sqrt{\psi_{B}^{\prime}}}.

The second formula is never evaluated at points where
\psi_{B}^{\prime}=0
.
Using (
A.3
),

\displaystyle\iint|u|^{3}\frac{|\psi_{B}^{\prime\prime}|}{\sqrt{\psi_{B}^{\prime}}}1_{\{\psi_{B}^{\prime}>0\}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{B}\iint_{\Omega_{B}}|u|^{3}\sqrt{\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{B}\mathcal{S}\left(\iint_{\Omega_{B}}u^{4}\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq C_{B}\mathcal{S}^{2}\mathcal{E}^{1/2},

where in the last line we used Lemma
4.3
with weight
\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}.

Also, for
j=1,2
,

\iint|u|^{2}|u_{y_{j}}|\sqrt{\psi_{B}^{\prime}}\leq\mathcal{S}\left(\iint u^{2}u_{y_{j}}^{2}\psi_{B}^{\prime}\right)^{1/2}.

Therefore

\left\lVert\nabla\mathcal{I}_{3}\right\rVert_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq C_{B}\left(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{I}^{1/2}+\mathcal{S}^{2}\mathcal{E}^{1/2}\right).

The Sobolev inequality
\left\lVert\mathcal{I}_{3}\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq C\left\lVert\nabla\mathcal{I}_{3}\right\rVert_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}

gives

\mathcal{I}_{6}=\left\lVert\mathcal{I}_{3}\right\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq C_{B}\left(\mathcal{S}^{2}\mathcal{I}+\mathcal{S}^{4}\mathcal{E}\right),

which proves (
A.4
).

For
j=1,2
, integration by parts gives

\iint u^{2}u_{y_{j}}^{2}\psi_{B}^{\prime}=-\frac{1}{3}\iint u^{3}u_{y_{j}y_{j}}\psi_{B}^{\prime}-\frac{1}{3}\delta_{1j}\iint u^{3}u_{y_{1}}\psi_{B}^{\prime\prime}.

There is no boundary contribution at
y_{1}=-B/2
because

\psi_{B}^{\prime}(-B/2)=0
. Summing over
j=1,2
and using Cauchy–Schwarz,

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}

\displaystyle\leq C_{B}\mathcal{I}_{6}^{1/2}\mathcal{A}^{1/2}+C_{B}\left(\iint u^{2}u_{y_{1}}^{2}\psi_{B}^{\prime}\right)^{1/2}\left(\iint u^{4}\frac{|\psi_{B}^{\prime\prime}|^{2}}{\psi_{B}^{\prime}}1_{\{\psi_{B}^{\prime}>0\}}\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq C_{B}\mathcal{I}_{6}^{1/2}\mathcal{A}^{1/2}+C_{B}\mathcal{I}^{1/2}\left(\iint_{\Omega_{B}}u^{4}\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}\right)^{1/2}.

Using Lemma
4.3
with weight
\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}
and (
A.4
),

\mathcal{I}\leq C_{B}\left(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{I}^{1/2}+\mathcal{S}^{2}\mathcal{E}^{1/2}\right)\mathcal{A}^{1/2}+C\mathcal{S}\mathcal{I}^{1/2}\mathcal{E}^{1/2}.

Young’s inequality yields

\mathcal{I}\leq\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{I}+C_{B}\mathcal{S}^{2}\mathcal{A}+C_{B}\mathcal{S}^{2}\mathcal{E}.

Hence

\mathcal{I}\leq C_{B}\mathcal{S}^{2}(\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{E}).

(A.5)

Combining (
A.5
), (
5.20
) with Lemma
4.3
with weight
\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}
proves
(
A.1
). Finally, substituting
(
A.5
) into (
A.4
) gives

\mathcal{I}_{6}\leq C_{B}\mathcal{S}^{4}(\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{E}),

which proves (
A.2
).
∎

Appendix B
Coercivity Criteria and Virial Estimates

This appendix proves the linearized coercivity estimate of Lemma
3.4
and the virial estimate of Lemma
5.11
. We begin with a generalization of Weinstein’s coercivity criterion
[
57
]
.

B.1
Generalized Weinstein Criterion

Proposition B.1

(Generalized Weinstein Criterion)
.

Let
T
be a self-adjoint operator on a real Hilbert space

\mathcal{H}
, and assume that the negative spectral subspace

\mathcal{H}_{-}:=\mathbf{1}_{(-\infty,0)}(T)\mathcal{H}

has finite dimension
n
. Set

\mathfrak{G}_{T}:=\operatorname{sgn}(T),\qquad\mathcal{Q}(T):=\mathcal{D}(|T|^{1/2}),

and define the quadratic form associated with
T
by

\mathfrak{t}[u,v]:=\bigl(\mathfrak{G}_{T}|T|^{1/2}u,\,|T|^{1/2}v\bigr),\qquad u,v\in\mathcal{Q}(T).

We write
\mathfrak{t}[u]:=\mathfrak{t}[u,u]
.

Let
m\geq n
, and let

f_{1},\ldots,f_{m}\in\operatorname{Ran}(T)
. For each
i
, choose

F_{i}\in\mathcal{D}(T)
such that
TF_{i}=f_{i}
, and define

M_{ij}:=(F_{i},f_{j}),\qquad 1\leq i,j\leq m,

and

\Phi:\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathcal{H},\qquad\Phi\xi:=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\xi_{i}f_{i}.

Assume that there exists an
n
-dimensional subspace

\mathcal{Z}\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
such that

\xi^{T}M\xi\leq 0\qquad\text{for every }\xi\in\mathcal{Z},

and such that
\Phi|_{\mathcal{Z}}
is injective. Then

\mathfrak{t}[u]\geq 0

for every
u\in\mathcal{Q}(T)
satisfying

(u,f_{i})=0,\qquad i=1,\ldots,m.

If, moreover,
M
is negative definite on
\mathcal{Z}
, then there exists

\kappa>0
such that

\mathfrak{t}[u]\geq\kappa\bigl\||T|^{1/2}u\bigr\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}

for every such
u
.

If, in this strictly negative case,
0\in\rho(T)
, then there exists
\delta>0

such that

\mathfrak{t}[u]\geq\delta\|u\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}

for every such
u
.

Proof.

The case
n=0
is immediate, so assume that
n\geq 1
.

First,
M
is independent of the choices of the preimages
F_{i}
.
Indeed, if
F_{i}^{\prime}
is another preimage of
f_{i}
, then

F_{i}^{\prime}-F_{i}\in\ker T
, while

\operatorname{Ran}(T)\perp\ker T
; hence

(F_{i}^{\prime}-F_{i},f_{j})=0.

Moreover,

M_{ij}=(F_{i},TF_{j})=(TF_{i},F_{j})=M_{ji},

so
M
is symmetric.

Choose a basis
c^{(1)},\ldots,c^{(n)}
of
\mathcal{Z}
, and let

C\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
be the matrix with these vectors as
columns. For
k=1,\ldots,n
, define

G_{k}:=\sum_{i=1}^{m}C_{ik}F_{i},\qquad g_{k}:=\sum_{i=1}^{m}C_{ik}f_{i},\qquad TG_{k}=g_{k}.

The vectors
g_{1},\ldots,g_{n}
are linearly independent. Indeed, if

\sum_{k=1}^{n}\xi_{k}g_{k}=0
, then

\Phi(C\xi)=0.

Since
C\xi\in\mathcal{Z}
and
\Phi|_{\mathcal{Z}}
is injective,
C\xi=0
. Since the
columns of
C
are linearly independent, it follows that
\xi=0
.

Set
N:=C^{T}MC
. Since
\operatorname{Ran}C=\mathcal{Z}
, we have
N\leq 0
,
and

N_{kl}=\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}C_{ik}C_{jl}(F_{i},f_{j})=(G_{k},g_{l}),\qquad 1\leq k,l\leq n.

If
M
is negative definite on
\mathcal{Z}
, then
N
is negative
definite.

Set

\mathcal{H}_{0}:=\ker T,\qquad\mathcal{H}_{+}:=\mathbf{1}_{(0,\infty)}(T)\mathcal{H}.

Then

\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}_{-}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{0}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{+}.

Recall that
\mathfrak{G}_{T}=\operatorname{sgn}(T)
. Thus
T=\mathfrak{G}_{T}|T|
, where

\mathfrak{G}_{T}=-I
on
\mathcal{H}_{-}
,
\mathfrak{G}_{T}=0
on
\mathcal{H}_{0}
, and

\mathfrak{G}_{T}=I
on
\mathcal{H}_{+}
.

For
u\in\mathcal{Q}(T)=\mathcal{D}(|T|^{1/2})
, set

z:=|T|^{1/2}u,

and, for
k=1,\ldots,n
, set

h_{k}:=\mathfrak{G}_{T}|T|^{1/2}G_{k}.

Since

\operatorname{Ran}(|T|^{1/2})\subset(\ker T)^{\perp},

both
z
and
h_{k}
belong to

(\ker T)^{\perp}=\mathcal{H}_{-}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{+}.

By the definition of the quadratic form associated with
T
,

\mathfrak{t}[u]=(\mathfrak{G}_{T}z,z).

Moreover, since
G_{k}\in\mathcal{D}(T)
and
TG_{k}=g_{k}
,

\displaystyle(u,g_{k})

\displaystyle=(u,TG_{k})

\displaystyle=\bigl(|T|^{1/2}u,\,\mathfrak{G}_{T}|T|^{1/2}G_{k}\bigr)

\displaystyle=(z,h_{k}).

Finally,

\displaystyle(\mathfrak{G}_{T}h_{k},h_{l})

\displaystyle=\bigl(|T|^{1/2}G_{k},\,\mathfrak{G}_{T}|T|^{1/2}G_{l}\bigr)

\displaystyle=(G_{k},TG_{l})=N_{kl}.

We claim that
h_{1},\ldots,h_{n}
are linearly independent. Suppose
that
\sum_{k=1}^{n}\xi_{k}h_{k}=0
, and set

G_{\xi}:=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\xi_{k}G_{k}.

Then
\mathfrak{G}_{T}|T|^{1/2}G_{\xi}=0
. Since

|T|^{1/2}G_{\xi}\in(\ker T)^{\perp}
and
\mathfrak{G}_{T}
is invertible on

(\ker T)^{\perp}
, it follows that

|T|^{1/2}G_{\xi}=0.

Thus
G_{\xi}\in\ker T
, and hence

\sum_{k=1}^{n}\xi_{k}g_{k}=TG_{\xi}=0.

The linear independence of
g_{1},\ldots,g_{n}
gives
\xi=0
.

Write

h_{k}=a_{k}+b_{k},\qquad a_{k}:=P_{-}h_{k}\in\mathcal{H}_{-},\qquad b_{k}:=P_{+}h_{k}\in\mathcal{H}_{+},

and define
A:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathcal{H}_{-}
and

B:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathcal{H}_{+}
by

A\xi:=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\xi_{k}a_{k},\qquad B\xi:=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\xi_{k}b_{k}.

For every
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,

\xi^{T}N\xi=\left(\mathfrak{G}_{T}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\xi_{k}h_{k},\sum_{l=1}^{n}\xi_{l}h_{l}\right)=-\|A\xi\|^{2}+\|B\xi\|^{2}\leq 0.

Consequently,

\|B\xi\|\leq\|A\xi\|.

If
A\xi=0
, then
B\xi=0
, so

\sum_{k=1}^{n}\xi_{k}h_{k}=0
. The linear independence of the
h_{k}

then gives
\xi=0
. Thus
A
is injective. Since

\dim\mathbb{R}^{n}=\dim\mathcal{H}_{-}=n,

the map
A
is an isomorphism from
\mathbb{R}^{n}
onto

\mathcal{H}_{-}
.

Define

K:=BA^{-1}:\mathcal{H}_{-}\to\mathcal{H}_{+}.

For
x=A\xi
, the preceding inequality gives

\|Kx\|=\|B\xi\|\leq\|A\xi\|=\|x\|,

and therefore
\|K\|\leq 1
. If
N
is negative definite, then the
inequality is strict for every
x\neq 0
. Since
\mathcal{H}_{-}
is
finite-dimensional, compactness of its unit sphere yields

\theta:=\|K\|<1.

Now let
u\in\mathcal{Q}(T)
satisfy

(u,f_{i})=0
for
i=1,\ldots,m
. Then
(u,g_{k})=0
for every

k
. Writing
z=z_{-}+z_{+}
, where
z_{\pm}:=P_{\pm}z
, we obtain

0=(z,h_{k})=(z_{-},a_{k})+(z_{+},b_{k}),\qquad k=1,\ldots,n.

Equivalently,

A^{*}z_{-}+B^{*}z_{+}=0.

Since
A^{*}
is invertible,

z_{-}=-(A^{*})^{-1}B^{*}z_{+}=-K^{*}z_{+}.

It follows that

\mathfrak{t}[u]=(\mathfrak{G}_{T}z,z)=\|z_{+}\|^{2}-\|z_{-}\|^{2}=\|z_{+}\|^{2}-\|K^{*}z_{+}\|^{2}\geq 0,

because
\|K^{*}\|=\|K\|\leq 1
.

Suppose now that
M
is negative definite on
\mathcal{Z}
. Then
N
is negative definite
and
\theta=\|K\|<1
. Hence

\mathfrak{t}[u]\geq(1-\theta^{2})\|z_{+}\|^{2},\qquad\|z\|^{2}=\|z_{-}\|^{2}+\|z_{+}\|^{2}\leq(1+\theta^{2})\|z_{+}\|^{2}.

Therefore, with

\kappa:=\frac{1-\theta^{2}}{1+\theta^{2}}>0,

we obtain

\mathfrak{t}[u]\geq\kappa\|z\|^{2}=\kappa\bigl\||T|^{1/2}u\bigr\|^{2},

Finally, if
0\in\rho(T)
, there exists
\eta>0
such that

|T|\geq\eta I
. Consequently,

\mathfrak{t}[u]\geq\kappa\eta\|u\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2},

and the conclusion follows with
\delta:=\kappa\eta
.
∎

Remark B.2
.

Suppose that

\operatorname{ind}_{-}(M)=n,

where
\operatorname{ind}_{-}(M)
denotes the number of negative
eigenvalues of
M
, counted with multiplicity. Let
\mathcal{Z}
be the
negative spectral subspace of
M
. Then
\dim\mathcal{Z}=n
and
M
is
negative definite on
\mathcal{Z}
. Moreover,
\Phi|_{\mathcal{Z}}
is automatically
injective. Indeed, if
\xi\in\mathcal{Z}
and
\Phi\xi=0
, then, setting

F_{\xi}:=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\xi_{i}F_{i}
, we have

\xi^{T}M\xi=(F_{\xi},\Phi\xi)=0.

The negative definiteness of
M
on
\mathcal{Z}
therefore implies

\xi=0
.

Remark B.3
.

The geometric hypothesis on
\mathcal{Z}
has an equivalent formulation in
terms of the inertia of
M
. First,

\ker\Phi\subset\ker M,

because, for
\xi\in\ker\Phi
,

(M\xi)_{i}=(F_{i},\Phi\xi)=0,\qquad i=1,\ldots,m.

Consequently, the existence of an
n
-dimensional subspace

\mathcal{Z}\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
such that

\xi^{T}M\xi\leq 0\quad\text{for every }\xi\in\mathcal{Z},\qquad\mathcal{Z}\cap\ker\Phi=\{0\},

is equivalent to

\operatorname{ind}_{-}(M)+\dim\ker M-\dim\ker\Phi\geq n.

Indeed, let

p:=\operatorname{ind}_{-}(M),\qquad z:=\dim\ker M,\qquad r:=\dim\ker\Phi.

If such a subspace
\mathcal{Z}
exists, then

\mathcal{Z}\oplus\ker\Phi
is
M
-non-positive, because

\ker\Phi\subset\ker M
. Since the maximal dimension of an

M
-non-positive subspace is
p+z
, we obtain

n+r\leq p+z.

Conversely, suppose that
p+z-r\geq n
. Let
V_{-}
be the negative
spectral subspace of
M
, and choose a subspace
Z
such that

\ker M=\ker\Phi\oplus Z.

Then
V_{-}\oplus Z
is
M
-non-positive, intersects

\ker\Phi
trivially, and has dimension
p+z-r
. Hence it contains
an
n
-dimensional subspace
\mathcal{Z}
with the required properties.

Remark B.4
.

Let

T=-\kappa_{1}\partial_{x_{1}}^{2}-\kappa_{2}\partial_{x_{2}}^{2}+V

on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, where
\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}>0
are constants and

V\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R})
. The constant-coefficient
operator

T_{0}:=-\kappa_{1}\partial_{x_{1}}^{2}-\kappa_{2}\partial_{x_{2}}^{2}

is self-adjoint on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
with

\mathcal{D}(T_{0})=H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}).

Since multiplication by
V
is a bounded self-adjoint operator on

L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, the bounded perturbation theorem gives

\mathcal{D}(T)=H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}).

The closed quadratic form associated with
T
is

\mathfrak{l}[u,v]:=\kappa_{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}u_{x_{1}}v_{x_{1}}\,dx+\kappa_{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}u_{x_{2}}v_{x_{2}}\,dx+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}Vuv\,dx,\qquad\mathcal{D}(\mathfrak{l})=H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}).

Indeed, if
\tau_{0}>\|V\|_{L^{\infty}}+1
, then

\displaystyle\mathfrak{l}[u]+\tau_{0}\|u\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\kappa_{1}\|u_{x_{1}}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\kappa_{2}\|u_{x_{2}}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(V+\tau_{0})u^{2}\,dx

\displaystyle\asymp\|u\|_{H^{1}}^{2}.

The representation theorem for closed semibounded forms therefore
yields

\mathcal{D}\bigl((T+\tau_{0}I)^{1/2}\bigr)=H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}).

Since
T
is bounded from below, the spectral weights

\lambda+\tau_{0}
and
1+|\lambda|
are comparable on
\sigma(T)
.
Consequently,

\mathcal{D}(|T|^{1/2})=\mathcal{D}\bigl((T+\tau_{0}I)^{1/2}\bigr)=H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}).

If
\mathfrak{G}_{T}=\operatorname{sgn}(T)
, the spectral representation of the
quadratic form gives

\displaystyle\mathfrak{t}[u]

\displaystyle=\bigl(\mathfrak{G}_{T}|T|^{1/2}u,|T|^{1/2}u\bigr)

\displaystyle=\kappa_{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|u_{x_{1}}|^{2}\,dx+\kappa_{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|u_{x_{2}}|^{2}\,dx+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}Vu^{2}\,dx,\qquad u\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}).

For
u\in H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, this also equals the genuine

L^{2}
-inner product
(Tu,u)
. For a general

u\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, it is understood only in the
quadratic-form sense.

Remark B.5
.

For
m=n=1
, the injectivity of
\Phi
is equivalent to

f_{1}\neq 0
, while the matrix condition reduces to

(F_{1},f_{1})\leq 0,\qquad TF_{1}=f_{1}.

Thus, if the negative spectral subspace of
T
has dimension one,
the proposition gives

\mathfrak{t}[u]\geq 0\qquad\text{whenever }(u,f_{1})=0,\qquad u\in\mathcal{Q}(T).

In particular, for

T=-\Delta+1-3Q^{2},\qquad f_{1}=Q,

using

\dim\mathcal{H}_{-}(T)=1,\qquad-\frac{1}{2}T(\Lambda Q)=Q,\qquad(\Lambda Q,Q)=0,

and
Q\neq 0
, one recovers Lemma E.1 in
[
57
]
.

While we give the explicit linear combination of orthogonal conditions for coercivity, sometimes identifying such a linear combination explicitly can be difficult. Motivated by this, we state the following lemma which shows the existence of such a linear combination without writing it explicitly.

Lemma B.6
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a real Hilbert space. Let
T:\mathcal{D}(T)\rightarrow\mathcal{H}
be a self-adjoint, linear operator with
0\in\rho(T)
. Consider
f_{1},f_{2},\ldots,f_{m}\in\mathcal{H}
, with
m\geq 1,
to be
m
linearly independent elements in
\mathcal{H}
and denote
V=\text{span}\{f_{1},\ldots,f_{m}\}.
We write
S
to be the
m\times m
matrix with its elements
S_{i,j}=(T^{-1}f_{i},f_{j})
for
1\leq i,j\leq m
. If the matrix
S
has at least one negative eigenvalue, then there exists
g\in V
, such that

(T^{-1}g,g)<0.

Proof.

Let
\Gamma:\mathbb{R}^{m}\rightarrow V
defined by
\Gamma(z_{1},\ldots,z_{m})=\sum_{i=1}^{m}z_{i}f_{i}\in V.
Since
\text{dim}V=m,

\Gamma
is bijective. If
w=\sum_{i=1}^{m}z_{i}f_{i},

(T^{-1}w,w)=\vec{z}^{T}S\vec{z},
where
\vec{z}^{T}=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{m})\in\mathbb{R}^{m}.

The matrix
S
is symmetric since
T^{-1}
is self-adjoint. Since
S
has at least one negative eigenvalue, from Sylvester’s law of inertia, the maximum dimension on which the quadratic form
Q:\mathbb{R}^{m}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
,
Q(\vec{z})=\vec{z}^{T}S\vec{z}
is negative definite is at least one. Therefore, there exists
\vec{c}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
, such that
Q(\vec{c})<0
.

Taking such
\vec{c}=(c_{1},\cdots,c_{m})^{T}
, we obtain that

(T^{-1}g,g)<0

with
g=\sum_{i=1}^{m}c_{i}f_{i}.

∎

From now on, let
\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}>0
and

V\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R})
. For

T=-\kappa_{1}\partial_{x_{1}}^{2}-\kappa_{2}\partial_{x_{2}}^{2}+V,

and
u,v\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, we use the quadratic-form notation

(Tu,v):=\kappa_{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}u_{x_{1}}v_{x_{1}}+\kappa_{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}u_{x_{2}}v_{x_{2}}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}Vuv.

In particular,
(Tu,u)
denotes the quadratic form of
T
and does
not require
u\in\mathcal{D}(T)=H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
.

B.2
Coercivity of the Linearized Operator

Lemma B.7
.

Define the subspace
\mathcal{X}\subset H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
by

\mathcal{X}=\left\{u\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}):(u,Q)=(u,\varphi(x_{1})Q_{x_{2}})=(u,\varphi(x_{1})\Lambda Q)=(u,\varphi(x_{1})Q_{x_{1}})=0\right\}.

Let
L\coloneqq-\Delta+1-3Q^{2}
and define its associated quadratic form

(Lu,u):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left(|\nabla u|^{2}+(1-3Q^{2})u^{2}\right)\,dx,\qquad u\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}).

a)

Coercivity on
\mathcal{X}
:
There exists
\delta>0
such that for all
u\in\mathcal{X}
,

(Lu,u)\geq\delta\|u\|_{H^{1}}^{2}.

b)

Global Coercivity:
There exists
\tilde{\delta}>0
such that for any
u\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
,

(Lu,u)\geq\tilde{\delta}\|u\|_{H^{1}}^{2}-\frac{1}{\tilde{\delta}}\left((u,Q)^{2}+(u,\varphi(x_{1})Q_{x_{2}})^{2}+(u,\varphi(x_{1})\Lambda Q)^{2}+(u,\varphi(x_{1})Q_{x_{1}})^{2}\right).

Proof.

a)

Recall that the negative spectral subspace of
L
has dimension one and that

\ker L=\operatorname{span}\{Q_{x_{1}},Q_{x_{2}}\}.

Set

A:=\bigl(\varphi(x_{1})\Lambda Q,Q_{x_{1}}\bigr),\qquad B:=\bigl(\varphi(x_{1})Q_{x_{1}},Q_{x_{1}}\bigr),

and define

\Xi:=B\varphi(x_{1})\Lambda Q-A\varphi(x_{1})Q_{x_{1}}.

By construction,

(\Xi,Q_{x_{1}})=0,

while
(\Xi,Q_{x_{2}})=0
by parity. Since
0
is an
isolated eigenvalue of
L
,
\operatorname{Ran}(L)
is closed. Hence

\Xi\in(\ker L)^{\perp}=\operatorname{Ran}(L).

Choose
R_{\Xi}\in\mathcal{D}(L)
such that
LR_{\Xi}=\Xi
.

We apply Proposition
B.1
with

f_{1}:=Q,\qquad f_{2}:=\Xi.

Since
L(\Lambda Q)=-2Q
, we may choose

F_{1}:=-\frac{1}{2}\Lambda Q,\qquad F_{2}:=R_{\Xi}.

The corresponding constraint matrix is

\mathfrak{M}:=\begin{pmatrix}(F_{1},Q)&(F_{1},\Xi)\\
(F_{2},Q)&(F_{2},\Xi)\end{pmatrix}.

Using
(Q,\Lambda Q)=0
and the self-adjointness of
L
, we obtain

\mathfrak{M}=\begin{pmatrix}0&-\frac{1}{2}(\Lambda Q,\Xi)\\[2.84526pt]
-\frac{1}{2}(\Lambda Q,\Xi)&(R_{\Xi},\Xi)\end{pmatrix}.

Moreover,

\displaystyle(\Lambda Q,\Xi)

\displaystyle=\bigl(\varphi Q_{x_{1}},Q_{x_{1}}\bigr)\bigl(\varphi\Lambda Q,\Lambda Q\bigr)-\bigl(\varphi\Lambda Q,Q_{x_{1}}\bigr)\bigl(\varphi Q_{x_{1}},\Lambda Q\bigr)=\det M^{*},

where
M^{*}
is defined in (
3.4
). Recall that
\det M^{*}\neq 0.

Thus

\det\mathfrak{M}=-\frac{1}{4}(\det M^{*})^{2}<0.

As
\mathfrak{M}
is symmetric, it follows that

\operatorname{ind}_{-}(\mathfrak{M})=1,

which equals the dimension of the negative spectral subspace of
L
.
By Proposition
B.1
and
Remark
B.2
, there exists
\kappa>0
such that

(Lu,u)\geq\kappa\bigl\||L|^{1/2}u\bigr\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

whenever
(u,Q)=(u,\Xi)=0
. In particular, this estimate holds for
every
u\in\mathcal{X}
.

For
u\in\mathcal{X}
, write

u=v+aQ_{x_{1}}+bQ_{x_{2}},\qquad v\perp\ker L.

By parity and the defining orthogonality conditions,

a=-\frac{(v,\varphi Q_{x_{1}})}{(Q_{x_{1}},\varphi Q_{x_{1}})},\qquad b=-\frac{(v,\varphi Q_{x_{2}})}{(Q_{x_{2}},\varphi Q_{x_{2}})}.

Since
\varphi>0
, the denominators are strictly positive, and hence

|a|+|b|\lesssim\|v\|_{L^{2}}.

Since
0
is isolated in
\sigma(L)
, there exists
\sigma_{0}>0
such
that

\bigl\||L|^{1/2}v\bigr\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\geq\sigma_{0}\|v\|_{L^{2}}^{2},\qquad v\perp\ker L.

Moreover,

\displaystyle\|v\|_{H^{1}}^{2}

\displaystyle=(Lv,v)+3\int Q^{2}v^{2}\leq\bigl\||L|^{1/2}v\bigr\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+3\|Q\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}\|v\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\lesssim\bigl\||L|^{1/2}v\bigr\|_{L^{2}}^{2}.

Since
|L|^{1/2}
vanishes on
\ker L
,

\bigl\||L|^{1/2}u\bigr\|_{L^{2}}^{2}=\bigl\||L|^{1/2}v\bigr\|_{L^{2}}^{2}.

Consequently,

\|u\|_{H^{1}}^{2}\lesssim\|v\|_{H^{1}}^{2}+a^{2}+b^{2}\lesssim\bigl\||L|^{1/2}u\bigr\|_{L^{2}}^{2}.

It follows that

(Lu,u)\geq\kappa\bigl\||L|^{1/2}u\bigr\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\geq\delta\|u\|_{H^{1}}^{2}.

b)

Let
u\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
. We construct a vector
v\in\mathcal{X}
by subtracting appropriate components from
u
. Define

v=u+aQ+b\Lambda Q+cQ_{x_{1}}+dQ_{x_{2}},

where the scalar coefficients
a,b,c,d
are chosen such that
v
satisfies the four orthogonality conditions defining
\mathcal{X}
.
The condition
(v,Q)=0
implies
a=-(u,Q)/\|Q\|_{L^{2}}^{2}
. Similarly,
(v,\varphi(x_{1})Q_{x_{2}})=0
determines
d
. The coefficients
b
and
c
are determined by the non-singular
2\times 2
system involving
M^{*}
arising from the conditions
(v,\varphi(x_{1})\Lambda Q)=0
and
(v,\varphi(x_{1})Q_{x_{1}})=0
.
Crucially, the vector
\vec{p}=(a,b,c,d)
depends linearly on the projections of
u
onto the finite set of functions
\{Q,\varphi(x_{1})\Lambda Q,\varphi(x_{1})Q_{x_{1}},\varphi(x_{1})Q_{x_{2}}\}
. Thus, there exists a constant
C>0
such that

|a|^{2}+|b|^{2}+|c|^{2}+|d|^{2}\leq C\left((u,Q)^{2}+(u,\varphi(x_{1})\Lambda Q)^{2}+(u,\varphi(x_{1})Q_{x_{1}})^{2}+(u,\varphi(x_{1})Q_{x_{2}})^{2}\right).

(B.1)

Let
w=aQ+b\Lambda Q+cQ_{x_{1}}+dQ_{x_{2}}
, so that
u=v-w
. Since
v\in\mathcal{X}
, part a) implies

(Lv,v)\geq\delta\|v\|_{H^{1}}^{2}.

(B.2)

We expand the quadratic form for
u
:

(Lu,u)=(L(v-w),v-w)=(Lv,v)-2(Lv,w)+(Lw,w).

Since
w
lies in a finite-dimensional subspace spanned by smooth functions, we have estimates
\|w\|_{H^{1}}^{2}\lesssim|\vec{p}|^{2}
and
|(Lw,w)|\lesssim\|w\|_{H^{1}}^{2}
. Using the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality for the cross term:

|2(Lv,w)|\leq 2\|Lv\|_{H^{-1}}\|w\|_{H^{1}}\lesssim\|v\|_{H^{1}}\|w\|_{H^{1}}\leq\frac{\delta}{2}\|v\|_{H^{1}}^{2}+C_{\delta}\|w\|_{H^{1}}^{2}.

Substituting these into the expansion and using (
B.2
):

(Lu,u)\geq\delta\|v\|_{H^{1}}^{2}-\frac{\delta}{2}\|v\|_{H^{1}}^{2}-C^{\prime}\|w\|_{H^{1}}^{2}=\frac{\delta}{2}\|v\|_{H^{1}}^{2}-C^{\prime}\|w\|_{H^{1}}^{2}.

Finally, using the triangle inequality
\|u\|_{H^{1}}\leq\|v\|_{H^{1}}+\|w\|_{H^{1}}
, we have
\|u\|_{H^{1}}^{2}\leq 2\|v\|_{H^{1}}^{2}+2\|w\|_{H^{1}}^{2}
, which implies
\|v\|_{H^{1}}^{2}\geq\frac{1}{2}\|u\|_{H^{1}}^{2}-\|w\|_{H^{1}}^{2}
.
Therefore,

(Lu,u)\geq\frac{\delta}{4}\|u\|_{H^{1}}^{2}-C^{\prime\prime}\|w\|_{H^{1}}^{2}.

Combining this with (
B.1
) and renaming constants yields the desired estimate.
∎

B.3
Coercivity of the Virial Operator

We now proceed with the proof of Lemma
5.11
, using
y_{1},y_{2}

for the spatial variables to preserve the notation of the aforementioned lemma. First, we prove that the Virial operator is coercive.

Lemma B.8
.

Recall that
\alpha_{1}=1.01.
There exists a constant
\mu>0
such that, for all
v\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),

\displaystyle\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\Bigg[3v_{y_{1}}^{2}+v_{y_{2}}^{2}+\Bigg(1-\frac{1}{4\alpha_{1}^{2}}-3Q^{2}+6\alpha_{1}\left(1+e^{-y_{1}/\alpha_{1}}\right)QQ_{y_{1}}\Bigg)v^{2}\Bigg]\,dy_{1}dy_{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\geq\mu\|v\|_{H^{1}}^{2}-\frac{1}{\mu}\Bigg[\left(v,\frac{Q}{\sqrt{\varphi_{y_{1}}}}\right)^{2}+\left(v,\frac{\varphi Q_{y_{1}}}{\sqrt{\varphi_{y_{1}}}}\right)^{2}+\left(v,\frac{\varphi\Lambda Q}{\sqrt{\varphi_{y_{1}}}}\right)^{2}+\left(v,\frac{\varphi Q_{y_{2}}}{\sqrt{\varphi_{y_{1}}}}\right)^{2}\Bigg].

Proof.

We first establish this result for functions
v\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
satisfying the orthogonality conditions:

\left(v,\frac{1}{\sqrt{\varphi^{\prime}}}Q\right)=\left(v,\frac{\varphi}{\sqrt{\varphi^{\prime}}}Q_{y_{1}}\right)=\left(v,\frac{\varphi}{\sqrt{\varphi^{\prime}}}\Lambda Q\right)=\left(v,\frac{\varphi}{\sqrt{\varphi^{\prime}}}Q_{y_{2}}\right)=0.

Define the weight function
\tilde{\varphi}(y_{1})=\frac{\varphi(y_{1})}{2\sqrt{\alpha_{1}\varphi^{\prime}(y_{1})}}=\cosh\left(\frac{y_{1}}{2\alpha_{1}}\right)
and the functions

f_{1}=e^{-\frac{y_{1}}{2\alpha_{1}}}Q,\quad f_{2}=\tilde{\varphi}Q_{y_{1}},\quad f_{3}=\tilde{\varphi}\Lambda Q,\quad\text{and}\quad f_{o}=\tilde{\varphi}Q_{y_{2}}.

Theorem
E.2
shows that the operator

\mathcal{L}=-3\partial_{y_{1}}^{2}-\partial_{y_{2}}^{2}+\left(1-\frac{1}{4\alpha_{1}^{2}}\right)-3Q^{2}+6\alpha_{1}(1+e^{-\frac{y_{1}}{\alpha_{1}}})QQ_{y_{1}}

possesses exactly two negative eigenvalues, counted with multiplicity, and
0\in\rho(\mathcal{L})
. Consequently,
\operatorname{Ran}\mathcal{L}=L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}).

Suppose there exist constants
c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}\in\mathbb{R}
such that
g=c_{1}f_{1}+c_{2}f_{2}+c_{3}f_{3}=0.
Taking the inner product with

\sqrt{\varphi^{\prime}}\varphi^{-1}Q
, and using

(Q,Q_{y_{1}})=(Q,\Lambda Q)=0,

we obtain

0=c_{1}(\varphi^{-1}Q,Q).

Hence
c_{1}=0
. Dividing the remaining
identity by the strictly positive function
\widetilde{\varphi}
, we
find

c_{2}Q_{y_{1}}+c_{3}\Lambda Q=0.

Since
Q_{y_{1}}
is odd and
\Lambda Q
is even in
y_{1}
, it
follows that
c_{2}=c_{3}=0
.

Consider the
3\times 3
matrix
A
with entries
a_{ij}=(\mathcal{L}^{-1}f_{i},f_{j})
for
1\leq i,j\leq 3.
Theorem
E.2
yields that
\det A<0.
Since the odd-dimensional matrix
A
is real and symmetric with negative determinant, it has at least one negative eigenvalue . Therefore, Lemma
B.6
ensures the existence of
f_{e}\in\text{span}\{f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}\}
such that
(\mathcal{L}^{-1}f_{e},f_{e})<0.

Since
f_{e}
is even in
y_{2}
and
f_{o}
is odd in
y_{2}
, and the operator
\mathcal{L}
preserves parity in
y_{2}
, we obtain
(\mathcal{L}^{-1}f_{e},f_{o})=0.
Theorem
E.2
verifies that
(\mathcal{L}^{-1}f_{o},f_{o})<0.
Thus, the matrix

\displaystyle M=\begin{bmatrix}(\mathcal{L}^{-1}f_{e},f_{e})&(\mathcal{L}^{-1}f_{e},f_{o})\\
(\mathcal{L}^{-1}f_{e},f_{o})&(\mathcal{L}^{-1}f_{o},f_{o})\end{bmatrix}

(B.3)

is a diagonal matrix with negative real numbers on the diagonal; hence, it is negative definite. Applying Proposition
B.1
and Remark
B.2
to
\mathcal{L}
and
v\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
satisfying
(v,f_{e})=(v,f_{o})=0
, we deduce that there exists
\mu>0
such that
(\mathcal{L}v,v)\geq\mu\|v\|_{L^{2}}^{2}.
Therefore, there exists
\mu_{1}>0
such that

(\mathcal{L}u,u)\geq\mu_{1}\|u\|_{H^{1}}^{2}\quad\text{if}\quad(u,f_{1})=(u,f_{2})=(u,f_{3})=(u,f_{o})=0.

Since
f_{1},f_{2},f_{3},f_{o}
are linearly independent, choose

g_{1},g_{2},g_{3},g_{o}\in\operatorname{span}\{f_{1},f_{2},f_{3},f_{o}\}
such that

(g_{j},f_{k})=\delta_{jk}
. For
u\in H^{1}
, set

w=\sum_{j\in\{1,2,3,o\}}(u,f_{j})g_{j},\qquad u_{\perp}=u-w.

Then
(u_{\perp},f_{j})=0
for every
j
, and hence, by constrained
coercivity and continuity of the quadratic form,

\displaystyle(\mathcal{L}u,u)

\displaystyle\geq\mu_{1}\|u_{\perp}\|_{H^{1}}^{2}-C\|u_{\perp}\|_{H^{1}}\|w\|_{H^{1}}-C\|w\|_{H^{1}}^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\mu_{1}}{4}\|u\|_{H^{1}}^{2}-C\sum_{j\in\{1,2,3,o\}}(u,f_{j})^{2}.

After renaming the constant, this is the desired global coercivity
estimate.
∎

Remark B.9

(Parity of the negative directions)
.

We prove analytically that the operator
\mathcal{L}
has at least two negative eigenvalues. Since the potential is even in
y_{2}
,
\mathcal{L}
commutes with the reflection

\mathcal{P}u(y_{1},y_{2})=u(y_{1},-y_{2})
and

L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})=\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{even}}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{odd}},

where

\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{even}}=\{u:u(y_{1},-y_{2})=u(y_{1},y_{2})\},\qquad\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{odd}}=\{u:u(y_{1},-y_{2})=-u(y_{1},y_{2})\},

is an orthogonal reducing decomposition.

We first show analytically that each parity sector contains a negative direction. Set

w(y_{1}):=\varphi^{\prime}(y_{1})=\frac{1}{\alpha_{1}}e^{y_{1}/\alpha_{1}},\qquad\rho_{\varphi}(y_{1}):=\sqrt{w(y_{1})}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\alpha_{1}}}e^{y_{1}/(2\alpha_{1})}.

For a sufficiently decaying real-valued function
f
, put

V=1-3Q^{2}
and
w=\varphi^{\prime}=\rho_{\varphi}^{2}
. Integration by parts gives

\displaystyle-2\bigl((Lf)_{y_{1}},\varphi f\bigr)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\Bigl(3wf_{y_{1}}^{2}+wf_{y_{2}}^{2}+\bigl(wV-w^{\prime\prime}-\varphi V_{y_{1}}\bigr)f^{2}\Bigr).

(B.4)

Since

\frac{\rho_{\varphi}^{\prime}}{\rho_{\varphi}}=\frac{1}{2\alpha_{1}},\qquad\frac{w^{\prime\prime}}{w}=\frac{1}{\alpha_{1}^{2}},\qquad\frac{\varphi}{w}=\alpha_{1}\bigl(1+e^{-y_{1}/\alpha_{1}}\bigr),\qquad V_{y_{1}}=-6QQ_{y_{1}},

the right-hand side of (
B.4
) is precisely

(\mathcal{L}(\rho_{\varphi}f),\rho_{\varphi}f)
. Hence

(\mathcal{L}(\rho_{\varphi}f),\rho_{\varphi}f)=-2\bigl((Lf)_{y_{1}},\varphi f\bigr).

Because
LQ=-2Q^{3}
, we obtain

\displaystyle(\mathcal{L}(\rho_{\varphi}Q),\rho_{\varphi}Q)

\displaystyle=12\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varphi Q^{3}Q_{y_{1}}=3\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varphi(Q^{4})_{y_{1}}=-3\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\rho_{\varphi}^{2}Q^{4}<0.

Since
\rho_{\varphi}Q\in\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{even}}
, it follows that

\operatorname{ind}_{-}\!\left(\mathcal{L}\big|_{\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{even}}}\right)\geq 1.

Next, differentiating the equation for
Q
yields
LQ_{y_{2}}=0
, and therefore, for

z_{o}=\rho_{\varphi}Q_{y_{2}}\in\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{odd}}
,

(\mathcal{L}z_{o},z_{o})=0.

Nevertheless,
\mathcal{L}z_{o}\neq 0
. Indeed,

\rho_{\varphi}^{-1}\mathcal{L}(\rho_{\varphi}Q_{y_{2}})=-2Q_{y_{1}y_{1}y_{2}}-\frac{3}{\alpha_{1}}Q_{y_{1}y_{2}}-\frac{1}{\alpha_{1}^{2}}Q_{y_{2}}+6\alpha_{1}\bigl(1+e^{-y_{1}/\alpha_{1}}\bigr)QQ_{y_{1}}Q_{y_{2}}.

(B.5)

If the left-hand side vanished identically, writing
Q(y_{1},y_{2})=q(r)
and restricting
(
B.5
) to
y_{1}=0
,
y_{2}=r>0
, would give

2\left(\frac{q^{\prime}}{r}\right)^{\prime}+\frac{1}{\alpha_{1}^{2}}q^{\prime}=0.

Thus
q^{\prime}(r)/r=Ce^{-r^{2}/(4\alpha_{1}^{2})}
, and the decay of
q
would force

q(r)=Ae^{-r^{2}/(4\alpha_{1}^{2})}
, which is incompatible with

-q^{\prime\prime}-r^{-1}q^{\prime}+q-q^{3}=0
. Hence
G_{o}:=\mathcal{L}z_{o}\neq 0
. Since
G_{o}
is odd in

y_{2}
, for all sufficiently small
\tau>0
,

\displaystyle(\mathcal{L}(z_{o}-\tau G_{o}),z_{o}-\tau G_{o})

\displaystyle=-2\tau\|G_{o}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\tau^{2}(\mathcal{L}G_{o},G_{o})<0.

Consequently,

\operatorname{ind}_{-}\!\left(\mathcal{L}\big|_{\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{odd}}}\right)\geq 1,\qquad\operatorname{ind}_{-}(\mathcal{L})=\operatorname{ind}_{-}\!\left(\mathcal{L}\big|_{\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{even}}}\right)+\operatorname{ind}_{-}\!\left(\mathcal{L}\big|_{\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{odd}}}\right)\geq 2.

The exponential decay of
Q
and
\alpha_{1}>1/2
also imply

\sigma_{\mathrm{ess}}(\mathcal{L})=\left[1-\frac{1}{4\alpha_{1}^{2}},\infty\right]\subset(0,\infty),

so these negative directions produce discrete negative eigenvalues.

Finally, the lowest eigenvalue
\lambda_{1}(\mathcal{L})
is simple and has a strictly positive eigenfunction

e_{1}
. Since
\mathcal{L}
commutes with
\mathcal{P}
, simplicity and positivity imply

\mathcal{P}e_{1}=e_{1}
, hence
e_{1}\in\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{even}}
. If, in addition, one knows that

\operatorname{ind}_{-}(\mathcal{L})=2
, with multiplicities counted, then the preceding estimates force

\operatorname{ind}_{-}\!\left(\mathcal{L}\big|_{\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{even}}}\right)=\operatorname{ind}_{-}\!\left(\mathcal{L}\big|_{\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{odd}}}\right)=1.

Therefore the second negative eigenvalue satisfies
\lambda_{1}(\mathcal{L})<\lambda_{2}(\mathcal{L})<0
, it is simple, and
its eigenfunction
e_{2}
is odd in
y_{2}
:

e_{2}(y_{1},-y_{2})=-e_{2}(y_{1},y_{2}).

Remark B.10
.

The choice
\alpha_{1}=1.01
is supported by the interval certificates in
Appendix
E
. For comparison, an exploratory computation
with
\alpha_{1}=1.1
gives the following numerical approximation to the
inverse constraint matrix:

A\approx\begin{bmatrix}3.7423&0.3435&-5.9056\\
0.3435&2.7157&4.0117\\
-5.9056&4.0117&17.9427\end{bmatrix}.

The corresponding approximate eigenvalues are

\lambda^{A}_{1}\approx 0.1254
,
\lambda^{A}_{2}\approx 3.4533
, and

\lambda^{A}_{3}\approx 20.8220
. These numerical approximations suggest
positivity of the even constraint matrix at this parameter. This would suggest that
-\mathcal{L}^{-1}
is negative definite on the finite-dimensional subspace
\text{span}\{f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}\}.
Consequently, there would be no linear combination
f\in\text{span}\{f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}\}
such that
(\mathcal{L}^{-1}f,f)<0.

Now, we can finally prove the Virial Lemma
5.11
.

Lemma B.11
.

Fix
i\geq 1
. There exist constants
\mu>0
,
C>0
, and

B_{0}:=B_{0}(\mu,i)>0
such that, for every
B\geq B_{0}
and every

v\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
satisfying

(v,Q)=(v,\varphi(y_{1})Q_{y_{1}})=(v,\varphi(y_{1})Q_{y_{2}})=(v,\varphi(y_{1})\Lambda Q)=0,

the following estimate holds:

\displaystyle\iint_{\{|y_{1}|<\frac{B}{2}\}}\Big[(3v_{y_{1}}^{2}+v_{y_{2}}^{2})\varphi^{\prime}+v^{2}(\varphi^{\prime}-\varphi^{\prime\prime\prime})-3Q^{2}v^{2}\varphi^{\prime}+6QQ_{y_{1}}v^{2}\varphi\Big]

\displaystyle\geq\mu\iint_{\{|y_{1}|<\frac{B}{2}\}}(v_{y_{1}}^{2}+v_{y_{2}}^{2}+v^{2})\varphi^{\prime}-Ce^{\frac{B}{4\alpha_{1}}}\iint v^{2}\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}.

Proof.

If
\iint v^{2}\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}=+\infty
, the conclusion is immediate. We
therefore assume that this integral is finite. Let

\zeta\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
satisfy
0\leq\zeta\leq 1
and

\zeta(s)=\begin{cases}1,&|s|<\frac{1}{4},\\
0,&|s|>\frac{1}{2}.\end{cases}

Set

\zeta_{B}(y_{1})=\zeta\left(\frac{y_{1}}{B}\right),\qquad\tilde{v}(y_{1},y_{2})=v(y_{1},y_{2})\zeta_{B}(y_{1}).

Since
\tilde{v}
is supported in
\{|y_{1}|<B/2\}
, we have

\tilde{v}\sqrt{\varphi^{\prime}}\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
.

Step 1: Coercivity estimate.

For
\tilde{v}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, integration by parts gives

\displaystyle\iint\Big[(3\tilde{v}_{y_{1}}^{2}+\tilde{v}_{y_{2}}^{2})\varphi^{\prime}

\displaystyle+\tilde{v}^{2}(\varphi^{\prime}-\varphi^{\prime\prime\prime})-3Q^{2}\tilde{v}^{2}\varphi^{\prime}+6QQ_{y_{1}}\tilde{v}^{2}\varphi\Big]

\displaystyle=\bigl(\mathcal{L}(\tilde{v}\sqrt{\varphi^{\prime}}),\tilde{v}\sqrt{\varphi^{\prime}}\bigr),

where
\mathcal{L}
is the operator defined in
Lemma
B.8
. Since
\tilde{v}
is supported in a bounded

y_{1}
-region, both sides are continuous quadratic forms on

H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
; the identity therefore extends to every

\tilde{v}\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
by density.

Let
\mu_{0}>0
be the constant in Lemma
B.8
. Applying
that lemma to
\tilde{v}\sqrt{\varphi^{\prime}}
yields

\displaystyle\bigl(\mathcal{L}(\tilde{v}\sqrt{\varphi^{\prime}}),\tilde{v}\sqrt{\varphi^{\prime}}\bigr)\geq{}

\displaystyle\mu_{0}\|\tilde{v}\sqrt{\varphi^{\prime}}\|_{H^{1}}^{2}

(B.6)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{\mu_{0}}\Big[(\tilde{v},Q)^{2}+(\tilde{v},\varphi Q_{y_{1}})^{2}+(\tilde{v},\varphi\Lambda Q)^{2}+(\tilde{v},\varphi Q_{y_{2}})^{2}\Big].

Since

\frac{\varphi^{\prime\prime}}{\varphi^{\prime}}=\frac{1}{\alpha_{1}},\qquad\frac{\varphi^{\prime\prime\prime}}{\varphi^{\prime}}=\frac{1}{\alpha_{1}^{2}},

another integration by parts gives the exact identity

\|\tilde{v}\sqrt{\varphi^{\prime}}\|_{H^{1}}^{2}=\iint\left[\tilde{v}_{y_{1}}^{2}+\tilde{v}_{y_{2}}^{2}+\left(1-\frac{1}{4\alpha_{1}^{2}}\right)\tilde{v}^{2}\right]\varphi^{\prime}.

Define

\tilde{\mu}=\min\left\{\mu_{0}\left(1-\frac{1}{4\alpha_{1}^{2}}\right),\frac{1}{2}\left(1-\frac{1}{\alpha_{1}^{2}}\right)\right\}.

Using

\varphi^{\prime}-\varphi^{\prime\prime\prime}=\left(1-\frac{1}{\alpha_{1}^{2}}\right)\varphi^{\prime},\qquad\varphi=\alpha_{1}\left(1+e^{-y_{1}/\alpha_{1}}\right)\varphi^{\prime},

we deduce from (
B.6
) that

\displaystyle(3-\tilde{\mu})\iint\tilde{v}_{y_{1}}^{2}\varphi^{\prime}+(1-\tilde{\mu})\iint\tilde{v}_{y_{2}}^{2}\varphi^{\prime}+\left(1-\frac{1}{\alpha_{1}^{2}}-\tilde{\mu}\right)\iint\tilde{v}^{2}\varphi^{\prime}

(B.7)

\displaystyle-3\iint Q^{2}\tilde{v}^{2}\varphi^{\prime}+6\iint\alpha_{1}\left(1+e^{-y_{1}/\alpha_{1}}\right)QQ_{y_{1}}\tilde{v}^{2}\varphi^{\prime}

\displaystyle\geq-\frac{1}{\tilde{\mu}}\Big[(\tilde{v},Q)^{2}+(\tilde{v},\varphi Q_{y_{1}})^{2}+(\tilde{v},\varphi Q_{y_{2}})^{2}+(\tilde{v},\varphi\Lambda Q)^{2}\Big].

Step 2: Localization errors.

The identity

\tilde{v}_{y_{1}}=v_{y_{1}}\zeta_{B}+v\zeta_{B}^{\prime}

and an integration by parts in the cross term give

\displaystyle\iint\tilde{v}_{y_{1}}^{2}\varphi^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\iint\zeta_{B}^{2}v_{y_{1}}^{2}\varphi^{\prime}-\iint\left(\zeta_{B}\zeta_{B}^{\prime\prime}+\frac{1}{\alpha_{1}}\zeta_{B}\zeta_{B}^{\prime}\right)v^{2}\varphi^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leq\iint_{\{|y_{1}|<\frac{B}{2}\}}v_{y_{1}}^{2}\varphi^{\prime}+\frac{C(\zeta)}{B}\iint_{\{\frac{B}{4}<|y_{1}|<\frac{B}{2}\}}v^{2}\varphi^{\prime}.

Moreover,

\iint\tilde{v}_{y_{2}}^{2}\varphi^{\prime}\leq\iint_{\{|y_{1}|<\frac{B}{2}\}}v_{y_{2}}^{2}\varphi^{\prime},\qquad\iint\tilde{v}^{2}\varphi^{\prime}\leq\iint_{\{|y_{1}|<\frac{B}{2}\}}v^{2}\varphi^{\prime}.

On the annulus
\{B/4<|y_{1}|<B/2\}
, the decay of
Q
and
Q_{y_{1}}

implies

Q^{2}\lesssim e^{-\frac{B}{2\alpha_{1}}},\qquad\alpha_{1}\left(1+e^{-y_{1}/\alpha_{1}}\right)|QQ_{y_{1}}|\lesssim e^{-\frac{B}{4\alpha_{1}}}.

Consequently,

\displaystyle\left|\iint Q^{2}\tilde{v}^{2}\varphi^{\prime}-\iint_{\{|y_{1}|<\frac{B}{2}\}}Q^{2}v^{2}\varphi^{\prime}\right|\lesssim e^{-\frac{B}{2\alpha_{1}}}\iint_{\{\frac{B}{4}<|y_{1}|<\frac{B}{2}\}}v^{2}\varphi^{\prime},

\displaystyle\begin{split}\bigg|\iint\alpha_{1}\left(1+e^{-y_{1}/\alpha_{1}}\right)QQ_{y_{1}}\tilde{v}^{2}\varphi^{\prime}&-\iint_{\{|y_{1}|<\frac{B}{2}\}}\alpha_{1}\left(1+e^{-y_{1}/\alpha_{1}}\right)QQ_{y_{1}}v^{2}\varphi^{\prime}\bigg|\\
&\lesssim e^{-\frac{B}{4\alpha_{1}}}\iint_{\{\frac{B}{4}<|y_{1}|<\frac{B}{2}\}}v^{2}\varphi^{\prime}.\end{split}

Step 3: Orthogonality errors.

Since
v
satisfies the exact orthogonality conditions,

(\tilde{v},h)=-(v(1-\zeta_{B}),h)

for

h\in\{Q,\varphi Q_{y_{1}},\varphi Q_{y_{2}},\varphi\Lambda Q\}.

Using
\varphi=1+\alpha_{1}\varphi^{\prime}
and Cauchy–Schwarz, we obtain

\displaystyle|(\tilde{v},\varphi Q_{y_{1}})|^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\iint v^{2}\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}\right)\left(\iint_{\{|y_{1}|>\frac{B}{4}\}}\frac{(\varphi^{\prime})^{2}Q_{y_{1}}^{2}+Q_{y_{1}}^{2}}{\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}}\right).

For
y_{1}<B/2
, the definition of
\phi_{i,B}
gives

\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}\gtrsim e^{-\frac{B}{2\alpha_{1}}}\varphi^{\prime}.

Using the decay of
Q_{y_{1}}
from Lemma
3.3
, we have

\displaystyle\iint_{\{y_{1}<-\frac{B}{4}\}}\frac{(\varphi^{\prime})^{2}Q_{y_{1}}^{2}}{\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\lesssim e^{-\frac{B}{4\alpha_{1}}},

\displaystyle\iint_{\{y_{1}<-\frac{B}{4}\}}\frac{Q_{y_{1}}^{2}}{\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\lesssim e^{\frac{B}{4\alpha_{1}}},

\displaystyle\iint_{\{\frac{B}{4}<y_{1}<\frac{B}{2}\}}\frac{(\varphi^{\prime})^{2}Q_{y_{1}}^{2}}{\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\lesssim e^{\frac{B}{4\alpha_{1}}},

\displaystyle\iint_{\{\frac{B}{4}<y_{1}<\frac{B}{2}\}}\frac{Q_{y_{1}}^{2}}{\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\lesssim e^{-\frac{B}{4\alpha_{1}}}.

For
y_{1}>B/2
, the lower bound
\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}\geq c_{i}>0,
the relation

\alpha_{2}=\alpha_{1}-\frac{1}{200}
from (
3.1
) and
(
3.3
) yield

\iint_{\{y_{1}>\frac{B}{2}\}}\frac{(\varphi^{\prime})^{2}Q_{y_{1}}^{2}}{\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}}\lesssim e^{-\frac{B}{200\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}},\qquad\iint_{\{y_{1}>\frac{B}{2}\}}\frac{Q_{y_{1}}^{2}}{\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}}\lesssim e^{-\frac{B}{\alpha_{1}}}.

It follows that

|(\tilde{v},\varphi Q_{y_{1}})|^{2}\lesssim e^{\frac{B}{4\alpha_{1}}}\iint v^{2}\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}.

The same argument, using the decay of
Q
and its derivatives on the
left and the pointwise bounds (
3.3
) on the right,
gives

|(\tilde{v},Q)|^{2}+|(\tilde{v},\varphi Q_{y_{2}})|^{2}+|(\tilde{v},\varphi\Lambda Q)|^{2}\lesssim e^{\frac{B}{4\alpha_{1}}}\iint v^{2}\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}.

Step 4: Conclusion.

Choose
B_{0}
sufficiently large that, for every
B\geq B_{0}
,

\frac{C(\zeta)}{B}+Ce^{-\frac{B}{4\alpha_{1}}}\leq\frac{\tilde{\mu}}{2}.

The localization estimates from Step 2, together with

1-\alpha_{1}^{-2}-\tilde{\mu}>0
, then imply

\displaystyle\iint_{\{|y_{1}|<\frac{B}{2}\}}\Big[(3-\tilde{\mu})v_{y_{1}}^{2}+(1-\tilde{\mu})v_{y_{2}}^{2}+\left(1-\frac{1}{\alpha_{1}^{2}}-\frac{\tilde{\mu}}{2}\right)v^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 110.96574pt-3Q^{2}v^{2}+6\alpha_{1}\left(1+e^{-y_{1}/\alpha_{1}}\right)QQ_{y_{1}}v^{2}\Big]\varphi^{\prime}

\displaystyle\qquad\geq\iint\Big[(3-\tilde{\mu})\tilde{v}_{y_{1}}^{2}+(1-\tilde{\mu})\tilde{v}_{y_{2}}^{2}+\left(1-\frac{1}{\alpha_{1}^{2}}-\tilde{\mu}\right)\tilde{v}^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 110.96574pt-3Q^{2}\tilde{v}^{2}+6\alpha_{1}\left(1+e^{-y_{1}/\alpha_{1}}\right)QQ_{y_{1}}\tilde{v}^{2}\Big]\varphi^{\prime}.

Combining this inequality with (
B.7
) and the
projection estimates from Step 3 yields

\displaystyle\iint_{\{|y_{1}|<\frac{B}{2}\}}\Big[(3-\tilde{\mu})v_{y_{1}}^{2}+(1-\tilde{\mu})v_{y_{2}}^{2}+\left(1-\frac{1}{\alpha_{1}^{2}}-\frac{\tilde{\mu}}{2}\right)v^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 110.96574pt-3Q^{2}v^{2}+6\alpha_{1}\left(1+e^{-y_{1}/\alpha_{1}}\right)QQ_{y_{1}}v^{2}\Big]\varphi^{\prime}

\displaystyle\qquad\geq-Ce^{\frac{B}{4\alpha_{1}}}\iint v^{2}\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}.

Finally, using

\varphi^{\prime}-\varphi^{\prime\prime\prime}=\left(1-\frac{1}{\alpha_{1}^{2}}\right)\varphi^{\prime},\qquad\varphi=\alpha_{1}\left(1+e^{-y_{1}/\alpha_{1}}\right)\varphi^{\prime},

and restoring the positive terms that were subtracted on the left,
we obtain

\displaystyle\iint_{\{|y_{1}|<\frac{B}{2}\}}\Big[(3v_{y_{1}}^{2}+v_{y_{2}}^{2})\varphi^{\prime}+v^{2}(\varphi^{\prime}-\varphi^{\prime\prime\prime})-3Q^{2}v^{2}\varphi^{\prime}+6QQ_{y_{1}}v^{2}\varphi\Big]

\displaystyle\qquad\geq\frac{\tilde{\mu}}{2}\iint_{\{|y_{1}|<\frac{B}{2}\}}(v_{y_{1}}^{2}+v_{y_{2}}^{2}+v^{2})\varphi^{\prime}-Ce^{\frac{B}{4\alpha_{1}}}\iint v^{2}\phi_{i,B}^{\prime}.

Renaming
\tilde{\mu}/2
as
\mu
completes the proof.
∎

Appendix C
Variational Compactness Near the Soliton Orbit

We prove the variational compactness result used to identify the blow-up behavior for non-positive energy. We restate it for convenience.

Theorem C.1
.

There exists
\alpha_{\text{orb}}>0
such that the following holds. For all
0<\alpha^{\prime}\leq\alpha_{\text{orb}},
there exists
\delta=\delta(\alpha^{\prime})>0,
with
\delta(\alpha^{\prime})\rightarrow 0
as
\alpha^{\prime}\rightarrow 0,
such that for all
u\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),u\not\equiv 0,
if

\|u\|_{L^{2}}^{2}-\|Q\|_{L^{2}}^{2}=\alpha(u)\leq\alpha^{\prime}\quad\text{and}\quad E(u)\leq\alpha^{\prime}\iint|\nabla u|^{2},

then there exists
\tilde{y}_{1},\tilde{y}_{2}

\in\mathbb{R}
and
\epsilon_{0}\in\{-1,1\}
such that

\|Q-\epsilon_{0}\lambda_{0}u(\lambda_{0}y_{1}+\tilde{y}_{1},\lambda_{0}y_{2}+\tilde{y}_{2})\|_{H^{1}}\leq\delta(\alpha^{\prime}),

with

\lambda_{0}=\frac{\|\nabla Q\|_{L^{2}}}{\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}}}.

In order to prove this result, we will go through several steps that appear in the works of Martel and Merle
[
50
]
,
[
44
]
.

Claim 1.
The variational characterization of
Q
: for
v\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),
if

0<\iint v^{2}\leq\iint Q^{2}\mbox{ and }E(v)\leq 0,

(C.1)

then there exists
\lambda_{1}>0,(z_{1},z_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2},\epsilon_{0}\in\{\pm 1\}
such that
v(y_{1},y_{2})=\epsilon_{0}\lambda_{1}Q(\lambda_{1}(y_{1}-z_{1}),\lambda_{1}(y_{2}-z_{2})).

This follows from the sharp Gagliardo–Nirenberg inequality

[
56
]
. The minimum of

J(u)=\frac{\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\|u\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}{\|u\|_{L^{4}}^{4}}

is
C_{GN}^{-1}=\|Q\|_{L^{2}}^{2}/2
, and its real optimizers are

aQ(b(\cdot-\mathbf{z}))
, with
a\neq 0
,
b>0
, and

\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Indeed, the hypotheses
(
C.1
) and the sharp inequality give

0\geq E(v)\geq\frac{1}{2}\|\nabla v\|_{2}^{2}\left(1-\frac{\|v\|_{2}^{2}}{\|Q\|_{2}^{2}}\right)\geq 0.

Thus
\|v\|_{2}=\|Q\|_{2}
and
v
is an optimizer. Its mass
normalization forces
|a|=b
, which gives the stated form.

As a corollary of this claim, we get that for any
v\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),
the condition

E(v)=0,\quad\iint v^{2}=\iint Q^{2},\quad\iint|\nabla v|^{2}=\iint|\nabla Q|^{2}

is equivalent to

v(y_{1},y_{2})=\epsilon_{0}Q(y_{1}-z_{1},y_{2}-z_{2}).

(C.2)

Lemma C.2
.

Let
(v_{n})_{n\geq 1}
be a sequence in
H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
such that

\iint v_{n}^{2}\rightarrow\iint Q^{2}\mbox{ as }n\rightarrow\infty,\quad\iint|\nabla v_{n}|^{2}=\iint|\nabla Q|^{2}\quad\text{and}\quad E(v_{n})\to 0.

Then there exists a sequence
\epsilon_{n}\in\{\pm 1\}
and a sequence
(\mathbf{z}_{n})_{n}
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
such that

\epsilon_{n}v_{n}(\cdot+\mathbf{z}_{n})\rightarrow Q\mbox{ in }H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}).

Proof.

We follow the compactness argument in the proof of

[
50
, Lemma 1]
.
Since
\|v_{n}\|_{L^{4}}^{4}\to 2\|\nabla Q\|_{L^{2}}^{2}>0
,
Lions’ vanishing lemma
[
36
, Lemma I.1]

and local compactness give, after extraction and translation,

\widetilde{v}_{n}:=v_{n}(\cdot+\mathbf{z}_{n})\rightharpoonup V\neq 0\quad\text{in }H^{1},\qquad\widetilde{v}_{n}\to V\quad\text{almost everywhere}.

Set
w_{n}=\widetilde{v}_{n}-V
.
Weak convergence gives

0<\|V\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq\|Q\|_{L^{2}}^{2},\qquad\|w_{n}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\to\|Q\|_{L^{2}}^{2}-\|V\|_{L^{2}}^{2}.

Expanding the energy, we obtain

E(v_{n})=E(V)+E(w_{n})+\mathcal{R}_{n},

where

\mathcal{R}_{n}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\nabla V\cdot\nabla w_{n}-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left(V^{3}w_{n}+\frac{3}{2}V^{2}w_{n}^{2}+Vw_{n}^{3}\right)\longrightarrow 0.

Indeed, the gradient term vanishes by weak convergence.
The nonlinear terms vanish by local compactness in
L^{4}
,
followed by Hölder’s inequality on the complement of a
large ball, using
V\in L^{4}
and the uniform
L^{4}
bound
on
w_{n}
.
By the sharp Gagliardo–Nirenberg inequality

[
56
]
, for sufficiently large
n
,

E(w_{n})\geq\frac{1}{2}\left(1-\frac{\|w_{n}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}{\|Q\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}\right)\|\nabla w_{n}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\geq\frac{\|V\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}{4\|Q\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}\|\nabla w_{n}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}.

Thus
E(V)\leq 0
. The same inequality applied to
V\neq 0

forces
\|V\|_{L^{2}}^{2}=\|Q\|_{L^{2}}^{2}
and
E(V)=0
.
Consequently,
E(w_{n})\to 0
and
w_{n}\to 0
in
H^{1}
.
The characterization (
C.2
) identifies
V

with
Q
up to sign and translation.

Applying this argument to every subsequence shows that

d_{n}:=\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}\epsilon\in\{\pm 1\}\\
\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\end{subarray}}\|\epsilon v_{n}(\cdot+\mathbf{z})-Q\|_{H^{1}}\longrightarrow 0.

By the definition of
d_{n}
, for each
n
we can choose

\epsilon_{n}\in\{\pm 1\}
and
\mathbf{z}_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}

such that

\|\epsilon_{n}v_{n}(\cdot+\mathbf{z}_{n})-Q\|_{H^{1}}<d_{n}+\frac{1}{n}.

Since
d_{n}\to 0
, these choices give the claimed strong

H^{1}
convergence.
∎

Proof of Theorem
C.1
.

We proceed by contradiction. Suppose the statement is false. This means there exists
\delta_{0}>0
and a sequence
\alpha_{n}\rightarrow 0
(representing the bound
\alpha^{\prime}
), such that for every
n
, there exists
u_{n}\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
satisfying the proximity conditions:

\|u_{n}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}-\|Q\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq\alpha_{n}\quad\text{and}\quad E(u_{n})\leq\alpha_{n}\iint|\nabla u_{n}|^{2},

but which remains bounded away from the soliton orbit:

\inf_{\lambda=\frac{\|\nabla Q\|_{L^{2}}}{\|\nabla u_{n}\|_{L^{2}}},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{2},\epsilon\in\{\pm 1\}}\|Q-\epsilon\lambda u_{n}(\lambda\cdot+\mathbf{y})\|_{H^{1}}\geq\delta_{0}.

We set
\lambda_{n}=\frac{\|\nabla Q\|_{L^{2}}}{\|\nabla u_{n}\|_{L^{2}}}
and
v_{n}(\mathbf{y})=\lambda_{n}u_{n}(\lambda_{n}\mathbf{y}).
We see that
\iint v_{n}^{2}=\iint u_{n}^{2}
and
\iint|\nabla v_{n}|^{2}=\iint|\nabla Q|^{2}.

By the sharp Gagliardo-Nirenberg inequality we get

\displaystyle\alpha_{n}\geq\frac{E(u_{n})}{\iint|\nabla u_{n}|^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{4}\frac{\|u_{n}\|_{L^{4}}^{4}}{\|\nabla u_{n}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}\geq\frac{1}{2}-\frac{C_{GN}}{4}\|u_{n}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\Big(1-\frac{\iint u_{n}^{2}}{\iint Q^{2}}\Big)\geq-\frac{1}{2\|Q\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}\alpha_{n}.

Therefore
(1-2\alpha_{n})\|Q\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq\|u_{n}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq\|Q\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\alpha_{n},

and it follows that

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\iint u_{n}^{2}=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\iint v_{n}^{2}=\iint Q^{2}\mbox{ and }\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{E(u_{n})}{\iint|\nabla u_{n}|^{2}}=0.

By direct calculations, we have:

\displaystyle E(v_{n})=\frac{E(u_{n})}{\iint|\nabla u_{n}|^{2}}\iint|\nabla Q|^{2}\to 0.

Therefore the sequence
(v_{n})_{n\geq 1}
satisfies the conditions of Lemma
C.2
. Hence,

\epsilon_{n}v_{n}(\cdot+\mathbf{z}_{n})\rightarrow Q\mbox{ in }H^{1}\mbox{ as }n\rightarrow\infty,
which yields a contradiction.
∎

Appendix D
An Analytic Lower Bound for the Rate Constant

Proposition D.1
.

Let
Q
be the positive radial exponentially decaying solution of

-\Delta Q+Q-Q^{3}=0
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, and put

g_{Q}(x_{2})=x_{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}Q_{x_{2}}(x_{1},x_{2})\,dx_{1}.

With the Fourier convention
\widehat{f}(\xi)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-ix\xi}f(x)\,dx
, we have

c:=\frac{\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{2}{1+\xi^{2}}\,|\widehat{g_{Q}}(\xi)|^{2}\,d\xi}{\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}|\widehat{g_{Q}}(\xi)|^{2}\,d\xi}>1.

Proof.

We write
z
for the variable
x_{2}
and define

h_{Q}(z):=\int_{\mathbb{R}}Q(x_{1},z)\,dx_{1},\qquad m_{Q}(z):=\int_{\mathbb{R}}Q(x_{1},z)^{3}\,dx_{1}.

The smoothness and exponential decay of
Q
imply that
h_{Q}
and
m_{Q}
are smooth and
exponentially decaying. Integrating the equation for
Q
in
x_{1}
yields

0=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\bigl(-Q_{x_{1}x_{1}}-Q_{zz}+Q-Q^{3}\bigr)(x_{1},z)\,dx_{1}=-h_{Q}^{\prime\prime}(z)+h_{Q}(z)-m_{Q}(z).

(D.1)

Set

k(z):=e^{-|z|},\qquad\mathcal{C}f:=k*f.

Since
(1-\partial_{z}^{2})(k/2)=\delta_{0}
in the sense of distributions, the unique decaying
solution of (
D.1
) is

h_{Q}=\frac{1}{2}k*m_{Q}.

Define

w_{Q}(z):=-g_{Q}(z)=-zh^{\prime}_{Q}(z).

The function
h_{Q}
is real and even, so
w_{Q}
is real and even. Hence
\widehat{w_{Q}}
is real and

\bigl(\widehat{w_{Q}}(\xi)\bigr)^{2}=|\widehat{w_{Q}}(\xi)|^{2}
; moreover,

\bigl(\widehat{w_{Q}}(\xi)\bigr)^{2}=\bigl(\widehat{g_{Q}}(\xi)\bigr)^{2}
. Our Fourier convention gives

\widehat{k}(\xi)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-|z|}e^{-iz\xi}\,dz=\frac{2}{1+\xi^{2}}.

Consequently, by Plancherel,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\frac{2}{1+\xi^{2}}-1\right)\widehat{g_{Q}}(\xi)^{2}\,d\xi

(D.2)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\bigl(\widehat{\mathcal{C}w_{Q}}(\xi)-\widehat{w_{Q}}(\xi)\bigr)\widehat{w_{Q}}(\xi)\,d\xi

\displaystyle=2\pi\int_{\mathbb{R}}w_{Q}(z)\bigl((\mathcal{C}w_{Q})(z)-w_{Q}(z)\bigr)\,dz.

Thus it remains to show that the last integral is strictly positive.

Write the radial soliton as
Q(x_{1},z)=q(r),
with
r=(x_{1}^{2}+z^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}.
From Theorem 2 of
[
23
]
, we have that
q^{\prime}(r)<0.
We have

m^{\prime}_{Q}(z)=3z\int_{\mathbb{R}}q^{2}(r)\frac{q^{\prime}(r)}{r}dx_{1}.

Since
m_{Q}(z)=M_{Q}(|z|)
is even and strictly decreasing in
|z|,
for every
\tau\in(0,M_{Q}(0)),
there is a unique
a(\tau)>0
such that
\{z:m_{Q}(z)>\tau\}=(-a(\tau),a(\tau)).
Therefore, by a layer-cake decomposition,

m_{Q}(z)=\int_{0}^{M_{Q}(0)}\textbf{1}_{\{m_{Q}(z)>\tau\}}d\tau=\int_{0}^{M_{Q}(0)}\textbf{1}_{\{|z|<a(\tau)\}}d\tau.

Denote
m_{a}=\textbf{1}_{\{|z|<a\}},

h_{a}=\frac{1}{2}k*m_{a}
and
w_{a}=-zh_{a}^{\prime}(z),
with

h_{Q}(z)=\int_{0}^{M_{Q}(0)}h_{a(\tau)}(z)d\tau,\quad w_{Q}(z)=\int_{0}^{M_{Q}(0)}w_{a(\tau)}(z)d\tau.

The first identity follows from Tonelli’s theorem. Since

|k^{\prime}|\leq k
almost everywhere, we have
|h_{a}^{\prime}|\leq h_{a}
.
For a fixed
z_{0}
, the bound

h_{a}(z)\leq e^{|z-z_{0}|}h_{a}(z_{0})
supplies an integrable majorant
on a neighborhood of
z_{0}
. Differentiation under the layer integral
is therefore justified by dominated convergence, proving the second
identity.

Lemma D.2
.

For
a>0
, the functions
h_{a}
and
w_{a}
are even. For
z\geq 0
,

h_{a}(z)=\begin{cases}1-e^{-a}\cosh z,&0\leq z\leq a,\\[5.69054pt]
\sinh(a)e^{-z},&z\geq a,\end{cases}

(D.3)

and

w_{a}(z)=\begin{cases}e^{-a}z\sinh z,&0\leq z\leq a,\\[5.69054pt]
z\sinh(a)e^{-z},&z\geq a.\end{cases}

(D.4)

In particular,
w_{a}(z)>0
for every
z>0
.

Proof.

Since
h_{a}
is the decaying distributional solution of

-h_{a}^{\prime\prime}+h_{a}=m_{a},

the Green kernel formula gives immediately

h_{a}(z)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{-a}^{a}e^{-|z-\tilde{z}|}d\tilde{z}.

For
0\leq z\leq a,

h_{a}(z)=\frac{1}{2}\big(\int_{-a}^{z}e^{-(z-\tilde{z})}\,d\tilde{z}+\int_{z}^{a}e^{-(\tilde{z}-z)}d\tilde{z}\big)=1-e^{-a}\cosh z.

For
z\geq a,

h_{a}(z)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{-a}^{a}e^{-(z-\tilde{z})}d\tilde{z}=e^{-z}\sinh a.

Differentiating it and using

w_{a}=-zh_{a}^{\prime}
gives (
D.4
). Both expressions in (
D.4
) are
strictly positive when
z>0.

∎

The explicit formulas give
w_{a}\geq 0
, and integration by parts yields

\|w_{a}\|_{L^{1}}=-\int_{\mathbb{R}}zh_{a}^{\prime}(z)\,dz=\int_{\mathbb{R}}h_{a}(z)\,dz=2a.

Since
m_{Q}\in L^{1}
, the layer-cake formula also gives

\int_{0}^{M_{Q}(0)}2a(\tau)\,d\tau=\|m_{Q}\|_{L^{1}}<\infty.

Thus the representation of
w_{Q}
converges in
L^{1}
, and Tonelli’s
theorem applies to its convolution with the positive kernel
k
.
In particular,

(\mathcal{C}w_{Q})(z)=\int_{0}^{M_{Q}(0)}(\mathcal{C}w_{a(\tau)})(z)\,d\tau.

We now compute
\mathcal{C}w_{a}
.

Lemma D.3
.

For
a>0
and
