z\geq 0
,

(\mathcal{C}w_{a})(z)=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\frac{e^{-a}}{2}\Bigl((a^{2}+a-z^{2})\cosh z+z\sinh z\Bigr),&0\leq z\leq a,\\[8.53581pt]
\displaystyle\frac{e^{-z}}{2}\Bigl((z^{2}+z-a^{2})\sinh a+a\cosh a\Bigr),&z\geq a.\end{cases}

(D.5)

Consequently,

\mathcal{D}_{a}(z):=(\mathcal{C}w_{a})(z)-w_{a}(z)=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\frac{e^{-a}}{2}\Bigl((a^{2}+a-z^{2})\cosh z-z\sinh z\Bigr),&0\leq z\leq a,\\[8.53581pt]
\displaystyle\frac{e^{-z}}{2}\Bigl((z^{2}-z-a^{2})\sinh a+a\cosh a\Bigr),&z\geq a.\end{cases}

(D.6)

Proof.

We first derive a convenient formula for convolution with
k
. If
v
is even and
integrable against
k(z-\cdot)
, then for
z\geq 0
,

\displaystyle(k*v)(z)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\bigl(e^{-|z-\tilde{z}|}+e^{-|z+\tilde{z}|}\bigr)v(\tilde{z})\,d\tilde{z}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{z}\bigl(e^{-z+\tilde{z}}+e^{-z-\tilde{z}}\bigr)v(\tilde{z})\,d\tilde{z}+\int_{z}^{\infty}\bigl(e^{-\tilde{z}+z}+e^{-z-\tilde{z}}\bigr)v(\tilde{z})\,d\tilde{z}

\displaystyle=2e^{-z}\int_{0}^{z}\cosh\tilde{z}\,v(\tilde{z})\,d\tilde{z}+2\cosh z\int_{z}^{\infty}e^{-\tilde{z}}v(\tilde{z})\,d\tilde{z}.

(D.7)

We shall use the following elementary antiderivatives:

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{1}(z)

\displaystyle:=\int_{0}^{z}\tilde{z}\sinh\tilde{z}\cosh\tilde{z}\,d\tilde{z}=\frac{z\cosh(2z)}{4}-\frac{\sinh(2z)}{8},

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{2}(z)

\displaystyle:=\frac{z^{2}}{4}+\left(\frac{z}{4}+\frac{1}{8}\right)e^{-2z},

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}^{\prime}_{2}(z)

\displaystyle=ze^{-z}\sinh z,

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{3}(z)

\displaystyle:=\frac{z^{2}}{4}-\left(\frac{z}{4}+\frac{1}{8}\right)e^{-2z},

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}^{\prime}_{3}(z)

\displaystyle=ze^{-z}\cosh z,

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{4}(z)

\displaystyle:=\int_{z}^{\infty}\tilde{z}e^{-2\tilde{z}}\,d\tilde{z}=\left(\frac{z}{2}+\frac{1}{4}\right)e^{-2z}.

Suppose first that
0\leq z\leq a
. In the first integral of
(
D.7
), formula (
D.4
) uses its interior branch. In the
second integral, we split at
\tilde{z}=a
. This gives

\displaystyle(\mathcal{C}w_{a})(z)

\displaystyle=2e^{-z}\int_{0}^{z}\cosh\tilde{z}\,e^{-a}\tilde{z}\sinh\tilde{z}\,d\tilde{z}

\displaystyle\quad+2\cosh z\int_{z}^{a}e^{-\tilde{z}}e^{-a}\tilde{z}\sinh\tilde{z}\,d\tilde{z}+2\cosh z\int_{a}^{\infty}e^{-\tilde{z}}\tilde{z}\sinh(a)e^{-\tilde{z}}\,d\tilde{z}

\displaystyle=2e^{-a-z}\mathcal{K}_{1}(z)+2e^{-a}\cosh z\bigl(\mathcal{K}_{2}(a)-\mathcal{K}_{2}(z)\bigr)+2\sinh(a)\cosh z\,\mathcal{K}_{4}(a).

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{-a}}{2}\Bigl((a^{2}+a-z^{2})\cosh z+z\sinh z\Bigr)

Now suppose that
z\geq a
. We split the first integral of
(
D.7
) at
\tilde{z}=a
; the second integral uses only the exterior branch of
(
D.4
). Thus

\displaystyle(\mathcal{C}w_{a})(z)

\displaystyle=2e^{-z}\int_{0}^{a}\cosh\tilde{z}\,e^{-a}\tilde{z}\sinh\tilde{z}\,d\tilde{z}+2e^{-z}\int_{a}^{z}\cosh\tilde{z}\,\tilde{z}\sinh(a)e^{-\tilde{z}}\,d\tilde{z}

\displaystyle\quad+2\cosh z\int_{z}^{\infty}e^{-\tilde{z}}\tilde{z}\sinh(a)e^{-\tilde{z}}\,d\tilde{z}

\displaystyle=2e^{-z}\left[e^{-a}\mathcal{K}_{1}(a)+\sinh(a)\bigl(\mathcal{K}_{3}(z)-\mathcal{K}_{3}(a)\bigr)\right]+2\sinh(a)\cosh z\,\mathcal{K}_{4}(z).

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{-z}}{2}\Bigl((z^{2}+z-a^{2})\sinh a+a\cosh a\Bigr).

Finally, (
D.6
) follows by subtracting the corresponding branch of
(
D.4
).
∎

In order to have positivity of the expression in (
D.2
), it suffices to prove for every
a>0
and every
z\in\mathbb{R}
,

(\mathcal{C}w_{a})(z)-w_{a}(z)>0.

Since
w_{a}
and
\mathcal{C}w_{a}
are even, it suffices to take
z\geq 0
. If
0\leq z\leq a
, then
by (
D.6
),

\displaystyle 2e^{a}\mathcal{D}_{a}(z)

\displaystyle=(a^{2}+a-z^{2})\cosh z-z\sinh z

\displaystyle=\cosh z\Bigl((a^{2}-z^{2})+(a-z\tanh z)\Bigr).

Here
a^{2}-z^{2}\geq 0
, while

a-z\tanh z\geq z-z\tanh z>0

when
z>0
, and for
z=0
it equals
a>0
. Thus
\mathcal{D}_{a}(z)>0
on
[0,a]
.

If
z\geq a
, then (
D.6
) gives

2e^{z}\mathcal{D}_{a}(z)=\sinh a\,\mathcal{P}_{a}(z),\qquad\mathcal{P}_{a}(z):=z^{2}-z-a^{2}+a\coth a.

Since
\sinh a>0
, it remains to prove
\mathcal{P}_{a}(z)>0
. If
a\geq\tfrac{1}{2}
, then

\mathcal{P}_{a}^{\prime}(z)=2z-1\geq 0
for
z\geq a
, and hence

\mathcal{P}_{a}(z)\geq\mathcal{P}_{a}(a)=a(\coth a-1)>0.

If
0<a<\tfrac{1}{2}
, the minimum of
\mathcal{P}_{a}
on
[a,\infty)
occurs at
z=\tfrac{1}{2}
.
Thus
a\coth a=a/\tanh a>1
, and

\mathcal{P}_{a}(z)\geq\mathcal{P}_{a}\!\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)=a\coth a-a^{2}-\frac{1}{4}>\frac{3}{4}-a^{2}>0.

Therefore
\mathcal{D}_{a}(z)>0
also for
z\geq a
.

For every
z\neq 0
, the layer representation gives

w_{Q}(z)=\int_{0}^{M_{Q}(0)}w_{a(\tau)}(z)\,d\tau>0.

The justified convolution interchange above gives,
for every
z\in\mathbb{R}
,

(\mathcal{C}w_{Q})(z)-w_{Q}(z)=\int_{0}^{M_{Q}(0)}\mathcal{D}_{a(\tau)}(z)\,d\tau>0.

The final pairing is finite:
w_{Q}\in L^{2}
by its exponential decay,
and convolution by
k\in L^{1}
is bounded on
L^{2}
. Since its integrand
is positive for every
z\neq 0
, we conclude

\int_{\mathbb{R}}w_{Q}(z)\bigl((\mathcal{C}w_{Q})(z)-w_{Q}(z)\bigr)\,dz>0.

∎

Appendix E
Interval-Arithmetic Verification

This appendix verifies, by interval arithmetic, the numerical assertions
used in the virial argument of Appendix
B
and in the
choice of the polynomial weight of
\mathcal{A}_{\alpha_{0}}^{(21)}
.
The verification has two branches. The first
encloses the potential, establishes the spectral index and the spectral
gap of the virial operator, and bounds the determinant of the even
inverse Gram matrix and the odd inverse pairing required by the virial
argument; the second encloses the constant
c
governing the blow-up
rate. Each finite approximation is accompanied by a bound for its entire
remainder. The calculations below use C++ with CAPD

[
26
]
for the ground state and scalar interval
computations, and verified binary64 matrix products for the spectral
tests. The analytic implications of the resulting interval bounds are
proved below.

This appendix is organized as follows. Section
E.1

describes the validation of the ground state and the bounds it
supplies. Section
E.2
transfers these bounds to
an explicit approximation of the potential, and
Section
E.3
derives from it the negative index and the
spectral gap of
\mathcal{L}
. Section
E.4
encloses the
inverse pairings, and Section
E.5
encloses the constant

c
. The main conclusions are collected in
Theorem
E.2
below.

Notation.
We keep the notation of the virial argument of
Appendix
B
. Thus

\alpha_{1}=\frac{101}{100},\qquad\varphi(y_{1})=1+e^{y_{1}/\alpha_{1}},\qquad\widetilde{\varphi}(y_{1})=\cosh\!\left(\frac{y_{1}}{2\alpha_{1}}\right),\qquad\Lambda Q=Q+y\cdot\nabla Q.

The operator to be verified is

\mathcal{L}=-3\partial_{y_{1}}^{2}-\partial_{y_{2}}^{2}+\kappa_{\alpha_{1}}+W,\qquad\kappa_{\alpha_{1}}=1-\frac{1}{4\alpha_{1}^{2}}=\frac{7701}{10201},

(E.1)

where

W=-3Q^{2}+6\alpha_{1}(1+e^{-y_{1}/\alpha_{1}})QQ_{y_{1}}.

(E.2)

We write
r=|y|
, and we also write
Q(r)
for the radial profile. All
functions and Hilbert spaces in the argument are real. For a
self-adjoint operator or a real symmetric matrix,
\operatorname{ind}_{-}

denotes the dimension of its negative spectral subspace. Unless a domain
is indicated, two-dimensional inner products and norms are taken on

\mathbb{R}^{2}
. For
u\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, the expression
(\mathcal{L}u,u)
denotes
the quadratic form

\mathcal{B}(u,u)=3\|u_{y_{1}}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\|u_{y_{2}}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(\kappa_{\alpha_{1}}+W)u^{2},

(E.3)

and
\mathcal{B}(u,v)
denotes the associated symmetric bilinear form,

\mathcal{B}(u,v)=3(u_{y_{1}},v_{y_{1}})+(u_{y_{2}},v_{y_{2}})+((\kappa_{\alpha_{1}}+W)u,v)
;
the identity
\mathcal{B}(u,v)=(\mathcal{L}u,v)
holds for

u\in H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
and
v\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
. The four constraint functions
used in Appendix
B
are

\displaystyle f_{1}

\displaystyle=e^{-y_{1}/(2\alpha_{1})}Q,

\displaystyle f_{2}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\varphi}Q_{y_{1}},

(E.4)

\displaystyle f_{3}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\varphi}\Lambda Q,

\displaystyle f_{o}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\varphi}Q_{y_{2}}.

The first three functions are even in
y_{2}
, and the last one is odd.
Once invertibility has been established, we write

A_{ij}=(\mathcal{L}^{-1}f_{i},f_{j}),\quad 1\leq i,j\leq 3,\qquad m_{oo}=(\mathcal{L}^{-1}f_{o},f_{o}),

and we enclose the
3\times 3
matrix
A
and the scalar
m_{oo}
.

The regularity and decay of the ground state are established in
Lemma
3.3
. In particular, the potential in
(
E.2
) is bounded and tends to zero exponentially, since

2-\alpha_{1}^{-1}>0
. We use the following operator properties, as in
Appendix
B
and Remark
B.9
; for the standard
bounded-perturbation and essential-spectrum results, see

[
52
, Chapter XIII]
. The quantitative exterior bound needed
by the computation is proved in Section
E.2
.

Lemma E.1
.

The operator
\mathcal{L}
, with domain
H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, is self-adjoint
on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
. Its form domain is
H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
and

\sigma_{\mathrm{ess}}(\mathcal{L})=[\kappa_{\alpha_{1}},\infty).

Moreover,
f_{1},f_{2},f_{3},f_{o}\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
and reflection in
y_{2}

commutes with
\mathcal{L}
.

An interval
[a]
always denotes a closed enclosure of the exact
quantity
a
. For a scalar interval
I
, we write
I^{-}
and
I^{+}
for
its endpoints and
\operatorname{rad}I=(I^{+}-I^{-})/2
. An interval matrix
encloses every entry of one exact matrix, and the symmetry of that exact
matrix is retained in all matrix tests. The polynomial coefficients,
mesh points, and preconditioners defining a numerical approximation are
specified as exact rational numbers or enclosed together with their
rounding errors. Finite integrals are enclosed cell by cell: if an
interval
[F]_{K}
contains the range of an integrand on a cell
K
, then

|K|[F]_{K}
encloses its integral, and polynomial integration and Taylor
remainders give sharper versions of the same inclusion. This elementary
enclosure principle, together with the explicit infinite-tail bounds
below, gives a finite meaning to every certificate quantity.

Theorem E.2

(Verified conclusions)
.

The executed ground-state construction and interval certificates below
prove the following conclusions for the operator (
E.1
).

(a)

The operator
\mathcal{L}
has exactly two negative
eigenvalues, both simple,
0\in\rho(\mathcal{L})
, and

\operatorname{ind}_{-}(\mathcal{L})=2,\qquad\operatorname{dist}(0,\sigma(\mathcal{L}))\geq 0.62076895409267.

(b)

The matrix
A
is enclosed by the interval matrix in
Section
E.4
, and

\displaystyle\det A

\displaystyle\in[-7.946966743614993375,-1.232927442846774158],

\displaystyle m_{oo}

\displaystyle\in[-0.669373,-0.624397].

(c)

The blow-up rate constant satisfies

c\in[1.66126332085379,1.66170874135113]\subset\left(\frac{21}{13},\frac{5}{3}\right).

Proof.

The CAPD construction verifies Proposition
E.4
.
The spectral certificate of Section
E.3
then verifies the
hypotheses of Proposition
E.7
, which gives (a); the
simplicity of the two negative eigenvalues is proved after that
proposition. The compact spline residual certificate of
Section
E.4
, through (
E.64
),
encloses
A
and
m_{oo}
and gives (b). Finally, the quotient
certificate of Section
E.5
proves both bounds in (c).
∎

E.1
Verification of the Ground State

We first describe the enclosures of the radial profile
Q
; the idea
comes from
[
13
]
. The construction joins a solution regular at
the origin to a decaying solution at a fixed radius
R
. Both solutions
and their parameter derivatives are enclosed, and the latter give the
Jacobian of the matching equations. All interval operations below,
including the evaluations of elementary functions, are understood with
outward rounding.

The Regular Solution at the Origin

For a real parameter
a
, we consider

Q_{0}^{\prime\prime}+r^{-1}Q_{0}^{\prime}-Q_{0}+Q_{0}^{3}=0,\qquad Q_{0}(0;a)=a,\qquad Q_{0}^{\prime}(0;a)=0.

(E.5)

Its derivative
P=\partial_{a}Q_{0}
with respect to the parameter satisfies

P^{\prime\prime}+r^{-1}P^{\prime}-P+3Q_{0}^{2}P=0,\qquad P(0;a)=1,\qquad P^{\prime}(0;a)=0.

(E.6)

We write

Q_{0}(r;a)=\sum_{n\geq 0}A_{n}(a)r^{2n},\qquad P(r;a)=\sum_{n\geq 0}B_{n}(a)r^{2n}.

(E.7)

The coefficients are determined by
A_{0}=a
,
B_{0}=1
, and the
recurrences

A_{n+1}=\frac{A_{n}-\sum_{i+j+k=n}A_{i}A_{j}A_{k}}{4(n+1)^{2}},\qquad B_{n+1}=\frac{B_{n}-3\sum_{i+j+k=n}A_{i}A_{j}B_{k}}{4(n+1)^{2}}.

(E.8)

We now give uniform remainder bounds for these series. Fix an interval

[a]
, let
a_{*}\geq\sup_{a\in[a]}|a|
, and set

b=\frac{1+a_{*}^{2}}{4},\qquad b_{P}=5b.

Then

|A_{n}(a)|\leq a_{*}b^{n},\qquad|B_{n}(a)|\leq b_{P}^{n}\quad(a\in[a],\ n\geq 0).

(E.9)

Indeed, if these estimates hold up to order
n
, the cubic sum is at
most
a_{*}^{3}b^{n}\binom{n+2}{2}\leq a_{*}^{3}b^{n}(n+1)^{2}
, while the
convolution in the sensitivity equation is bounded by

a_{*}^{2}b_{P}^{n}\sum_{k=0}^{n}(k+1)5^{-k}\leq\frac{25}{16}a_{*}^{2}b_{P}^{n}.

Consequently,

|B_{n+1}|\leq b_{P}^{n}\frac{1+75a_{*}^{2}/16}{4(n+1)^{2}}\leq b_{P}^{n+1},

where the last inequality follows from

1+75a_{*}^{2}/16\leq 5(1+a_{*}^{2})
; the bound for
A_{n+1}
follows in the
same way from its recurrence.

Choose
0<r_{0}\leq\tilde{r}_{0}
with
b_{P}\tilde{r}_{0}^{2}<1
; the larger
radius allows the same remainder bound on the origin interval and on an
overlap with the continuation. For
N\geq 0
,
d>0
, and

0\leq t<d^{-1/2}
, set

G_{N}(t;d)=\frac{(dt^{2})^{N+1}}{1-dt^{2}}.

If
S_{Q,N}=\sum_{n=0}^{N}A_{n}r^{2n}
and

S_{P,N}=\sum_{n=0}^{N}B_{n}r^{2n}
, then, for every required derivative
order
j
,

\begin{split}\sup_{0\leq r\leq\tilde{r}_{0}}|\partial_{r}^{j}(Q_{0}-S_{Q,N})(r)|&\leq a_{*}\partial_{t}^{j}G_{N}(\tilde{r}_{0};b),\\
\sup_{0\leq r\leq\tilde{r}_{0}}|\partial_{r}^{j}(P-S_{P,N})(r)|&\leq\partial_{t}^{j}G_{N}(\tilde{r}_{0};b_{P}).\end{split}

(E.10)

These bounds follow by differentiating the convergent majorant series
with nonnegative coefficients; in particular, the right-hand sides are
explicit rational functions of
\tilde{r}_{0}
,
b
, and
b_{P}
. Uniform
convergence of the series and of their derivatives verifies
(
E.5
) and (
E.6
).
Uniqueness at the origin also follows from the integral equation

Q_{0}(r)=a+\int_{0}^{r}\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t}s\bigl(Q_{0}(s)-Q_{0}(s)^{3}\bigr)\,ds\,dt:

setting
B_{*}=a_{*}+1
and choosing
0<r_{*}\leq r_{0}
such that

\frac{r_{*}^{2}(B_{*}+B_{*}^{3})}{4}<1,\qquad\frac{r_{*}^{2}(1+3B_{*}^{2})}{4}<1,

the right-hand side maps the ball
\|u-a\|_{C([0,r_{*}])}\leq 1
into
itself and is a contraction there, since the integral of the constant
function
1
equals
r^{2}/4
. These inequalities hold uniformly for

a\in[a]
and prove uniqueness near the origin.

Each
A_{n}
is a polynomial in
a
. Differentiating its recurrence and
grouping the three equal cubic sums gives the recurrence for
B_{n}
, so
that
A_{n}^{\prime}=B_{n}
by induction. The bound for
B_{n}
makes the
parameter-derivative series uniformly convergent on

[a]\times[0,\tilde{r}_{0}]
, and since the strict radius inequality
persists after a small enlargement of
[a]
, termwise differentiation
proves
P=\partial_{a}Q_{0}
, including at the endpoints of the parameter
interval. For
r>0
, local existence and uniqueness follow from the
Picard–Lindelöf theorem
[
55
, Theorem 2.2]
, and the
continuation to
R
is justified by the estimates below.

Validated Continuation on a Finite Interval

The origin series supplies an enclosure at
r_{0}>0
for each
a\in[a]
.
Away from the origin, we propagate the four quantities

Y=(Q_{0},Q_{0}^{\prime},P,P^{\prime})
through
Y^{\prime}=F(r,Y)
, where

F(r,(q,v,p,t))=\bigl(v,\ q-q^{3}-v/r,\ t,\ (1-3q^{2})p-t/r\bigr).

(E.11)

The parameter
a
enters through the initial state. Smooth dependence
on the initial data
[
55
, Theorem 2.11]
, together with the
origin construction and uniqueness for the differentiated equation,
shows that the last two components remain
\partial_{a}Q_{0}
and

\partial_{a}Q_{0}^{\prime}
. The letter
t
in this display is a state coordinate;
the independent variable is
r
. In particular, the Jacobian needed
for matching requires the flow of the whole initial interval, including
its parameter dependence.

We use the validated Taylor integrator of CAPD::DynSys

[
26
]
. The following form of its remainder inclusion
explains both existence on a complete step and the interval polynomial
exported to the subsequent computations. Since
r\geq r_{0}>0
, the vector
field is smooth on every bounded set used below. Define its normalized
derivative functions recursively by

F^{[0]}(r,Y)=Y,\qquad F^{[j+1]}=\frac{\partial_{r}F^{[j]}+D_{Y}F^{[j]}F}{j+1}.

Along an exact solution,
F^{[j]}(r,Y(r))=Y^{(j)}(r)/j!
.
Automatic differentiation evaluates these functions and their interval
extensions without differentiating an unknown numerical remainder.

Lemma E.3

(Validated Taylor step)
.

Let
I_{k}=[s_{k},s_{k+1}]\subset[r_{0},R]
, let

h_{k}=s_{k+1}-s_{k}>0
, and let
[Y_{k}]
enclose the incoming states.
Choose an order
n\geq 1
and coefficient boxes
[C_{j,k}]
such that

F^{[j]}(s_{k},[Y_{k}])\subset[C_{j,k}],\qquad 0\leq j\leq n.

Let
[\Pi_{k}]
contain all vectors

\sum_{j=0}^{n}c_{j}\tau^{j}
with
c_{j}\in[C_{j,k}]
and

0\leq\tau\leq h_{k}
. Choose a box
[E_{k}]
with

0\in\operatorname{int}[E_{k}]
, and set

[Z_{k}]=[\Pi_{k}]+[E_{k}]
. If

[0,h_{k}]^{n+1}[R_{k}]\subset\operatorname{int}[E_{k}],\qquad F^{[n+1]}(I_{k},[Z_{k}])\subset[R_{k}],

(E.12)

then each incoming state has a unique solution throughout
I_{k}
, and

Y(s_{k}+\tau)\in\sum_{j=0}^{n}[C_{j,k}]\tau^{j}+[R_{k}]\tau^{n+1},\qquad 0\leq\tau\leq h_{k}.

(E.13)

Proof.

Local existence and uniqueness follow from the Picard–Lindelöf
theorem
[
55
, Theorem 2.2]
. The initial state belongs to

\operatorname{int}[Z_{k}]
. While its solution remains in
[Z_{k}]
,
Taylor’s formula with a componentwise Lagrange remainder gives
(
E.13
). The polynomial part belongs to

[\Pi_{k}]
and, by (
E.12
), the remainder belongs
to
\operatorname{int}[E_{k}]
. Thus the solution belongs to

\operatorname{int}[Z_{k}]
, also at any alleged first exit time.
No first exit is possible. Boundedness in
[Z_{k}]
and
s_{k}>0
permit
continuation to
s_{k+1}

[
55
, Corollary 2.15]
.
The same argument applies to every initial state in
[Y_{k}]
.
∎

CAPD encloses the Taylor polynomial, predicts a remainder box, and
recomputes the order-
n+1
coefficient on the resulting state box.
Its high-order enclosure routine accepts a step only after the strict
remainder inclusion in (
E.12
). If the current
box does not pass, it is enlarged and the inclusion is tested again;
an unresolved step stops the calculation. The endpoint set is propagated
using CAPD’s change-of-coordinates representation to control repeated
overestimation of correlations. This representation does not replace
the complete-step inclusion. These operations are performed with interval
coefficients and outward arithmetic.

In the executed calculation,
r_{0}=1/512
,
R=15
, and
n=20
.
Every time endpoint and time difference is checked to be an exact
dyadic point. The step is chosen from dyadic values between
1/4096

and
1/8
, and the continuation is checked to reach exactly
R
.
The four-dimensional continuation encloses
P(R;a)
and
P^{\prime}(R;a)

for every
a
in the matching search interval. A separate
two-dimensional continuation encloses the residual at the proposed
matching center. After the strict matching inclusion is established,
the two-dimensional flow is recomputed from the new parameter interval
and supplies the profile used in the remaining certificates.

For each of its
184
cells, the saved profile contains the interval
coefficients
[C_{j,k}]
for the first two components and the
order-
21
remainder coefficient
[R_{k}]
. Formula
(
E.13
), evaluated by Horner’s rule, encloses

Q_{0}
and
Q_{0}^{\prime}
on any subinterval of that cell. The second derivative
is evaluated from
Q_{0}^{\prime\prime}=Q_{0}-Q_{0}^{3}-Q_{0}^{\prime}/r
, and further derivatives
are obtained from the differential equation. No derivative of a
saved remainder is taken. Eight interval subcells covering each full
cell verify
Q_{0}>0
and
Q_{0}^{\prime}<0
; these are range tests, not tests at
sampled points. The origin series and the exterior fixed point cover
the remaining radii.

Modified Bessel Functions

We use the standard modified Bessel functions
K_{\nu}
and
I_{\nu}
.
Their differential equation, derivative identities, and Wronskian

[
15
, 10.25.1, 10.29.3, 10.28.2]
give

K_{0}^{\prime}=-K_{1},\quad I_{0}^{\prime}=I_{1},\quad K_{0}^{\prime\prime}=K_{0}+\frac{K_{1}}{r},\quad I_{0}^{\prime\prime}=I_{0}-\frac{I_{1}}{r},\quad K_{0}I_{1}+K_{1}I_{0}=\frac{1}{r};

(E.14)

in particular, second radial derivatives are evaluated through these
identities. For the decaying functions we use the large-argument
expansions with explicit remainders. Define
a_{0}^{K}=b_{0}^{K}=1
and

a_{n+1}^{K}=\frac{(2n+1)^{2}}{8(n+1)}a_{n}^{K},\qquad b_{n+1}^{K}=\frac{4-(2n+1)^{2}}{8(n+1)}b_{n}^{K}.

Then, for
r>0
and
N\geq 1
,

\displaystyle K_{0}(r)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{2r}}e^{-r}\left(\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}(-1)^{n}a_{n}^{K}r^{-n}+\rho_{0,N}(r)\right),

(E.15)

\displaystyle|\rho_{0,N}(r)|

\displaystyle\leq a_{N}^{K}r^{-N},

\displaystyle K_{1}(r)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{2r}}e^{-r}\left(\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}b_{n}^{K}r^{-n}+\rho_{1,N}(r)\right),

\displaystyle|\rho_{1,N}(r)|

\displaystyle\leq|b_{N}^{K}|r^{-N}.

Indeed,
(-1)^{n}a_{n}^{K}
and
b_{n}^{K}
are the coefficients of Hankel’s
expansions of
K_{0}
and
K_{1}

[
15
, 10.40.2]
, and for real
positive argument the remainder after
N\geq 1
terms is bounded in
absolute value by the first neglected term and has the same sign

[
15
, §10.40(ii)]
. The sign information gives, for every
r>0
,

\left(1-\frac{1}{8r}\right)\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{2r}}e^{-r}\leq K_{0}(r)\leq\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{2r}}e^{-r},\qquad K_{1}(r)\leq\left(1+\frac{3}{8r}\right)\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{2r}}e^{-r},

(E.16)

since the first neglected terms are
-1/(8r)
for
K_{0}
and
3/(8r)

for
K_{1}
.

The following uniform bounds suffice on the entire exterior interval.
Set

k_{0}=\sqrt{\pi/2},\qquad k_{1}=k_{0}\left(1+\frac{3}{8R}\right),\qquad i_{0}=\sqrt{\pi/8}.

(E.17)

Then, for
r\geq R>0
,

0<K_{0}(r)\leq k_{0}\frac{e^{-r}}{\sqrt{r}},\quad 0<K_{1}(r)\leq k_{1}\frac{e^{-r}}{\sqrt{r}},\quad 0<I_{1}(r)\leq I_{0}(r)\leq i_{0}\frac{e^{r}}{\sqrt{r}}.

(E.18)

The bounds for
K_{0}
and
K_{1}
follow from
(
E.16
), and their positivity from the
integral representation
[
15
, 10.32.9]
. For
I_{0}
, we use

[
15
, 10.32.1]
, namely

I_{0}(r)=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\pi}e^{r\cos\theta}\,d\theta,\qquad\cos\theta\leq 1-\frac{2\theta^{2}}{\pi^{2}}\quad(0\leq\theta\leq\pi),

and extend the resulting Gaussian integral to infinity; the cosine
inequality follows from
\sin(\theta/2)\geq\theta/\pi
. Finally, the
integral representation
[
15
, 10.32.3]
gives
|I_{1}|\leq I_{0}
,
and the positive series
[
15
, 10.25.2]
gives
I_{1}>0
.

The Decaying Solution and Its Parameter Derivative

Let
\Gamma=[\gamma_{-},\gamma_{+}]\subset(0,\infty)
; in what follows,

\gamma
denotes the coefficient at the matching radius
R
. Define

\mathcal{X}_{R}=\left\{u\in C([R,\infty)):\|u\|_{\mathcal{X}_{R}}:=\sup_{r\geq R}e^{r}\sqrt{r}\,|u(r)|<\infty\right\}.

(E.19)

Multiplication by
e^{r}\sqrt{r}
identifies
\mathcal{X}_{R}
isometrically
with the Banach space of bounded continuous functions on

[R,\infty)
, so that
\mathcal{X}_{R}
is complete. For

u\in\mathcal{X}_{R}
, set

\begin{split}T_{\gamma}u(r)={}&\gamma K_{0}(r)+K_{0}(r)\int_{R}^{r}I_{0}(s)su(s)^{3}\,ds\\
&+I_{0}(r)\int_{r}^{\infty}K_{0}(s)su(s)^{3}\,ds.\end{split}

(E.20)

Let

d_{R}=\frac{3k_{0}i_{0}}{4R}e^{-2R},\qquad d_{R,1}=\frac{i_{0}(2k_{1}+k_{0})}{4R}e^{-2R},\qquad\theta_{R}=3d_{R}z^{2}.

(E.21)

The two finite inequalities

\gamma_{+}k_{0}+d_{R}z^{3}<z,\qquad\theta_{R}<1

(E.22)

give a unique fixed point
Q_{\infty}(\,\cdot\,;\gamma)
in

\{u:\|u\|_{\mathcal{X}_{R}}\leq z\}
for every
\gamma\in\Gamma
.
Indeed, for
\|u\|_{\mathcal{X}_{R}}\leq z
,

I_{0}(s)s|u(s)|^{3}\leq i_{0}z^{3}s^{-1}e^{-2s},\qquad K_{0}(s)s|u(s)|^{3}\leq k_{0}z^{3}s^{-1}e^{-4s},

and since
\int_{t}^{\infty}s^{-1}e^{-as}ds\leq e^{-at}/(at)
, the two
nonlinear terms in (
E.20
) have norms at most

k_{0}i_{0}z^{3}e^{-2R}/(2R)
and

k_{0}i_{0}z^{3}e^{-2R}/(4R)
. The same estimates and

|u(s)^{3}-v(s)^{3}|\leq 3z^{2}\frac{e^{-2s}}{s}|u(s)-v(s)|

give the Lipschitz constant
\theta_{R}
on the ball. Banach’s
fixed-point theorem
[
55
, Theorem 2.1]
proves the
assertion, together with

\|Q_{\infty}-\gamma K_{0}\|_{\mathcal{X}_{R}}\leq d_{R}z^{3}.

(E.23)

For brevity, we write

A(r)=\int_{R}^{r}I_{0}(s)sQ_{\infty}(s)^{3}\,ds,\qquad B(r)=\int_{r}^{\infty}K_{0}(s)sQ_{\infty}(s)^{3}\,ds.

Differentiating (
E.20
) and using
(
E.14
), we obtain

\begin{split}Q_{\infty}^{\prime}&=-K_{1}(\gamma+A)+I_{1}B,\\
Q_{\infty}^{\prime\prime}&=K_{0}^{\prime\prime}(\gamma+A)+I_{0}^{\prime\prime}B-Q_{\infty}^{3}.\end{split}

(E.24)

The first differentiation cancels the two endpoint terms, while in the
second their sum equals

-r(K_{1}I_{0}+I_{1}K_{0})Q_{\infty}^{3}=-Q_{\infty}^{3}
.
These formulas verify the ground-state equation on
[R,\infty)
, and
they also show that

\|Q_{\infty}^{\prime}+\gamma K_{1}\|_{\mathcal{X}_{R}}\leq d_{R,1}z^{3}.

(E.25)

Thus valid tail constants for the first three orders are

C_{Q,0}=z,\qquad C_{Q,1}=\gamma_{+}k_{1}+d_{R,1}z^{3},\qquad C_{Q,2}=z+R^{-1}C_{Q,1}+R^{-1}e^{-2R}z^{3},

(E.26)

in the sense that
|Q_{\infty}^{(j)}(r)|\leq C_{Q,j}e^{-r}/\sqrt{r}
.
The matching coefficient and the asymptotic coefficient are related by

\lim_{r\to\infty}\frac{Q_{\infty}(r;\gamma)}{K_{0}(r)}=\gamma+\int_{R}^{\infty}I_{0}(s)sQ_{\infty}(s;\gamma)^{3}\,ds.

(E.27)

Indeed, the integral converges by the bounds above, while the lower
bound for
K_{0}
in (
E.16
) shows that the
remaining quotient
I_{0}(r)B(r)/K_{0}(r)
tends to zero.

We next justify the differentiation with respect to the parameter in
the weighted space. Write
\mathcal{N}(u)=T_{\gamma}u-\gamma K_{0}
, which
is independent of
\gamma
. The same integral bounds show that

\mathcal{N}
is a continuously differentiable cubic map on

\mathcal{X}_{R}
, with

\displaystyle\|D\mathcal{N}(u)\|

\displaystyle\leq 3d_{R}\|u\|_{\mathcal{X}_{R}}^{2},

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{N}(u+v)-\mathcal{N}(u)-D\mathcal{N}(u)v\|_{\mathcal{X}_{R}}

\displaystyle\leq d_{R}\|v\|_{\mathcal{X}_{R}}^{2}(3\|u\|_{\mathcal{X}_{R}}+\|v\|_{\mathcal{X}_{R}}).

Indeed, the derivative replaces
u^{3}
in both integrals by
3u^{2}v
,
and expanding the cubic proves the displayed remainder bound and the
continuity of the derivative. At
u=Q_{\infty}(\cdot;\gamma)
we have

\|D\mathcal{N}(u)\|\leq\theta_{R}<1
, so that
I-D\mathcal{N}(u)
is
invertible, with inverse given by the Neumann series and of norm at
most
(1-\theta_{R})^{-1}
. The implicit function theorem, applied to
the
C^{1}
map
(\gamma,u)\mapsto u-\gamma K_{0}-\mathcal{N}(u)
on

\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{X}_{R}
, therefore gives a
C^{1}
branch of fixed points
through
Q_{\infty}(\cdot;\gamma)
. Since

\|Q_{\infty}\|_{\mathcal{X}_{R}}\leq\gamma_{+}k_{0}+d_{R}z^{3}<z
by
(
E.22
), this branch stays in the ball for nearby
parameters and coincides there with
Q_{\infty}
by uniqueness. Thus

Q_{\infty}
is
C^{1}
as an
\mathcal{X}_{R}
-valued function of
\gamma
,
with
\partial_{\gamma}Q_{\infty}=(I-D\mathcal{N}(Q_{\infty}))^{-1}K_{0}
;
since the differentiated radial integral operator satisfies the same
bounds with
d_{R}
replaced by
d_{R,1}
, the same argument applies to

Q_{\infty}^{\prime}
.

Writing
Z=\partial_{\gamma}Q_{\infty}
, we obtain

\begin{split}Z(r)={}&K_{0}(r)+3K_{0}(r)\int_{R}^{r}I_{0}(s)sQ_{\infty}(s)^{2}Z(s)\,ds\\
&+3I_{0}(r)\int_{r}^{\infty}K_{0}(s)sQ_{\infty}(s)^{2}Z(s)\,ds,\\
Z^{\prime}(r)={}&-K_{1}(r)-3K_{1}(r)\int_{R}^{r}I_{0}(s)sQ_{\infty}(s)^{2}Z(s)\,ds\\
&+3I_{1}(r)\int_{r}^{\infty}K_{0}(s)sQ_{\infty}(s)^{2}Z(s)\,ds.\end{split}

(E.28)

Equivalently, the first equation is solved by the convergent Neumann
series of a linear operator of norm at most
\theta_{R}
; in particular,

\begin{split}C_{Z}:=\frac{k_{0}}{1-\theta_{R}},\qquad\|Z\|_{\mathcal{X}_{R}}&\leq C_{Z},\\
\|Z-K_{0}\|_{\mathcal{X}_{R}}&\leq 3d_{R}z^{2}C_{Z},\qquad\|Z^{\prime}+K_{1}\|_{\mathcal{X}_{R}}\leq 3d_{R,1}z^{2}C_{Z}.\end{split}

(E.29)

Finally, positivity and monotonicity hold on the whole tail under the
additional finite condition

ze^{-R}/\sqrt{R}<1.

(E.30)

Indeed,
T_{\gamma}
preserves nonnegative functions, so that its
iteration gives
Q_{\infty}\geq\gamma K_{0}>0
, while
(
E.30
) gives
Q_{\infty}<1
there. Since the
derivative bound gives
rQ_{\infty}^{\prime}(r)\to 0
, integration of the
differential equation shows that

rQ_{\infty}^{\prime}(r)=-\int_{r}^{\infty}sQ_{\infty}(s)(1-Q_{\infty}(s)^{2})\,ds<0.

Matching and Identification of the Profile

We apply the interval Newton method to the two matching equations;
see
[
51
, Chapter 8]
. Define the matching map on

X=[a]\times\Gamma
by

\mathcal{F}(a,\gamma)=\begin{pmatrix}Q_{0}(R;a)-Q_{\infty}(R;\gamma)\\
Q_{0}^{\prime}(R;a)-Q_{\infty}^{\prime}(R;\gamma)\end{pmatrix},\qquad D\mathcal{F}=\begin{pmatrix}P(R;a)&-Z(R;\gamma)\\
P^{\prime}(R;a)&-Z^{\prime}(R;\gamma)\end{pmatrix}.

(E.31)

Let
X
be a box with positive radii, fix an explicitly represented
point
x_{0}\in\operatorname{int}X
, and let

J
be an interval matrix enclosing
D\mathcal{F}(X)
; we require that
every real matrix in
J
be nonsingular. An interval linear solve gives
an enclosure
K
satisfying

K\supset\{x_{0}-A^{-1}y:A\in J,\ y\in[\mathcal{F}(x_{0})]\}.

(E.32)

The interval Newton inclusion

K\subset\operatorname{int}X

(E.33)

then proves that
\mathcal{F}
has a unique zero in
X
, and that this
zero lies in
K
. Indeed, the convexity of the interval matrix implies

\overline{J}(x):=\int_{0}^{1}D\mathcal{F}(x_{0}+s(x-x_{0}))\,ds\in J.

The continuous map

x\mapsto x_{0}-\overline{J}(x)^{-1}\mathcal{F}(x_{0})
sends
X
into

K\subset X
; Brouwer’s theorem gives a fixed point, and the integral
mean-value identity shows that it is a zero. If two points of
X
were
zeros, the mean derivative on the segment joining them would belong to

J
and annihilate their difference, so that nonsingularity proves
uniqueness.

It remains to identify the matched solution. We require positive lower
bounds for
Q_{0}
and negative upper bounds for
Q_{0}^{\prime}
on every
continuation cell in
[r_{0},R]
. At the origin, a convenient sufficient
condition is

a_{-}-\frac{a_{*}br_{0}^{2}}{1-br_{0}^{2}}>1,\qquad a_{-}:=\inf[a].

(E.34)

The series estimate then gives
Q_{0}>1
on
[0,r_{0}]
, and

(rQ_{0}^{\prime})^{\prime}=rQ_{0}(1-Q_{0}^{2})<0
proves
Q_{0}^{\prime}<0
on
(0,r_{0}]
. Together with
(
E.30
), these checks give a positive, strictly
decreasing solution on
(0,\infty)
. The two matching equations join
the solutions together with their first derivatives, and the
differential equation gives the agreement of all higher derivatives at

R
. Regularity at zero and the tail bounds place the resulting radial
function in
H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, and the uniqueness of the positive radial
solution of
-\Delta Q+Q-Q^{3}=0

[
32
]
identifies it with the
ground state
Q
.

Proposition E.4
.

Suppose that the following conditions hold:

(i)

the series condition
b_{P}\tilde{r}_{0}^{2}<1
and the origin
condition (
E.34
);

(ii)

the validated Taylor continuation and coefficient enclosures
(
E.12
)–(
E.13
)
on every cell of
[r_{0},R]
, together with the positivity and
monotonicity bounds for
Q_{0}
and
Q_{0}^{\prime}
stated above;

(iii)

the tail conditions (
E.22
) and
(
E.30
);

(iv)

the nonsingularity of every matrix in
J
and the matching
inclusion (
E.33
).

Then the unique matching pair in
X
represents the positive radial
ground state
Q
. Its values and radial derivatives are enclosed on

[0,R]
by the origin series, the validated continuation, and the
differential equation. On
[R,\infty)
, the first three orders satisfy
(
E.26
), and further derivatives at positive radii
are enclosed by differentiating the differential equation.

Proof.

The origin, continuation, and tail arguments construct both solutions
with the stated enclosures, and the interval Newton inclusion gives
their unique matching pair. The sign conditions make the joined
solution positive, while its regularity and decay place it in

H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
; the uniqueness theorem
[
32
]
then identifies it
with
Q
. The derivative enclosures follow from the series and from the
differential identities already proved.
∎

Ground-State Construction and Downstream Evaluators

The program
groundstate/validate_groundstate.cpp
carries out
an independent validation using CAPD
[
26
]
. The origin
values and their parameter derivatives are initialized, with

\tilde{r}_{0}=r_{0}
, by the recurrences
(
E.8
) through order
20
, with the remainders
(
E.10
). The continuation starts at

r_{0}=1/512
and ends at
R=15
; its Taylor order is
20
, with an
interval remainder coefficient of order
21
. The four-dimensional
system is integrated over the search interval for
a
to enclose the
matching Jacobian , and the two-dimensional system is integrated at the
parameter center to enclose the matching residual. After the interval
Newton test, the two-dimensional flow is recomputed for the enclosed
matching values of
a
and exported on
184
radial cells.

The exact search box
X=[a]\times\Gamma
, polynomial coefficients,
and all interval endpoints are stored as hexadecimal binary numbers in
the certificate. The following decimals are outward enclosures of the
recorded search intervals:

\begin{split}[a]&\subset[2.206200864650349,2.206200864651151],\\
\Gamma&\subset[2.700000000000000,2.900000000000001].\end{split}

(E.35)

The interval Jacobian satisfies

\displaystyle J_{11}

\displaystyle\in[-112452.500021809,-112452.500021327],

(E.36)

\displaystyle J_{12}

\displaystyle\in[-9.819536482400306\cdot 10^{-8},-9.819536482380195\cdot 10^{-8}],

\displaystyle J_{21}

\displaystyle\in[-108636.937664357,-108636.937663879],

\displaystyle J_{22}

\displaystyle\in[1.014172936975801\cdot 10^{-7},1.014172936977937\cdot 10^{-7}].

Its determinant is contained in

[-0.022072271949077,-0.022072271948935]
, so every matrix in the
Jacobian enclosure is nonsingular. Direct interval inversion gives an
interval Newton image whose outward decimal enclosure is

\displaystyle K\subset{}

\displaystyle[2.206200864650683,2.206200864650809]

(E.37)

\displaystyle\times[2.737760223369866,2.876254800126656]\subset\operatorname{int}X.

The last inclusion is verified against the exact stored endpoints of

X
. The matching residual, the four Jacobian intervals, and both
strict inclusion margins are recorded in

groundstate/groundstate_certificate.txt
.

The tail fixed-point ball has radius
z=4
. On the search interval,
its self-mapping margin is greater than
0.365389
, and its contraction
constant satisfies
\theta_{R}<1.764\cdot 10^{-13}
. The matching
calculation retains
16
terms in the Bessel expansions, together with
the first omitted term as remainder. At
R
, the integral from
R
to

r
in (
E.20
) vanishes. The remaining integral gives

|Q_{\infty}(R)-\gamma K_{0}(R)|,\ |Q_{\infty}^{\prime}(R)+\gamma K_{1}(R)|\leq\frac{k_{0}i_{0}z^{3}}{4R^{3/2}}e^{-3R}.

The analogous errors for
Z(R)
and
Z^{\prime}(R)
are bounded by replacing

z^{3}
by
3z^{2}C_{Z}
. These are the tail errors included in the matching
residual and Jacobian.

The origin positivity bound in (
E.34
) is
greater than
2.2061885>1
. Subdivision of every continuation cell
gives the uniform bounds
Q>2.61122\cdot 10^{-7}
and

-Q_{r}>2.70797\cdot 10^{-7}
throughout
[r_{0},R]
; the origin and tail
arguments prove the corresponding signs on the remaining intervals.
The matched solution therefore is the positive radial ground state,
by Proposition
E.4
. For all subsequent evaluations,
we restrict the parameter intervals to the coordinate projections of the
interval Newton image. In particular, the exterior constants use the
upper endpoint of the new interval for
\gamma
. For its exterior derivatives,
(
E.26
) gives the conservative constants

C_{Q,0}=4,\qquad C_{Q,1}<3.694973.

The common evaluator
common/profile_eval.hpp
uses the exported
Taylor coefficients and their order-
21
remainder on each finite
cell. A query crossing a cell boundary is split at that boundary, and
the resulting interval values are combined by their interval hull.
The basic origin evaluator uses
S_{Q,10}
on
[0,r_{0}]
; the potential
and quotient evaluators use
A_{0},\ldots,A_{40}
for
r\leq 1/2
, and
the spline derivative evaluator uses
A_{0},\ldots,A_{80}
there. These
orders for
Q
require only
br^{2}<1
, which holds at
r=1/2
;
the stronger condition involving
b_{P}
is used only for the initial
sensitivity series near
r_{0}
. Each evaluation includes its differentiated
geometric remainder. These orders are distinct from the order-
20
initialization and Taylor flow.
For
r\geq R
, the common evaluator retains ten Bessel terms and
bounds both the omitted linear terms and the nonlinear correction in
(
E.23
)–(
E.25
).
Higher derivatives away from zero follow from the exact differential
equation. In particular, no derivative of an unspecified Taylor
remainder enters the potential, residual, or quotient calculation.

E.2
Approximation of the Potential

We now transfer the enclosures of the exact radial profile to a
bilinear approximation of
W
. The interval evaluations enclose the
true potential and its derivatives, including the uncertainty in
Q
.
Let
0<d<L
, let
\Omega=(-L,L)^{2}
, and define

\begin{gathered}\chi_{L,d}(y_{1},y_{2})=\theta\left(\frac{|y_{1}|-L+d}{d}\right)\theta\left(\frac{|y_{2}|-L+d}{d}\right),\\
\theta(t)=\begin{cases}1,&t\leq 0,\\
1-10t^{3}+15t^{4}-6t^{5},&0<t<1,\\
0,&t\geq 1.\end{cases}\end{gathered}

(E.38)

The values and the derivatives up to order two agree at both joins of

\theta
, and the factors are constant near
y_{i}=0
. Thus

\chi_{L,d}\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
,
\chi_{L,d}
equals one on

[-L+d,L-d]^{2}
, and it vanishes outside
\overline{\Omega}
. Moreover,

0\leq\chi_{L,d}\leq 1,\qquad|\partial_{y_{i}}\chi_{L,d}|\leq\frac{15}{8d},\qquad|\partial_{y_{i}}^{2}\chi_{L,d}|\leq\frac{10\sqrt{3}}{3d^{2}},\qquad|\partial_{y_{1}}\partial_{y_{2}}\chi_{L,d}|\leq\frac{225}{64d^{2}}.

(E.39)

Indeed,
\theta^{\prime}=-30t^{2}(1-t)^{2}
on
(0,1)
, and maximizing

|\theta^{\prime\prime}|=60t(1-t)|1-2t|
gives
10\sqrt{3}/3
.

Set
H=\chi_{L,d}W
. On a rectangular mesh of
\overline{\Omega}
, we
choose at each node an exact rational number
w_{ij}
from the
validated enclosure of
H(y_{1,i},y_{2,j})
, with zero boundary
values, and we let
W_{h}
be their bilinear interpolant, extended by
zero outside
\Omega
. More explicitly, on

K=[y_{1,i},y_{1,i+1}]\times[y_{2,j},y_{2,j+1}]
, put

s=\frac{y_{1}-y_{1,i}}{h_{1,K}},\qquad t=\frac{y_{2}-y_{2,j}}{h_{2,K}}.

On
K
, the bilinear interpolant is

\displaystyle W_{h}(y_{1},y_{2})={}

\displaystyle(1-s)(1-t)w_{ij}+s(1-t)w_{i+1,j}

\displaystyle+(1-s)tw_{i,j+1}+stw_{i+1,j+1}.

The four coefficients are nonnegative and sum to one on
K
, and
adjacent cells have the same interpolated trace on their common edge.
Thus
W_{h}
is a bounded continuous function, and hence
it defines a bounded multiplication operator. The boundary conditions
introduced below apply to the functions on which the differential
operator acts.

Proposition E.5
.

Let
h_{1,K},h_{2,K}
be the side lengths of a mesh cell
K
.
Suppose that the validated profile gives bounds

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\mathrm{cut},K}

\displaystyle\geq\|(1-\chi_{L,d})W\|_{L^{\infty}(K)},

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\mathrm{interp},K}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{h_{1,K}^{2}}{8}\|\partial_{y_{1}}^{2}H\|_{L^{\infty}(K)}+\frac{h_{2,K}^{2}}{8}\|\partial_{y_{2}}^{2}H\|_{L^{\infty}(K)},

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\mathrm{node},K}

\displaystyle\geq\max_{v\text{ vertex of }K}\ \sup_{z\in[H(v)]}|z-w_{v}|.

Then

\|W-W_{h}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}\leq\varepsilon_{\Omega},\qquad\varepsilon_{\Omega}=\max_{K}\bigl(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{cut},K}+\varepsilon_{\mathrm{interp},K}+\varepsilon_{\mathrm{node},K}\bigr).

(E.40)

Suppose also that
L\geq R>0
and that the tail certificate gives

|Q(r)|\leq C_{0}\frac{e^{-r}}{\sqrt{r}},\qquad|Q_{r}(r)|\leq C_{1}\frac{e^{-r}}{\sqrt{r}}\qquad(r\geq R).

With
\beta_{W}=2-\alpha_{1}^{-1}>0
and

E=\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\overline{\Omega}
, we have

\|W\|_{L^{\infty}(E)}\leq\varepsilon_{E},\qquad\varepsilon_{E}=\frac{(3C_{0}^{2}+6\alpha_{1}C_{0}C_{1})e^{-2L}+6\alpha_{1}C_{0}C_{1}e^{-\beta_{W}L}}{L}.

(E.41)

Proof.

For
f\in C^{2}([a,b])
, let
If
denote its linear interpolant at
a

and
b
. At an interior point
t
, Rolle’s theorem applied twice to

f-If-c(\,\cdot-a)(\,\cdot-b)
, with
c
chosen so that this function
vanishes at
t
, gives

f(t)-If(t)=\frac{1}{2}f^{\prime\prime}(\xi)(t-a)(t-b)\quad\text{for some }\xi\in(a,b).

Since
|(t-a)(t-b)|\leq(b-a)^{2}/4
, the uniform error is at most

(b-a)^{2}\|f^{\prime\prime}\|_{L^{\infty}}/8
. For a
C^{2}
function on a rectangle, we
write the bilinear interpolant as
I_{h}f=I_{1}I_{2}f
, where
I_{i}

interpolates in
y_{i}
. Each
I_{i}
is a convex combination of endpoint
values, so that it does not increase the uniform norm, and the
decomposition
f-I_{h}f=(f-I_{1}f)+I_{1}(f-I_{2}f)
therefore gives

\|f-I_{h}f\|_{L^{\infty}(K)}\leq\frac{h_{1,K}^{2}}{8}\|\partial_{y_{1}}^{2}f\|_{L^{\infty}(K)}+\frac{h_{2,K}^{2}}{8}\|\partial_{y_{2}}^{2}f\|_{L^{\infty}(K)}.

The four bilinear basis functions are nonnegative and sum to one;
replacing the exact nodal values of
H
by
w_{v}
thus contributes at
most
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{node},K}
on
K
. Applying these estimates
to

W-W_{h}=(1-\chi_{L,d})W+(H-I_{h}H)+(I_{h}H-W_{h})

and taking the maximum of the cellwise sums, we obtain
(
E.40
).

On
E
, we have
r\geq L
,

|Q_{y_{1}}|=|Q_{r}y_{1}/r|\leq|Q_{r}|
, and

e^{-y_{1}/\alpha_{1}}\leq e^{r/\alpha_{1}}
. Consequently,

|W|\leq\frac{3C_{0}^{2}e^{-2r}+6\alpha_{1}C_{0}C_{1}(e^{-2r}+e^{-\beta_{W}r})}{r}.

Since both
e^{-2r}/r
and
e^{-\beta_{W}r}/r
decrease for
r>0
, this
proves (
E.41
).
∎

Every upper bound used in this certificate is rounded upward. In
particular, the nodal error includes the distance to both endpoints of

[H(v)]
, even when the chosen exact rational value
w_{v}
is not its
exact midpoint. Subdivision sharpens the derivative and cutoff bounds,
since it pairs the growing exponential factor with local profile
bounds before the maxima are taken.

The Cartesian derivatives needed for the interpolation can be evaluated
explicitly. Set
U=Q^{2}
,
b=e^{-y_{1}/\alpha_{1}}
, and
a=1+b
. Then

\displaystyle W

\displaystyle=-3U+3\alpha_{1}a\partial_{y_{1}}U,

\displaystyle\partial_{y_{1}}W

\displaystyle=-3\partial_{y_{1}}U+3\alpha_{1}a\partial_{y_{1}}^{2}U-3b\partial_{y_{1}}U,

\displaystyle\partial_{y_{2}}W

\displaystyle=-3\partial_{y_{2}}U+3\alpha_{1}a\partial_{y_{1}}\partial_{y_{2}}U,

\displaystyle\partial_{y_{1}}^{2}W

\displaystyle=-(3+6b)\partial_{y_{1}}^{2}U+3\alpha_{1}a\partial_{y_{1}}^{3}U+\frac{3b}{\alpha_{1}}\partial_{y_{1}}U,

(E.42)

\displaystyle\partial_{y_{2}}^{2}W

\displaystyle=-3\partial_{y_{2}}^{2}U+3\alpha_{1}a\partial_{y_{1}}\partial_{y_{2}}^{2}U,

\displaystyle\partial_{y_{1}}\partial_{y_{2}}W

\displaystyle=-(3+3b)\partial_{y_{1}}\partial_{y_{2}}U+3\alpha_{1}a\partial_{y_{1}}^{2}\partial_{y_{2}}U,

and the product rule gives

\partial_{y_{i}}^{2}H=\chi_{L,d}\partial_{y_{i}}^{2}W+2(\partial_{y_{i}}\chi_{L,d})(\partial_{y_{i}}W)+(\partial_{y_{i}}^{2}\chi_{L,d})W.

At the origin, the regular series avoids the division by
r
: writing

U(y)=p(y_{1}^{2}+y_{2}^{2})
locally, where
p
is the analytic function
obtained by squaring the series for
Q
, the chain rule gives

\displaystyle\partial_{y_{i}}U

\displaystyle=2y_{i}p^{\prime},

\displaystyle\partial_{y_{i}}\partial_{y_{j}}U

\displaystyle=2\delta_{ij}p^{\prime}+4y_{i}y_{j}p^{\prime\prime},

\displaystyle\partial_{y_{i}}\partial_{y_{j}}\partial_{y_{k}}U

\displaystyle=4(\delta_{ij}y_{k}+\delta_{ik}y_{j}+\delta_{jk}y_{i})p^{\prime\prime}+8y_{i}y_{j}y_{k}p^{\prime\prime\prime}.

The order-
40
origin evaluator encloses the required derivatives in

s=r^{2}
by the series and its differentiated geometric majorant. Away
from zero, the enclosed
Q
,
Q_{r}
and the identities

Q_{rr}=Q-Q^{3}-\frac{Q_{r}}{r},\qquad Q_{rrr}=(1-3Q^{2})Q_{r}-\frac{Q_{rr}}{r}+\frac{Q_{r}}{r^{2}}

give all the radial derivatives needed here, and the conversion to
Cartesian coordinates then uses the chain rule; for example,

\partial_{y_{i}}\partial_{y_{j}}U=U_{rr}\frac{y_{i}y_{j}}{r^{2}}+\frac{U_{r}}{r}\left(\delta_{ij}-\frac{y_{i}y_{j}}{r^{2}}\right).

Thus the nonradial exponential in
W
is retained in every derivative
bound, and the profile uncertainty remains in each interval evaluation.

The Recorded Potential Certificate

The recorded CAPD potential calculation uses (
E.40
)
with the square and cutoff parameters
L=16
and
d=2
, and with a mesh
of
1409
nodes per axis, of spacing
1/128
on
[-4,4]
,
1/32
for

4\leq|y_{i}|\leq 8
, and
1/8
for
8\leq|y_{i}|\leq 16
. The profile tail
enters through
R=15
,
C_{0}=4
, and
C_{1}<3.694973
. The
resulting outward-rounded upper bounds are

\displaystyle\|W_{h}\|_{L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\leq 36.609366495687,

\displaystyle\max_{K}\varepsilon_{\mathrm{node},K}

\displaystyle\leq 8.226155064000\cdot 10^{-8},

\displaystyle\max_{K}\varepsilon_{\mathrm{interp},K}

\displaystyle\leq 0.004231045898735,

\displaystyle\max_{K}\varepsilon_{\mathrm{cut},K}

\displaystyle\leq 1.121028250570\cdot 10^{-6},

and, in (
E.40
) and (
E.41
),

\varepsilon_{\Omega}\leq 0.004231045907326,\qquad\varepsilon_{E}\leq 5.376665828\cdot 10^{-7}.

The specified nodal values are retained as exact dyadic numbers.
The exact nodal values and the interval output are recorded in

spectrum/data/potential128_nodes.txt

potential/results/potential128_certificate.txt
.

Since the separate maxima
of the interpolation, nodal, and cutoff errors need not occur on the
same cell, the bound
\varepsilon_{\Omega}
uses the maximum of their
cellwise sums, as in (
E.40
).

E.3
Spectral Bounds

The spectral information on
\mathcal{L}
is obtained in two steps. We
first bound eigenvalues of the operator with the explicit potential

W_{h}
on the square
\Omega
, with Dirichlet and with Neumann boundary
conditions, by finite-dimensional tests; we then transfer these bounds
to
\mathcal{L}
on the whole space by Dirichlet–Neumann bracketing,
using the potential errors of Section
E.2
.

Bounded-Domain Problems

On
L^{2}(\Omega)
, let
T_{h}^{D}
and
T_{h}^{N}
be the self-adjoint
operators associated with the form

a_{h}(u,u)=3\|\partial_{y_{1}}u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}+\|\partial_{y_{2}}u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}+\int_{\Omega}(\kappa_{\alpha_{1}}+W_{h})|u|^{2},

(E.43)

on
H_{0}^{1}(\Omega)
and on
H^{1}(\Omega)
, respectively. The form is
closed and bounded from below, since
W_{h}
is bounded, and after the
addition of a sufficiently large constant its norm is equivalent to
the
H^{1}
norm; the compactness of the embedding of either form domain
into
L^{2}(\Omega)
therefore gives compact resolvents. On this
rectangle, odd and even reflections in the sides reduce the Dirichlet
and the Neumann problems to periodic Fourier expansions, so that the
sine and cosine products introduced below form orthonormal bases of

L^{2}(\Omega)
, and the operator domains are

H^{2}(\Omega)\cap H_{0}^{1}(\Omega)
and

\{u\in H^{2}(\Omega):(3\partial_{y_{1}}u,\partial_{y_{2}}u)\cdot n=0\text{ on each boundary edge}\}
, where
n
is the outward unit normal;
indeed, the summability of the squared kinetic eigenvalues times the
squared coefficients is precisely
H^{2}
regularity with the indicated
boundary conditions, and bounded multiplication by

\kappa_{\alpha_{1}}+W_{h}
preserves these domains. We denote the
eigenvalues, in increasing order and counted with multiplicity, by

\mu_{k}^{D}
and
\mu_{k}^{N}
. The two boundary conditions play different
roles: the Dirichlet problem supplies upper bounds, while the Neumann
problem controls the number of eigenvalues below a prescribed level.

The form version of the min–max principle

[
29
, Theorem I.3 and Corollary I.2]
gives

\mu_{k}^{D}=\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\subset H_{0}^{1}(\Omega)\\
\dim S=k\end{subarray}}\ \sup_{0\neq u\in S}\frac{a_{h}(u,u)}{\|u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}},

(E.44)

and replacing
H_{0}^{1}(\Omega)
by
H^{1}(\Omega)
gives the Neumann
formula. Let
\psi_{1},\ldots,\psi_{m}\in H_{0}^{1}(\Omega)
be linearly
independent finite linear combinations, with rational coefficients, of
the orthonormal Dirichlet products
s_{m}(y_{1})s_{n}(y_{2})
, where

s_{j}(t)=L^{-1/2}\sin(j\pi(t+L)/(2L))
for
j\geq 1
, and define

\mathbf{K}_{ij}=a_{h}(\psi_{i},\psi_{j}),\qquad\mathbf{M}_{ij}=(\psi_{i},\psi_{j})_{L^{2}(\Omega)}.

(E.45)

If
d_{k}
is a real number and
\mathbf{K}-d_{k}\mathbf{M}
has at least
k

negative eigenvalues, then

\mu_{k}^{D}<d_{k}.

(E.46)

Indeed,
\xi^{T}\mathbf{M}\xi=\|\sum_{i}\xi_{i}\psi_{i}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}
, so that

\mathbf{M}>0
and the coefficient map is injective. A
k
-dimensional
negative subspace of
\mathbf{K}-d_{k}\mathbf{M}
therefore gives a

k
-dimensional space of trial functions on which the Rayleigh quotient
is strictly below
d_{k}
, and its maximum on the compact unit sphere of
that space is strictly below
d_{k}
; (
E.44
) then proves
(
E.46
). We use two trial functions for
k=2
.
Sine orthogonality gives the mass and the kinetic terms, while the
identity
\sin A\sin B=(\cos(A-B)-\cos(A+B))/2
reduces the potential
terms to polynomial–cosine integrals on the mesh cells; the finite
moment formulas below enclose these integrals, including every Taylor
remainder and arithmetic rounding.

For the lower bound, we use the Schur complement of a finite cosine
space; this is the elementary form of the Feshbach–Schur method, see

[
17
,
16
]
. The argument below includes the eigenvalue
count, so that the index of the eigenvalue being bounded is part of the
certificate. Let

c_{0}(t)=(2L)^{-1/2},\qquad c_{j}(t)=L^{-1/2}\cos\frac{j\pi(t+L)}{2L}\quad(j\geq 1).

The functions
\phi_{mn}(y)=c_{m}(y_{1})c_{n}(y_{2})
form an orthonormal
Neumann basis, and for
H_{0}=-3\partial_{y_{1}}^{2}-\partial_{y_{2}}^{2}
their
eigenvalues are

d_{mn}=\frac{\pi^{2}}{4L^{2}}(3m^{2}+n^{2}).

Fix integers
N_{1},N_{2}\geq 0
, and let
P
be the orthogonal projection
onto the modes with
0\leq m\leq N_{1}
and
0\leq n\leq N_{2}
. Write

P^{\perp}=I-P
, let
M=(N_{1}+1)(N_{2}+1)
, and assume that
M\geq 3
. The
modes omitted from
P
are those with
m>N_{1}
or
n>N_{2}
, and their
least kinetic eigenvalue is

d_{*}=\frac{\pi^{2}}{4L^{2}}\min\{3(N_{1}+1)^{2},(N_{2}+1)^{2}\}.

(E.47)

We index the retained modes by
i,j\in\{1,\ldots,M\}
and define the
real symmetric matrices

\displaystyle\mathbf{V}_{ij}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}W_{h}\phi_{i}\phi_{j},

\displaystyle\mathbf{C}_{ij}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}W_{h}^{2}\phi_{i}\phi_{j},

(E.48)

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}_{ij}

\displaystyle=(d_{i}+\kappa_{\alpha_{1}})\delta_{ij}+\mathbf{V}_{ij},

\displaystyle\mathbf{G}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{C}-\mathbf{V}^{2}.

For every vector
z
, with
p=\sum_{i}z_{i}\phi_{i}
, orthonormality gives

z^{T}\mathbf{C}z=\|W_{h}p\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}
and

z^{T}\mathbf{V}^{2}z=\|P(W_{h}p)\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}
, and Pythagoras’ theorem therefore
yields

z^{T}\mathbf{G}z=\|P^{\perp}(W_{h}p)\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}.

(E.49)

In particular,
\mathbf{G}
is positive semidefinite. We choose a
certified upper bound
V_{*}\geq\|W_{h}\|_{L^{\infty}}
and a rational number

\tau
satisfying

\tau\leq d_{*}+\kappa_{\alpha_{1}}-V_{*}.

(E.50)

The maximum of the absolute nodal values is a valid choice for
V_{*}
,
since bilinear interpolation is a convex combination of those values.
Thus
\tau
bounds from below the operator restricted to the
complementary Fourier space, as proved below. The word “omitted”
refers only to the exclusion from the finite projection: these modes
are controlled by
\tau
and by the Schur correction, rather than
discarded.

Proposition E.6
.

Let
t<\tau
, and put

\mathbf{F}(t)=\mathbf{A}-tI-\frac{\mathbf{G}}{\tau-t}.

(E.51)

If
\mathbf{F}(t)
has at most
k-1
negative eigenvalues, where

1\leq k\leq M
, then

\mu_{k}^{N}\geq t.

(E.52)

Proof.

The decomposition
L^{2}(\Omega)=\operatorname{Ran}P\oplus\operatorname{Ran}P^{\perp}
gives

T_{h}^{N}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{A}&B^{*}\\
B&D\end{pmatrix},\qquad B=P^{\perp}W_{h}P,\qquad D=P^{\perp}T_{h}^{N}P^{\perp}\big|_{\operatorname{Ran}P^{\perp}},

where the operator
D
is defined by restricting the form to

H^{1}(\Omega)\cap\operatorname{Ran}P^{\perp}
. Since
P
commutes with

H_{0}
, (
E.47
) and (
E.50
) imply

D\geq\tau I
, so that
D-t
has a positive bounded inverse. Functional
calculus gives
\|(D-t)^{-1/2}\|\leq(\tau-t)^{-1/2}
and

\|(D-t)^{1/2}(D-t)^{-1}v\|\leq(\tau-t)^{-1/2}\|v\|
; hence this
inverse maps
L^{2}
into the form domain of
D
. Completing the square,
for
p\in\operatorname{Ran}P
and
q
in that form domain, we obtain

\displaystyle a_{h}(p+q,p+q)-t\|p+q\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}

\displaystyle=\bigl((\mathbf{A}-tI-B^{*}(D-t)^{-1}B)p,p\bigr)

\displaystyle\quad+\bigl\|(D-t)^{1/2}(q+(D-t)^{-1}Bp)\bigr\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}.

The change of variables
(p,q)\mapsto(p,q+(D-t)^{-1}Bp)
is invertible
on the form domain, and therefore the negative index of
T_{h}^{N}-t

equals that of
\mathbf{S}(t)=\mathbf{A}-tI-B^{*}(D-t)^{-1}B
. Since

0<(D-t)^{-1}\leq(\tau-t)^{-1}I
and
B^{*}B=\mathbf{G}
by
(
E.49
), we have
\mathbf{S}(t)\geq\mathbf{F}(t)
.
Consequently, every subspace on which
\mathbf{S}(t)
is negative
definite is a negative subspace for
\mathbf{F}(t)
, and hence

\#\{j:\mu_{j}^{N}<t\}=\operatorname{ind}_{-}\mathbf{S}(t)\leq\operatorname{ind}_{-}\mathbf{F}(t)\leq k-1,

which is precisely (
E.52
).
∎

The finite matrices in (
E.48
) are enclosed by
integrating the same exact
W_{h}
as in the potential comparison.
Products of two cosines reduce to sums of cosines, and the cell
restrictions of
W_{h}
and
W_{h}^{2}
have degree at most one and two,
respectively, in each variable. The interval Taylor moments and the
normwise inertia test below give the corresponding finite certificate.

The Whole-Space Spectral Gap

The estimates just proved concern operators on a bounded square; their
connection with
\mathcal{L}
follows from the comparison of form
domains in Dirichlet–Neumann bracketing, see

[
29
, Section II.2.1]
. We state the complete implication
needed for the inverse estimates.

Proposition E.7
.

Suppose that the potential certificate gives

\|W-W_{h}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}\leq\varepsilon_{\Omega}
and

\|W\|_{L^{\infty}(E)}\leq\varepsilon_{E}
.
Let
\overline{\mu}_{2}^{D}
and
\underline{\mu}_{3}^{N}
be certified bounds
for the second Dirichlet and the third Neumann eigenvalues of the
operators in (
E.43
). Assume that

\overline{\mu}_{2}^{D}+\varepsilon_{\Omega}<0,\qquad\underline{\mu}_{3}^{N}-\varepsilon_{\Omega}>0,\qquad\kappa_{\alpha_{1}}-\varepsilon_{E}>0.

(E.53)

Then
\mathcal{L}
has exactly two negative eigenvalues, counted with
multiplicity,
0\in\rho(\mathcal{L})
, and

\operatorname{dist}(0,\sigma(\mathcal{L}))\geq\delta_{0},\quad\delta_{0}=\min\left\{-\overline{\mu}_{2}^{D}-\varepsilon_{\Omega},\underline{\mu}_{3}^{N}-\varepsilon_{\Omega},\kappa_{\alpha_{1}}-\varepsilon_{E}\right\}>0.

(E.54)

Consequently,

\|\mathcal{L}^{-1}\|_{L^{2}\to L^{2}}\leq\delta_{0}^{-1}.

(E.55)

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{D}
,
\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{N}
, and

\mathcal{L}_{E}^{N}
denote the interior Dirichlet, the interior Neumann,
and the exterior Neumann realizations with the exact potential
W
.
Writing
\mathfrak{l}_{\Omega}
for the exact interior form, on either
interior form domain we have

\bigl|\mathfrak{l}_{\Omega}[u]-a_{h}(u,u)\bigr|=\left|\int_{\Omega}(W-W_{h})|u|^{2}\right|\leq\varepsilon_{\Omega}\|u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}.

Taking the supremum on each trial space and then the infimum in
(
E.44
) proves that

|\lambda_{k}(\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{D,N})-\mu_{k}^{D,N}|\leq\varepsilon_{\Omega}.

Since zero extension embeds
H_{0}^{1}(\Omega)

into
H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, the second whole-space min–max value obeys

\lambda_{2}(\mathcal{L})\leq\lambda_{2}(\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{D})\leq\overline{\mu}_{2}^{D}+\varepsilon_{\Omega}<0.

As the essential spectrum starts at the positive number

\kappa_{\alpha_{1}}
, this produces two negative eigenvalues and places
both at or below
\overline{\mu}_{2}^{D}+\varepsilon_{\Omega}
.

Conversely, the restriction of a whole-space
H^{1}
function gives an
element of
H^{1}(\Omega)\oplus H^{1}(E)
, a space which also admits
independently chosen traces on the common boundary. Its quadratic form
is the same integral on this larger domain, so that

\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{N}\oplus\mathcal{L}_{E}^{N}\leq\mathcal{L}

in the sense of forms: the enlarged form domain admits every
whole-space trial space, and its min–max infimum can only decrease.
The exterior Neumann operator satisfies

\mathcal{L}_{E}^{N}\geq\kappa_{\alpha_{1}}-\varepsilon_{E}
, and the third
eigenvalue of
\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{N}
is at least

\underline{\mu}_{3}^{N}-\varepsilon_{\Omega}
. Set

\eta_{+}=\min\{\underline{\mu}_{3}^{N}-\varepsilon_{\Omega},\kappa_{\alpha_{1}}-\varepsilon_{E}\}>0.

The decoupled operator has at most two spectral directions strictly
below
\eta_{+}
, and the min–max principle therefore gives

\lambda_{3}(\mathcal{L})\geq\eta_{+}
, where a min–max value is allowed to
equal the bottom of the essential spectrum. Consequently, there are
exactly two negative eigenvalues, and the remaining spectrum is
contained in
[\eta_{+},\infty)
. Combining the negative and the positive
separations proves (
E.54
). Finally, functional
calculus gives

\|\mathcal{L}^{-1}\|=\sup_{\lambda\in\sigma(\mathcal{L})}|\lambda|^{-1}\leq\delta_{0}^{-1},

which proves (
E.55
).
∎

Recall that Remark
B.9
supplies a negative direction in
each parity sector with respect to
y_{2}
. The lowest eigenvalue is
simple and has a positive eigenfunction: under the unitary change of
variables
(Uf)(x)=3^{1/4}f(\sqrt{3}x_{1},x_{2})
, the operator

\mathcal{L}-\kappa_{\alpha_{1}}
becomes
-\Delta+W(\sqrt{3}x_{1},x_{2})
, to
which
[
29
, Theorem II.7]
applies, since its lowest
eigenvalue lies below its essential spectrum and the transformed
potential is real, bounded, and tends to zero.
Proposition
E.7
then leaves precisely one negative
direction in each parity sector. Reflection preserves the positive
normalized lowest eigenfunction, and simplicity makes it even; the
other negative eigenfunction is odd, and the two negative eigenvalues
are distinct and simple.

The Recorded Spectral Certificate

The spectral source specifies the following inputs: the square
L=16

and the modes
(N_{1},N_{2})=(48,84)
, so that
M=4165
; the spectral
thresholds
t=80/128=5/8
and
d_{2}=-5/2
; the kinetic level

d_{*}=7203\pi^{2}/1024
; the block bound

\tau=[d_{*}+\kappa_{\alpha_{1}}-V_{*}]^{-}>33
with

V_{*}=\max_{i,j}|w_{ij}|
; and the moment-series order
J=48
used in
the interval implementation below. The endpoint in the definition of

\tau
is taken after outward interval evaluation, so that
\tau
is an
exact dyadic lower bound, and the construction requires
\tau>t
before
the division by
\tau-t
. For the selected inputs, outward scalar
evaluation establishes
\tau>33>t
, independently of the matrix tests;
the C++ replay checks this strict inequality before the division. The
actual value of
\tau
is written to the spectral transcript, together
with the potential digest and the matrix error bounds.

The finite matrices are large, and the certificate represents them by
floating-point centers with normwise error radii. We first fix the
rounding model and the propagation rules, then describe the enclosure
of the Fourier matrices, and finally the inertia tests and the recorded
bounds.

Floating-point model and propagation rules.

A matrix enclosure is represented by a pair
(A_{0},e_{A})
satisfying

\|A-A_{0}\|_{F}\leq e_{A}
, where every entry of
A_{0}
is a binary64
number interpreted as its exact dyadic value. Here
\|\cdot\|_{F}

denotes the Frobenius norm and
\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{op}}
the Euclidean
operator norm of a matrix. All scalar error bounds in what follows are
evaluated with outward interval arithmetic.

We first justify the arithmetic bounds used in the finite assembly.
Set
u_{\rm fp}=2^{-53}
,
\lambda_{\rm fp}=2^{-1022}
, and

\gamma_{j}=ju_{\rm fp}/(1-ju_{\rm fp})
, with
ju_{\rm fp}<1/4
.
The ordinary floating-point operations use rounding to nearest, gradual
underflow, and no overflow, so that each rounded operation, including a
fused multiply-add when one is used, has the representation

\operatorname{fl}(z)=z(1+\epsilon)+\zeta,\qquad|\epsilon|\leq u_{\rm fp},\qquad|\zeta|\leq u_{\rm fp}\lambda_{\rm fp}.

When the exact result lies in the normal range, the additive term may
be taken equal to zero; in the subnormal range, the absolute rounding
error is at most half the least positive subnormal number, namely

u_{\rm fp}\lambda_{\rm fp}
. Expanding a classical dot product into
its rounded operations gives one factor
1+\vartheta
on each exact
product, with
|\vartheta|\leq\gamma_{j}
if its path contains at most

j
roundings; see
[
25
, Chapter 3]
. Each additive error
is amplified by at most
(1+u_{\rm fp})^{j}\leq(1-ju_{\rm fp})^{-1}
.
For a dot product of length
k
, we may take
j=2k+2
, and there are
at most
j
error terms, whose sum in absolute value is at most

ju_{\rm fp}\lambda_{\rm fp}/(1-ju_{\rm fp})<j\lambda_{\rm fp}
.
Thus the following deliberately larger matrix bound is valid:

\|\operatorname{fl}(A_{0}B_{0})-A_{0}B_{0}\|_{F}\leq\gamma_{2k+2}\|A_{0}\|_{F}\|B_{0}\|_{F}+(4k+4)mn\lambda_{\rm fp},

(E.56)

where
A_{0}
is
m\times k
and
B_{0}
is
k\times n
. Indeed, the
componentwise relative-error bound is
\gamma_{j}|A_{0}||B_{0}|
, whose
Frobenius norm is at most
\gamma_{j}\|A_{0}\|_{F}\|B_{0}\|_{F}
, while the
absolute-error contribution is at most

j\sqrt{mn}\lambda_{\rm fp}\leq 2jmn\lambda_{\rm fp}
. Reordering the
additions or using fused multiply-adds preserves these bounds for
classical dot products, whereas reduced-precision products and
algorithms with a different multiplication scheme are excluded.

For a stored array with
N
entries, let
s_{\rm fl}
be the computed
nonnegative sum of squares, and set
j=2N+2
. The same expansion has
at most
N
squarings and
N-1
nontrivial additions, and since the
exact squared terms are nonnegative, it gives

s_{\rm fl}\geq(1-\gamma_{j})\|A_{0}\|_{F}^{2}-j\lambda_{\rm fp}
.
Consequently,

\|A_{0}\|_{F}\leq\left(\frac{s_{\rm fl}+j\lambda_{\rm fp}}{1-\gamma_{j}}\right)^{1/2}.

(E.57)

All norms of stored centers below are bounded by this formula. The
finiteness of the computed entries and of the norm reductions, as well
as
ju_{\rm fp}<1/4
, are checked, and the arithmetic assumptions and
the BLAS implementation used are part of the recorded environment.

We now record the error-propagation rules used throughout. Write

n_{A}\geq\|A_{0}\|_{F}
and
n_{B}\geq\|B_{0}\|_{F}
, and let the output center
be the indicated floating-point operation. Valid output radii are

\displaystyle e_{AB}

\displaystyle=\gamma_{2k+2}n_{A}n_{B}+(4k+4)mn\lambda_{\rm fp}+e_{A}n_{B}+e_{B}n_{A}+e_{A}e_{B},

\displaystyle e_{A\pm B}

\displaystyle=e_{A}+e_{B}+\gamma_{2}(n_{A}+n_{B})+N\lambda_{\rm fp},

\displaystyle e_{sA}

\displaystyle=|s_{0}|e_{A}+r_{s}(n_{A}+e_{A})+\gamma_{2}|s_{0}|n_{A}+N\lambda_{\rm fp},\qquad|s-s_{0}|\leq r_{s},

where in the last two lines
N
is the number of output entries. For
the first line, we expand

AB-A_{0}B_{0}=(A-A_{0})B_{0}+A_{0}(B-B_{0})+(A-A_{0})(B-B_{0})

and use the submultiplicativity of the Frobenius norm together with
(
E.56
); the other two lines follow from the
same scalar rounding model, the triangle inequality, and

sA-s_{0}A_{0}=s_{0}(A-A_{0})+(s-s_{0})A
. For an entrywise product of two exact
dyadic arrays, the analogous radius is

\gamma_{2}n_{A}n_{B}+N\lambda_{\rm fp}
, since

\|A_{0}\mathbin{\circ}B_{0}\|_{F}\leq n_{A}n_{B}
. An entrywise interval array
is converted into a norm enclosure by choosing dyadic centers
a_{ij}

and outward radii
r_{ij}\geq\sup_{x\in[A_{ij}]}|x-a_{ij}|
, and then
using
e_{A}\geq(\sum_{ij}r_{ij}^{2})^{1/2}
.

Enclosure of the Fourier matrices.

For the cell integration, we write
x=a+hs
,
0\leq s\leq 1
, and

\sigma=\omega h
. The required moments are

\int_{0}^{1}s^{j}e^{i\sigma s}\,ds=\sum_{\ell=0}^{J}\frac{(i\sigma)^{\ell}}{\ell!(\ell+j+1)}+r_{j,J},\qquad|r_{j,J}|\leq e^{|\sigma|}\frac{|\sigma|^{J+1}}{(J+1)!},\quad 0\leq j\leq 2.

(E.58)

To prove this, we integrate the Taylor polynomial termwise; the
absolute remainder is bounded uniformly on
[0,1]
by

\sum_{\ell\geq J+1}|\sigma|^{\ell}/\ell!\leq e^{|\sigma|}|\sigma|^{J+1}/(J+1)!
, and multiplication by
s^{j}

and integration do not increase this bound. Multiplying by

he^{i\omega(a+L)}
and taking real parts gives the cosine moments.
Every phase, coefficient, sum, and remainder is enclosed by scalar
interval arithmetic.

The linear Bernstein functions
1-s
and
s
represent
W_{h}
exactly
on the cells, and the quadratic functions
(1-s)^{2}
,
2s(1-s)
,
s^{2}

represent
W_{h}^{2}
. If a cell has nodal values
a,b,c,d
at

(0,0),(1,0),(0,1),(1,1)
, the latter tensor coefficients, with rows
indexed by the first coordinate, are

\begin{pmatrix}a^{2}&ac&c^{2}\\
ab&(ad+bc)/2&cd\\
b^{2}&bd&d^{2}\end{pmatrix}.

This identity follows by squaring the bilinear polynomial; in
particular, the mixed coefficient retains both products
ad
and
bc
.
If
H_{x}
and
H_{y}
are the integrated linear hat matrices, the full
moment array for
W_{h}
is
H_{x}(w_{ij})H_{y}^{T}
, and for
W_{h}^{2}
the full
moment array is obtained by summing the nine products of the
corresponding integrated quadratic Bernstein matrices and their
cell-coefficient arrays. The preceding product, addition, scaling, and
entrywise-product bounds enclose these operations. Reflection symmetry,
when verified exactly in the nodal array, allows the odd moments in the
second coordinate to be set to zero, which cannot increase the error.

For completeness, the following bound controls the last product-to-sum
assembly. Let
(T_{0},e_{T})
enclose either full moment array, write

s_{i}
for the exact product of the one-dimensional basis normalization
constants, and choose dyadic
s_{i}^{0}
with

|s_{i}-s_{i}^{0}|\leq r_{s},\qquad s_{\max}\geq\max_{i}|s_{i}|,\qquad s_{0}^{\max}=\max_{i}|s_{i}^{0}|,\qquad m_{T}=\max_{p,q}|(T_{0})_{pq}|.

Each assembled entry has the form

\tfrac{1}{4}s_{i}s_{j}(T_{pq}+T_{p^{\prime}q}+T_{pq^{\prime}}+T_{p^{\prime}q^{\prime}})
for cosines, the two
middle signs being negative for sines; repeated indices are retained,
so that this formula includes the constant cosine modes. For an

M\times M
output, the implemented radius is

\begin{split}e_{\rm ass}={}&Ms_{\max}^{2}e_{T}+M\bigl(2s_{0}^{\max}r_{s}+r_{s}^{2}\bigr)m_{T}\\
&+\gamma_{10}M(s_{0}^{\max})^{2}m_{T}+20M^{2}\lambda_{\rm fp}.\end{split}

(E.59)

Indeed, each moment error is at most
e_{T}
, and the factor
1/4

cancels the four terms; the normalization error is bounded by

|s_{i}s_{j}-s_{i}^{0}s_{j}^{0}|\leq 2s_{0}^{\max}r_{s}+r_{s}^{2}
; and for the central
arithmetic there are three additions and three multiplications, so
that the relative-error expansion is bounded by

\gamma_{10}(s_{0}^{\max})^{2}m_{T}
. In this calculation,
L=16
and

|s_{i}^{0}|\leq 1
, hence none of these multiplications amplifies an
additive underflow error by a factor exceeding one, apart from the
rounding factors, and their combined contribution per entry is less
than
20\lambda_{\rm fp}
. Passing from a uniform entry bound to the
Frobenius norm multiplies by
M
, and the larger

20M^{2}\lambda_{\rm fp}
used in (
E.59
) is
therefore valid. This proves the radii
e_{V}
and
e_{C}
for the
assembled matrices.

The same rules enclose
\mathbf{G}=\mathbf{C}-\mathbf{V}^{2}
and

\mathbf{V}-\mathbf{G}/(\tau-t)
. The scalar bound
\tau>33>t
verifies
the hypothesis of Proposition
E.6
and makes the interval
reciprocal positive. To add the kinetic diagonal, each new diagonal
entry is evaluated as an interval and recentered; if the resulting
rounding radii are
r_{i}
, it is valid to add
(\sum_{i}r_{i}^{2})^{1/2}
to
the existing radius. Finally, the arithmetic operation

(F_{0}+F_{0}^{T})/2
, enclosed with the addition and scaling rules, gives an
exactly symmetric center for the exact symmetric matrix
\mathbf{F}(t)
.
Thus its radius includes the integration, normalization,
kinetic-diagonal, and symmetrization errors.

The inertia tests.

We use the following normwise congruence test. Let

\|H-H_{0}\|_{F}\leq e_{H}
with
H
and
H_{0}
symmetric, and let
R
and

D=\operatorname{diag}(d_{i})
be exact dyadic matrices proposed by an
ordinary eigensolver. We certify

q\geq\|R^{T}R-I\|_{\mathrm{op}},\quad q<1,\qquad b\geq\|R^{T}H_{0}R-D\|_{\mathrm{op}}+(1+q)e_{H}.

Here
q
is the
congruence invertibility defect
: since

(1-q)I\leq R^{T}R\leq(1+q)I
, the inequality
q<1
proves the
invertibility of
R
and
\|R\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}\leq 1+q
, and in particular

\|R^{T}(H-H_{0})R\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq(1+q)e_{H}.

The
congruence perturbation radius

b
therefore bounds

\|R^{T}HR-D\|_{\mathrm{op}}
, including the uncertainty in
H
. We define the

smallest signed margin
by

s_{*}:=\min_{i}(|d_{i}|-b).

If
s_{*}>0
, then
H
has exactly as many negative eigenvalues as
D
.
Indeed,
R^{T}HR
remains invertible along the straight line joining it
to
D
, since the smallest singular value of
D
exceeds the
perturbation norm; the inertia is constant on this path, and it is
preserved by the invertible congruence. Both displayed operator norms
are bounded from above by certified Frobenius norms, and the factor

1+q
retains the operator-norm estimate for
R
. Note that the margin

s_{*}
concerns the congruent matrix and certifies its inertia; it is
not itself a lower bound for an eigenvalue of the differential
operator. In each test,
q
and
b
are bounded upward and
s_{*}

downward.

For the Dirichlet bound, let
\widehat{H}_{D}
be the finite sine matrix
of
T_{h}^{D}-d_{2}
on the
48\cdot 84=4032
retained modes. The two columns
of an exact dyadic matrix
U
define the trial functions; interval
multiplication proves
q_{U}\geq\|U^{T}U-I\|_{\mathrm{op}}
with
q_{U}<1
, and hence
their mass matrix
\mathbf{M}=U^{T}U
is positive definite. If

\|\widehat{H}_{D}-H_{D,0}\|_{F}\leq e_{D}
, then

\mathbf{K}-d_{2}\mathbf{M}=U^{T}\widehat{H}_{D}U,\qquad\|U^{T}(\widehat{H}_{D}-H_{D,0})U\|_{F}\leq(1+q_{U})e_{D},

because
\|AXB\|_{F}\leq\|A\|_{\mathrm{op}}\|X\|_{F}\|B\|_{\mathrm{op}}
. The arithmetic error of
the two products with
H_{D,0}
and of the final symmetrization is
added according to the rules above, and the normwise inertia test is
then applied to this
2\times 2
enclosure. An ordinary eigensolver proposes
U
,
R
, and
D
; these are stored
as exact dyadic witnesses, and only the subsequent interval inequalities
enter the proof. The Neumann test acts
on
H=\mathbf{F}(5/8)
, of size
4165\times 4165
, and the Dirichlet
test acts on
H=\mathbf{K}+(5/2)\mathbf{M}
, of size
2\times 2
. Their
successful inertia tests give the explicit one-sided bounds

\mu_{1}^{D}\leq\mu_{2}^{D}<-\frac{5}{2},\qquad\mu_{3}^{N}\geq\frac{5}{8},

the first because two Ritz directions are negative at
d_{2}=-5/2
, and
the second because
\mathbf{F}(5/8)
has two negative directions. The
same certified upper threshold suffices for both Dirichlet eigenvalues,
and no separate sharper bound for the first eigenvalue is used. These
are eigenvalues of the bounded-domain operators with the potential

W_{h}
; including the potential errors in the whole-space comparison
gives

\displaystyle\delta_{0}

\displaystyle\geq\min\biggl\{\frac{5}{2}-0.004231045907326,\ \frac{5}{8}-0.004231045907326,

\displaystyle\frac{7701}{10201}-5.376665828\cdot 10^{-7}\biggr\}

\displaystyle=0.620768954092674>0.62.

Recorded bounds.

The C++ calculation reconstructs the Fourier matrices from the exact
potential nodes and encloses every arithmetic operation as described
above. For the Neumann Schur test, the proposed diagonal has two
negative and
4163
positive directions, and the certified bounds are

q<3.865263\cdot 10^{-9},\qquad b<8.373108\cdot 10^{-5},\qquad s_{*}>0.04395517893669.

For the Dirichlet trial test, there are two negative and no positive
directions, with

q<1.960305\cdot 10^{-15},\qquad b<9.966066\cdot 10^{-6},\qquad s_{*}>0.31005658808225.

The trial-space independence defect satisfies

q_{U}<1.792935\cdot 10^{-12}
. The matrix radii satisfy

e_{V}<1.028800\cdot 10^{-5},\qquad e_{C}<5.007202\cdot 10^{-4},\qquad e_{H}<7.014940\cdot 10^{-5}.

The threshold also satisfies
\tau-t>32.94513029376356
; its
positivity is checked before the reciprocal is formed. Together with
the fresh profile and potential comparison, these finite tests give

\operatorname{dist}(0,\sigma(\mathcal{L}))\geq 0.62076895409267>0.62,\qquad\|\mathcal{L}^{-1}\|_{L^{2}\to L^{2}}<1.610906.

The matrices
\mathbf{V}
and
\mathbf{C}
each have
4165^{2}
entries.
Their enclosures consist of the constructed dyadic centers and the
Frobenius radii
e_{V},e_{C}
, and the scalar margins above do not replace
those matrix data. The source
spectrum/spectrum_replay.cpp

constructs both matrices and the two congruence tests. Three
binary witness files store the exact congruence matrices and diagonal
proposals, and the two sine trial vectors. Their dimensions are

4165\times 4166
,
2\times 3
, and
4032\times 2
, respectively, the
first two appending the diagonal proposal to the congruence matrix.
They serve only as explicitly specified finite approximations; their
validation is given by the present interval tests. The transcript

spectrum/results/cpp_spectral_certificate.txt
records all
bounds, and its comparison input identifies the exact potential and
the freshly validated profile by SHA-256 digests.

E.4
The Constraint Matrix and the Virial Form

The spectral gap supplies the inverse bound needed for the constraint
matrix. For the functions in (
E.4
), we set

m_{ab}=(\mathcal{L}^{-1}f_{a},f_{b}),\qquad a,b\in\{1,2,3,o\},\qquad A=(m_{ij})_{1\leq i,j\leq 3}.

Self-adjointness gives
m_{ab}=m_{ba}
. If
\mathcal{R}
denotes the
reflection in
y_{2}
, then
\mathcal{R}\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{L}\mathcal{R}

and the invertibility of
\mathcal{L}
imply

\mathcal{R}\mathcal{L}^{-1}=\mathcal{L}^{-1}\mathcal{R}
; thus

\mathcal{L}^{-1}
preserves parity, and
m_{io}=m_{oi}=0
for

i=1,2,3
. We enclose
A
and
m_{oo}
by means of compact approximate
inverses and of their true whole-space residuals.

Tail Bounds for the Constraints

Let
\beta=1-(2\alpha_{1})^{-1}>0
, and suppose that the ground-state
certificate supplies, for
r\geq R_{Q}>0
,

|Q(r)|\leq C_{0}\frac{e^{-r}}{\sqrt{r}},\qquad|Q^{\prime}(r)|\leq C_{1}\frac{e^{-r}}{\sqrt{r}}.

For
z=4
and
R_{Q}=15
, the bounds of Section
E.1

allow the choices
C_{0}=4
and
C_{1}<3.694973
. Since both

e^{-y_{1}/(2\alpha_{1})}
and
\widetilde{\varphi}(y_{1})
are at most

e^{r/(2\alpha_{1})}
, we have

\displaystyle|f_{1}|

\displaystyle\leq C_{0}r^{-1/2}e^{-\beta r},

\displaystyle|f_{2}|+|f_{o}|

\displaystyle\leq 2C_{1}r^{-1/2}e^{-\beta r},

(E.60)

\displaystyle|f_{3}|

\displaystyle\leq(C_{0}+C_{1}r)r^{-1/2}e^{-\beta r}.

Setting

(a_{1},b_{1})=(C_{0},0),\quad(a_{2},b_{2})=(a_{o},b_{o})=(C_{1},0),\quad(a_{3},b_{3})=(C_{0},C_{1}),

we thus have
|f_{a}|\leq(a_{a}+b_{a}r)r^{-1/2}e^{-\beta r}
. If

L\geq R_{Q}>0
, polar integration gives

\|f_{a}\|_{L^{2}(r\geq L)}^{2}\leq 2\pi\bigl(a_{a}^{2}J_{0}(L)+2a_{a}b_{a}J_{1}(L)+b_{a}^{2}J_{2}(L)\bigr),

(E.61)

where

J_{0}(L)=\frac{e^{-2\beta L}}{2\beta},\qquad J_{k}(L)=\frac{L^{k}e^{-2\beta L}}{2\beta}+\frac{k}{2\beta}J_{k-1}(L),\quad k=1,2;

these identities follow by integration by parts.

Compact Cubic Splines and the Inverse Residual

Let
h=2L/N
and
x_{j}=-L+jh
, and define the cardinal cubic B-spline

b(t)=\begin{cases}(4-6t^{2}+3|t|^{3})/6,&|t|\leq 1,\\
(2-|t|)^{3}/6,&1\leq|t|\leq 2,\\
0,&|t|\geq 2.\end{cases}

For explicitly stored binary64 coefficients
c_{ij,a}
, interpreted
as exact real numbers, we set

u_{h,a}(y_{1},y_{2})=\sum_{i,j=1}^{N-1}c_{ij,a}b\!\left(\frac{y_{1}-x_{i}}{h}\right)b\!\left(\frac{y_{2}-x_{j}}{h}\right).

(E.62)

The spline
b
belongs to
C^{2}(\mathbb{R})
and vanishes outside

[-2,2]
; consequently, (
E.62
) is globally

C^{2}
, compactly supported in
[-L-h,L+h]^{2}
, and belongs to

H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
. On every knot rectangle, it is an exact tensor
cubic polynomial, and all polynomial derivatives used below are taken
within that rectangle; fourth derivatives across knot lines are not
assumed. The coefficients are proposed by a floating-point
sine-transform collocation solve: one requires the approximate equation

\mathcal{L}u_{h,a}=f_{a}
at the interior mesh nodes and solves the
resulting finite linear system. A discrete sine transform diagonalizes
the constant-coefficient tensor-grid part of this proposal system;
multiplication by the potential is evaluated at the nodes and couples
the sine modes. The resulting coefficients define the compact spline
in (
E.62
). Only its enclosed whole-space
residual below validates the inverse pairing; convergence of the
proposal solve alone is not a certificate.

For each
a\in\{1,2,3,o\}
, we define

\displaystyle r_{a}

\displaystyle=f_{a}-\mathcal{L}u_{h,a},\qquad\|r_{a}\|_{L^{2}}\leq\rho_{a},

\displaystyle B_{ab}

\displaystyle=(u_{h,a},f_{b})+(f_{a},u_{h,b})-\mathcal{B}(u_{h,a},u_{h,b}).

The bilinear form
\mathcal{B}
is the one defined by (
E.3
);
since
u_{h,a}\in H^{2}
, it equals
(\mathcal{L}u_{h,a},u_{h,b})
here.

Proposition E.8

(Quadratic residual bound)
.

Suppose
\operatorname{dist}(0,\sigma(\mathcal{L}))\geq\delta>0
.
Then, for every
a,b\in\{1,2,3,o\}
,

m_{ab}=B_{ab}+(\mathcal{L}^{-1}r_{a},r_{b}).

(E.63)

Consequently, any interval enclosure
[B_{ab}]
gives

m_{ab}\in[B_{ab}]+[-\rho_{a}\rho_{b}/\delta,\rho_{a}\rho_{b}/\delta].

(E.64)

Proof.

Since
f_{a}=\mathcal{L}u_{h,a}+r_{a}
, self-adjointness gives

\displaystyle m_{ab}

\displaystyle=(u_{h,a}+\mathcal{L}^{-1}r_{a},\mathcal{L}u_{h,b}+r_{b})

\displaystyle=(\mathcal{L}u_{h,a},u_{h,b})+(u_{h,a},r_{b})+(r_{a},u_{h,b})+(\mathcal{L}^{-1}r_{a},r_{b})

\displaystyle=B_{ab}+(\mathcal{L}^{-1}r_{a},r_{b}).

The spectral inverse bound and the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality then
imply
|(\mathcal{L}^{-1}r_{a},r_{b})|\leq\rho_{a}\rho_{b}/\delta
.
∎

Both
r_{a}
and
B_{ab}
are evaluated directly with the enclosed true

Q
,
W
, and
f_{a}
, so that all the profile uncertainty remains in the
integrands.

Certified Cell Quadrature

We subdivide each spline knot rectangle into rectangles
K
, with
center
z_{K}
, half-widths
d_{1},d_{2}
, and area
|K|=4d_{1}d_{2}
. For a

C^{2}
integrand
G
, the iterated midpoint theorem gives

\int_{K}G\in|K|\left([G(z_{K})]+\frac{d_{1}^{2}}{6}[\partial_{y_{1}}^{2}G(K)]+\frac{d_{2}^{2}}{6}[\partial_{y_{2}}^{2}G(K)]\right).

(E.65)

Indeed, in one dimension, Taylor’s formula with integral remainder
gives an average error
d^{2}G^{\prime\prime}(\xi)/6
on an interval of half-width

d
, and applying it successively in the two coordinates proves the
enclosure; the remainder kernels are nonnegative, so that derivative
ranges suffice, and no mixed derivative is needed in this formula. For
the residual, we set

e_{a,K}=\frac{d_{1}^{2}}{2}\sup_{K}|\partial_{y_{1}}^{2}r_{a}|+d_{1}d_{2}\sup_{K}|\partial_{y_{1}}\partial_{y_{2}}r_{a}|+\frac{d_{2}^{2}}{2}\sup_{K}|\partial_{y_{2}}^{2}r_{a}|.

Taylor’s formula writes
r_{a}
as its affine polynomial at
z_{K}
plus a
remainder of absolute value at most
e_{a,K}
, and the triangle
inequality in
L^{2}(K)
together with the vanishing odd moments of the
centered rectangle yields

\|r_{a}\|_{L^{2}(K)}^{2}\leq|K|\left(\sqrt{|r_{a}(z_{K})|^{2}+\frac{d_{1}^{2}|\partial_{y_{1}}r_{a}(z_{K})|^{2}+d_{2}^{2}|\partial_{y_{2}}r_{a}(z_{K})|^{2}}{3}}+e_{a,K}\right)^{2}.

(E.66)

Every quantity on the right-hand side is replaced by an interval upper
bound. The Hessian of
r_{a}
uses spline derivatives up to order four
